# Tiger Sisters on YouTube: Ideas by Year and Topic

Source: the 178 long-form videos on the Tiger Sisters YouTube channel (Cherie Brooke and Jean Luo). Transcripts came from the Tiger Sisters podcast feed, which carries the same episodes: Spotify-published transcripts where available, otherwise local Whisper transcription of the episode audio. Videos were matched to episodes by title and by exact duration. 105 videos yielded 1930 dated ideas. 73 videos have no transcript source, mostly pre-podcast vlogs and short clips from 2022 to 2024; they are listed at the end. Shorts (910) were excluded.

### Career strategy & promotions (15)

- **If you are graduating and don't know what you want to do, consulting is one of the top options to pursue because of the industry exposure it gives.** Cherie recommends it for new grads and undergrads considering internships precisely because it lets you learn many industries and ways of building strategy before committing. — [2024-08-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YuZD6_Z11Xo)
- **Employers and schools hire from consulting and finance as shorthand for rigorous training: the person has been vetted multiple times and will present and behave professionally even in unrelated roles.** Because getting an offer at McKinsey, Bain or Goldman is so competitive, the name serves as a proxy that the candidate has gone through 'boot camp' and their work will meet a high bar. _[McKinsey, Bain, Goldman Sachs]_ — [2024-08-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YuZD6_Z11Xo)
- **Your first five years out of school shape your professional habits permanently; as a hiring manager you can see someone's career upbringing in their work product.** Ex-consultants are consummately professional while startup-raised people are scrappy and fast but hand in memos with typos. Jean says she could never submit work with typos after years of 100-hour weeks being her North Star of quality, though whether typos matter depends on company culture. — [2024-08-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YuZD6_Z11Xo)
- **If getting into a top business school is genuinely your goal, it is legitimate to mold your career toward the feeder industries because that is simply where the odds are best.** Jean, the pragmatist, notes that schools rely on the vetting shorthand of those industries and that outsiders face structural disadvantages, so the truth is you are in a better position in one of the big three. — [2024-08-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YuZD6_Z11Xo)
- **Get your reps in early by taking on assignments that force you to interview senior people.** Cherie's Morgan Stanley internship required her to interview technologists and VPs for an internal resource directory, which trained her to talk to senior people and to see that she had something to offer. _[Morgan Stanley]_ — [2024-09-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q2D1agAvgkw)
- **Say yes to random low-stakes speaking opportunities; they become reps that prepare you for high-stakes ones.** Jean accepted several small speaking gigs without knowing what she was getting into (one while sick), and is now being flown out by reputable companies to speak on panels to investors managing trillions of dollars. — [2024-10-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zVmcq70kghU)
- **Hard work is a good starting point but not a panacea; treating it as one becomes a limiting belief because most of the world is not a meritocracy like school.** Immigrant parents lack context for the new social structure and assume you can hammer your way through anything; many things are out of your control. — [2024-10-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zVmcq70kghU)
- **Immigrant families push doctor, lawyer and engineer careers because those paths are grade-and-test meritocracies with few exogenous factors, unlike finance, tech or media.** Med school and law school admission come down to grades and the LSAT, which correlate with effort. In finance, technology and media, success requires an edge that comes from information and interpersonal relationships, which you cannot simply work harder to get. — [2024-10-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zVmcq70kghU)
- **Top performance is not just doing good work; it requires people who will shine a light on you and make sure your work is recognized.** Cherie cites having male allies in a male-dominated workplace; promotion depends not only on your boss buying in but on everyone on the promotion committee who has power over you, so perception matters. — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **It is very hard to be a top performer in the corporate world without the support of your boss, so find people who believe in you and never pin your whole self-view on one person.** Jean had one boss she would 'walk through walls' for and another who undermined her in ways she calls traumatic; when the boss relationship is bad, seek a second, third and fourth opinion from people you respect who can reflect back what you're doing well. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **If you are stuck with a bad boss in a political situation, either learn what you can from it or get out so you have agency to put yourself in a better one.** The hosts acknowledge much is out of your control and leaving is hard in today's job market, but that is the reality of the options. — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **Top performers ask for what they need, unapologetically but respectfully, because the worst outcome is a 'no' and the upside is far greater than not asking.** In her first job at Goldman, Jean agonized for weeks over a colleague who kicked her under the table before finally asking her VP to swap seats; he said yes immediately, she sat next to a normal associate, and her life improved '50X', leaving her wondering why fear and a scarcity mindset kept her from asking sooner. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **No one will care more about your career than you do, so you must advocate for yourself, especially in salary negotiations; a good boss is only a close second.** Being a top performer also means being compensated commensurately for it. — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **Corporate fulfillment comes from scale of influence, while entrepreneurial fulfillment comes from percentage of ownership over the outcome.** Jean led M&A worth hundreds of millions of dollars in corporate; at Sisters each sister is by definition 50% of the company's impact, i.e. more ownership of a smaller pie versus less ownership of a much larger one. — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)
- **Politicking and layers of abstraction drain builders in corporate roles; know whether you are a builder or a manager archetype.** Cherie, a computer scientist who identifies as a builder, was frustrated by PRDs, strategy docs, design reviews and approvals at big tech; now the sisters ship faster because they trust each other and 90% of the time approve each other's work. _[LinkedIn, Snapchat, Zynga]_ — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)

### Networking, communication & influence (29)

- **Speaking on a panel forces you to formulate opinions in real time, and speaking for yourself is a different, more nervous experience than representing a company.** Jean did her first public speaking since the pandemic on an XPRIZE AI panel about empathic, humanist AI; she had previously spoken in Vegas, Shanghai, Tel Aviv and Toronto on behalf of Snapchat. _[XPRIZE, Snapchat]_ — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zGuTX6a53LI)
- **Small women's gatherings where each person says what they are working on and how others can help are unusually warm, grounded and energizing forms of networking.** A 25-woman breakfast in Malibu across industries left Jean feeling uplifted and hopeful about what women can do. — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zGuTX6a53LI)
- **Whatever the age gap in a classroom, be kind and say hi; engaging warmly with strangers is a skill and a habit.** Cherie chats with cashiers and Uber drivers while Jean tends to dissociate; Jean says it is something she admires about her sister. — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zGuTX6a53LI)
- **'Networking' needs redefining: at business school it is simply friendship with people who share interests and want to help each other succeed.** The hosts find the word icky (LinkedIn as 'the networking platform', 'networking events') and say career talk comes up naturally because everyone thinks building companies is fun; Thursday-night beer pong league at GSB counts. — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0GAr7mNJpPk)
- **In large meetings where you are not a key player, multitasking is fine and back-channeling is actually part of doing the meeting well, but never go camera-off and away from keyboard without notice when you may be called on.** From the forced ranking of Zoom archetypes: roughly 90% of people multitask in 30-plus-person meetings; if you are in an important meeting you should be back-channeling; leaving without warning when the meeting leader needs you ranks as the worst offense. — [2024-08-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YuZD6_Z11Xo)
- **Improv and play classes build comfort with your body in space and make you unafraid of being cringe, which feeds public speaking.** Cherie took Stanford's 'The Spirit of Play' theater class on bringing playfulness into work and teams; she thinks it subconsciously helped her deliver a TED Talk and Jean credits her extemporaneous speaking ability. _[The Spirit of Play, Stanford, TED Talk]_ — [2024-08-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YFCLLvNPOH4)
- **When building community in a new city, say yes to invitations, especially the first or second time someone reaches out.** Cherie's friend Eric taught her that if you decline early bids people stop thinking to invite you; in Stanford GSB language, meet people's 'bids' for connection. Once established you can be pickier. _[Stanford GSB]_ — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **Create recurring events around your own interests; consistency is what turns acquaintances into community.** Jean's Bachelorette watch party became a 7-plus-year book club that is the cornerstone of her LA community; Cherie hosted weekly Monday poker nights with a core four women at Stanford. A recurring calendar hold gives everyone something to look forward to when life gets busy. — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **Back-channel before proposing group plans so someone endorses immediately and creates momentum.** Cherie learned from a work mentor that big executive meetings require back-channeling ahead of time, then applied it to group chats: line up a 'yes person' privately, propose the plan publicly, and have them heart it right away to avoid five hours of crickets. — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **Platonic friendships with the opposite sex bring different perspectives and, in practice, better access to investment deal flow.** Cherie's guy friends up-level her on personal finance; Jean says most of her unique investing opportunities came through male friend groups, and she is trying to change that with her fund Sunbird Ventures by getting women to share deals intentionally. _[Sunbird Ventures]_ — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **To make a friend, act as if the person is already your friend and do not feel embarrassed about reaching out too much.** From a Washington Post article ('Would you be mine? Could you be mine? Won't you be my friend?'); Jean recognized people who had done this to her and it worked through assumed familiarity and multiple points of contact. _[Washington Post]_ — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **Never use attacking language or assume another person's intent; you have no idea what is going on with them.** Example: a coworker, Jason, does not wave back on the street; instead of spiraling to 'Jason hates me', ask him and learn he was lost in thought after a meeting. This mindset helped Cherie let go because it is hardly ever about her. — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **Talking to executives gets easier when you remember they are just a person and connect human-to-human with genuine curiosity, not sucking up.** Cherie struggled at 22 when her self-worth was tied to what she said; repetitions and a mindset shift that even someone five levels above you is just a person made it less scary. — [2024-09-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q2D1agAvgkw)
- **Never go into a coffee chat cold; do the research so you ask 'level 10' questions only that person can answer, not 'level 1' questions you could Google.** The bare minimum is five minutes on the person's LinkedIn profile, especially when you are the one asking for the chat and for help. Cherie's other podcast, Cherie's Corner, is built on heavy preparation for interviewing industry titans. _[Cherie's Corner, LinkedIn]_ — [2024-09-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q2D1agAvgkw)
- **The most powerful way to talk to anyone is to find, or actively create, shared interests and shared experiences.** Cherie co-organized a three-week Stanford Women's Golf Clinic and a creator club at Stanford; Jean runs an annual retreat in Newport with about 20 HBS girlfriends. Corporate affinity groups and traveling together (a Stanford GSB staple) serve the same purpose. _[Stanford Women's Golf Clinic, Stanford GSB, Harvard Business School]_ — [2024-09-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q2D1agAvgkw)
- **Being the organizer of a cross-functional event is a low-stakes way to get known by name across teams.** Cherie organized a baking competition at LinkedIn twice, drawing engineers, designers and data scientists from sister teams; afterwards people she did not remember would approach her in the micro kitchen as the organizer. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2024-09-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q2D1agAvgkw)
- **To cultivate allies, have coffee with someone you admire, then pull them aside right after a shared meeting and ask for feedback on how you presented and how to have more influence next time.** Walk out of the meeting room beside them and ask; if they are rushing, say they can jot down three thoughts later. Jean, as a former manager, says she would find such a request thoughtful and receive it well. — [2024-09-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q2D1agAvgkw)
- **Harmless gossip and quick inside jokes cut through three layers of small talk and build connection; people who claim to never gossip are hurting their ability to connect.** Cherie bonded with a gym manager by mentioning a rumor about the gym's CFO, after which they greet each other with 'tea' jokes. A TikTok hack she endorses: make an inside joke with someone right away to befriend them. — [2024-09-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q2D1agAvgkw)
- **Statement pieces and things bought abroad start conversations without words because they carry a story you are excited to tell.** Jean wears a Van Gogh-print scarf from a Sydney thrift store as a top and has a $17 vintage leather jacket from a Paris thrift store; when complimented, adding 'I got it in Barcelona' opens the conversation. — [2024-09-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q2D1agAvgkw)
- **Memorabilia like golf-course hats, school gear, hotel caps and concert merch signal your identity and groups, inviting like-minded people to approach you.** Cherie owns roughly 50 pieces of Stanford clothing plus Barnard and Columbia gear; wearing it near Palo Alto reliably triggers conversations with alumni. Examples given include Bandon Dunes sweaters, Amangiri hats and a Chamois hat from Palisades Tahoe. _[Stanford, Barnard, Columbia, Bandon Dunes, Amangiri, Palisades Tahoe]_ — [2024-09-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q2D1agAvgkw)
- **Keep a personal 'board of advisors': different trusted friends for career, love life and other domains, because you can't see your own situation clearly.** When someone from Cherie's past resurfaced, she consulted several trusted friends; Jean noted their advice matched hers, showing they were high-quality friends with good judgment. — [2024-10-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zVmcq70kghU)
- **Podcasts let you 'eavesdrop' on smart friend groups; there is one for every niche interest.** Jean listens to 'Here to See It' (Bachelor recaps through a feminist, cultural-dissection lens) and 'All-In', which she secretly likes but finds a frustrating boys' club; the sisters want to build a women's version of that group-chat feel. _[Here to See It, All-In Podcast, The Bachelor]_ — [2024-11-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p30odvPhoPY)
- **When your friends know what you are building, your network becomes an infinite board of advisors with thousands of years of combined expertise across industries.** Because friends see their posts, 30-minute conversations regularly produce nuggets that could change the company's trajectory, and they discover hidden skills, like a fellow head of product who turned out to be a great marketer. — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **Conference networking is draining, so learn to exit conversations gracefully.** At the Snap Partner Summit, Cherie crashed by 3:30 after floating between conversation circles and they left before Rufus Du Sol played; both admit they need to get better at ending conversations with 'so great talking to you, I need to run'. _[Snap Partner Summit, Rufus Du Sol, Lala Milan]_ — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **At the executive level, influence depends on intuiting the true drivers, motivations and core beliefs behind another executive's behavior and shaping your approach to conversations accordingly.** Jean says you must deliberately take time to think about what each person actually wants before meetings in order to reach a broader meeting of the minds. — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **Offline community, such as alumni networks, is a grounding counterweight to life online.** Cherie served on the executive team of Columbia in Tech in San Francisco and is now organizing Columbia alumni in Los Angeles alongside Stanford alumni events; she enjoys bringing people together and calls it a safe space. _[Columbia in Tech, Stanford]_ — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **To make friends in your late 20s and 30s, openly tell existing friends the kind of people you want to meet; people genuinely want to connect you.** Jean has connected friends who are new or expecting moms with each other after they voiced that need; new life stages, interests or careers call for proactively seeking like-minded people. — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)
- **Seek out spaces built around shared experiences or activities, such as business school, a pottery class or a networking event, to cultivate friendships.** Cherie found a two-year MBA program with peers seeking the same experience extremely rich for friendships; activity-based settings produce fruitful conversations. — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)
- **The taxi-cab theory applies to friendship: an unreturned bid usually means the other person's light is off, not that it's personal.** Some people lack bandwidth for new relationships at a given time, so you have to put out enough bids to find someone in the same open mindset and not be discouraged by non-reciprocation. _[Taxi cab theory]_ — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)

### Money, investing & personal finance (7)

- **Financial independence is what finally frees you from needing family buy-in and lets you fund an investment period in yourself.** After 15 years in finance and tech optimizing for financial independence, Jean can bootstrap the sisters' company entirely with her own money; Cherie, nearing 30, adds that at some point family must trust that they raised you to make your own decisions. _[Tinx]_ — [2024-10-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zVmcq70kghU)
- **It is fine to love both thrift-store deals and heritage luxury; high-end craftsmanship deserves appreciation as art and history.** The sisters say they 'live for a deal' at Goodwill and consignment shops but refuse to hide that they love nice things too, so the episode includes a high-end section. _[Goodwill, Salvation Army]_ — [2024-11-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p30odvPhoPY)
- **Judge expensive purchases by cost per wear: a $700 item worn daily beats a $700 dress worn once a year.** Jean's Jacques Marie Mage sunglasses (handmade in Japan, limited editions like #358 of 450, popularized by the Kendall Roy actor on Succession) start around $700 but she wears them multiple times daily and cares for them better as a result. _[Jacques Marie Mage, Succession, Luxottica]_ — [2024-11-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p30odvPhoPY)
- **Fine jewelry is an investment piece amortized over a lifetime and passed to children, and it boosts confidence and starts conversations.** Cherie wears a Van Cleef Alhambra mother-of-pearl necklace ($2-3K, gift from Jean) almost daily and a small rose-gold Cartier Juste un Clou with diamonds; she protects them (off for workouts, showers, sleep) and says wearing something beautiful reminds you that you are beautiful. _[Van Cleef & Arpels Alhambra, Cartier Juste un Clou]_ — [2024-11-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p30odvPhoPY)
- **Consumers do fast fashion and intentional consumption at the same time, choosing where in their daily habits to express themselves based on their wallet.** People buy Shein for clothes but pay $7 for matcha at their favorite shop every week because it says something about them; vintage shopping is booming because it provides story, uniqueness and environmental clout at an affordable price. _[Shein, Loewe]_ — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **Share compensation data with peers at your level so you have data points to strengthen your salary negotiation narrative.** Cherie's LinkedIn associate product manager cohort, at her friend Logan's suggestion, filled out a Google Sheet with promotions, bonuses and salaries, a private Glassdoor; cite the data without naming individuals, because 'he got paid X so I should' is a bad look and her boss told her so. Jean once discovered a direct report had a higher base salary than she did. _[LinkedIn, Glassdoor]_ — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **Financial independence is the antidote to the powerlessness of early hardship, and the drive to be a top performer often comes from never wanting to be in that position again.** Jean rediscovered sophomore-year emails with her college financial aid office when she thought she'd have to drop out, worked many jobs (tutor, museum security guard, RA, research assistant), and says nothing will ever be as hard as that time of having no control; both sisters are driven so neither they nor their children face that again. — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)

### Entrepreneurship & startups (38)

- **Both schools now offer founder-lifecycle courses covering everything from co-founder chemistry to IPO or acquisition decisions.** Cherie took Formation of New Ventures at GSB with Jim Ellis, Patricia Nakache and Scott Brady; the HBS corollary is Scaling Tech Ventures with Jeff Bussgang, and entrepreneurship curriculum has expanded greatly since Jean graduated. _[Formation of New Ventures, Jim Ellis, Patricia Nakache, Scott Brady, Scaling Tech Ventures, Jeff Bussgang]_ — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0GAr7mNJpPk)
- **Secondhand retail is a serious business: Goodwill generates billions in revenue, not junk.** Cherie learned this preparing a Stanford case when the founder of ThredUp visited class; the hosts also praise the Salvation Army's twice-yearly $2.99 clothing sale. _[Goodwill, ThredUp, Salvation Army]_ — [2024-08-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MbHnwiOF8hA)
- **Business school can be two risk-free years to build a startup with a built-in downside case: if it fails, you still graduate with the degree.** Jean lacked the chutzpah to quit her job and applied partly because her GMAT score was expiring after five years; she chose HBS for its entrepreneurship push via the Harvard Innovation Lab, a reaction to Stanford's strength, planning to build in year one, go full time in summer, get customers and fundraise at graduation. _[HBS, Harvard Innovation Lab, Stanford]_ — [2024-08-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YFCLLvNPOH4)
- **Trying entrepreneurship in a supportive cohort taught Jean that she loves building companies but that the specific startup was not worth the next 5-10 years, which was itself a valuable result.** She worked on the startup heavily in year one at the expense of classes and social life, intentionally; she notes programs like Y Combinator and South Park Commons now offer similar cohort experiences outside school. _[Y Combinator, South Park Commons]_ — [2024-08-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YFCLLvNPOH4)
- **If you are going to be really stressed anyway, be stressed about something you deeply care about.** (Farah Jesani) That feeling, plus curiosity about why an $8-9 almond milk mocha is justified when coffee beans are a commodity, pushed Farah to explore the specialty beverage supply chain instead of waiting for the next corporate bonus. _[One Stripe Chai, Folgers]_ — [2024-09-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w6oGHVFGfEY)
- **Before quitting to start a business, go work the front line of that industry to learn how it actually operates.** (Farah Jesani) Farah's father, who made his daughters hold mall jobs in high school to understand money, told her to become a barista before opening a coffee shop; she did a barista training program at an old church coffee shop, which is where she discovered chai was served as pre-brewed concentrates and powders. — [2024-09-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w6oGHVFGfEY)
- **The idea you cannot stop thinking about is the business worth pursuing, and it need not start as a master plan.** (Farah Jesani) Farah could not sleep because no chai brand told the story through lived South Asian experience; it started as a side project tinkering on a recipe until customers made it a business, and she went full time in 2018. Jean notes founders usually rewrite history into a five-year plan. _[One Stripe Chai]_ — [2024-09-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w6oGHVFGfEY)
- **Getting into a cult-status retailer like Erewhon is a marketing play for brand recognition more than a profit channel.** (Farah Jesani) Erewhon's margins are squeezed and it goes through a distributor, but shoppers go there for curation and the staff champion brands. Farah applied through the black-box portal, declined the expensive marketing program, and a month later they offered a December end cap with the program thrown in because the product stood on its own. _[Erewhon]_ — [2024-09-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w6oGHVFGfEY)
- **Food service is a better channel than grocery retail for a small CPG brand: recurring B2B orders, bulk packaging and no slotting fees or free fills.** (Farah Jesani) One Stripe Chai is roughly 65-70% food service and retail and about 30% direct-to-consumer; grocery stores charge slotting fees to put you on the shelf, while cafes reorder whenever they run low. The pandemic forced the brand to add DTC with the Chai at Home blend. _[One Stripe Chai, Whole Foods, Chai at Home]_ — [2024-09-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w6oGHVFGfEY)
- **Meet customers where they are and make the packaging educational.** (Farah Jesani) When customers were stuck at home during COVID, Farah launched at-home blends; every bag teaches a word, such as Chai means tea and Gulab means rose. _[Gulab Chai]_ — [2024-09-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w6oGHVFGfEY)
- **Name products what they actually are in your own language, as with 'Haldi Doodh' instead of 'golden milk', because culture can go mainstream without being translated.** (Farah Jesani) Farah's analogy: gnocchi is not called potato pillows, and if you can pronounce Timothee Chalamet you can pronounce naan. After launch, non-South Asians adopted the term and other companies started calling their turmeric blends Haldi Doodh; boba went mainstream the same way. _[Haldi Doodh, boba]_ — [2024-09-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w6oGHVFGfEY)
- **A cofounder buyout works when you are willing to walk away and put the choice in their court: either you take it on alone or I do.** (Farah Jesani) Farah, the only one who had invested money, told her cofounder she would take her money off the table if they wanted to run it, otherwise she would buy them out; it closed cleanly in one to two months. Afterward she felt free to launch Chai at Home and Haldi Doodh and finally comfortable as a South Asian woman running a chai company. — [2024-09-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w6oGHVFGfEY)
- **Before a cofounder split, think through your ideal outcome, what you can afford, how far along the business is, and whether you can live without their expertise.** (Farah Jesani) Farah accepted a likely short-term struggle without her cofounder's skills because a clean break was worth it; she hopes never to repeat the process. — [2024-09-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w6oGHVFGfEY)
- **Female and brown founders are routinely not taken seriously, and the right response is to call it out and treat it as a filter for bad partners.** (Farah Jesani) A tech investor asked why her husband did not join One Stripe Chai, and a farm manager in India directed every question to her husband despite knowing it was her company. Farah now makes the snarky comment and concludes anyone who cannot see her value will not be part of her journey. — [2024-09-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w6oGHVFGfEY)
- **Representation compounds: seeing founders who look like you shortens the time it takes to believe you can do it.** (Farah Jesani) Growing up, Indra Nooyi was the one visible South Asian woman CEO; Farah says having no role model is why it took her so long to consider entrepreneurship, and she hopes her daughter Yara grows up seeing running a business as second nature. _[Indra Nooyi]_ — [2024-09-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w6oGHVFGfEY)
- **Many immigrant family businesses were startups in everything but name; they were just called small businesses and funded by bank or family loans rather than VC.** (Farah Jesani) 'Startup' only became a cool word after Farah graduated; her mother once ran a restaurant and her parents were scrappy entrepreneurs without the label. — [2024-09-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w6oGHVFGfEY)
- **Embrace your identity and intersectionality as a brand's biggest differentiator.** The hosts' first takeaway: competitors' chai concentrates may have been fine products, but they lacked the authentic story customers want to connect with, and Farah's lived heritage set One Stripe Chai apart. _[One Stripe Chai]_ — [2024-09-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w6oGHVFGfEY)
- **Lululemon's edge was fabric innovation (silver antimicrobial weave) but its colors run one or two seasons behind, while Alo is thin fabric marked up by branding.** The hosts argue activewear fabrics now come from the same manufacturers (even Costco), so the differentiation has shifted to cut, color and brand; Jean's Alo leggings pill and are only worn on long-haul flights. _[Lululemon, Alo Yoga, Costco]_ — [2024-11-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p30odvPhoPY)
- **Building in public with raw, founder-written emails and Slack screenshots is a fresh way to build brand community that people actually read.** Set Active's founder sends unedited stream-of-consciousness updates about launches, failures and sold-out colors; Jean reads them more than polished brand emails and calls it a company to keep an eye on. _[Set Active]_ — [2024-11-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p30odvPhoPY)
- **The sisters are launching a matcha/tea brand built in public, starting with matcha and expanding to tools and gift kits, and want community input.** They preview sourcing, story and behind-the-scenes; Cherie whisks matcha daily with a bamboo whisk and bowl from Japan as a soothing morning ritual and says the community is a core reason for building the company. _[Sisters Matcha]_ — [2024-11-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p30odvPhoPY)
- **Start with an ambitious long-term vision and make sure short-term goals ladder up to medium-term goals which ladder up to the long-term goal.** The hosts' stated vision is to build the 'new-age Disney', a media empire combining content, IP, commerce and experiences, with several hypotheses being tested in parallel toward that end. _[Disney]_ — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **Be unafraid to edit strategy and pivot early, even when it feels like a major change; 'strong convictions, loosely held'.** Their first plan was a Tiger Sisters reality TV show; after researching with LA agents and producers they concluded it was not viable in this market and let it go within about a week. Jean notes many companies she has worked with would have been better off pivoting earlier, and that corporate pressures (aligning people, morale, politics) make course changes much harder than for two founders managing only themselves. _[Tiger Sisters]_ — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **Be sensitive and reactive to market trends when positioning a company.** Every company now pitches itself as an AI company; four years ago it was web3, which nobody would claim today, and five years ago pitching as AI was less fruitful. — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **Build on your existing strengths rather than always starting as a newbie, while leaving room for a few bets in new territory.** Feedback they received was to leverage Cherie's year of podcasting and self-built audience, which led to the podcast; Sisters Matcha, a CPG product they know nothing about (supply chain etc.), is the deliberate opposite bet. _[Sisters Matcha]_ — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **Run the startup like a tech team: Trello boards with P0/P1/P2 priorities and an in-progress column, weekly sprint planning and standups on Monday, and a shared calendar for everything.** They keep three boards (podcast planning, broader startup/admin planning, and Sisters Matcha), assign each other tasks, and use the Monday session to knock out annoying operational tasks together. _[Trello]_ — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **Stealth mode is dead; building in public gets you help, engagement and rapid customer feedback that stealth startups miss.** Jean came from secretive cultures (Goldman's no-talking-in-the-elevator rule, siloed secret projects at Snap, similar at Apple) and found building in public uncomfortable, but people want to be involved because they find the work interesting, the same reason she advises three startups. Comments on the matcha canister's blue color (appetite suppressant vs versatile) gave immediate customer input. _[Goldman Sachs, Snap, Apple, Sisters Matcha]_ — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **Building in public requires protecting yourself: secure handles and domains before announcing names.** After announcing Sisters Matcha before creating the Instagram page, someone grabbed the account name; after a LinkedIn post announcing another name, the free domain was bought within two days and listed for $200; a sisters.com-style domain was offered to them for three quarters of a million dollars. _[Sisters Matcha, Instagram, LinkedIn]_ — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **It is fine to test several initiatives at once because each one teaches something invaluable and leads to opportunities that would never have happened otherwise.** Beyond the podcast and matcha they speak at conferences, write for Forbes, invest, build community and plan a Japan trip in May 2025; Cherie calls herself a 'multi-hyphenate' and admits it is hard to package for friends. _[Forbes]_ — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **They are building a new kind of enterprise, a 'content-to-commerce company' that builds and monetizes IP, brands and community, which has no acronym or playbook yet.** Jean says she has always worked on the cutting edge; Emma Chamberlain (lifestyle vlogs, podcast) and Mr. Beast (media company on new platforms) are adjacent but different flavors. Having total conviction and ownership in your own thing is like the best corporate 100-hour-week project 'times a million'. _[Emma Chamberlain, Mr. Beast]_ — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **Physical products have far slower turnaround than software: with software an idea can ship the next day and be used billions of times, while a physical canister means waiting on samples.** At Snap Jean could conceive an AR lens one day, have it built the next and in the app the day after; with matcha the digital logo work was fast but the physical prototype now sits in a wait cycle. _[Snap]_ — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **Software scales indefinitely once built, but an ultra-premium physical product is constrained by the supplier's operational capacity, which can be a deliberate fit for a small-batch brand.** Sisters Matcha is single cultivar, single estate, ceremonial grade from one family farm with three non-farmer employees; they are selling 300 canisters small batch, and scaling to 3 million would be a problem, but small batch matches their ethos versus influencer matcha brands made in giant factories. _[Sisters Matcha]_ — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **Differentiate a consumer product through storytelling, because every purchase is now 'ego-expressive' and people opt in with their dollars.** The matcha story is a 7th-8th generation family tea farm Cherie worked on, and Japan's aging population leaving farmers without successors; buying the matcha supports the regeneration of small family tea farming, a documentary-style angle no other brand is telling. _[Sisters Matcha]_ — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **The 'yassification' of everyday categories: consumables like olive oil have become heavily branded, artisanal and intentional over the last decade, partly because inflation makes people more deliberate about even small purchases.** Ten years ago nobody had a favorite olive oil brand; now people have favorite varietals (Jean's is Picual); younger consumers care about provenance and heritage, unlike the Forever 21/Zara generation. _[Forever 21, Zara]_ — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **Paper prototypes are a cheap way to iterate on a physical product design before manufacturing.** Cherie applied an engineering-class technique to the matcha canister; the sisters designed it themselves with principles guidance from her designer-turned-PM friend Ian rather than hiring an agency, and every design element has an intentional meaning. _[Sisters Matcha]_ — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **Most creators fail because they give up; the first 100 episodes of 20VC never broke 1,000 plays and now it gets 1,000 plays in 90 seconds.** Jean found Harry Stebbings' post a helpful benchmark while bemoaning that their episodes don't break 10K views, and saw it as a meta example of why sharing your vulnerable early struggles helps others keep going. _[Harry Stebbings, 20VC]_ — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **Co-founders are valuable because you can spiral at different times and talk each other out of it; being a solo founder means being strong for yourself always.** Cherie feels she must stay overly positive when an episode underperforms so as not to kill momentum; she recalls a co-founder breakup at her earlier startup Outdoor Pass that left her effectively solo. The sisters debate a friend's claim that three co-founders is the sweet spot for tie-breaks, with Jean pushing back that one person can end up ganged up on. _[Outdoor Pass]_ — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)
- **Buyer beware: in entrepreneurship you have massive control but you are also the sole point of failure, with no one to blame.** In corporate you can say a decision wasn't approved by the CFO or a designer ignored feedback; as founders every outcome is felt fully and belongs to you. — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)
- **At a lean startup, candidates who ask about work-life balance usually don't work out, according to a founder friend.** A Columbia-in-Tech co-founder said he has worked 100-hour weeks and weekends for three years; hires who asked about balance tended to underperform within three months. Jean doesn't find this crazy since Goldman Sachs, Zynga and Snap all expected working past midnight to 2-3 AM. _[Goldman Sachs, Zynga, Snap, Columbia in Tech]_ — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)

### MBA & elite education (33)

- **The average business school student enrolls at 27 to 29, typically four to five years out of undergrad.** Jean entered HBS at 26 turning 27 and Cherie entered Stanford GSB at 27 turning 28, both exactly five years after college. _[Harvard Business School, Stanford GSB]_ — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zGuTX6a53LI)
- **Apply to business school when your learning curve has flattened and you want a more diverse set of experiences.** After four and a half years in product management at LinkedIn, Jean told her boss she would either join a startup or go to business school; her VP had gone to GSB and a director to HBS, so it was culturally normal, and prep like the GRE took three and a half years in the background. _[LinkedIn, GRE]_ — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zGuTX6a53LI)
- **Deferred 2+2 programs admit you in undergrad and hold the seat while you work at least two years, and you can often defer even longer.** The HBS 2+2 admission rate is reportedly lower than the regular rate, so the committee is betting on potential rather than a work track record. _[HBS 2+2]_ — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zGuTX6a53LI)
- **Arriving at 24 with only two years of experience can be isolating: in case-method classrooms the richness of discussion comes from work stories, and classmates may condescend to or write off 'the baby of the class'.** People come to business school to learn from peers and quietly dismiss those they think have nothing to offer; the flip side is the youngest have the most to gain from everyone else. _[McKinsey, Bain, BCG]_ — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zGuTX6a53LI)
- **If you get into a 2+2 program, take three or more years in the workforce before enrolling so learning is experiential rather than purely academic.** The hosts' direct advice given the experience gap between 24 and 29 year olds. — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zGuTX6a53LI)
- **Going to business school in your mid-to-late 30s can be great if it is the right path, but you must tolerate the antics of 20-somethings for whom it is a two-year vacation.** The oldest classmates were about 36 to 38; some rolled their eyes at Thursday-night party buses to San Francisco, and many came with partners and children, which by definition creates a different experience. — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zGuTX6a53LI)
- **Stanford's one-year MSx program serves people about ten years into their careers, and there is a real if unspoken tension between MSx and MBA students.** MSx students (about 90 vs 420 MBAs) see MBAs as lazy vacationers, MBAs see MSx students as grade-focused try-hards, which clashes in shared group projects. _[Stanford MSx]_ — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zGuTX6a53LI)
- **Executive MBA programs, often company-sponsored and about a week long, are reportedly how HBS makes the majority of its revenue, essentially a B2B business.** Stanford also has a Distinguished Careers fellowship where people in their 60s apply, are vetted on their careers, and sit ungraded in classes like real estate and private equity. _[HBS Executive Education, Stanford Distinguished Careers Institute]_ — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zGuTX6a53LI)
- **The best business school classes are defined by professors who genuinely care about students, not just by content.** Jean's favorite HBS class was Founder's Dilemmas with Shikhar Ghosh, who built and sold $100M companies yet gave every student a book with a personalized note; the class became a close-knit community she still draws friends from. _[Founder's Dilemmas, Shikhar Ghosh, Harvard Business School]_ — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0GAr7mNJpPk)
- **Make the effort to get to know your professors in grad school; given the opportunity they will invest in you, including hosting students for dinners at their homes.** Jean's one regret from HBS was not knowing more professors, so she told Cherie to prioritize it at GSB; small classes make one-on-one relationships possible, unlike 300-person undergrad lectures. — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0GAr7mNJpPk)
- **HBS was worth it, but not for the expected reasons: the real payoff is a set of organic friendships with ambitious people who naturally peer-mentor each other.** Jean almost turned HBS down because trusted Silicon Valley mentors said it would waste valuable career time; seven years later most of her closest friends are from HBS and she expects to keep making friends-of-friends through it. _[Harvard Business School]_ — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0GAr7mNJpPk)
- **HBS relies entirely on the case method with cold-calling, which makes class preparation far more intense than at GSB where formats range from lectures to relaxed workshops.** Every HBS class of about 90 students requires reading a mini research paper about a company and being ready to be picked at random to argue, for example, why a CEO chose VC fund A over B; it was obvious and comical when someone had not read the case. _[Case study method]_ — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0GAr7mNJpPk)
- **The case method works because you put yourself in the protagonist's shoes and decide what you would do, accumulating hundreds of snapshots of business decisions.** After the discussion you learn what the CEO actually did and why, then reflect on why you chose differently and what to apply going forward. _[Case study method]_ — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0GAr7mNJpPk)
- **Credentials matter more if you were not 'born on third base'; elite alumni networks and even a .edu email open doors for people without inherited access.** Analogy from one of Cherie's professors; the hosts joke about getting a PhD just to keep the .edu address. — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0GAr7mNJpPk)
- **Plan business school by working backwards from the goal in a spreadsheet with columns for age, milestone and deadline.** Cherie's 'Adult Planned-Out Life' Google Sheet: graduate in 2024 means enroll in 2022 at 26-27, submit applications more than a year before that, and take the GRE/GMAT, study and secure recommendation letters even earlier. _[GRE, GMAT, Stanford GSB, Harvard Business School]_ — [2024-08-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MbHnwiOF8hA)
- **To understand which industries feed into top MBAs, flip the question and ask what an admissions director is optimizing for: business schools are institutions that need successful, donating alumni.** Schools want alumni who become heads of state or make enough money to give back, so they look to industries that train people to think strategically and lead. Admissions is portfolio construction: enough conventional 'sure bets' (e.g. finance) plus some potential outlier successes in each class. — [2024-08-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YuZD6_Z11Xo)
- **The three main feeder industries into business school today are management consulting (MBB), finance (especially investment banking and private equity) and big tech.** Within finance the hosts list banking, PE, VC, hedge funds, long-only, fixed income, endowments and less commonly corporate finance; big tech means the FAANG companies plus Microsoft, which has taken off since Azure and Satya Nadella. Tech is treated separately from startups. _[McKinsey, Bain, BCG, FAANG, Microsoft, Satya Nadella]_ — [2024-08-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YuZD6_Z11Xo)
- **Consultants are prized by business schools because they have seen a breadth of industries and are trained to structure and enumerate their thinking into digestible recommendations.** The running joke in Jean's HBS class was consultants starting every answer with 'when I was at McKinsey' and 'there are three things.' In their first two years consultants rotate across oil, healthcare and tech projects and learn to pull strategic themes across them. _[McKinsey, Bain]_ — [2024-08-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YuZD6_Z11Xo)
- **Finance signals to admissions that you are smart (quantitative skills) and that you have survived a high-pressure environment with an expectation of excellence.** Jean recalls that in finance everyone believed every deliverable was the end of the world even though half the deadlines were self-imposed; the quantitative bar is an indicator of academic prowess rather than a predictor of success. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2024-08-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YuZD6_Z11Xo)
- **Tech became a major MBA feeder in the last 10-15 years because schools saw outsized startup outcomes and wanted to recruit the next Mark Pincus.** Pincus, founder and CEO of Zynga (Farmville), went to HBS, which is where Jean worked before business school. Both HBS and GSB have expanded their startup curricula, drawing big-tech employees who want a traditional business foundation and exposure to startups. _[Mark Pincus, Zynga, Farmville, Harvard Business School, Stanford GSB]_ — [2024-08-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YuZD6_Z11Xo)
- **Non-traditional applicants (energy, healthcare, CPG, military, teachers) should still apply but must craft a narrative showing strategic thinking, team leadership and the impact they want to create.** CPG is only about 5% of the class at Stanford GSB. Be very specific about why you need business school for your goals and why each particular school, e.g. Stanford's entrepreneurship program and cross-registration at the d.school, so admissions doesn't have to extrapolate what they get out of admitting you. _[Stanford GSB, Stanford d.school]_ — [2024-08-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YuZD6_Z11Xo)
- **If your industry lacks MBA role models, find alumni who made the leap and talk to them, because your seniors may not even know how to write a compelling business school recommendation.** At LinkedIn many of Jean's directors and VPs had MBAs, so she could see the path; friends in traditional engineering who followed their passion were not set up for top programs partly because of the recommendation X-factor. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2024-08-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YuZD6_Z11Xo)
- **The most noticeable change from business school is confidence and groundedness that third parties can hear in your voice.** Cherie's best friends and classmates' former bosses said they sounded way more self-assured after; she attributes it to two years of reflection at GSB on who you are, what you care about and how you want to leave your impression on the world. Former coworkers scoff that 'a quarter million dollars for confidence' does not sound worth it. _[Stanford GSB]_ — [2024-08-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YFCLLvNPOH4)
- **Business school teaches you enough to be dangerous in every field, so you can spot when something is totally off even if you never practice it yourself.** Jean will never be an accountant but knows from her accounting class when numbers are wrong; a breadth of finance, econ, entertainment and accounting classes makes you more knowledgeable and confident as a person. — [2024-08-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YFCLLvNPOH4)
- **Knowing the vocabulary of an industry puts you light years ahead and lets you walk into meetings unafraid and unlikely to be taken advantage of.** Cherie's commercial real estate investing class, a 180 from her software and VC background, taught her to ask about foot traffic, vacancy rates, mall versus strip mall and proximity to hotels or airports if the sisters ever opened a cafe, even while still hiring a real estate lawyer or agent. — [2024-08-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YFCLLvNPOH4)
- **Go into business school intentionally with a list of specific gaps to fill, including social and community gaps, not just academic ones.** After a lonely pandemic year living with her mom, Cherie, a longtime LinkedIn product manager with brief VC experience, wanted to grow socially, meet professors and add tools to her tool belt outside her existing expertise. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2024-08-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YFCLLvNPOH4)
- **Business school differs from other grad programs because everyone is bought into the social and community aspect as a core part of the value, not just academics.** Jean's best friends at Harvard Law and the Harvard Ed School had far more academically oriented experiences; at HBS it is expected that you never eat dinner alone, which is why people joke MBAs party all the time. _[HBS, Harvard Law School]_ — [2024-08-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YFCLLvNPOH4)
- **Introverts can thrive in business school by budgeting their emotional and mental capacity, including deliberately eating some dinners alone.** Cherie ate many dinners alone to recover from full days of socializing; she says she has many introvert friends who found their balance, and this is worth self-reflection before applying. — [2024-08-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YFCLLvNPOH4)
- **The $250,000 tuition plus lost salary is worth it if you believe in yourself, because a long career lets you recoup the money and an environment where your only job is to learn is rare.** Jean does not like to think about the opportunity cost because it is depressing, but she almost always says yes when people ask whether to go; a future episode could cover who should not go. — [2024-08-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YFCLLvNPOH4)
- **Stanford GSB's Interpersonal Dynamics ('Touchy Feely') teaches a repair vocabulary: share observed behavior and how it made you feel, and never 'cross the net' to assume intent.** The course puts about 12 people in an unstructured T-group where people comment on how others' disclosures landed; people cry, it is ranked first, and skipping it is questioned. Because Cherie's friends took the same class, they share a language for making up after conflict. _[Stanford GSB, Interpersonal Dynamics, Touchy Feely]_ — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **A traditional credential like an engineering degree buys family comfort and employability even if you take a less conventional path.** Cherie originally aimed to be a doctor and interned at a Mount Sinai neuroscience lab slicing mouse brains before choosing computer science and LinkedIn; their mom still proudly calls her 'my little engineer' whenever she fixes a printer. _[Mount Sinai, LinkedIn, Morgan Stanley]_ — [2024-10-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zVmcq70kghU)
- **Business school is a long-term play whose payoff comes from people and experiences over the fullness of time, not just the first job out.** Cherie is about to collaborate with an MBA classmate on Sisters merchandise, a connection she never anticipated; in the soft 2024-2025 hiring market fewer grads get finance, tech and consulting jobs and more turn to entrepreneurship. — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)
- **The best time to attend business school is when the economy is soft, because the opportunity cost of being off the job market for two years is lowest.** Jean contrasts this with the 2020-2021 hiring frenzy when you wanted to be on the market; being in school during a downturn means building skills to rejoin at a higher level rather than 'convalescing'. She qualifies that some people attend for a discrete short-term pivot. — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)

### Leadership & management (6)

- **Infrastructure and behind-the-scenes work is thankless because it is only noticed when something breaks, so its success criteria are effectively reversed.** Cherie draws the analogy from doing all the podcast's tech setup to infra and security teams and backend PMs in tech who get all the blame when something goes wrong and no credit when it runs smoothly. — [2024-08-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YuZD6_Z11Xo)
- **Owner-operators who 'just do it' themselves build no boundaries, so hire operations help you trust before life forces you to.** (Farah Jesani) One Stripe Chai made products in house, packed orders and built pallets itself; pregnancy made that impossible and having someone take over operations was essential. — [2024-09-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w6oGHVFGfEY)
- **Intelligence gets you into the room, but emotional intelligence is the skill that takes you to the executive or VP level.** Cherie cites studies naming EQ as the number-one skill for advancement and Travis Bradberry's 'Emotional Intelligence 2.0' as a way to test and grow it; the best performers read the room and understand how others feel. _[Travis Bradberry, Emotional Intelligence 2.0]_ — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **Once you lead a team, the majority of your time goes to people problems, which will be either the death of you or the making of you.** The hosts say team dynamics start to show at about five people and dominate a manager's time at twelve or more. — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **Manage teams with a T-shaped philosophy: double down on each person's one or two standout skills and make them the team's tutor for it, rather than forcing everyone to be well-rounded.** Jean ran her teams this way so everyone was a top performer in a niche; being acknowledged for a strength makes people more receptive to improving weaknesses, seniors learn from juniors, and it creates more surface area than the zero-sum game of everyone chasing well-roundedness, though basic role requirements still must be met. _[T-shaped person]_ — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **Radical ownership, the mindset that if you see a problem it is yours to figure out, is rare in big tech and a must-have when hiring.** Jean refuses to work with people who pass the buck; Zynga's motto 'be your own CEO' embodied this, and she saw it in the best PMs and engineers but also many who said 'not my problem'. Cherie references Kim Scott's Radical Candor by analogy. _[Zynga, Radical Candor, Kim Scott]_ — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)

### Power, status & office politics (9)

- **For a woman in male-dominated tech and finance, an elite MBA is an 'unspoken totem' that nobody in the room can take away and that gets people to take the call.** Jean quotes her former CMO; people may know nothing else about you, but Harvard or Stanford on the resume adds credibility to everything you say. — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0GAr7mNJpPk)
- **People are often looking for a reason to dismiss a woman in business, and a grad degree makes you less dismissable; it is wrong, but it is the reality.** Cherie says the stamp adds immediate credibility with people who have never met you, and hopes the dynamic changes. — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0GAr7mNJpPk)
- **Seek out non-work interactions with senior people, like company videos, baking competitions or organizing events, so they see you as a person and not a robotic minion.** At Goldman Sachs Jean produced the group's SNL-style holiday music video, getting MDs and VPs to lip sync Taylor Swift, and won the annual baking competition with sticky toffee pudding cupcakes. She notes those moments are what she remembers most from Goldman despite being 1 percent of her time. _[Goldman Sachs, Lloyd Blankfein]_ — [2024-09-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q2D1agAvgkw)
- **In male-dominated fields, the number one tip for women is to find male allies who will pull you into conversations and coach you to take the 'jump ball'.** Cherie's boss Albert back-channeled with her, coached her that an unanswered question in a meeting is a jump ball anyone can grab, and DMed her on Slack mid-meeting to take questions she knew the answer to, sometimes setting up a layup for her. — [2024-09-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q2D1agAvgkw)
- **Be aware that you are perceived as a category, not just as yourself; young women get 'ingenue' leeway that later turns into being seen as competition.** Jean's 'cold hard truth': a workplace version of the Manic Pixie Dream Girl means a fresh grad is not seen as a threat and seniors will give honest feedback, but the same tactics land differently once you are more senior. Whether fair or not, perception is often reality. _[Manic Pixie Dream Girl]_ — [2024-09-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q2D1agAvgkw)
- **Manage your reputation against the stereotype people hold of you, and surprise them by breaking it in a good way.** A friend who became one of the youngest partners at a VC firm deliberately wanted a wedding ring to seem older among partners 20-35 years his senior. The hosts note married men with kids get an automatic 'responsible' halo that women do not receive, and may even face the reverse. — [2024-09-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q2D1agAvgkw)
- **People in the workplace are far more self-oriented than a new grad assumes, and some will actively undermine you, so learn this early rather than through a painful lesson.** Jean says she was naive for years, assuming everyone was working toward the team's stated goal, without considering colleagues' private incentives that run counter to the team's goals or to her success; it is usually not personal, you may just be in their way. — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **In political corporate environments, people's stated desires may overlap only about 15 percent with their true desires, so learn to read what is under the surface.** The hosts say that at startups and small companies everyone is aligned on company success and there is less obfuscation, but past a certain size everything becomes political and most people will work at a big company at some point. — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **The meritocracy formula of school (study hard, ace the SATs, get into a good school) does not carry over to the corporate world, which has variables nobody teaches you about.** Both hosts entered the workforce with 'rose-colored glasses' and were hit hard because their family had no corporate background and no one in college explains how corporations actually function; politics exists even in academia over first-name authorship and grants. — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)

### Relationships, dating & marriage (25)

- **Pouring time and emotional bandwidth into ushering best friends into marriage while you are in an in-between period creates a dissonance worth noticing, so take some time for yourself.** Cherie is bridesmaid for two best friends getting married while she is still figuring out her own new chapter. — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zGuTX6a53LI)
- **A friend referral is a more serious and safer path to a partner than dating apps because the person is already vetted by someone who knows you both.** Jean used dating apps until 2015 and is now hoping to meet people organically; Cherie notes she has never been set up and would love to be, while cautioning that a stranger's DM recommendation is risky. _[Hinge]_ — [2024-08-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=V2gOF7NC6BI)
- **Meeting a stranger at a bar happens far less now because people are on their phones and have so many touch points on Instagram.** Neither sister has ever dated a random person met while out; all of Jean's serious relationships came through school or work. — [2024-08-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=V2gOF7NC6BI)
- **After a date, ask whether you had fun because the other person was engaging or because you yourself are engaging.** Cherie's girlfriends challenged her after a fun first date; Jean recalls dates with a car-collecting investment banker (Michelin restaurant, Scuderia) who was so boring she realized she was the entertainment. — [2024-08-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=V2gOF7NC6BI)
- **Playfulness and laughter should be a core tenet of a relationship, even at 50 or 60; someone who does not take themselves seriously is more fun.** Cherie's last relationship felt like high school with constant giggling, a contrast to her past pattern of attractive but stoic partners; your partner is the number one person you talk to and build a life with, so they should be as fun as you. — [2024-08-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=V2gOF7NC6BI)
- **The question 'how do you balance work and dating' falsely implies they are separate buckets; for people working 40-100 hours a week they become one and the same.** Proximity and repeated exposure build interest, which is why people end up dating coworkers. — [2024-08-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=V2gOF7NC6BI)
- **Do not date coworkers, especially on your immediate team; treat colleagues as a pool of people you could date after one of you leaves the company.** Both have dated coworkers and it did not work out; seeing an ex in meetings every day is stressful. Jean's more positive relationships came from people she met through work but dated only a year or more later. — [2024-08-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=V2gOF7NC6BI)
- **Look for a partner who challenges you physically and intellectually and from whom you learn, in a relationship safe enough to admit you do not understand something.** Cherie's ex, a strong snowboarder, took her down expert runs and coached her through; her last partner pushed her to understand economic markets without making her feel dumb because of mutual trust and respect. — [2024-08-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=V2gOF7NC6BI)
- **Know your own dating style: some people are slow-burn and date only after long platonic familiarity, others jump in fast.** Jean says nearly everyone she dated seriously she had known and hung out with platonically for a month to years first; Cherie says she is the opposite. — [2024-08-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=V2gOF7NC6BI)
- **The power-couple ideal, two influential high-status people strategizing together, typically favors the man in heterosexual relationships because the woman steps back to invest in the family.** Holding age, potential and performance constant, the woman usually takes the step back; the famous examples (Michelle and Barack Obama, Serena Williams and Alexis Ohanian) mostly became couples only after both reached career peaks. _[Michelle and Barack Obama, Serena Williams and Alexis Ohanian]_ — [2024-08-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=V2gOF7NC6BI)
- **Someone who has survived trauma can get put on a pedestal so you read maturity into everything they do; beware the 'I can fix him' rose-colored glasses.** Jean dated a man who beat cancer in college and assumed he was mature and thoughtful, missing red flags; she sees Bachelorette Jenn Tran doing the same with Army Ranger Marcus, who grew up in foster care and would need years of therapy she should not have to take on. _[The Bachelorette, Jenn Tran, Marcus]_ — [2024-08-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=V2gOF7NC6BI)
- **Friendships are for different seasons and different reasons; expecting them all to be evergreen sets you up for disappointment.** Jean's biggest lesson from 35 years: you and your best friend from ten years ago are both different people, so unless you grow in parallel you will not be friends in the same way, and that is okay. The sisters lost touch with childhood friends after moving from Long Island to California. — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **Choose close friends you genuinely respect in at least one specific way, because you are the product of the five people closest to you.** Jean's closest girlfriends are mostly HBS classmates she never really knew during school (a class of about 1,000); she recommends asking whether there is one thing you respect and learn from in each friend. Since a romantic partner is only one of the five, platonic friends arguably matter more. _[Harvard Business School]_ — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **Group trips and novel shared experiences deepen friendships and romantic relationships alike.** Traveling shows different sides of people, and experiencing something for the first time together creates a serotonin spike; Jean notes The Bachelor engineers skydiving and bungee dates for exactly this reason. Cherie tries to plan a new group experience intentionally every year. _[The Bachelor]_ — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **Friendship breakups are more painful than romantic ones because you invest for years and never anticipate an ending, and most people avoid closure.** Citing a New York Magazine/The Cut article, most friendship breakups are not clean and get no conversation; both sisters' most painful breakups were in college when neither party had the vocabulary or courage to address the hurt. _[The Cut, New York Magazine]_ — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **Stop expecting friends to show up exactly the way you do; adjust expectations per person rather than holding everyone to your own standard.** Jean, deeply loyal and conscientious, used to be hurt by flakes until a friend showed her she double-books plans with a chronically canceling friend and stays unoffended. Friends for different reasons means a fun event friend need not be a dinner-with-parents friend. — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **When people tell you who they are (e.g., how they split bills), temper your own behavior to match, or resentment builds and becomes conflict.** A friend who Venmo-charges $4.55 for a split Uber is signaling they are stringent; if Jean kept treating expecting reciprocity that never comes, she would set herself up for disappointment. Unresolved resentment is the root of friendship conflict, and money comes up in every friendship. _[Venmo]_ — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **Be selective about where your genuine energy goes and look for reciprocity, especially as you get older.** Cherie was less discerning in college but now gives deep care only to friends where love and care flow both ways, since one-sided 100% effort friendships are draining. — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **Repairing a friendship follows steps: commit to talk, state the observed behavior, say how it made you feel, listen and acknowledge, then commit to a way forward.** The approached party must receive the bid, confirm or deny intent, and acknowledge the hurt even if they disagree; you can also request a specific behavior change. Repair of lost trust may take many conversations and changed behaviors, not one 45-minute talk. _[Interpersonal Dynamics]_ — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **Swallowing a grievance is the short-term easy path, but it breeds resentment and growing distance.** Despite being conflict-avoidant, Cherie set aside time to talk through friction with close friends; part of repair is also not harboring resentment afterward. — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **In dating, communication starts with energy: being open to receive and radiating openness brings people to you before a word is spoken.** Jean noticed that after her energy shifted (and she stopped wearing an engagement ring) people began approaching her in the same places as before. You can read whether someone 'glimmers' before they speak. — [2024-09-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q2D1agAvgkw)
- **Banter is attractive because humor demonstrates both intelligence and the EQ to know what will land with a specific person; it cannot be faked.** Leading with jokes also lets you read a person quickly by which references they pick up. If you are not naturally funny, watch comedy to learn what you find funny, and use self-deprecating humor or punch up, never down. — [2024-09-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q2D1agAvgkw)
- **After a breakup, watching friends live the life you would have had is a trippy grief, but that life is still possible with someone else.** Jean describes seeing friends do the Europe-and-weddings summer she used to do with her ex-fiance as 'remnants of the life that never will be'; Cherie reframes it as a life still possible with a different person. — [2024-10-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zVmcq70kghU)
- **If you have lost trust in a friend, it is for a good reason, and rebuilding requires both parties giving 100%, not 50/50.** Jean's instinct is to write off a friendship once trust is gone; Cherie believes repair is possible with time, work and tears, but the bigger red flag is the other person avoiding the conversation and not being equally invested. — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)
- **Ask whether a breach of trust was circumstantial or endemic to the person's personality, because that predicts whether it will happen again.** A one-time situational lapse can be repaired; a trait the person refuses or cannot reasonably be expected to change is a different matter. — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)

### Family, childhood & siblings (10)

- **Representation changes what gets discussed: the first Asian Bachelorette brought family trauma and self-confidence to a franchise historically white, Christian and traditional.** Jenn Tran's openness about a father who abandoned her made the season deeper and more relatable than prior seasons centered on breakups and infidelity. _[The Bachelorette, Jenn Tran]_ — [2024-08-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=V2gOF7NC6BI)
- **Immigrant families with only daughters tend to place different, often positive, expectations on the women, especially the eldest.** Jean's nascent theory, endorsed by Farah, is that without sons, daughters are pushed toward self-sufficiency and setting an example; Farah's dad wanted them able to take care of themselves if he died early and not take their privilege for granted. — [2024-09-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w6oGHVFGfEY)
- **Growing up as one of the only Asian kids in a white, affluent Long Island community was painful but taught the sisters to be comfortable with people different from them, a strength you can opt into by leaving your comfort zone.** Jean contrasts California friends who grew up mostly around Asians and still do not feel fully comfortable talking to white people. Jean's view: the world is the world, and you cannot change it without becoming a person of influence within it; leaning into that discomfort helps your career and widens your worldview. — [2024-09-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q2D1agAvgkw)
- **A chaotic immigrant childhood produces a chip on the shoulder that becomes the drive to strive and survive.** The sisters are seven years apart, children of divorce, raised on Long Island largely by a strong single mother in an environment they call 'very chaotic, and that's sugar coating it.' They say much of their personality came from adapting and surviving, and it made them extremely close. — [2024-10-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zVmcq70kghU)
- **Children of immigrants feel that every achievement is 'making good on a promise' to the people who sacrificed before them.** Cherie reads a Duke Chronicle quote from student Susan Cheminor: studying for a test is keeping a promise not to let prior sacrifices go to waste. Parents often gave up guaranteed upper-middle-class lives at home to arrive with '$5 in their pockets.' _[Duke Chronicle]_ — [2024-10-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zVmcq70kghU)
- **The stories immigrant families pass down are about sacrifice, loss, hard work and duty, never love or laughter, and those narratives shape how you feel about your history.** Cherie finds it sad that she's never heard family stories of fun and wants to change that going forward, while acknowledging the stories are also about resilience and grit. — [2024-10-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zVmcq70kghU)
- **'Bicultural straddling' names the constant adaptation between two cultures that generates guilt for first-generation children.** From a Washington Post piece 'But what will people say?': being very American at school in a mostly white neighborhood, then Chinese at home; friends being friends with their parents versus the obedience-based parent-child dynamic in immigrant homes. _[Washington Post, bicultural straddling]_ — [2024-10-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zVmcq70kghU)
- **Children of immigrants over-explain adult life decisions to family because every choice feels imbued with 100 years of others' investment in you.** Jean had to justify going into finance (not one of the three accepted jobs), then justify leaving Goldman Sachs for a barely-public mobile games company. This stems from communal versus individualistic decision-making cultures; the US is the most individualistic. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2024-10-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zVmcq70kghU)
- **Tea is a consumable that doubles as a ritual and a universal, healthy gift, rooted in Asian family culture.** Herbal tea at night signals wind-down; Harney & Sons gift sets run roughly $25-60; every dim sum meal begins with a tea choice like chrysanthemum. Cherie also worked on a Japanese tea farm. _[Harney & Sons]_ — [2024-11-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p30odvPhoPY)
- **An immigrant or first-generation upbringing of deferring to adults and authority figures can hinder your career because you don't learn to talk to seniors as equals or question them.** Jean contrasts herself with white friends who grew up 'shooting the shit' with their parents' friends; Cherie wishes she had questioned teachers more in school instead of absorbing everything unquestioningly. — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)

### Mindset, confidence & psychology (18)

- **Framing a hard period as temporary lets you sit more comfortably in the discomfort and focus on what the time is good for, like working on yourself.** Jean applies Sheryl Sandberg's three P's, it's not personal, not pervasive, not permanent, to Cherie's post-MBA transition. _[Sheryl Sandberg, three P's]_ — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zGuTX6a53LI)
- **Get things out of your head and onto paper or a document; unwritten ideas swirl, stress you and disappear.** Cherie's parting advice: writing everything down is how she stays efficient and how she manifests and visualizes; printing lists and putting them in your closet or bathroom keeps them in your face. — [2024-08-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MbHnwiOF8hA)
