- **A deep-seated fear of being a burden can make you doubt whether even close friends want to celebrate you; name it and let friends reassure you.** Cherie spiraled when two friends couldn't attend her birthday in San Francisco; her friend Maddie told her 'you're not in the way, you are the way,' and the birthday dinner made her feel loved. — [2024-08-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MbHnwiOF8hA)
- **A friendly book race with a friend or sibling is effective motivation to read again after a dry spell.** The hosts log every book and compete on annual count; Jean has done this for years and runs an HBS girlfriends' book club. Recent reads: Kevin Kwan's 'Lies and Weddings' (A-minus) and Maye Musk's 'A Woman Makes a Plan' (strong B). _[Kevin Kwan, Lies and Weddings, Maye Musk, A Woman Makes a Plan]_ — [2024-08-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MbHnwiOF8hA)
- **Be honest about the gap between your 'old type' and the type you say you want; the ideal is the Venn diagram of dependable and playful.** Cherie says she wants a funny, witty, fit nerd but her Bachelorette favorite is the stoic, sturdy Marcus, the same archetype as her exes; she realizes she likes responsible, dependable people but wants to evolve toward someone who is both. — [2024-08-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=V2gOF7NC6BI)
- **A friendship breakup is usually not about you; look at what is going on in the other person's life and whether it is their pattern.** Jean says the 'it's not you, it's me' trope is actually true for many friendship endings, and though it is hard to remember in the moment, it is what she would tell anyone going through one. — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **Use a feelings chart to label emotions precisely, because you cannot discuss a feeling you cannot name.** Cherie keeps a feelings chart (like Hoffman's feeling list, 50-plus words) above her desk and suggests both parties can bring it into a repair conversation. She recently had tearful repair conversations with two close friends and the friendships came out stronger. _[Hoffman feeling list]_ — [2024-09-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gu4yFYM90j0)
- **Loneliness after moving to a new city passes; the pendulum swings, so don't over-index on the low moment.** Cherie's Season 1 thorn was profound loneliness after moving to LA; now her social battery is so full she has dinners every night and needs to schedule rest. — [2024-10-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zVmcq70kghU)
- **The 'my trauma will never compare to my ancestors' mindset is toxic: 'I didn't live through war or starvation and I still can't get into Yale.'** Jean admits she thought this way for a long time; both agree it puts enormous pressure on you and isn't feasible for mental health, even though it is common among minority peers. _[Yale]_ — [2024-10-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zVmcq70kghU)
- **Whether your motivation comes from guilt and duty or from positive internal drive doesn't change the results, but it matters for your mental health.** The sisters credit their inherited pressure with the massive ambitions behind the podcast ('we want to change the world with what we're doing'), while questioning whether people without that chip need a different one to have the same edge. — [2024-10-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zVmcq70kghU)
- **Money can't buy happiness, but it can buy confidence, and confidence is a building block ('the amino acid') of happiness.** The hosts frame their favorite things not as knick-knacks but as items that weave into daily life, make life better and make them feel more put together. — [2024-11-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p30odvPhoPY)
- **Scent is transformative: perfume can change your mood, make you feel confident for a presentation, and mark a period of your life like a photograph.** Jean wore Jo Malone Orange Blossom to set the episode's vibe and uses Wood Sage & Sea Salt when she needs to feel most confident; both note an ex's cologne can trigger a visceral reaction years later. _[Jo Malone, Byredo Bal d'Afrique]_ — [2024-11-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p30odvPhoPY)
- **Persistence is a free trait available to anyone: if you don't get an answer, a no-answer is not an answer yet, so keep emailing until you get a response.** As a Columbia freshman, Cherie emailed Dr. Sia's microfluidics lab three or four times (she remembers six) with no reply until he finally invited her in for an interview; repeated follow-ups demonstrate determination before you've ever had an interaction with someone. _[Columbia University, Sia lab]_ — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **Traits like determination and a growth mindset cannot be developed by sitting still; they are earned by being rejected repeatedly and getting back up.** Jean applied for finance internships through the 2007-2010 recession, was rejected by Goldman Sachs multiple times, and still ended up with a Goldman offer out of undergrad; you must believe success can come, or there is no point in trying, and you must not take rejection personally. _[Goldman Sachs, Lehman Brothers, Bear Stearns]_ — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **Practice asking for things at a micro level so you have the reps in when a high-stakes ask like a salary negotiation comes up.** Jean once forced herself through a challenge of asking for things, including asking for the whole bag of sample chips at Trader Joe's and getting it; harmless low-stakes asks are a form of rejection exposure therapy. _[Trader Joe's]_ — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **Putting things on the calendar to look forward to, big and small, makes the grind of building a company easier.** Cherie plans a May trip to Japan, a board game night with GSB friends and a symphony outing with a new friend; having outlets and breaks scheduled is a psychological tool for her. — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)
- **Before deciding whether to reconcile, run a thought exercise of the best case, worst case and most likely scenario to feel out the range of outcomes.** Jean describes this as a derivation of cognitive behavioral therapy: a safe 'dry run of the feelings' done alone that often produces a gut answer about whether the friendship has run its course. _[Cognitive behavioral therapy]_ — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)
- **Trust your gut; it is faster than cognitive understanding and ignoring it has always been to your peril.** Jean notes that leaving an interaction with a bubbly, seemingly nice person yet feeling uncomfortable inside is your gut flagging caution, and the earlier you learn to trust it the better. — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)

### Health, wellness & burnout (15)

- **When you are sick, resting is your number one job and action item, not a guilty lapse in productivity.** Jean pushed through illness on adrenaline out of what Cherie calls a trauma response from finance; once she turned off she realized she was sicker than she thought and recovered properly. — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zGuTX6a53LI)
- **Unlimited PTO in tech is a psychological trick: people take fewer days off than in traditional industries with a mandated 10 days or two weeks.** Without carefully monitoring their days, tech workers end up working more under discretionary time-off policies. — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zGuTX6a53LI)
- **Pre-fill a month-by-month calendar of events you're looking forward to, so dreary stretches like winter have something ahead.** Jean writes out all 12 months, adds major events as they approach, and keeps it as a running account of her year. — [2024-08-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MbHnwiOF8hA)
- **Give yourself grace to cancel commitments while recovering, rather than showing up as a diminished version of yourself.** Post-COVID, Jean over-scheduled herself, judged Boston University media venture theses while exhausted and left early; her friend convinced her to skip a Dartmouth Club of LA event. Cherie adds: if you feel guilty canceling, don't commit in the first place. _[Boston University, Dartmouth Club of LA]_ — [2024-08-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MbHnwiOF8hA)
- **Sleep is foundational to health, aging and memory, and a night-owl schedule inherited from early finance and tech jobs is hard to unwind.** Jean's natural sleep window is 1:30am to 9:30am, a habit from working 10am to midnight at her first jobs; she uses magnesium glycinate, no caffeine after 2pm and melatonin as a last resort. Cherie, citing Andrew Huberman, says put the phone away in bed. _[Andrew Huberman]_ — [2024-08-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YuZD6_Z11Xo)
- **Founders planning to have a baby should intentionally design a real maternity leave of at least three months with no work.** (Farah Jesani) Farah answered emails from the hospital after an emergency C-section and still carries trauma and shame from it; she will not have another child unless the business is set up with a trusted leader who can make decisions without deferring to her. Her husband's paternity leave helped but was not the same as taking time herself. — [2024-09-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w6oGHVFGfEY)
- **Immigrant guilt shows up as equating productivity with suffering and a Google Calendar mapped out to the minute.** Cherie relates to the Duke Chronicle line 'to be productive means to toil, to suffer, and to drown in Google Calendar appointments'; Jean calls that mindset unhealthy, and Cherie agrees it is a coping mechanism, not a model. _[Duke Chronicle]_ — [2024-10-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zVmcq70kghU)
- **Track your biometrics daily with a wearable to understand and improve your body over time, especially sleep.** Cherie wears a Garmin Venu S2 every day and to bed for heart rate, body battery, sleep score, cycle and stress. Jean switched from Apple Watch (two broken, nightly charging prevents sleep tracking) to Garmin, which charges once every 7-10 days; improving sleep is her number one goal. _[Garmin, Apple Watch, Oura Ring]_ — [2024-11-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p30odvPhoPY)
- **Anything that lowers the mental barrier to going to the gym, like matching sets you grab without thinking, is worth the money.** Jean's pick is Set Active (LA, female-founded, 'Sport Body' line), priced below Alo and Lululemon with more current colors; she admits early runs had inconsistent sizing and a zipper failure. _[Set Active, Lululemon, Alo Yoga]_ — [2024-11-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p30odvPhoPY)
- **Regular acid exfoliation with peel pads is the underrated basis of healthy glowing skin and preps skin so makeup goes on well.** Jean rebuys Arcona Triad Pads and Dr. Dennis Gross Alpha Beta Extra Strength peels (two-step, requires sunscreen) at the Nordstrom Anniversary Sale; pads are travel-friendly and can be used down the neck, hands and elbows. _[Arcona, Dr. Dennis Gross, Nordstrom Anniversary Sale]_ — [2024-11-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p30odvPhoPY)
- **Shift toward clean beauty and multi-use products; a single blush stick can be cheeks, eyes and lips.** Cherie champions Tower 28 (Asian-woman-founded by CEO Amy Liu, Sephora clean seal): lip jellies, Bronzino bronzer, mascara; she notes a mascara that lasts 48 hours is 'nothing clean'. Jean values ease and versatility above all. _[Tower 28, Sephora, Dior, Chanel]_ — [2024-11-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p30odvPhoPY)
- **When stretched thin across many good projects, get deliberate about which are meaningful and don't sacrifice things like monthly volunteering for more work.** Cherie juggles matcha, the podcast, a weekly newsletter, Forbes writing, LinkedIn growth, social life and dating; Jean has consciously been less social lately, noting there are different seasons, and that the same load in a corporate job would feel far more stressful because here they are excited about every project. — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **Going viral has a toxic side: a near-million-view video eventually reaches misogynistic corners of the internet, and you can logic your way through hate comments without it ceasing to hurt.** Cherie's dating-response video drew abusive comments; celebrities have teams scrub non-constructive comments and distill the rest like a performance review, and Hasan Minhaj's bit says critics should put their government name and photo to what they say. _[Hasan Minhaj, Instagram]_ — [2024-11-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KgwTEg5ZVEQ)
- **A consistent daily movement practice, even walking at 3.0 on a treadmill while calling a friend, changes how you feel and show up.** Jean moved her body seven of seven days (gym classes, pickleball, tennis) and her Garmin shows a 90% body battery on six hours of sleep, which she attributes to sleeping deeper from exercise. _[Garmin]_ — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)
- **Intense finance work cultures that push analysts into health and mental-health problems deserve open discussion.** The sisters flag this as a future episode topic, noting Jean has 'war stories' from coworkers and friends. — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)

### Life design, goals & reinvention (16)

- **Working for yourself without a boss or program sharply limits incidental human interaction, so you have to manufacture it through alumni events and weekend plans.** Cherie, approaching 29 and newly in LA with no roots, feels a profound loneliness after leaving the density of business school; New York or SF would have offered friends every night of the week. — [2024-08-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zGuTX6a53LI)
- **Create a vision board every year around January by cutting out magazine images and words on gut instinct, then revisit, reinvent and reinvest in it multiple times a year.** Jean taught Cherie the practice; Cherie's 2015 board was word-heavy and career-focused because she was anxious about getting a job, while Jean's are mostly photos of things she loves. Doing it with friends reveals how differently people approach it. _[vision board]_ — [2024-08-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MbHnwiOF8hA)
- **Put your vision board somewhere you see it every day; the daily visual reminder of what you want your life to look like is where the power comes from.** Jean describes making a vision board as vulnerable because it is a visualization of all your hopes and dreams; she also made a second board mid-year for the first time. — [2024-08-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MbHnwiOF8hA)
- **The first step is having a goal, but goals need not be fixed; they change on macro and micro levels and sometimes life changes them for you.** At 21 as a Goldman Sachs analyst Jean's goal was to become a Goldman partner; two years later she wanted out entirely. Without a goal you have nothing to work toward. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2024-08-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MbHnwiOF8hA)
- **Have a plan, but plans are written in pencil.** Cherie learned this from 77-year-old professor Joel Peterson's Stanford class 'Engineering a Remarkable Life', whose final assignment was a 10-page life plan covering career, spouse and children. _[Joel Peterson, Engineering a Remarkable Life, Stanford GSB]_ — [2024-08-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MbHnwiOF8hA)
- **Write a yearly list of about 70 aspirations across life categories and score yourself out of 35, so hitting half counts as 100%.** Jean's list mixes easy lifts, recurring habits like gym three times a week, stretch goals and a separate longer-term bucket list; categories include health and wellness, family, friendships and relationships. Cherie has copied the practice. — [2024-08-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MbHnwiOF8hA)
- **Aspirations should be tangible and intentional, like 'dinner with Sarah five times' or 'try this specific bakery', not just lofty goals.** Writing down that you want quality time with a close friend is the only way you'll remember to schedule it; small items like a bakery visit or a monthly hike with 12 check boxes give easy wins and track what already brings joy, such as Jean's HBS book club. — [2024-08-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=MbHnwiOF8hA)
- **Careers are long and business school is only one chapter, so follow your passion and treat an MBA as a bonus rather than sacrificing five unhappy years to get in.** Cherie, the self-described romantic, argues that finding an industry you are excited to wake up for is the successful start, and if it happens to be consulting, finance or tech, all the better. — [2024-08-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YuZD6_Z11Xo)
- **A joyful, wealthy life needs a holistic approach; early-career focus on academic and professional achievement often comes at the cost of the personal department.** The hosts say balancing work and dating is a real thing girlfriends talk about, and that is why they created the podcast to cover relationships alongside careers. — [2024-08-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=V2gOF7NC6BI)
- **Talk publicly about hard family topics because the discourse you wish existed from peers you respect often doesn't, so you have to create it.** The sisters chose this topic despite it being emotionally difficult and normally private, because they wished they could hear respected peers discuss how immigrant upbringing shaped them. — [2024-10-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=zVmcq70kghU)
- **Once you find one great pair of jeans, you only need two or three dependable pairs; try on 20 to find them.** Cherie's pick is revived Abercrombie & Fitch: petite/short lengths, high-waisted cuts, plus basic scoop-neck tank tops (long over cropped for a professional look) that last years. _[Abercrombie & Fitch]_ — [2024-11-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p30odvPhoPY)
- **Buy products in their home country to honor the craftsmanship (Birkenstocks in Germany, Rimowa in Germany, Jo Malone in London).** Cherie's love of shopping while traveling began with a family trip to Germany; her first Birkenstocks cost $77 in 7th grade and both sisters still wear Arizonas and Boston clogs, recommending the big-buckle style. _[Birkenstock, Rimowa, Jo Malone]_ — [2024-11-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p30odvPhoPY)
- **Get into perfume by buying travel sizes or rollers first, layering stackable scents, and exploring niche brands to make a signature scent your own.** Jean layers Jo Malone Peony & Blush Suede with Wild Bluebell; Cherie explores Le Labo (Jasmin 17, Lys 41 roller), Vanilla Riviera by Matiere Premiere and a leathery passion-fruit scent from Maison Crivelli, bought cheaper in Paris; scented body oil like Nuxe is a gentle entry point. _[Le Labo, Matiere Premiere, Maison Crivelli, Nuxe]_ — [2024-11-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p30odvPhoPY)
- **The 'why' behind the company is to empower a billion people to level up in their personal and professional lives, going beyond Disney's goal of making people happy.** Media plus commerce channels (like Disney's movies, Disneyland and cruises) are the model for reaching the most people with educational content. _[Disney, Disneyland]_ — [2024-11-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=12mku1NPpE8)
- **There are different seasons for different things; a period of intense work after a life change is fine as long as you later rebalance socializing.** Newly single after eight years and adjusting to a couples-oriented social circle, Jean has thrown herself into building Sisters with Cherie, working every day and weekend, and accepts this as a season rather than a permanent state. — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)
- **If you want an environment where you can sometimes say 'not my problem', corporate life will be more fulfilling; you need to be set up for the phase of life where entrepreneurship can succeed.** The sisters stress that fulfillment is personal and that different life phases suit different environments. — [2024-12-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=C1ifm66GEy8)

### Career strategy & promotions (71)

- **The 'Gen Z doesn't want to work' stereotype does not match the Zillennials the sisters know in tech, who are establishment-minded.** Cherie says her Ivy League friends in consulting, finance and PE skew millennial, are drawn to capitalism and work extremely hard, so the generalization has not shown up in her circle. — [2025-01-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_8AlNMC2l4)
- **If you want to build something great, throw yourself entirely into your work; work-life balance is not how Google, Snapchat or nuclear energy got built.** (David Shim) Advice to his 29- and 35-year-old selves: make major sacrifices to find out quickly whether your idea works rather than spending four more years at nine-to-five pace; you'll only regret not going all in. _[Google, Snapchat]_ — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **Long hours in banking are mostly a sign of inefficiency, not of workload, so efficiency and focus matter more than hours.** (Imran Khan) Imran worked 90-100 hour weeks as a telecom banking analyst in 2000; afterward 50-60 hour weeks felt like a massive upgrade, and he cites Wall Street Journal reporting on Bank of America health problems as evidence the industry still has not fixed its work process. _[Bank of America, Wall Street Journal]_ — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **Failure can give you clarity: being a bad analyst and getting laid off pushed Imran into the small-shop equity research role that became his big career breakthrough.** (Imran Khan) He was bottom quartile as a banking analyst (poor attention to detail, weak English writing, hated PowerPoint), was laid off in 2001 when the telecom bubble burst, and could only get a job at a tiny research shop, which he needed to keep his visa. _[University of Denver]_ — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **Working 30 more hours a week than your peers compounds into an enormous knowledge edge within a year.** (Imran Khan) Carrying a 100-hour banking work ethic into a 70-hour research job made Imran far more productive than colleagues, which is what freed him to take on extra coverage. — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **At a small firm you can volunteer to cover what nobody else is covering; being a big fish in a small pond creates opportunities a big-name firm would never give you.** (Imran Khan) Imran asked his boss to let him cover internet stocks in his free time and picked up Yahoo, Amazon and eBay at the bottom of the market; when they rallied he looked like a genius, JP Morgan and other big banks called, and he says at a big bank someone more qualified would already have covered those names. _[Yahoo, Amazon, eBay, JP Morgan]_ — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **If the person next to you loves the work in a way you never will, they will excel in ways you can't, which is a signal to change paths.** Jean's desk neighbor at Goldman traded volatility in every spare minute while she read fashion blogs, which showed her that finance was not her calling. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **The number one advice to a 29-year-old is to take risks sooner in life, professionally and personally, because the only thing stopping you from creating what you want is usually yourself.** (John Redgrave) Type A people in particular take fewer risks than they could because of fear of failure. Redgrave, now 42, calls this his biggest regret. — [2025-02-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pAWpvZ2vn_Q)
- **If you love what you do you will win, because you think about it 24/7 while 9-to-5 competitors stop at 5pm on Friday.** (Peter Pham) You do not need to be the smartest person in the subject; loving it means it never feels like work and you naturally out-work people who treat it as a regular job. Be willing to give something up, even move in with your parents, to pay rent while you do it. — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **Coming from nothing makes you unafraid and makes it easier to take big career risks, because you know the worst case is survivable.** Cherie links Pham's refugee background to the Vu Tran episode and says peers afraid to leave corporate 9-to-5s have not actually run the worst-case analysis, e.g. moving in with family to save money, which requires living below your means and sacrifice. _[Vu Tran]_ — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **When evaluating a job offer, ask about egg-freezing and fertility benefits, which can cover a roughly $40,000 procedure.** Big tech companies like LinkedIn, Microsoft, Meta and Snapchat cover it; a friend weighing a Snapchat offer against Meta specifically asked Jean about fertility benefits, and you may also be covered under a partner's plan. _[LinkedIn, Microsoft, Meta, Snapchat]_ — [2025-02-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaMVodL23LE)
- **If you know what you want to do, go directly to that thing rather than collecting prerequisite experiences first; the more experience you have doing the thing, the better you get at it.** (Jeremy Liew) He gives the example of aspiring VCs who think they must first get startup operating experience; he says no, just go be an investor. The hosts add the caveat that some people need to build a financial safety net or skills for 2-4 years first, but agree that 'I have to do these three things first' often becomes complacency. _[Lightspeed Venture Partners]_ — [2025-03-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=109Xypx-hM4)
- **If you don't know what you want to do, find people you greatly admire and blindly follow them wherever they go, slipstreaming behind them into new organizations where they lack trusted people.** (Jeremy Liew) Truly good people make well-informed choices about where to go next, and they arrive somewhere needing people they trust personally and professionally. Jeremy followed IAC's president to AOL in 2002 and became his chief of staff and SVP of corporate development. _[IAC, AOL]_ — [2025-03-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=109Xypx-hM4)
- **Do every job with pride, even painting a fence and picking up trash at Dairy Queen, because early independence and hard work stay with you.** (Jonah Goodhart) Jonah loved earning his own small paycheck at his first job; the hosts add that if you honestly are not proud of your output, you may be in the wrong role for this moment. _[Dairy Queen]_ — [2025-03-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UYtMgkz8brA)
- **Being thrown into brand-new fields in your first jobs gives you the lasting confidence that you can pick anything up.** (Michelle Huang) Michelle did consulting for under a year, which felt like being paid to learn on a playground, then joined private equity in New York and Hong Kong with no finance background while her cohort had built models before; being behind on day one taught her a growth mindset. Jean adds that if you stop learning and growing it is the death knell of curiosity. _[Blackstone]_ — [2025-03-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fb8gYzg6ikg)
- **If you're going to do something, do it well; otherwise don't do it.** (John Wu) From Wu's first job as a high-school pool lifeguard, where he pulled a drowning kid out by the hair; the easy job could have been daydreamed through but he took it seriously. The hosts lament that pride in work is rare today. — [2025-03-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VuBM_XODr7A)
- **If you don't feel proud of your current work, treat that as a signal to examine whether it is the right role, company or people.** Cherie adds the flip side: she stayed in questionable working situations because she took so much pride in what she was building, which shows how you weight values differently at different times. — [2025-03-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VuBM_XODr7A)
- **Failure isn't always your fault; when exogenous factors dominate, restructure the situation so more is within your control.** Example: a startup can't control the fundraising environment or timing, but can piece together conditions that set it up for success, or change situations entirely. — [2025-03-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VuBM_XODr7A)
- **Being fired for performance early on is a blip in a long career: both Imran Khan and Jeremy Liew were fired, learned from it, and their next big opportunity was born from that moment.** Neither firing appears on their LinkedIn or in any Forbes or Time profile; the world would never have known had the hosts not asked about failures. Jean cautions it's not a guarantee everyone bounces back, but a bad moment doesn't have to define you. _[Imran Khan, Jeremy Liew]_ — [2025-03-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=I04_Nc8odHw)
- **Use Porter's Five Forces (threat of new entrants, supplier power, buyer power, threat of substitutes, rivalry) to understand any company's competitive landscape.** Invented at HBS, it is the classic tool to ask of any company you evaluate. Applied to Starbucks: new-entrant threat is low because of brand, supply chain and real estate spend; supplier power is weak because Starbucks buys in huge volume (like Walmart and Costco); substitutes are moderate; rivalry is high but Starbucks wins on in-store experience and premium pricing. _[Michael Porter, Porter's Five Forces, Starbucks, Walmart, Costco]_ — [2025-04-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JGitajVC4IM)
- **The single biggest contributor to becoming a self-made millionaire in your 20s is the first job out of college, which traces back to what you choose to study.** Jean studied economics at a liberal arts school, noticed which seniors were celebrated for landing McKinsey, Goldman and Morgan Stanley jobs, set a four-year goal of getting into Goldman Sachs, and worked backwards to a junior-year internship and a valuable sophomore-year role. _[Goldman Sachs, McKinsey, Morgan Stanley]_ — [2025-04-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e9C6UDbAIdw)
- **It is legitimate to copy the paths of people you admire: be curious, ask questions, gather information, then build a plan you can pivot when reality hits.** Jean's Goldman internship plan was derailed by the Great Recession, but having a target let her adjust; today the equivalent legwork is Google, Glassdoor, LinkedIn salary data and networking, but you still have to piece together how to get there. _[Glassdoor, LinkedIn]_ — [2025-04-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e9C6UDbAIdw)
- **Choose a major that maximizes your options and earning potential out of school if you have student loans and no family money to fall back on.** Cherie wanted to be a doctor but Jean, having found it nearly impossible to become a product manager without a CS degree after Goldman, told her in 2013 to study computer science; Cherie admits she could not have pulled off the art-history-to-McKinsey route others did and needed a more bulletproof plan. — [2025-04-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e9C6UDbAIdw)
- **A beauty editor is a revenue-driving business role, not just creative play: you must know your advertisers and craft ideas live in the room that serve both the brand's customer and your reader.** (Leah Wyar) At Cosmo, beauty drove so much revenue that Leah reported almost equally to the editor in chief and the publisher, sitting in live brainstorms with CMOs from P&G, Unilever and L'Oreal who wanted a first-to-market idea, not a recycled one. She realized that if she got really good at this she could have a very long-lasting career. _[Cosmopolitan, P&G, Unilever, L'Oreal, Health magazine, Self magazine, Fitness magazine]_ — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **When someone sees a capability in you that you don't see in yourself, trust it and try; you would not have raised your hand for it on your own.** (Leah Wyar) After 20+ years in beauty, Leah initially said no when her boss asked her to run the entertainment group (People, EW, People en Espanol) after the Meredith acquisition; she slept on it, said yes, and calls it the best decision she ever made even though it was really hard. _[Dotdash Meredith, People, Entertainment Weekly, Byrdie, Brides]_ — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **There is beauty in being naive: switching from print to digital was like on-the-job postgraduate work, emailing herself 'Google this later' and relying on smart people around her.** (Leah Wyar) She is now launching an app knowing nothing about apps; you can figure it out if you give up control and spread empowerment so everyone does what they're best at. She credits the familial leadership at her company for letting her succeed this way. — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **Jean attributes her entire career to a growth mindset: throwing herself into jobs she was unqualified for and trusting her ability to learn fast.** She joined Goldman barely knowing Excel, never became top-50 percent at it yet was a top-performing analyst; that gave her the courage to become a product manager knowing nothing about product management, and later to go on camera for the podcast and start a CPG matcha brand after a career in digital products. _[Goldman Sachs, Sisters Matcha]_ — [2025-05-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xPAIT2MpsuY)
- **Manage your own career: no one cares more about your career than you do and no one will promote you on a whim, so be intentional and proactive.** Rule one on the Goldman Sachs University training sheet; at a big company Cherie mapped herself against the internal PM promotion ladder criteria, while at startups without HR guidelines you must create visibility yourself. _[Goldman Sachs University 'Top 10 Tips for Success']_ — [2025-05-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qZvfC4TOAD0)
- **Keep a 'wins' folder on your work computer with screenshots of every kudos email and strong deliverable, for promotion packets and for bad days.** Cherie's manager told her to do this at LinkedIn; Jean says she never did because Goldman's grind culture left no time, and admits it is not hard, just annoying, but the better way. — [2025-05-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qZvfC4TOAD0)
- **Grinding hard is not enough; you need proof and testimonials captured along the way to be recognized for your work.** Cherie contrasts her 'zillennial' expectation of recognition with the millennial finance culture where having no life was a point of pride and good work was rewarded with more work piled on. — [2025-05-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qZvfC4TOAD0)
- **Defy intellectual boredom by assigning yourself homework that follows your interests and adds value to your team.** On Goldman's internal fund-of-funds team Jean invented a daily news write-up on companies like Starbucks that the team then used to probe fund managers' investment theses; it also revealed her pull toward consumer tech and led to her next career. _[Goldman Sachs, Starbucks]_ — [2025-05-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qZvfC4TOAD0)
- **Regularly check whether you are still learning as fast as you were, and if not, introduce something to make the job engaging again since you are the captain of your own ship.** Cherie noticed after her first year and a half at LinkedIn that learning had slowed and treats that as a trigger to act. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2025-05-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qZvfC4TOAD0)
- **New grads with zero professional experience need explicit training on what is and isn't okay, and simple printed rules like these still hold up 15 years later.** The sheet came from Goldman Sachs University's 2010 first-year analyst training packet; Jean kept it pinned on a bulletin board through every move. _[Goldman Sachs University]_ — [2025-05-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qZvfC4TOAD0)
- **Bringing your whole, obviously-caring self to work makes managers give you the benefit of the doubt, and likability plus relating to all kinds of people is a career superpower.** Cherie says her bosses know she is a 'try hard' who cries because she cares, so they extend grace; Jean identifies likability and relating across types of people as one of Cherie's superpowers. — [2025-05-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p4gBwESgzPI)
- **Limit your daily to-do list to the three work things that matter, because we are always more ambitious than our abilities.** (Kofi Ampadu) A giant to-do list guarantees you finish nothing; after crossing off three, Kofi knows he had a good day, and usually gets more done anyway. — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **Taking notes during an informational coffee chat is so rare that it can get you hired.** Cherie prepared questions from Albert's LinkedIn, wrote down his answers in a notebook and sent a follow-up email; after she joined his team he told her nobody had ever written down anything he said and that was when he knew he wanted her. — [2025-06-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eFpgcrlf2gk)
- **Being smart and working hard is not enough; you must present well and look put together to be taken seriously and put in front of clients.** Jean says this lesson took her 15 years because she grew up believing her worth was her mind and grades; a Zynga manager, Harvey, once asked whether she had brushed her hair after a 3am night, the first time anyone had commented on her appearance. _[Zynga]_ — [2025-06-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eFpgcrlf2gk)
- **In your early 20s, prioritize relationships over the grind; relationships are the make-or-break of careers and businesses.** On a 2018 LinkedIn cohort trip to Japan and India, Cherie stayed in her hotel room sending emails to her San Francisco team instead of bonding with colleagues and VPs, pressure nobody put on her; she now has FOMO when friends reminisce about that trip. New jobs come via referrals and promotions via people vouching for you, so give people undivided attention and active listening. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2025-06-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DubkuAz2meM)
- **Take career risks now, before dependents and responsibilities; the fear of regret and not knowing outweighs the fear of failure.** Cherie went from a stable post-college job to business school and is now in an experimental, risk-taking era she feels energized by. — [2025-06-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DubkuAz2meM)
- **The grind in your 20s can make you unstoppable and unafraid to take on new things, even if it stops being what gets you ahead later.** Jean's first job in finance rewarded hours at the desk (70 vs 90 vs 100 hours were noticed) and taught her she could learn anything and outwork anyone. That confidence let her become a product manager at Zynga at 24 without a computer science background, when peers were 28-30+, directing people in their 30s-50s, after joining Goldman not even knowing Excel. _[Zynga, Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-06-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DubkuAz2meM)
- **Whether grinding is rewarded depends on context: it is required in finance, but in big tech it is not the culture and is less incentivized than in traditional sectors.** Cherie says in big tech people notice hard work and excellence is expected, but grinding is not rewarded the way it is in finance; Jean adds role matters too, since a PM wins hearts and minds while a Goldman analyst was 'a machine with flesh'. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-06-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DubkuAz2meM)
- **Hard work does not speak for itself; you must be vocal about your work, and using 'we' language makes you sound more senior than 'I' statements.** (Lorraine K. Lee) Harvard Business Review data shows collaborative language reads as more senior while heavy 'I' statements read as junior. Frame wins as team learnings, ground them in data and tie them to the company's bottom line. _[Harvard Business Review]_ — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **Start the promotion conversation six months before review time, know who else is in the room, and close the skill gaps that could block you.** (Lorraine K. Lee) Lee did not realize early on that other people beyond her manager decide promotions or that raising it when writing her review was far too late. Jean, speaking as a former manager, describes the legwork: identifying who must vote yes, who will object and how to mollify them months ahead. — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **Explicitly tell your manager you want to be promoted, and repeat it, because many people are content where they are and managers will not assume ambition.** (Lorraine K. Lee) Bringing the manager in early makes it a joint plan and lets them plant seeds and advocate on your behalf; it is a lot of work for them, so they must be invested. — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **Charge for the value you create, not the hourly service you perform.** (Fernando) At 14 he was paid a few hundred dollars for translation, then realized he was actually enabling developers to execute their business plans and decided he ought to get paid more. — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **When changing industries, find the entry point that uses what you already know.** The hosts draw the lesson from Crexi and Cherie's own move from Goldman Sachs to consumer tech after two years: you get into the new field through the door you have a competitive edge in. _[Goldman Sachs, Crexi]_ — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **Bake optionality into your life the way VCs bake pro-rata rights into contracts: always have multiple options in a job search or school applications so you choose from information, not desperation.** Apply to reach, target and safety schools; Cherie applied to both HBS and Stanford GSB, got into both and turned down Harvard, while Jean was rejected by Goldman Sachs twice. _[Harvard Business School, Stanford GSB, Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-07-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bu50AgK6UIw)
- **Publicly sharing a layoff on LinkedIn, though it feels shameful, generates empathy and can lead directly to job opportunities.** Cherie admits she might feel too much shame to post, but has seen people get jobs from such posts; shame versus empathy is a central construct in Brene Brown's Daring Greatly. _[Brene Brown, Daring Greatly, LinkedIn]_ — [2025-07-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=McWujpZMzLw)
- **If your employer is bought by PE, expect three things: efficiency moves and tighter budgets, new leadership often brought in from the PE firm's own operators, and restructuring layoffs.** PE firms install people they trust to execute their strategy. — [2025-07-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y95go30wv18)
- **High-achieving women settle more because they are trained to endure, be grateful, not want too much and wait their turn.** A 2020 Stanford study found women in leadership consistently underestimate their readiness for promotion even though they objectively outperformed male peers; the issue is often lack of self-belief reinforced by patriarchal systems, not just lack of opportunity. _[Stanford]_ — [2025-08-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QUN5LWuRwu0)
- **Reaching a hard-won dream job can still be settling if you are disengaged and drained once you're there.** After 15 years Cherie became head of product for AR shopping and monetization at Snapchat, did less actual work than ever, yet was exhausted and disengaged; the title, pay, sunk cost and having defied the odds kept her from admitting it. _[Snapchat, Snap]_ — [2025-08-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QUN5LWuRwu0)
- **Apply Jobs to be Done to yourself: be honest about what people hire you for at work, whether competence, confidence, calm in a storm or personality.** The first mini exercise asks listeners to name the job their role or service actually performs for others and post it in the comments. _[Jobs to be Done]_ — [2025-08-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IyFtCATnRpA)
- **A job offer that looks amazing on paper can be a 'meh' offer once you weight best, worst and most likely scenarios by probability.** Jean mapped three outcomes for an impressive offer, assigned values and likelihoods, and found the expected value underwhelming; by contrast entrepreneurship looks risky and low-value early on but has huge option value and upside when mapped the same way. — [2025-08-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IyFtCATnRpA)
- **OKRs are a guardrail against urgency: if a request does not ladder up to your stated OKRs, you can decline it.** Without OKRs everything feels urgent and you tackle things as they come; with them you can say 'I'd love to, but I have to focus on XYZ first', a valid excuse that everyone has visibility into. It is disastrous when your OKRs do not ladder up to your org's OKRs, a topic for their Power and Influence episode. _[OKRs]_ — [2025-08-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IyFtCATnRpA)
- **Ask for feedback before you think you're ready; shipping early and often separates fast-growth leaders from those who plateau.** From Kim Scott's 'Radical Candor', which Jean's LinkedIn manager Albert gave her early in her career. Feedback is a gift, not a weakness. _[Kim Scott, Radical Candor, LinkedIn]_ — [2025-09-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HjebjpDU8-Q)
- **Flip SBI to ask for feedback: don't wait for feedback, design for it.** Example: 'When I shared the product plan in the meeting, what stood out about how I presented it, and what was the impact on the team's decision making?' — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **Sheryl Sandberg's career is a model of accumulating influence across sectors rather than titles.** She learned where power sits as chief of staff at the US Treasury, built a cross-functional web of influence in biz ops at Google reporting to Eric Schmidt, and by the time she met Mark Zuckerberg at a 2007 Christmas party her multi-sector power map was her most valuable asset. _[Sheryl Sandberg, US Treasury, Google, Eric Schmidt, Mark Zuckerberg, Facebook]_ — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **Sharing compensation with peers exposes disparities and gives your manager the information to advocate for you.** In Cherie's LinkedIn associate product manager cohort, a colleague's spreadsheet revealed she was set to get 50-60% less in stock refresh than peers; raising it with her boss led senior leaders to distribute more evenly. The highest earner had an aggressive self-advocating manager and sat in the well-funded ads/monetization org. _[LinkedIn, LinkedIn Learning]_ — [2025-09-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_L_wX5RMxh4)
- **Staying too long in your zone of excellence feels like settling; leave for your zone of genius even when you have the money and the dream role.** Jean spent 6.5 years at Snapchat running hundreds of millions of dollars of M&A, large teams and new initiatives, and stayed because she kept learning, but by the end she was excellent without using her 'special brand of genius'; quitting to start the podcast was one of the hardest decisions of her life. _[Snapchat, zone of excellence vs zone of genius]_ — [2025-09-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TkXvlgPXitc)
- **Certain groups at big companies hire interns from the same fraternity year after year, and as long as you do not mess up the internship you get the full-time role.** Hiring managers want people they know, like and trust, so referrals from school and clubs are the first place they look; an MD at Morgan Stanley choosing between two equally talented candidates will be drawn to the one who knows the same fraternity songs. _[Morgan Stanley]_ — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **The Ivy League funnels students toward banking and MBB consulting through on-campus recruiting, which is both the leg up of these schools and the path of least resistance that brainwashes a narrow definition of success.** At Dartmouth, Bain, McKinsey, Goldman, Morgan Stanley and the bulge-bracket banks all came to campus, so all students had to do was drop resumes; anything creative or off the beaten path is looked down on, and majors are ranked by prestige. _[Bain, McKinsey, Goldman Sachs, Morgan Stanley, MBB consulting]_ — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **A values mismatch with your employer means going to work every day 'in a suit three sizes too small', so use your values to test whether you will last at an organization.** (Suzy Welch) People with high achievement and affluence and low voice fit investment banks like Goldman Sachs; someone with very high voice will find that a different kind of fit. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-11-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eRJyWu1CgI0)
- **If you're young, your fresh perspective is something your VPs and bosses don't have, so stop second-guessing and say it.** Exercise: take one permission-seeking phrase you use and practice saying the same idea without hedging, out loud in your room as a dry run so it comes naturally when it matters. — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **Three power moves for the week: remove sorry from your vocabulary, speak one unfiltered truth, and own one win without shrinking it.** The hosts frame these as the actionable summary of the four habits (sorry reflex, shrink to fit, asking for permission, toxic gratitude) and invite listeners to share a win in the comments. — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **What got you here will not get you to the next level; for high achievers the next level requires letting go rather than white-knuckled grinding.** Cherie says a decade of teeth-clenched pushing ends in a breakdown, and growth now means surrender. — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)
- **Throwing yourself into a job you're unqualified for and succeeding gives you the courage for every later leap.** Jean joined Goldman barely knowing Excel, never became top-50% at Excel yet was a top-performing analyst, which gave her the confidence to become a product manager knowing nothing about product, and later to go on camera and launch a matcha CPG brand (Sisters Matcha) despite only ever working on digital products. _[Goldman Sachs, Sisters Matcha]_ — [2025-12-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NmdHxviASBg)
- **Never answer 'my greatest weakness is I'm a perfectionist' in an interview; recruiters see through it.** The hosts joke about weakness-as-strength answers, though Jean admits perfectionism really is hers: she takes 10 times longer than Cherie to write an email. — [2025-12-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NmdHxviASBg)
- **Hard work does not speak for itself; you must be vocal about your work, and using collaborative 'we/us/our' language makes you seem more senior than 'I' statements.** (Lorraine K. Lee) Lorraine cites Harvard Business Review data showing collaborative language reads as more senior and impressive, while heavy 'I' statements read as junior. Frame wins as team learnings, ground them in data, and tie them to the company's bottom line. _[Harvard Business Review]_ — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)
- **Find where the decision makers are actually looking and repeat your wins there, because people need to hear things seven times in seven different ways to retain them.** (Lorraine K. Lee) At Prezi, with no wins listserv, Lorraine used the company intranet; she also suggests the Slack channel the CEO or target leader reads. A 10am Slack misses APAC and Europe, so post in Slack, then email, then mention it in the team meeting. _[LinkedIn, Prezi]_ — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)
- **Promotions are decided by a room of people months in advance, so tell your manager you want one at least six months early and repeat it.** (Lorraine K. Lee) Lorraine initially assumed her manager alone decided and that ambition was obvious; many people do not actually want promotion, so you must say so. Find out who is in the room, what skills are missing, and give yourself time to address gaps. Cherie, as a former manager, describes the legwork of pre-empting objections from other voters. — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)
- **A beauty editor is a business role: you must know your reader as deeply as the advertiser across the table and pitch ideas live that serve both.** (Leah Wyar) At Cosmo, beauty drove so much revenue that Leah reported almost equally to the publisher as to the editor-in-chief, sitting in live brainstorms with CMOs from P&G, Unilever and L'Oreal who wanted first-to-market ideas. She decided that mastering this would give her a long career. _[Cosmopolitan, P&G, Unilever, L'Oreal, Joanna Coles]_ — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)

### Networking, communication & influence (129)

- **Just reach out to people; no one cares that you contacted them and they will probably forget, so there is no downside to outreach.** (David Shim) David credits the My First Million podcast for this day-to-day advice and links it to 'failure training' from selling newspapers door to door and recruiting for his fraternity. _[My First Million]_ — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **The philosophy of selling is to forget quickly: log what didn't work, iterate the pitch, and never let a few rejections kill the idea.** (David Shim) Placed was rejected in Series A and B and almost failed to raise the C; every objection ('what if Google does this', 'no one will grant location permissions') became something to answer next time. _[Placed, Google, Microsoft]_ — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **In a high-stakes meeting, focus only on delivering what you came to communicate and don't try to predict their reaction.** (David Shim) David used to overthink scenarios; now he asks 'what am I in this meeting for' and delivers the value proposition, because worrying about reactions throws you off. — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **Don't practice in the hour or two before a speech; do the prep earlier and let your brain process it in the background so you can respond to the room.** (David Shim) At a recent talk David saw 75% of the audience on their phones during the prior speaker and changed his opening on the spot, which was only possible because he knew his tenets and wasn't locked to scripted lines. — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **Great public speakers are made through reps: 100-200 startup pitch competitions turned speaking into second nature.** (David Shim) Early on David spoke to four or five people at bad conferences, which was freeing because 'what's the worst that can happen'; the sisters add that their best-speaking friends did debate or model Congress, learning to argue ideas without taking it personally. — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **You can judge a conference's content quality by how many laptops and phones are open in the audience.** (David Shim) At this conference people were engaged, raising hands, staying for sessions and trying to meet each other rather than sell. — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **Relationships compound too; aim for a few high-quality relationships that grow over time and add a few more each year.** (Imran Khan) It is not about having many relationships but about strong roots across different areas by the time you get older; Imran also says true friends are texting-relationship friends, since email is impersonal and if someone won't pick up your call it is not a real relationship. — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **For public speaking, read the audience and adjust in real time, and do not over-rehearse.** (Imran Khan) Imran was a high school debater, makes eye contact to check whether the message resonates, refuses to see interview questions in advance because rehearsing makes him worse, and treats a talk to 2,000 people like a regular conversation. — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **If people aren't engaging, find the relevancy that connects your topic to what they care about rather than pushing on.** (Imran Khan) His example: he knows nothing about baseball and will zone out, so someone who wants to talk baseball should bring in cricket, which he grew up watching in Bangladesh, to get him engaged. — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **Build a community of interesting, heterogeneous people rather than a conference of big names, and keep it small enough that people actually talk.** (Imran Khan) Conference mixes founders, CEOs, regulators, lawyers, nonprofit leaders and young people to avoid group-think, grew from 50 to 100 to 160 attendees, and requires speakers to contribute four to six hours instead of 'speak and leave'; Imran says he doesn't need to watch Elon Musk on stage since his thoughts are on X in real time. _[Conference, Elon Musk, X]_ — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **A curated, hand-selected community makes people feel safe, so everyone attends sessions and genuinely wants to meet each other.** Cherie noticed everyone at Conference sat through sessions and hung out at dinners and drinks because each person was a first-degree connection of Imran and trusted his curation. _[Conference]_ — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **Being responsive to friends in small consistent ways is how relationships compound into a powerful network.** Jean notes Imran manages hundreds of millions of dollars yet texts friends back within a minute about 90% of the time, and that consistent small acts of care are what made Conference possible. — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **Become an incredible storyteller, because storytelling drives recruiting, fundraising, selling and nearly all institutional change.** (John Redgrave) Build the skill by mimicking great storytellers from music, the arts and corporate America (Steve Jobs). Narrow exposure means telling stories through one lens; you must tailor the story to the people you are trying to move. _[Steve Jobs]_ — [2025-02-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pAWpvZ2vn_Q)
- **Public speaking rule one: it is your message, and any question can be bridged back to your core talking points however long it takes.** (John Redgrave) Learned through media training, a Frontline special and being grilled by aggressive interviewers and government officials worldwide. The hosts compare it to debate candidates who pivot from a question about the LA fires to the state budget. _[Frontline]_ — [2025-02-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pAWpvZ2vn_Q)
- **Public speaking rule two: stay calm and breathe, because visible nerves (shaky voice or hands) erode your message by signaling you lack confidence.** (John Redgrave) Even if you are sweating through your jacket, remain cool under pressure; confidence is the key. — [2025-02-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pAWpvZ2vn_Q)
- **Do a two-to-three-minute physical warm-up like ten jumping jacks before a presentation so your racing heart rate matches what your body is doing.** Cherie's former boss Eric taught her that the mismatch between adrenaline and standing still is what feels like nerves; a brief 'body pump' makes the state feel congruous without leaving you out of breath. — [2025-02-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pAWpvZ2vn_Q)
- **Model your storytelling on a diverse set of speakers, not just tech founders like Steve Jobs, so your style is not one narrow lens.** The hosts list Martin Luther King Jr., Winston Churchill, Walter Cronkite, Oprah (who can say what a million others have said, e.g. how GLP-1s quiet food noise, and be the face of trust), Michelle Obama, Jon Stewart, and actors who tell stories on behalf of others. _[Steve Jobs, Martin Luther King Jr., Winston Churchill, Walter Cronkite, Oprah Winfrey, Michelle Obama, Jon Stewart]_ — [2025-02-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pAWpvZ2vn_Q)
- **Great leaders answer unprepped questions compellingly because they have already done deep reflection on their own story.** No guest in the series was given questions ahead of time or had PR prep; the hosts note the podcast itself is their reps for speaking extemporaneously, since a narrative often sounds smoother in your head than out loud. — [2025-02-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pAWpvZ2vn_Q)
- **Conferences are about the people: get outside the core business context, break bread, and use the shared host as an icebreaker.** (John Redgrave) Redgrave attends Imran's conference for the friends at the cutting edge of their industries and was especially interested in conversations on AI and national security, because investing in the future is what matters. — [2025-02-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pAWpvZ2vn_Q)
- **People want to help those who are visibly passionate because most people never follow their own dreams and live vicariously through yours.** (Peter Pham) Like tipping a street musician or wanting to help someone who says they are an artist, people see a bit of their own unlived dream in you. Jean confirms that once she started publicly sharing her passion for Sisters Matcha, people began reaching out to help. _[Sisters Matcha]_ — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **Two to four percent of any population just loves to hate; haters are the high-school instigators at scale and you should ignore them rather than fuel them.** (Peter Pham) Pham says people are 100 times more likely to respond to a hate comment, which is exactly what instigators want; human nature does not change, which is why he loves investing in culture. Cherie adds that haters actually love you most because they follow you obsessively. — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **Conferences are about people, not panels: the value compounds when you show up consistently and turn two-minute hellos into 20-minute conversations year after year.** (Peter Pham) Having attended tech conferences for 20 years since TechCrunch launched in Mike Arrington's apartment, Pham says late-night bar conversations build relationships; 20 people downstairs are 15-year relationships, including showing Evan Spiegel around LA when Snapchat was young. He also mines conversations with people in big non-Silicon-Valley industries, like the largest mini-storage owner or NIL athlete manager, for business ideas. _[TechCrunch, Mike Arrington, Evan Spiegel, Snapchat, Harry Stebbings, All-In Podcast, 20 Minute VC]_ — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **The value of a cross-industry conference is perspectives orthogonal to your day-to-day, unlike tech conferences that are purely transactional networking.** (Jeremy Liew) He learned that the next administration's attention will go to the urgent expiring Trump-era tax cuts in 2025 rather than AI, which was obvious to political people but new to him. The hosts add that different worlds run different algorithms and the cross-learning is where the best ideas come from. — [2025-03-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=109Xypx-hM4)
- **Tech, finance and consulting are small worlds: the people you work for and with all go on to run things, so relationships compound.** Jeremy's CFO boss Dara became CEO of Uber and the IAC president became CEO of AOL; the hosts say this is especially true at FAANG companies and bulge bracket banks. _[Dara Khosrowshahi, Uber, AOL, IAC]_ — [2025-03-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=109Xypx-hM4)
- **A deeper layer of intelligence is breaking a complicated idea into a McKinsey-style set of numbers anyone can follow, like the best professors versus brilliant ones no one can understand.** Jeremy backed his hot take with the $1B/$10B/$100B training cost progression, 18-month cycles, a $600B software market and 5 foundation model companies; the hosts recommend he become a professor as his next act. _[McKinsey]_ — [2025-03-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=109Xypx-hM4)
- **Advertising is just paid distribution for storytelling, and storytelling is the most foundational concept of humanity.** (Jonah Goodhart) Jonah's contrarian take after 26 years in digital advertising and measurement: everyone loves to hate ads, but done well they are inspirational, informative and fun. _[Mobian]_ — [2025-03-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UYtMgkz8brA)
- **The best conferences are small, invite-only gatherings of intellectually curious, humble people who come to interact, including CEOs of $50-100 billion companies walking around like everyone else.** (Jonah Goodhart) Jonah credits Imran's curated event; business, in the end, is about people. _[Imran]_ — [2025-03-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UYtMgkz8brA)
- **The most successful people are humble and never mention their accomplishments; their superpower is making you feel like the only person in the room.** Jonah never mentioned his $850 million Oracle exit, working with both brothers, or co-owning the Surf Lodge; the sisters spent 90 minutes off camera swapping life stories and describe him as mesmerizing. _[Oracle, Surf Lodge]_ — [2025-03-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UYtMgkz8brA)
- **The most valuable conferences have no industry agenda and simply bring interesting people from different walks of life together so connections form organically.** (Michelle Huang) Michelle contrasts Conference 2024 with investor-, entrepreneur- or healthcare-specific events; Cherie agrees the dinner conversations with people she would never normally meet held the most learning. _[Conference 2024]_ — [2025-03-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fb8gYzg6ikg)
- **The value of a conference is a salon of people one degree from the host, where cross-industry viewpoints actually produce things.** (John Wu) At Imran Khan's fourth annual conference of roughly 190 people, Wu estimates 90% are direct relationships of Imran, who spans East Coast banking to West Coast tech; Wu has known him since he was 24. _[Imran Khan]_ — [2025-03-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VuBM_XODr7A)
- **'Go max dumb' to succeed: drop to first principles, ask why repeatedly, and admit when you don't understand, because trying to seem a little smart leaves you a 'midwit'.** From the Season 3 episode with Vu Tran, which resonated most with subscribers. The hosts add that intellectually honest people who say 'we can't move forward until I understand this' earn more respect than those who pretend. _[Vu Tran]_ — [2025-03-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=I04_Nc8odHw)
- **The power of a network is casual, reciprocal access: being able to text an expert friend without thinking twice and get a fast answer.** When a company in an industry the sisters knew nothing about sent a collaboration contract, Jean texted a friend who is a foremost expert in that industry; she reviewed it and sent back a quick spreadsheet on how the math would work. This echoes Imran Khan's line that 'you're not really my friend unless I can just text you'. _[Imran Khan, Sisters Matcha]_ — [2025-04-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dIFWvJ_i7HY)
- **Maintaining relationships requires intentionality: when an article makes you think of someone, send it right away with a two-line note instead of waiting for networking to happen.** Cherie regularly sends articles to Glenn Kramon, a former New York Times editor of 36-37 years, and uses the note to update him on what she and Jean are working on; do not spend 20 minutes rereading the email, just say 'thought of you, here is why' and send. _[Glenn Kramon, New York Times]_ — [2025-04-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dIFWvJ_i7HY)
- **Treat networking contacts like friends and text-message casually; people generally want to be friends with you and will treat you as a friend if you treat them as one.** Jean admits she over-thinks and over-edits outreach emails, creating a barrier to sending; unless you are emailing the Prime Minister of Norway, a casual mindset is fine. Outreach is a muscle that gets less scary with repetition. — [2025-04-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dIFWvJ_i7HY)
- **To replicate an MBA network without the degree, offer help before asking for it; networking and mentorship are far more about giving than receiving.** Networking gets a bad reputation because people take, take, take; before asking someone to mentor you, open your own network and resources first. — [2025-04-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dIFWvJ_i7HY)
- **Even a junior person can help someone five steps ahead: a relevant article is an overture anyone can make and it builds goodwill without trying.** Cherie sent Glenn Kramon an article about LinkedIn profiles that matched his lecture topic; he replied within 15 minutes saying he would include it in his next lecture, a tangible difference from a small gesture. _[Glenn Kramon, LinkedIn]_ — [2025-04-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dIFWvJ_i7HY)
- **You have to be as much of an expert about your reader as about the partner across the table; the magic moment is when one idea serves both.** (Leah Wyar) Leah says there were 3 or 4 moments in her career where she 'nailed it' this way and she got addicted to that feeling, which is what she wanted to keep doing as long as possible. — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **Work wives are a survival mechanism: you spend most of your life with your work family, and you need people to vent, cry and be real with to get through hard stretches without resentment.** (Leah Wyar) Jumping ship rarely resolves frustration because you find the same frustrations elsewhere; a trusted colleague helps you flow into the next stage. Cherie recalls rage-quit moments at Zynga with her work wife Maya solved by a venting walk and a 3-minute hug. _[Holly (Cosmo, Hearst), Zynga, Maya]_ — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **Decades-long work friendships become therapists and shorthand partners; even a supportive husband 'gets it but doesn't get it' like your girls at work do.** (Leah Wyar) Leah's friends Holly, Meredith, Melissa and Jo have moved companies together since Cosmo. Her therapist prescribes a walk with a friend or a 3-minute hug in eject-button weeks. _[Holly, Cosmopolitan]_ — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **Build your network broadly, meaning both peers and senior leaders; the two common traps are only befriending your cohort or only brown-nosing executives.** You need seniors to vouch for you at promotion time, but peers go on to do incredible things and vouch for you later for startups or business school; skipping peers reads as political and alienates them. — [2025-05-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qZvfC4TOAD0)
- **Telling peers what you want next puts you top of mind when opportunities appear; that is how Jean got recruited from Goldman to Zynga.** Zynga's founder Mark Pincus (ex-finance, HBS) poached second-year analysts from Goldman's TMT banking group, reasoning Goldman, Bain and McKinsey had already screened for driven people he could teach product management; Jean only heard about the info session because banking friends knew she wanted consumer tech. _[Zynga, Mark Pincus, Goldman Sachs TMT group, Bain, McKinsey]_ — [2025-05-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qZvfC4TOAD0)
- **Building in public and stating your goals openly beats hustling in private because people can only pull you into opportunities they know you want.** The hosts contrast the 'grind is private, show up with your funding round' philosophy with their own approach of sharing ups and downs and goals. — [2025-05-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qZvfC4TOAD0)
- **You don't have to go to a networking event alone; bring a buddy you can regroup, strategize and debrief with, or make it your goal to find a 'conference buddy' if you go solo.** Cherie forwards every relevant conference to friends who would benefit. Jean adds that if you attend the same industry conferences you will see the same people, and someone who came alone will be relieved to pair up for future events. — [2025-05-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p4gBwESgzPI)
- **Networking events are one of the safest places to introduce yourself to strangers because everyone is there for a positive social experience; open with 'is this your first time at this event?'.** Cherie contrasts it with the middle-school cafeteria feeling; the first-timer opener (e.g. 'is this your first VidCon?') works whether the answer is yes or 'I've been three or four times'. _[VidCon]_ — [2025-05-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p4gBwESgzPI)
- **Read body language before approaching: never interrupt an intense one-on-one, but join large open circles, people standing alone, or someone getting food or a drink at the bar.** Jean says two people locked in conversation are a 'see yourself out' zone; the bar or food area is a safe place to intercept someone and break the ice. — [2025-05-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p4gBwESgzPI)
- **How much you get out of a conference depends mostly on your own internal mindset, mood and openness going in, so hype yourself up, or give yourself grace and skip it if you truly can't.** Jean flew to the Shoptalk conference in London as a Snapchat vendor after an exhausting week of onsites, was in a 'state of non-openness', could barely talk to people, and the conference was almost a total wash. Cherie once had to give a speech in Chinese at a Shanghai conference and had no energy left for networking; attending for work with vendor goals is very different from attending for yourself. _[Shoptalk, Snapchat]_ — [2025-05-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p4gBwESgzPI)
- **There is serendipity in networking: each additional conversation raises the odds one is great, and one event tends to open doors to after-parties, lunches and future events.** A friend who went to a conference alone after failing to recruit buddies shared an Uber with a conference organizer and got invited to another conference for free. Cherie's line: instead of 'what's the worst that can happen', ask 'what's the best that can happen?'. — [2025-05-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p4gBwESgzPI)
- **Skip the $3,000 ticket and post up in the lobby of the main conference hotel taking meetings all day; the serendipity happens there too.** A friend of Jean's arranges back-to-back meetings with attendees without buying a ticket. Cherie, ticketless at the most recent VidCon, sat working in the Hilton lobby without headphones, started chatting with a man four feet away, and he became a LinkedIn connection who introduced her to a useful media contact. It requires getting over the fear of being cringe or rejected. _[VidCon, LinkedIn]_ — [2025-05-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p4gBwESgzPI)
- **Prepare by getting the attendee list (ask organizers if needed), checking Eventbrite/Partiful guest lists for side events, and researching speakers, then reach out to targets before the conference to set up time.** Jean's template: 'I saw you're going to this conference, I'd love to connect while we're both in Miami.' Looking someone up takes three seconds and can surface a career overlap or business deal. Summary: be prepared, then attend with a fun, open attitude rather than pressure to achieve. _[Eventbrite, Partiful]_ — [2025-05-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p4gBwESgzPI)
- **When communicating with executives above your direct boss, think 'Twitter, not Tolstoy': keep it high level and lead with the new information or value you bring so they can grok it in minimal time.** Cherie learned this framework in her Stanford GSB communications class; the goal is to make a good impression without wasting a senior person's time. _[Stanford Graduate School of Business]_ — [2025-05-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p4gBwESgzPI)
- **Practice the 15-minute, 5-minute and 2-minute versions of any update for a senior leader; deliver the two-minute version and let them ask for more or send details by email.** The exercise forces you to distill the takeaways; Jean notes you often only catch an executive for a couple of minutes between meetings and they need enough to decide quickly. The same applies to written emails with sub-bullets. _[Stanford Graduate School of Business]_ — [2025-05-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p4gBwESgzPI)
- **Don't be rigid or scripted with executives; know your material deeply enough to react to curveballs and have a real conversation, because they want to work with a person, not a robot, and can sniff out inauthenticity.** Cherie says knowing the material is the deeper layer of preparedness that frees you from talking points; 'be chill, be funny, be yourself' if the environment is safe, without revealing every inner demon. — [2025-05-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p4gBwESgzPI)
- **To exit a conversation, pre-arrange for your buddy to come pull you out in 15-20 minutes, with a signal to wave them off if the conversation is good.** This takes the pressure off you, like the friend rescue signal at a party but in a professional setting; the risk is the buddy forgets or is in the bathroom. — [2025-05-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p4gBwESgzPI)
- **Otherwise the onus is on you to speak up: at networking events you only need the thinnest excuse plus a close, such as 'I need to say goodbye to Amy before I go, let's connect on LinkedIn'.** Politely pivot, thank them, and propose exchanging information to continue later. Just saying 'I'm going to get water' is not enough because they may follow you or expect you back; pair the refill with a clear goodbye. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2025-05-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p4gBwESgzPI)
- **Listening with empathy is the most underrated workplace skill; the trick is not just listening but making the other person feel heard.** As a cross-functional senior PM at LinkedIn (Jean) and head of product at Snap (Cherie), most of the senior job was managing relationships, which starts with listening; many workplace problems stem from people feeling ignored or overridden. _[LinkedIn, Snap]_ — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **Repeat back what someone said ('What I'm hearing is X, did I get that right?') as a forcing function for active listening and to confirm your comprehension.** It stops you from planning your next point while they talk, lets them correct you, and builds goodwill even when you can't act on their concern. Cherie says it is a good starting method for people who are not naturally empathetic listeners. — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **Speak last in a meeting, summarize others' points by name, then add your own: it builds consensus and allies because people who are named perk up and back your point.** Exercise: in a low-stakes meeting say 'what I heard Brittany say was X, Tim said Y, I like that because...' then make your point. Great storytellers are seen as the summarizer who connects everything; referencing others gives them automatic buy-in. — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **Executive presence is confidence, calm and gravitas conveyed mostly through nonverbal cues such as eye contact, posture and voice, not what you say.** The hosts cite studies that people focus more on how you look and sound than on content; it is the number one skill to be taken seriously in any room. — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
