- **Executive presence is useless without allies in the room, so in any new company deliberately cultivate mentors and allies across every part of the organization, not just your own group.** Notice who you gravitate toward and who gravitates toward you, then lean in; a network spread across the company is far more powerful than one concentrated in your team. — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **Never open with 'will you be my mentor?'; let mentorship emerge organically after two or three coffee chats where you bring real questions.** A direct ask is jarring when the person doesn't know you. The exception is a formal mentor-matching program, which is like an explicitly romantic date. Jean keeps a tracker of admired colleagues to reach out to and follows up if they don't reply. — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **Ask your friends who their mentors are and get introduced, and seek mentors in other orgs who can sponsor you from a different vantage point.** Early at Goldman in investment management, Cherie asked a friend in sales and trading about her mentor Teresa, got an intro, and built a lasting relationship; senior people are often eager to mentor juniors because any skill can help them. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **Come to every mentor conversation with an explicit agenda of two or three things you want, which earns you the mentor's network and resources.** A mentee who asked Jean what she was watching on Netflix left her feeling unhelpful; a prepared, directive mentee signals they won't waste the time of anyone the mentor introduces them to, so mentors open up their network. — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **Negotiation and influence is the 'final boss' skill that only works once you have listened, built a network and developed presence; it starts with knowing your own resources and everyone else's.** At Snap, Cherie and colleague Jeff were pulled into a last-minute exec presentation in New York; she worried about building slides until she learned Jeff had a team that excelled at decks, so they split content and production. _[Snap]_ — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **Most negotiations are not zero-sum: gather information on what the other side actually wants and you can both get 100% (the orange peel and the juice) instead of splitting 50/50.** From Jean's Stanford negotiation class with Michele Gelfand: splitting an orange in half satisfies no one, but if Sally wants the peel for dessert and you want the juice, both win fully. Applies to comp talks too, where a manager may have more room on bonus than salary. _[Michele Gelfand, Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **Three-step influence framework: research the other party's goals and KPIs, frame your ask so it helps them hit those goals, then enter ready to trade off and invent creative solutions.** The hosts note 'negotiation' sounds adversarial but every conversation is really a negotiation; in a comp talk, start from the shared premise that both sides want you to stay and grow at the company. — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **Turn macro stress into a specific action within your expertise: during the BLM protests Kofi analyzed startup podcast guest lists and published 'Black Minds Matter' on TechCrunch.** (Kofi Ampadu) He found Black people were underrepresented as guests on prominent startup podcasts and argued that getting Black experts on product, engineering and investing onto those shows changes perception of a whole race; the hosts call this the ideal use of data and the bully pulpit. _[TechCrunch, Black Minds Matter (article), Black Lives Matter]_ — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **Come to every mentor coffee chat with a prepared list of roughly ten things to discuss and why this person specifically is the one to ask.** Goldman rule 7 is 'seek out mentors and manage them'; time is the only resource you cannot get more of, so respect it by preparing for five minutes, arriving on time and having looked at their LinkedIn profile. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-06-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eFpgcrlf2gk)
- **Follow up within 15 minutes of a coffee chat with a specific thank-you: name one thing you took away and how you will apply it.** Template: 'Thanks so much for your time, I really enjoyed our conversation, especially XYZ, and I'm going to apply it in ABC way.' ChatGPT can help; the small touchpoint makes mentors feel heard and is what managing relationships means. Jean admits her Goldman-bred perfectionism makes her procrastinate such emails. _[ChatGPT]_ — [2025-06-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eFpgcrlf2gk)
- **There is such a thing as a stupid question: one that has already been asked and answered to you, so carry a notebook, write the answer down and never ask twice.** Jean's addendum to rule 9; a clarifying re-ask ('could you explain it another way?') is fine, but repeating the same question because you did not listen disrespects the other person's time. — [2025-06-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eFpgcrlf2gk)
- **First impressions form in the first 30 seconds from three Vs, visual, verbal and vocal, and much of it happens before you open your mouth.** Goldman rule 10, 'first impressions last a long time', combined with a Stanford GSB communications lecture: how you dress and hold yourself, what you say, and how you say it. Cherie adds a fourth V, vibes. You get one shot and impressions are hard to change. _[Stanford GSB, Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-06-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eFpgcrlf2gk)
- **Being genuinely yourself, even if nerdy or awkward, earns more respect and is more endearing than a fake cool persona, and people can see through pretending.** Cherie recalls pretending to be a people-pleasing version of herself in a college relationship, losing herself, and finally finding her voice; the sisters reconcile their lessons as 'focus on your needs so that if it doesn't work out, you know you gave it your all as yourself'. — [2025-06-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DubkuAz2meM)
- **First impressions form in a tenth of a second and are mostly non-verbal, so posture, eye contact, a solid handshake and not being on your phone matter before you say a word.** (Lorraine K. Lee) If an interviewer walks out and you are on your phone, or you open a video call with 'one second, I just have to send this message', you have already signaled distraction. Jean adds a dating parallel: do not look at your phone when your date goes to the bathroom. _[Unforgettable Presence]_ — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **Fix 'resting business face' on video by smiling, nodding slowly and raising your eyebrows, because neutral expressions read as angry on camera.** (Lorraine K. Lee) At home we are relaxed and not thinking about our faces, and video magnifies that. Cherie notes this is especially pertinent for women, who are societally expected to exude warmth; Lee says perception is reality and the skill becomes natural with practice. — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **Repeat things seven times in seven different ways, because a single 10am Slack message misses APAC, Europe and everyone drowning in messages.** (Lorraine K. Lee) Her former CEO's rule; send it in Slack, then email, then mention it in the team meeting so people do not miss it. — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **LinkedIn content has three pillars, personal, professional and educational, plus a bonus promotional one, and mixing them buffers the 'I'm humbled and honored' bragging tone.** (Lorraine K. Lee) Most people start with educational, then professional industry commentary; personal stories are hardest but resonate most, and you can build up to them over time. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **Nothing on LinkedIn is original, so stop waiting for a perfect thought; people stand out through their own experiences and packaging.** (Lorraine K. Lee) The most common question she gets is from people hesitant to click post; the content is all the same, the people and framing differ. Jean admits she never posts and only drafts off Cherie's tags. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **Adding the word 'because' to a request increases compliance by up to 93%, even when the reason is weak.** (Lorraine K. Lee) Use it for deadlines: 'fill this out by Friday because I'm excited to share what you did with the company' or 'because I'm out Friday'. Cherie recalls the Xerox copier line study where 'because I need to make copies' still got people to yield, and a friend's mom who asks for discounts at Louis Vuitton just 'because'. _[Xerox copier study, Louis Vuitton]_ — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **Write an 'email haiku': a message concise enough to fit on a smartphone screen without scrolling, respecting the recipient's time.** (Lorraine K. Lee) The idea comes from Kim Scott, author of Radical Candor, one of three dozen leaders Lee interviewed for her book. Spending 20-30 extra minutes eliminating the excess avoids back-and-forth clarification and makes teams more productive. _[Kim Scott, Radical Candor]_ — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **The subject line is prime real estate and your email's first impression; tag it 'action needed', 'FYI, no response needed' or 'due April 7'.** Jean cites Stanford's Winning Writing class with Glenn Kramon, a 37-year New York Times editor, on email hygiene since people read on phones. Cherie says in finance they wrote the whole message in the subject line ending with EOM (end of message), a power move. _[Glenn Kramon, Stanford GSB, New York Times]_ — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **Showing your hands on video makes you appear warmer, more energetic and more trustworthy, and gestures raise recall of what you say by 33%.** (Lorraine K. Lee) Frame the camera with three to five fingers of space above your head and show some torso, then lift your hands slightly higher than feels natural so they are visible instead of twitching shoulders. Cherie compares the added movement to CoComelon changing the screen every two seconds, and says gesturing can trick her into higher energy on low-energy Zooms. _[CoComelon]_ — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **On stage, match your hand movements and gaze to your message, e.g. counting one-two-three or placing each point in a different part of the room, so vocal and visual align.** From a Stanford executive presence and public speaking lecture Jean took; it makes you seem locked into and believing your own message. _[Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **Cut minimizing language: replace 'does that make sense?' with 'let me know if you have any questions', 'sorry I'm late' with 'thank you for your patience', and 'I think' with 'I recommend' or 'the data suggests'.** (Lorraine K. Lee) 'Does that make sense' is the phrase everyone in her workshops admits using three times a day; it implies you are unsure of what you just said, while the swap stays collaborative but confident. Small tweaks compound into more authority over time. — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **Map your social capital: who in your network trusts you enough to vouch for you and make introductions.** This exercise comes from Stanford GSB's 'Paths to Power' class and mirrors how warm intros work in VC. _[Paths to Power (Stanford GSB)]_ — [2025-07-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bu50AgK6UIw)
- **Vulnerability takes courage but strengthens connection and creates social accountability so you don't have to pursue goals alone.** Citing Brene Brown, the hosts note 84% of people don't tell even their closest friends about major life changes for fear of judgment; sharing a gym goal turns a friend into an accountability buddy. _[Brene Brown]_ — [2025-07-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=McWujpZMzLw)
- **Apply design thinking to a stuck personal problem by writing down what the other person (boss, partner, customer) fears and needs before brainstorming any solution.** Observe feelings and behaviors first; the mini exercise is to walk one life problem through the five steps. — [2025-08-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NLxFGD_WaBw)
- **Let Them can become a conflict-avoidance excuse and create an accountability vacuum with people you see constantly, because you never give them the information or tools to fix the relationship.** With close friends, coworkers or family, just saying 'let them' without a conversation removes agency from both sides; Cherie's version worked because she communicated her expectations first. — [2025-08-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H-xnPI_rUqk)
- **Don't navigate existential questions alone; keep a personal board of advisors you can call.** Both hosts have one; they also invite listeners to treat the comment section as their board of advisors. _[personal board of advisors]_ — [2025-08-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QUN5LWuRwu0)
- **Language doesn't just reflect power, it creates it; the powerful person in a room speaks most clearly, not loudest.** Taught by Stanford GSB professor Deb Gruenfeld; hedges like 'I just wanted to say' or 'I think maybe' weaken the message and read as uncertainty rather than niceness. HBR's 'Your Words Matter, Talk Like a Leader' covers the gendered dimension. _[Deb Gruenfeld, Harvard Business Review, Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-09-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HjebjpDU8-Q)
- **Record yourself, count your hedges and filler words ('just', 'sorry', 'I think', 'like'), and rewrite them; replace fillers with a deliberate pause.** Practice from Jean's Stanford executive communications class. Michelle Obama, in 'Becoming', rehearsed speeches for weeks to strip fillers and studied cadence and pauses like a performer. 'I just wanted to follow up' becomes 'Following up on our last note'. Tools like Read AI now count filler words for you. _[Michelle Obama, Becoming, Read AI]_ — [2025-09-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HjebjpDU8-Q)
- **Feedback fails when people jump straight to impact ('you made me feel bad') without the situation and behavior; say 'I felt X when Y happened' instead of 'you made me feel.'** The small change in syntax and ordering defuses defensiveness and makes the conversation feel conversational rather than confrontational. — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **Never assume another person's intent, only describe the impact of their behavior; assuming intent is 'crossing the net.'** Intent versus impact is a key distinction from Touchy Feely; you cannot know what is going on in someone else's head. _[Interpersonal Dynamics]_ — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **If you can't get to your point in 30 seconds, people tune out; being clear is more powerful than being smart.** Jean used to think being smart meant long detailed explanations; the hosts note Donald Trump's communication is powerful precisely because he reduces everything to the simplest words, e.g. 'big beautiful bill.' _[Donald Trump, Matt Abrahams]_ — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **Structure any pitch with SCQA: situation, complication, question, answer.** Example: 'CAC is rising faster than LTV (S); if we don't adjust we'll overspend (C); what do we do (Q); shift 40% of spend to UGC creators (A).' The method comes from Barbara Minto and is taught by Matt Abrahams, who coached Cherie for her TED talk. _[SCQA, Barbara Minto, Matt Abrahams]_ — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **Practice simplifying language deliberately, the way Glenn Kramon's writing class at Stanford opens each session by simplifying ten terms.** Cherie offers this 'simplify, simplify, simplify' drill as a bonus exercise for clearer communication. _[Glenn Kramon, Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **Test your pitch by writing it in SCQA, saying it aloud, and reading it to a friend; if they can repeat the idea back in one sentence you've done it right.** This is the mini exercise for the executive communication framework. — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **Role-play salary negotiations and difficult conversations with a trusted friend so you freeze in practice instead of in the real thing.** Stanford GSB did role play in every class, including firing an employee, with multiple partners and reactions (sad, then angry) so you rehearse the emotions and anticipate pushback; the hosts think it is badly underused. _[Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-09-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_L_wX5RMxh4)
- **Form a wealth circle of four or five trusted girlfriends to share numbers, because men already do this in group chats and information is power.** Set ground rules of confidentiality and weekly or monthly topics like rent or pay; NBER research shows social accountability raises savings by 45% versus a control group via descriptive social norms plus mild competition. _[National Bureau of Economic Research]_ — [2025-09-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_L_wX5RMxh4)
- **To learn to read people without childhood trauma, read fiction, watch drama and reality TV, and read memoirs.** Reality TV situations are manufactured but people react true to their nature, so you can study why one person acted one way and another did not; the hosts recommend this even for people on the spectrum or who struggle with faces and micro-emotions. _[Grey's Anatomy, Succession]_ — [2025-09-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TkXvlgPXitc)
- **Build a library of interactions by deliberately meeting people from backgrounds different from your own.** Jean argues wide variety in who you talk to lets you attune to different types of people and builds deep empathy, complementing Cherie's fiction recommendation. — [2025-09-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TkXvlgPXitc)
- **To find motivated, aligned friends, go where those people already gather: run clubs for health, women's entrepreneurship events for ambitious peers.** Paid events in New York, SF and LA are effectively paying for access and give you a reason to talk; ask what type of person you want to attract and where they naturally go. — [2025-09-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TkXvlgPXitc)
- **The power move is the platonic ask: when you feel like-mindedness with someone, follow up and invite them to coffee, lunch or drinks.** Jean is rebuilding a single-person friend roster after years of couple friends with her ex-fiance; the worst case is no reply, the best case is a beautiful friendship, so there is only upside and no ego. It is a 'bid for a relationship'. — [2025-09-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TkXvlgPXitc)
- **Successful people apply the same relationship lessons to business, work and love; they are the same unlock.** Jean gives the example of a friend who runs a $100M+ fund calling her right back with 'I have 9 minutes, tell me what you need' as the kind of reciprocity that works across all relationships. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **People remember how you treated them, not the skills you taught them, and it always comes back around.** (Maggie Sellers) Former assistants from jobs 5-6 years ago texted congratulations on her marriage, a reminder that relationship building beats transactional relationships. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **Storytelling is the most powerful skill in business today, and hardship is what gives you a story.** (Maggie Sellers) Her most viral video was crying on the couch after calling off her engagement at 30, contrasted with being married 2.5 years later; without the detour there is no story, and as AI arrives storytelling matters even more. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **Treat similar female creators as collaborators rather than competitors; that mindset is why so few women are on the top charts.** Tiger Sisters and Hot Smart Rich were paired through Spotify's mash-up program by partner manager Trace Gaynor; different personas (Ivy League MBA hosts vs. a non-Ivy creative problem solver) reach different women toward similar goals. _[Spotify mash-up program, Trace Gaynor, Hot Smart Rich]_ — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **Referrals put you at the top of a 1,000-resume stack, and no hiring manager reads the whole stack once they find a great candidate in the first five.** Senior hires are still merit-based, but for brevity and efficiency the first look always goes to referrals and people from the hirer's own school network. — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **Joining an affinity group like Women in Computer Science gives you peer mentors and a safe haven among the sharp elbows of a competitive major.** Cherie found upperclassmen who had survived the classes, bonded over the curriculum and internship hunt, and used the group as her support system at Columbia. _[Women in Computer Science]_ — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **In the workplace too, brilliance is useless if you cannot translate it into common language, because what you cannot communicate will not get built.** The hosts compare untranslatable professors to the brilliant machine-learning PhD engineer on a tech team who is 10,000 leagues above everyone and cannot explain what they are saying. — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **Tiny insider insights can change lives, which is why the podcast exists: making the mistakes so you do not have to.** The Harvard football story shows outsiders whose parents never attended elite schools have no one to consult; knowing what questions to ask and what is appropriate is the missing information. _[Tiger Sisters]_ — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **Assuming good intent in strangers can change the trajectory of your life and career, even though you will occasionally get burned.** (Suzy Welch) Jack treated every stranger as a friend not yet met and found a party of strangers 'could anything be more exciting'; going in with your dukes up costs you relationships and conversations. _[Jack Welch]_ — [2025-11-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eRJyWu1CgI0)
- **Having a shared vocabulary for values lets people name what they know in their heart and build a bridge to understanding each other.** A Stanford student connected the work to Wittgenstein's 'the limits of my language are the limits of my world'; the sisters say having the word eudaimonia lets them finally express something they felt. _[Wittgenstein, Stanford]_ — [2025-11-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eRJyWu1CgI0)
- **When you feel yourself getting hot, pull out, breathe and respond afterward; and if you react anyway, apologize fast because apologies cost nothing and mean a lot.** (Mike Rabil) Mike lets his body signal when he's hot and adjusts; Cherie apologized within five minutes. Missing one email over a working weekend doesn't kill a deal and following up shows you care. — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **Apologizing when you've done nothing wrong doesn't make you relatable, it makes you look uncertain and undermines whatever idea follows.** Hedging phrases like 'I might be wrong, but,' 'sorry, quick question' and 'this might sound dumb' act as a pre-rejection of your own idea; the hosts cite a Harvard Business Review article that women use hedging language nearly three times more than men and a Psychological Science study that women apologize 37% more. _[Harvard Business Review, Psychological Science]_ — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **Replace apologies with clarification or gratitude: 'thank you for waiting' instead of 'sorry I'm late,' 'do you have a moment' instead of 'sorry to bother you,' 'let me be more clear' instead of 'sorry if that's confusing.'** 'Thank you for waiting' is the best one to start with because it shifts the moment to gratitude and makes the other person feel good; in personal life, say 'thanks for listening' instead of 'sorry for venting.' — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **Exercise: write down three sorry phrases you use in emails, meetings or texts and rewrite each into a confident, direct alternative.** The hosts say this one small shift changes how you are perceived by others and by yourself. — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **Over-preparation holds women back in the room: make the point with an approximation and offer to pull the exact stat afterward, as men routinely do.** Because women are trained to be A+ students they freeze looking up the exact number instead of speaking; if challenged, say 'let me find it for you right after this meeting.' — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **Clarity comes from simplicity: break things down into simple terms through inbound clarity (listening effectively) and outbound clarity (how you communicate).** (Jim Keyes) He cites da Vinci and Coco Chanel on elegance in simplicity; in the information age we overcomplicate everything. Jean sees clarity as a force field protecting your goals from negative inputs. _[Leonardo da Vinci, Coco Chanel]_ — [2025-12-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wondntTp_cQ)
- **In Japanese business, 'hai' means 'I acknowledge you', not 'I agree', and Americans who mistake it for yes wrongly read renegotiation as an integrity problem.** (Jim Keyes) Learning even 'taxi cab' Japanese helped him steer 7-Eleven through its transition to Japanese ownership; non-Latin languages have no direct translation. _[7-Eleven Japan]_ — [2025-12-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wondntTp_cQ)
- **Every guest has come organically through the sisters' network, never cold outreach, so the through line across varied guests is the hosts themselves as curators.** Guests are 'nerd Hollywood': niche celebrities like billionaire real-estate and startup investor Fernando De Leon (a personal friend who built himself from nothing) and the Rabil brothers, founders of the Premier Lacrosse League who raised over $100 million, connected via Cherie's Dartmouth network. The ethos is sharing the one-on-one coffee chats they would otherwise have privately. _[Fernando De Leon, Rabil brothers, Premier Lacrosse League, Dartmouth]_ — [2025-12-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=M4HYavwtWzY)
- **Vulnerability creates social accountability and opens opportunities; sharing a goal or even a layoff invites support.** 84% of people don't tell their closest friends about major life changes for fear of judgement, yet people who post about layoffs on LinkedIn get empathy and job offers. Shame versus empathy is a core construct of Daring Greatly. _[Brene Brown, Daring Greatly, LinkedIn]_ — [2025-12-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NmdHxviASBg)
- **Openly admitting 'I don't know anything about this, teach me' is an invitation for help and has been a strength in building Tiger Sisters.** Neither host worked in media before; Cherie's mindset is 'I'm not an expert here but I'm an expert elsewhere and I can learn,' so inexperience isn't a weakness. _[Tiger Sisters]_ — [2025-12-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NmdHxviASBg)
- **First impressions form in as little as a tenth of a second, and most people get them wrong by ignoring nonverbal signals.** (Lorraine K. Lee) Posture, eye contact, a solid handshake, and not being on your phone matter; being on your cell phone when the interviewer walks out, or sending 'one quick message' at the start of a video call, signals distraction before you have said a word. _[Unforgettable Presence]_ — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)
- **'Resting business face' on video calls is a silent career killer; the fix is to smile, nod slowly, raise your eyebrows and consciously show engagement.** (Lorraine K. Lee) At home we relax into neutral or angry-looking expressions, which video magnifies. With limited touch points in a remote day, video presence has to do the work; the hosts note this is especially penalized in women, who are expected to project warmth. — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)
- **LinkedIn posts should draw on three content pillars, personal, professional and educational, plus a bonus promotional pillar, and nothing on LinkedIn needs to be original.** (Lorraine K. Lee) Educational is the easiest starting point, professional covers industry news, and personal stories are hardest but resonate most. People connect with the person and the packaging, not the novelty of the content, so stop waiting for a perfectly original thought. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)
- **Adding the word 'because' to a request raises compliance by up to 93 percent, even when the reason is weak.** (Lorraine K. Lee) Lorraine uses it for deadlines ('fill this out by Friday because I'm excited to share it with the company'). The hosts recall the Xerox copier study where 'because I need to make copies' still got most people to let the requester cut the line, and a friend's mother who gets designer-store discounts just by asking with a 'because'. _[Xerox copier study, Louis Vuitton]_ — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)
- **Write 'email haikus': messages short enough to fit on a smartphone screen without scrolling, with subject lines that carry the action.** (Lorraine K. Lee) The idea comes from Kim Scott, author of Radical Candor. Spending 20-30 minutes to trim saves back-and-forth clarification. Cherie learned similar email hygiene in Glenn Kramon's 'Winning Writing' class at Stanford; Jean notes finance teams write the whole message in the subject line and end it with 'EOM'. _[Kim Scott, Radical Candor, Glenn Kramon, Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)
- **Using your hands on camera makes you appear warmer, more energetic and more trustworthy, and gestures increase recall of what you say by 33 percent.** (Lorraine K. Lee) Frame yourself with three to five fingers of space above your head and show some torso so your hands appear naturally; most people keep hands in their lap so viewers only see shoulder twitches. Cherie's Stanford executive presence class taught matching gestures to enumerated points so vocal and visual align. _[Cocomelon]_ — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)
- **Cut minimizing language like 'does that make sense?', 'sorry I'm late' and 'I think', replacing them with 'let me know if you have any questions', 'thank you for your patience' and 'I recommend' or 'the data suggests'.** (Lorraine K. Lee) 'Does that make sense?' implies you are unsure of what you just said; the replacement keeps collaboration while signaling confidence. Arriving with 'thank you for your patience' starts the meeting on positive footing instead of forcing the other person to reassure you. — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)
- **Work wives are essential to career survival: they are the only people who truly understand your context and let you vent instead of rage-quitting.** (Leah Wyar) Leah's core group (Holly, Meredith, Melissa, Jo) has moved companies together since Cosmo and speaks in shorthand. Cherie recalls her Zynga work wife Maya and their vent walks ending in three-minute hugs. Leah's therapist prescribes the walk and hug when she wants to hit the eject button, and notes that jumping ship rarely resolves the same frustrations. _[Zynga, Cosmopolitan]_ — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)

### Money, investing & personal finance (63)

- **A fully paid-off family home can be the entirety of a household's net worth, which makes concentrated wealth catastrophic in a disaster.** For many multigenerational Black families in Altadena the house was their foothold into the middle class and their only generational wealth, with no diversified stock portfolio to fall back on when it burned; Jean cites an NPR segment on this. _[NPR, Altadena]_ — [2025-01-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_8AlNMC2l4)
- **After a disaster, the housing market moves off-market: agents go beyond pocket listings to solicit past clients to sell directly to displaced buyers.** A pocket listing is a home shown to select buyers before hitting the MLS; in post-fire LA, agents were calling old clients who had considered selling in a few years and asking them to sell now to displaced families buying homes or long-term rentals under the radar. _[MLS]_ — [2025-01-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_8AlNMC2l4)
- **Most people underestimate the power of compounding: start investing early, keep it conservative, and let time do the work rather than chasing lottery wins.** (Imran Khan) Imran's advice to his 35-year-old self: a dollar growing 15% a year doubles in five years, quadruples in ten and becomes 16x in twenty, which builds substantial financial safety without doing anything fancy. — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **Use your health insurance for therapy whenever you can, because out-of-pocket sessions run $300-$400 for 45-50 minutes.** Cherie paid $10 per session on LinkedIn's insurance and now pays far more without it but still considers it worth it; Jean always stayed in-network, which made finding a new therapist after job or insurance changes a burden since in-network therapists are often not taking new patients. A friend in 2011 already paid $350 a session. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2025-03-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=temJGDdDgwM)
- **Pre-allocate a fixed annual budget for therapy, like a friend's $10,000 a year, so you do the worrying up front and never agonize over each session.** Cherie's friend treats the $10,000 as the price of his mental health and skips a vacation instead; the hosts generalize this to any major expense, which requires a good handle on your overall budget. — [2025-03-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=temJGDdDgwM)
- **Having means to pay is not the same as willingness to pay; each person must decide their own price for mental health.** It feels weird to put a price on mental health, but Cherie went from $10 copays to full price and still chooses to invest in it, while friends pay $350-$400 per session out of pocket. — [2025-03-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=temJGDdDgwM)
- **Revenue is money coming through the door; profit is what is left after bills are paid, and billions in revenue can still be unprofitable if costs are high.** For Netflix, revenue is subscription fees and profit is what remains after servers, employees and content costs. _[Netflix]_ — [2025-04-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JGitajVC4IM)
- **Cash flow is the item closest to your bank account and differs from profit because upfront investments can sit on the balance sheet as assets while the cash is gone.** Netflix spends billions upfront producing shows that are booked as assets. Some businesses are cash poor while boring businesses can throw off recurring cash. _[Netflix]_ — [2025-04-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JGitajVC4IM)
- **Unit economics, the margin per unit or per customer, always comes up when evaluating a company, and customer acquisition cost must make sense against lifetime value.** If a Netflix subscription brings in $10 but content costs $12 per subscriber, the company loses money; Netflix's data scientists spend a lot of time on this. For Sisters Matcha the unit is a single can: cost to make and ship one can to a first-time customer. _[Netflix, Sisters Matcha]_ — [2025-04-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JGitajVC4IM)
- **401(k) matching is free money that most new grads leave on the table because retirement feels 40 years away.** Configure it once in the payroll portal and contributions come out automatically every two weeks; Cherie's company matched dollar for dollar, and many match up to a percentage such as 3%. Both hosts maxed out the match. — [2025-04-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e9C6UDbAIdw)
- **401(k) contributions are pre-tax, so roughly 30% more money goes in to compound for decades and you are taxed only on withdrawal, usually at a lower post-retirement rate.** Jean's example: $100,000 contributed pre-tax all goes to investments, versus paying about $30,000 in taxes if received as income; the hosts cite roughly 10% average annual growth and stress the power of compounding over 40 years. — [2025-04-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e9C6UDbAIdw)
- **Whether you front-load or drip your 401(k) contributions depends on your need for liquidity; money taken out before you see your paycheck is money you do not miss.** Cherie's ex-boyfriend maxed out contributions in a short window so his take-home was about $1,000 a month to 'get it over with,' while Cherie spread contributions across the year to avoid seeing a $700 paycheck. — [2025-04-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e9C6UDbAIdw)
- **Ramit Sethi's I Will Teach You to Be Rich changed both sisters' financial lives because it gives directive, specific actions rather than vague advice.** The book told Jean 15 years ago to open a Charles Schwab checking account online for its ATM-fee refunds and to put money in specific Vanguard funds; those remain her standards today. The hosts note it is really about healthy habits that compound into wealth. _[Ramit Sethi, I Will Teach You to Be Rich, Charles Schwab, Vanguard]_ — [2025-04-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e9C6UDbAIdw)
- **There is no silver bullet to getting rich; wealth is built by small actions accruing over a long period.** The hosts did not get rich overnight; everything they did was compounding good financial habits and having a strong foundation after graduation. — [2025-04-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e9C6UDbAIdw)
- **Budget guideline: about 50% of take-home to fixed costs, 10% to investments, 5-10% to savings, and 20-25% to guilt-free spending.** Fixed costs are rent, food, car, utilities and student loans, though the sisters always optimized for cheap housing; investments are 401(k) and index funds; savings cover emergencies, travel or a wedding; guilt-free spending on spa, matcha or nails is the bucket Cherie most needed, from Ramit Sethi's framework. _[Ramit Sethi]_ — [2025-04-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e9C6UDbAIdw)
- **Once you check that your natural spending fits the guidelines, you can stop actively budgeting, but do not be so frugal that you forget to enjoy life.** Jean assessed her situation once or twice and never thought about it again, but regrets not being more permissive with clothes she loved; she and her banker and Facebook roommates furnished their first apartment with an $80 smelly Facebook Marketplace couch and a $10 scratched IKEA table while a friend paid $2,500 for a couch. _[Facebook Marketplace, IKEA]_ — [2025-04-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e9C6UDbAIdw)
- **Skipping car ownership and using public transit in San Francisco is a real money saver worth being proud of.** Cherie took the bus to work every day for all her years in SF (the 38R Rapid from Pacific Heights); Jean took the 22 line from Noe Valley. — [2025-04-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e9C6UDbAIdw)
- **Advanced tools to research once the basics are set: backdoor Roth, mega backdoor Roth and HSA.** The hosts have used these themselves but only name them as terminology to Google, since they are more complex and situation-dependent. _[backdoor Roth, mega backdoor Roth, HSA]_ — [2025-04-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e9C6UDbAIdw)
- **Tech employees paid heavily in RSUs end up with dangerously concentrated portfolios and should diversify rather than hold 90%+ of net worth in one stock.** Cherie learned this from a friend who leads a private wealth management team at Morgan Stanley and wishes she had known 5-6 years earlier; she uses NVIDIA engineers as the archetype. _[Morgan Stanley, NVIDIA]_ — [2025-04-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e9C6UDbAIdw)
- **An exchange fund lets you pool concentrated shares with others and swap into a diversified portfolio without selling and triggering capital gains taxes.** Minimums are typically several million dollars, often around $5 million, and you must keep the money in the fund for about seven years; Cherie instead sold and reinvested, paying high short-term gains taxes, and was told exchange funds are not new. _[exchange fund]_ — [2025-04-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e9C6UDbAIdw)
- **A zero-cost collar, buying a put and selling a call on a concentrated stock, limits your downside while capping upside, and the call premium pays for the put.** Most retail investors with options approval can do it, but it requires being in the weeds or working with a financial advisor; the hosts place it in the ultra-advanced category. _[zero-cost collar]_ — [2025-04-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e9C6UDbAIdw)
- **You do not need to come from money to build wealth, but you need knowledge, discipline and an understanding of the trade-offs.** Closing takeaway, paired with the reminder that money does not buy happiness but it can buy freedom and peace of mind; the hosts are not financial advisors, though Jean passed the Series 7 and 63 fifteen years ago. — [2025-04-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e9C6UDbAIdw)
- **Money is deeply emotional, so a good advisor starts by asking what you learned about money growing up.** (Ryan Edlifson) Ryan opens initial meetings with that question and says it regularly brings clients to tears; he calls himself part financial advisor, part therapist, and clients end up telling him everything. — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **An advisor should be financially transparent about their own money and never recommend anything they have not done themselves.** (Ryan Edlifson) Because he asks such personal questions, Ryan shares his own planning, savings and college accounts with clients, even showing Jean his own spreadsheets; it builds trust for relationships that may last 30-50 years. — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **The beginner's first step is a low-cost S&P 500 index fund with automatic monthly contributions of even $50-100.** (Ryan Edlifson) Set it up like paying rent or a 401(k) contribution; it feels like a bill at first and becomes a habit quickly. Index funds simply hold the index while mutual funds pay a manager to trade, and very few managers beat the market over any extended period. _[S&P 500, VOO, SPY]_ — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **Define speculative investing as a fixed dollar amount of 'play money' you can afford to lose, not a percentage of your portfolio.** (Ryan Edlifson) Ryan literally uses the phrase play money with clients: if it tanks, you should not feel bad or have to tell anyone. Cherie compares it to capping Vegas gambling at $300 at $25-minimum tables. — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **Keep about six months of expenses as an emergency fund in a high-yield savings account.** (Ryan Edlifson) Three months if you accept more risk, a year for the cautious; the number tends to grow as clients age and earn more. At roughly 4%, $10,000 earns $400 a year and $100,000 earns $4,000 for doing nothing, so the first question for anyone holding cash is how much interest they are getting. — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **The one non-negotiable automation is contributing to your 401(k) at least up to the employer match; it is free money.** (Ryan Edlifson) The sisters note this matched the advice they gave in their own earlier personal finance episode. — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **Intermediate and advanced savers should use the after-tax 401(k), or 'mega Roth', to shelter an extra $30-40K a year that grows and withdraws tax-free.** (Ryan Edlifson) Beyond the roughly $23,000 standard limit ($30,000 if over 50), many employers allow after-tax contributions that most people do not know about. _[mega Roth, after-tax 401(k)]_ — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **High earners in high-tax states should hold cash in Treasuries, which are exempt from state tax and easy to buy at Fidelity or Vanguard.** (Ryan Edlifson) Ryan uses Treasuries heavily for clients in California, New York and New Jersey; bond and Treasury index ETFs exist alongside stock ETFs, which was news to Cherie. _[Fidelity, Vanguard, Treasury bills]_ — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **Work becomes optional at roughly 25-30 times your annual income, or by the 4% safe withdrawal rule: $1M supports $40K a year, $5M supports $200K.** (Ryan Edlifson) Ryan suggests starting from how much you want to live on and working back to the required balance, accounting for taxes; the sisters were surprised how little $1M yields and joked they will not be retiring after the episode. _[4% rule]_ — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **The core 'stealth wealth' habit at any income level is saving at least 15% of gross income.** (Ryan Edlifson) Ryan works clients up to 15% over time if they are not there; if they are above it, great. — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **Lifelong savers often need permission to spend; much of an advisor's later work is helping clients spend guilt-free.** (Ryan Edlifson) Ryan shows clients what they can mathematically spend and encourages more fun, citing a 60-year-old client raised in India who cannot break a lifetime of saving every penny; he references the book Die With Zero and says his father's death shifted him from saving every penny to taking the trip now rather than 'next year'. _[Die With Zero]_ — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **Kids are not the worst financial decision, but they are expensive; there is always something to buy.** (Ryan Edlifson) Ryan, about to have his fourth child, says every outing costs money though a park day with a kid is still a great experience. — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **Company and asset ownership is the most hidden secret in American capitalism; you are never too young to own an asset.** (Fernando) He told his 21-year-old nephew to stop renting and buy a duplex, lease the other half to pay the mortgage, and own it outright by 36 after a 15-year amortization, even if it means living within his means and skipping restaurants. — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **Everyone has to find their own entry point to ownership: buy a stock, a bond, a house, a duplex, anything, and let it grow.** (Fernando) He says he cannot write anyone else's story; the simple statement 'own assets' is hard to do, so the job is to find the openings for yourself. — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **If you cannot invest in a great company directly, own the real estate it leases so rent becomes a royalty on its revenue.** (Fernando) Chick-fil-A is a family-owned private company, so he built its first building in San Angelo, TX on land he bought from a bank; $200,000 rent on $2 million of sales meant one in ten chicken sandwiches was effectively sold on his behalf. He repeated this with Starbucks, 7-Eleven, Raising Cane's, Chase Bank and CVS. _[Chick-fil-A, Starbucks, 7-Eleven, Raising Cane's, Chase Bank, CVS]_ — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **Crypto is a legitimate store of wealth in countries with currency devaluations and dysfunctional governments, but it must be treaded lightly and is hard to invest in on fundamentals.** (Fernando) He saw Mexican peso devaluations every presidential transition; a friend's crypto exchange serves mostly Latin America, Africa and Asia. He made money in crypto but calls it luck, and would not go as far as Charlie Munger's dismissal. _[Charlie Munger, Mexico, Nigeria, Angola]_ — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **As a customer of a PE-acquired brand, expect higher prices, worse service or leaner offerings, though operations can sometimes improve.** When Zimmermann was PE-acquired, Cherie and her friends rushed to buy current stock expecting lower quality and higher prices as margins get squeezed. Devil's advocate: cutting bloat can improve customer experience. _[Zimmermann]_ — [2025-07-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y95go30wv18)
- **PE as a financing mechanism isn't inherently evil, but you should understand it and follow the money to see what firms are optimizing for, because it affects your daily life.** 'Hot people understand incentives.' Knowing what PE is and what it optimizes for lets you predict how it affects your salad chain, hospital or clothing brand. — [2025-07-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y95go30wv18)
- **Women forfeit over half a million dollars over a career through invisible wealth avoidance: equity left on the table, raises never asked for and investments made too late.** Based on a McKinsey study; the hosts group the causes into three Ds, delay, doubt and default, and say this conversation is rarely had among women. _[McKinsey]_ — [2025-09-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_L_wX5RMxh4)
- **Delaying investing is far costlier than people think because of compounding: waiting 10 years to invest $5,000 a year costs almost $500,000 by retirement.** From Fidelity's calculator; compounding means you earn interest on interest, and it applies to stock market investments as well as savings accounts. _[Fidelity]_ — [2025-09-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_L_wX5RMxh4)
- **Beat delay by automating recurring investments and transfers so you never have to press the buttons again.** Jean routes her income into savings by default and auto-transfers to checking only what bills require, so cash earns interest and checking stays low. — [2025-09-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_L_wX5RMxh4)
- **Don't run your checking account so thin that orders bounce; an unfilled order can be a million-dollar mistake.** Jean once placed an order for 10 Bitcoin at about $40 each, the order bounced because her checking balance was too low, and she never re-entered it; automation would have retried the next week. _[Bitcoin]_ — [2025-09-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_L_wX5RMxh4)
- **Default is letting someone else choose: accepting the first offer, a 0% 401(k) contribution, or a 0.01% savings rate because that's how it was set up.** UBS found women hold 71% of assets in cash versus 60% for men, costing hundreds of millions in lost growth; Jean admits she naively assumes systems are set up in her favor when they are not. Automation also needs periodic checking. _[UBS]_ — [2025-09-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_L_wX5RMxh4)
- **In any relationship a woman should have her own money, career and ambitions, because money is used as power and control when a relationship goes south.** Cherie cites women who contribute enormously to a relationship but have no financial backing to show for it when it ends; she would rather work 90 hours a week, which she has done, than depend on someone financially. — [2025-10-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q49Hzzez6Fo)
- **Autonomy over your money, body and time is the top of Maslow's hierarchy; call it feminism or humanism, money is what gives you the means to decide.** Jean broadens Cherie's framing of feminism as control over your own destiny to 'humanism', with money as the enabler of autonomy. _[Maslow's hierarchy]_ — [2025-10-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q49Hzzez6Fo)
- **Spend guilt-free on experiences like travel and great restaurants because they are an investment in your worldview that you keep until you are 80.** Cherie's biggest spending category is experiences (surfing lessons, kayaking, luaus, sushi); designer bags do not fulfill her the same way, and she brings home new perspectives and cooking techniques. — [2025-10-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q49Hzzez6Fo)
- **Treat angel investments as money that will go to zero and as tuition for learning, ownership mindset and access to a new network.** Jean's most 'ridiculous' purchases are angel checks; diversify, only invest what you can afford to lose, and value being on the cap table for the inside view and connections to other angels. — [2025-10-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q49Hzzez6Fo)
- **Wealthy people play offense, not defense, with money, treating it as a tool to deploy and seeing abundant opportunities to create.** Attributed to Fernando de Leon, a 10-out-of-10 self-made billionaire on the Forbes list who grew up poor; his lesson is to make something out of nothing with creativity and elbow grease. _[Fernando de Leon, Forbes billionaires list]_ — [2025-10-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q49Hzzez6Fo)
- **You do not get rich overnight; the power of compounding only works if you start investing today rather than trying to time the market.** Cherie admits past mistakes trying to time ETF purchases; delaying investing can cost roughly half a million dollars over a career. Her advisor Brian says when you exit a single stock, put the proceeds straight into the S&P 500 or a diversified fund. _[S&P 500, Brian (financial advisor)]_ — [2025-10-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q49Hzzez6Fo)
- **The most successful investors are dead people: invest, forget about it, and do not chase a quick buck.** Untouched portfolios keep reinvesting for decades; get-rich-overnight plays have massive downside and are not the long game the sisters or their circles pursue. — [2025-10-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q49Hzzez6Fo)
- **Lend money to friends or family only if you can afford to lose it, because needing it back on a timeline puts the relationship at risk.** Set parameters and expectations with them and yourself; if there is any risk of resentment, it is not worth it. — [2025-10-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q49Hzzez6Fo)
- **Young people, especially women, should take more investing risk early with a small 'play money' learning account that expresses their interests.** Jean's controversial answer: treat investing like a hobby to build a positive lifelong relationship with money, without gambling your whole portfolio. — [2025-10-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q49Hzzez6Fo)
- **The biggest 20s money mistake is doing nothing because you feel you do not know enough; not making a decision is a decision, so act with 80% of the information.** Jean's default of waiting for the perfect answer was the worst outcome; perfectionists waiting for perfect timing lose over the long term. — [2025-10-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q49Hzzez6Fo)
- **Do an internal audit of your finances so you can set aside a guilt-free spending bucket instead of being scared to spend.** Ramit Sethi's I Will Teach You to Be Rich was a light-bulb moment for both sisters at different times; Cherie's takeaway as a new grad was guilt-free spending on travel once the money was earmarked. _[Ramit Sethi, I Will Teach You to Be Rich]_ — [2025-10-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q49Hzzez6Fo)
- **Everyone has a prenup: you write it or the government does.** (Maggie Sellers) She plans to share the prenup process (picking a lawyer, who starts the negotiation, who drafts first) to strip away the gold-digger stereotype, though she will never reveal what is in her own prenup. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **A prenup protects the woman too, not just the wealthier partner.** (Maggie Sellers) With a 14-year age gap, Maggie has 14 years to reach her husband's position; she came in with a decade of investing in women's businesses and a media company, so the prenup protects her future growth as well. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **The most expensive money lesson is 'ostriching': ignoring a problem makes it 10 times worse and costs real money.** (Maggie Sellers) She once paid for three places to live at once (a Canadian mortgage, a temporary LA apartment, and a lease for an ex she did not want to leave stranded) because she avoided a breakup; her therapist at nearly $400/hour weekly was 'a Range Rover payment' spent on unaddressed problems. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **Money rule one: there is no last best deal, so refuse the frenzy that advertisers, realtors and stock sellers create.** (Suzy Welch) She says this frame has saved her from many wrong turns, especially in real estate, because there will always be another house and another good deal. — [2025-11-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eRJyWu1CgI0)
- **Money rule two: fight the sunk cost fallacy and cut your losses the moment you know you made a mistake, publicly owning it.** (Suzy Welch) She sold a house after two years and real renovation spending, telling friends 'I made a mistake, I'm out, I may take a hit.' Decision science shows we are wired to pour more money on problems; resisting is a discipline that applies to jobs and relationships too. — [2025-11-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eRJyWu1CgI0)
- **Money talks reveal a partner's true financial picture, including whether they are subsidized by parents or still supporting them.** A teacher splitting travel and dining 50/50 with a tech earner may have family money behind him, which is more common than people admit; conversely, many people must provide for parents, which changes what they bring to the table. — [2025-11-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e2AV_kUtiVs)
- **Rent does not have to be split 50/50; the hosts recommend splitting proportionally to income.** Whoever earns 70% of the income pays 70% of rent and expenses. A friend's higher-earning boyfriend, now husband, paid more because he drove the decision to upgrade their apartment. — [2025-11-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e2AV_kUtiVs)

### Entrepreneurship & startups (100)

- **You won't find product-market fit until you've been rejected a dozen times or more, so rejection is a built-in part of founding.** (David Shim) David frames finding customers and product-market fit as a rejection-heavy process he was trained for by early sales jobs. — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **Start a business with whatever resources you have, even library printers and hand drawings; the key is near-zero input cost.** (David Shim) In elementary school David hand-drew Desert Storm trading cards, printed them on library computers (getting shut down for the $0.50/page cost), wrapped them with candy and sold them for $1-2 each, then moved to selling candy at school. _[Desert Storm trading cards, Topps]_ — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **Don't set out to 'be an entrepreneur'; instead notice a specific problem nobody is working on and build for it.** (David Shim) Placed began when David saw no one was converting smartphone location data into store visits; it grew to measuring a few billion dollars of annual advertising before Snap acquired it. _[Placed, Snapchat]_ — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **Break the rules within the law: Read AI broke the assumption that everyone should be in meetings by measuring real-time engagement.** (David Shim) An LLM summarizing a pitch would say 'great meeting', but tracking whether 20 people on a call are paying attention reveals a bad meeting, which is useful because it tells you to have fewer of them or to pivot in real time. _[Read AI]_ — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **Investor objections become a feature map: track the questions people ask and come back with one-two-three reasons each is solved.** (David Shim) Cherie adds that a friend pitched about 30 VCs while not raising, wrote down every question, and later closed a seed round with a tight argument; she plans to do the same for Sisters Worldwide. _[Sisters Worldwide]_ — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **Competition can be good for a novel business because competitors do the market education for you.** (David Shim) Read AI grew 10x on Microsoft in the year after Copilot launched; Cherie saw the same when TikTok launched AR ads competing with Snapchat's, and the sisters see it with Starbucks matcha helping Sisters Matcha, though low-quality cafe matcha also forces re-education. _[Microsoft Copilot, Zoom AI Companion, Google Gemini, TikTok, Starbucks, Sisters Matcha]_ — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **If you're solving a real problem, a flubbed pitch doesn't matter; the product speaks for itself.** (David Shim) When Snap's Imran Khan asked what he'd do with Snapchat big-picture, David gave a narrow answer about measuring store visits; Imran later called it 'the worst answer ever', yet Snapchat acquired Placed a couple of quarters later. _[Imran Khan, Snapchat, Placed]_ — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **The way to start anything is to just start; the first few attempts will be questionable quality but that's how you learn.** Jean answers the frequent question 'how did you make a podcast' by saying you minimize time-to-start and use the resources available, as David did with the library printer. — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **Product education and changing user behavior is the hardest part of a launch, so competitors doing that work with you cuts your job in half.** Building Snapchat's AR advertising platform, Cherie had to explain, demo, prove efficacy and then sell each advertiser; TikTok entering meant Coca-Cola and Pizza Hut had already heard the pitch. _[Snapchat, TikTok, Coca-Cola, Pizza Hut]_ — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **Question your constraints and know which rules you can break and which you cannot.** (Vu Tran) As a kid Vu always asked why; if his mother's answer was 'because that is how it is' or 'because I am the parent', that rule could not be derived from first principles and was potentially worth breaking, whereas a sufficient answer meant the rule made sense. — [2025-02-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0OopMnFAeoE)
- **Young founders succeed because they see with fresh naive eyes, but you also have to know what you don't know.** (Vu Tran) Vu cites Evan Spiegel and Mark Zuckerberg; at 19 he did not know how to hire or manage, what D30 retention was, bounded vs unbounded, power user curves, or how important distribution is. Zuckerberg credits Sheryl Sandberg for filling in what he did not know while he kept fresh eyes. _[Evan Spiegel, Mark Zuckerberg, Sheryl Sandberg]_ — [2025-02-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0OopMnFAeoE)
- **Go max smart or go max dumb; being a little smart lands you in the middle, which is the worst.** (Vu Tran) His team mantra is 'learn what you need to learn, but go max dumb': why do people use consumer apps? Because they are bored and lonely. So a successful consumer AI product must entertain; getting more sophisticated leads you into traps of false assumptions. _[Butterflies AI]_ — [2025-02-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0OopMnFAeoE)
- **Act as if there are no constraints when building something new; the rules often get written after you have already won.** At Zynga building FarmVille, Jean's team shipped features like one button that sent requests to all 1,000 of your Facebook friends because no explicit rule forbade it; Facebook banned it later, after the games were already on an upward trajectory. _[Zynga, FarmVille, Facebook]_ — [2025-02-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0OopMnFAeoE)
- **Dating apps' incentives are misaligned with users: a successful match means less time on the app, so the product is built to keep you swiping rather than find you love.** Cherie cites a16z investor Andrew Chen, who says he will never invest in a dating app because positive customer outcomes reduce engagement; Jean notes Hinge lost its original friends-of-friends premise and now feels like Tinder did ten years ago, 'unhinged'. _[Andrew Chen, a16z, Hinge, Match Group, Tinder]_ — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **Rules are generally put in place to stifle innovation, so figure out which rules are meant to be broken and break them mindfully.** (John Redgrave) Redgrave was always a rule breaker as a kid and believes that is what makes him a good entrepreneur, even as he tries to teach his own kids otherwise. The hosts dub it 'mindful rule breaking'. — [2025-02-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pAWpvZ2vn_Q)
- **Entrepreneurship starts with identifying a market need and realizing no one but you will address it, so the outcome rides entirely on your own effort.** (John Redgrave) His first job was building a web solutions business in the 1990s making websites for small and medium businesses. His work ethic came from family who worked hard for everything with nothing given, and he tells his kids daily that hard work brings good things. — [2025-02-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pAWpvZ2vn_Q)
- **To invest in culture you must participate in culture: get off the coasts, travel the Midwest, and reset your social feeds to see how everyone else lives.** (Peter Pham) He rarely visits a city twice, was just in Nebraska, and says if you only watch LA and New York you have no idea what is happening in the world. He recommends resetting TikTok and Instagram feeds and searching for unfamiliar things so the algorithm shows you other people's worlds. _[Liquid Death]_ — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **The test of a durable brand is whether people in its comment section treat it like customer service or like a friend, because relationships last longer than customer support.** (Peter Pham) On Liquid Death's TikTok and Instagram, fans joke with the brand and defend it against critics; on American Airlines' pages people just complain and ask for DMs. Pham told investors that day that this is how you evaluate culture brands, and says Liquid Death's community was built by design. _[Liquid Death, American Airlines, Dollar Shave Club]_ — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **Consumers are smarter than you think and know they are being marketed to, so treat the audience like they are in on the joke.** (Peter Pham) At Dollar Shave Club and Liquid Death the stance is 'we know that they know that we know'. Pick a position, own it and do not waver, because someone will hit you and if you flinch you get destroyed. _[Dollar Shave Club, Liquid Death]_ — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **Comment-led marketing works only for brands that are 'comment native' and authentic; copying Duolingo's snark does not work for Ryanair, Spirit or Quaker Oats.** The hosts predict a comment-led marketing playbook within two years and praise Duolingo's Gen Z social manager, but say budget airlines are only halfway there: to be truly in on the joke they would own how bad they are and repost passengers wearing all their clothes. Quaker Oats should start from first principles, e.g. recipe content, rather than snark. _[Duolingo, Ryanair, Spirit Airlines, Frontier, Delta, United, Wendy's, Quaker Oats]_ — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **Deeply understanding people and culture is how you create something out of nothing, like turning commodity water into a hardcore brand.** Jean recalls HBS marketing professor Youngme Moon assigning students to invent a branded water because water is a commodity; Liquid Death later proved there is room for 30+ water brands by creating a culture around canned water. _[Youngme Moon, Harvard Business School, Liquid Death]_ — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **Make customers feel part of the story: the hosts write every Sisters Matcha buyer's name on their office wall so customers appear in their videos.** People want to feel they contributed 1-10% to a win; the wall plus the story of the small family farm in Wazuka, Japan, differentiates their matcha the way heritage lets a handmade Hermes bag command $6,000-$150,000 versus $150 for another handmade French bag. _[Sisters Matcha, Hermes, Wazuka]_ — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **Growth and retention depend on shortening the time to the customer's 'aha moment', and the challenge is delivering that moment at scale.** Cherie borrows the concept from her LinkedIn onboarding work; for matcha the aha moment is standing on the farm hearing head farmer Daiki-san's story, which few can do, so the hosts plan a documentary and a May 2025 group trip to Japan with 15 spots. _[LinkedIn, LinkedIn Learning, Sisters Matcha]_ — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **Following a leader is hierarchical and only requires that they trust you, which jump-starts a junior career; cofounding is peer-based and trust must run both ways.** (Jeremy Liew) Jeremy draws this distinction when Cherie compares slipstreaming to how founders pick cofounders from people they've worked with before. — [2025-03-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=109Xypx-hM4)
- **The failure to be most grateful for: turning down a Lycos acquisition in college, watching the company collapse within a year, and having that lead to the companies that followed.** (Jonah Goodhart) Jonah and his middle brother, kids on financial aid, thought the company would never stop growing; losing a life-changing offer felt worse than never having it, but it led to a partnership, a second and a third company, ultimately including the $850 million Oracle exit. _[Lycos, Oracle, Moat]_ — [2025-03-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UYtMgkz8brA)
- **Failing at something once does not mean you are not destined for it; get back on the horse with more experience.** Jean's current startup is her second: she ran a startup full-time in 2016 with customers and a team and shut it down after a year because it was not the path she wanted after business school. Since then she has done $100 million deals, run major teams and built business units. Guest Vu similarly did Y Combinator at 18-19 and again at 22-23 and called the two a world of difference. _[Y Combinator, Vu (prior podcast guest), Sisters Matcha, Sisters Worldwide]_ — [2025-03-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fb8gYzg6ikg)
- **'I have everything I need' is printed on top of every Sisters Matcha can as a daily mantra to say while preparing the matcha.** The hosts chose the Peterson mantra as their brand's message; Jean notes repeating a mantra is functionally similar to standardized prayers asking for patience and grace. _[Sisters Matcha, Joel Peterson]_ — [2025-03-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=temJGDdDgwM)
- **The sleep-under-your-desk founder archetype is not the only or right path; you also don't have to be a tech entrepreneur to be a startup founder.** Jean absorbed the Silicon Valley 'founder is everything' mindset from very successful friends who slept in the office, and now sees it as context-dependent rather than required. Cherie admits she moves a mile a minute and doesn't give herself enough time to let things marinate. — [2025-03-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=I04_Nc8odHw)
- **Season 4 will shift from guest interviews toward topic-based sister-to-sister explainers on networking, public speaking and business school, with more personalized interview questions.** The hosts say Season 3's interview experiment worked, and the lesson learned is to be more nimble and personalized in interview questions going forward. _[Tiger Sisters]_ — [2025-03-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=I04_Nc8odHw)
- **Companies reduce buyer bargaining power by building loyalty and switching costs, such as loyalty programs, seasonal drinks, and a prepaid app balance that acts as a ledger with the customer.** Customers could go to Dunkin', Philz or Peet's, but Starbucks locks them in through the app and by owning the 'third space' idea, so 'let's meet at Starbucks' is ingrained from middle school on. _[Starbucks, Dunkin' Donuts, Philz, Peet's]_ — [2025-04-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JGitajVC4IM)
- **Mitigate the threat of substitutes by expanding into adjacent categories rather than staying a single-channel retailer.** Coffee has many substitutes (home, office), so Starbucks sells pods, beans and ground coffee to diversify beyond its retail stores. _[Starbucks]_ — [2025-04-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JGitajVC4IM)
- **Porter's generic strategies say you must pick cost leadership or differentiation; you cannot be the cheapest, the best and the highest quality at the same time.** Walmart ('everyday savings') is the cost leader and strips amenities to hit the lowest price; Starbucks chose differentiation with premium prices, third spaces, and high-touch operations like writing names and now personalized messages ('I love you so mucha') on cups. _[Porter's generic strategies, Walmart, Starbucks]_ — [2025-04-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JGitajVC4IM)
- **Sisters Matcha deliberately chose differentiation over cost leadership because its supplier cost, rarity and the founders' personal brand made a premium positioning the only coherent choice.** Their matcha uses the Okumidori cultivar, which is only 2% of matcha produced in Japan, hand-picked, ceremonial grade and first harvest; a cost-leadership brand would not align with something you put in your body daily for health. _[Sisters Matcha, Okumidori]_ — [2025-04-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JGitajVC4IM)
- **STP (segmenting, targeting, positioning) is the backbone of any marketing campaign or go-to-market: identify customer groups, choose one, craft a message for it and make it stick.** Warby Parker segmented eyewear buyers into high-end brand seekers (Prada, Gucci), the cost-conscious, and millennials wanting trendy but affordable, then targeted the third group. _[STP, Warby Parker]_ — [2025-04-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JGitajVC4IM)
- **If you try to serve everyone you serve no one: pick one target segment and build the whole brand around their hopes, frustrations and values.** Warby Parker built for online-first millennials with $95-100 pricing and a buy-a-pair-give-a-pair program, positioned as modern, chic, accessible, digital-first and socially conscious; it disrupted Luxottica, which controlled 80-90% of eyewear (including Ray-Ban) when cute glasses cost $300+, and is now valued at over $1 billion. The best products the hosts have used are hyper-focused on understanding their customers deeply. _[Warby Parker, Luxottica, Ray-Ban]_ — [2025-04-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JGitajVC4IM)
- **Conscious consumerism was a novel differentiator 10-15 years ago (TOMS, Warby Parker) that created brand loyalists, and it is now taken for granted as part of many companies' DNA.** TOMS gave a pair of shoes to a child in a developing country for every pair bought; later brands like Rothy's (recycled water bottle shoes) and Blueland built on the sustainability bend. _[TOMS, Warby Parker, Rothy's, Blueland]_ — [2025-04-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JGitajVC4IM)
- **In product development you cannot guess at a customer's problem; identify it very specifically, iterate like crazy, test everything, and pivot when new data arrives.** Between them the sisters have almost 20 years in product. Netflix is the case: it famously A/B tests every detail, including per-user thumbnails, so Jean sees Henry Golding in a thumbnail even when he is a secondary character because Netflix learned she is likelier to click. _[Netflix, Reed Hastings, Henry Golding]_ — [2025-04-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JGitajVC4IM)
- **Runway is how long your money lasts at the current burn rate, and a CEO must always watch it to stay afloat.** Spending $100,000 a year with $200,000 in the bank means two years of runway. — [2025-04-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JGitajVC4IM)
- **Your best ideas come in the shower or in the 10 minutes before sleep and after waking, when there are no incoming inputs; the difference is actually acting on them.** (Leah Wyar) Once you've taken an idea from brain to finished thing once, you have a playbook and the confidence you can do it again; 'that's the secret to it all.' — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **Working for yourself on something you believe in lets you give 120 percent with no regrets, which you would never do 'working for the man'.** The sisters describe building the airplane while flying it with Sisters Matcha, knowing no one in the industry, and finding it both scary and the most fun year of their lives. _[Sisters Matcha]_ — [2025-05-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xPAIT2MpsuY)
- **A founder's responsibility is to do everything they said they would do, not to carry the weight of the next black or female founder getting funded.** (Kofi Ampadu) Founders lose trust when they promise XYZ and do ABC; if you execute your plan and it still fails, investors are astute enough to know most companies fail. Resilience is a double-edged sword because being too resilient compounds your opportunity cost. — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **The founder instinct shows up early as side hustles, and being a founder is the time you make the least money but are happiest because you have complete agency over every problem.** (Kofi Ampadu) In high school Kofi burned and sold mixtape CDs and charged underclassmen a delivery fee for off-campus lunch ('I invented DoorDash'). As an employee you often cannot solve the issue in front of you; as a founder you can hire, learn or fix it yourself. _[H2Melon, DoorDash]_ — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **Going all in as a founder means cutting your own burn, even swallowing your pride to give up the nice apartment and move into your sister's basement.** (Kofi Ampadu) Kofi left a Manhattan apartment with a balcony to live in his sister's basement while bootstrapping, contributing groceries and free childcare; he argues you cannot claim the company needs money while paying rent you could redirect. Founders who raise should pay themselves the least, enough to live but not to save for vacations, because the payoff comes at the end. — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **False that founders must work 90-hour weeks: building a company is a marathon run at a fast pace, with some 5-minute miles and some 7-minute miles.** (Kofi Ampadu) Hard work does not mean constant long hours; there will be 90-hour weeks but not always, and TxO tells founders at its retreat to take breaks. _[a16z TxO]_ — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **Y Combinator is not a scam; it has product-market fit because people would still apply if it took 5 percent and gave no money, thanks to its network of potential customers.** (Kofi Ampadu) A SaaS startup in YC can sell to every successful YC alumni company, so even just for traction and feedback the network is worth it. _[Y Combinator]_ — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
