- **You do not need to code to start a tech company, but you must bring something else that makes you valuable: selling, marketing, or recruiting the best talent.** (Kofi Ampadu) AI tools lower the coding bar; the danger is being the idea person whose only valuable asset is the engineer you hired. — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **Building in public is not performative: it yields customer discovery, real-time feedback, and signals to investors how fast you move.** (Kofi Ampadu) If every post shows a founder jumping from B to F, investors want to get on that 'movement train'; the hosts note this validates their own build-in-public approach with Sisters Matcha. _[Sisters Matcha]_ — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **Capitalism is fundamentally fair: you are only rewarded when a group of people actually derive value from your good or service.** (Fernando) He contrasts this with venture groupthink, where a bubble of insiders assign a valuation, and the bubble bursts when consumers decide the product gives them nothing. — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **Turn access and relationships into equity: trade what you can uniquely obtain for a piece of the asset.** (Fernando) At 15 he used his grandmother's friendship with a union leader to secure job permits a developer had chased for a year, then negotiated 5% equity in the building instead of a fee, and repeated it four or five times; those buildings were his first owned assets. _[Leon Capital]_ — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **Do the small deals the big players think are beneath them.** (Fernando) Giant real estate firms would not bother with small single-tenant buildings for Chick-fil-A, so he did whatever it took and built long partnerships with those brands. _[Chick-fil-A]_ — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **To find a new business, ask what the opposite of a bad business is and enter a new field through your existing edge.** (Fernando) Offered an office building he disliked (high capex, tenant rollover, unpredictable cash flow), he asked what its opposite was and co-founded Crexi nine years ago to own commercial real estate data; it now has about 5 million users, hundreds of thousands of subscribers, roughly 600 employees, and is the second-largest CRE data purveyor in the US. _[Crexi, Zillow]_ — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **Starting from zero, find maneuvers that are not about capital: ideas, insight, persuasion, salesmanship and sweat equity.** (Fernando) For his first Dallas subdivision he optioned land from an 80-year-old landowner, Harold Pullman, who said it was unbuildable, got zoning changed from agricultural to residential to raise its value and unlock financing, and convinced engineers to work before being paid; it took a year and Pullman had failed for 40 years. _[Harold Pullman, Army Corps of Engineers]_ — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **When the traditional path demands capital and gatekeepers, pivot to a version you can build on your own terms.** Tiger Sisters was conceived as a reality TV show, but Hollywood strikes and the capital and relationships required led the hosts to mourn the idea for three days and launch a podcast at their kitchen table with a $400 camera instead. _[Tiger Sisters]_ — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **Authenticity plus real value to the audience is what people respond to; you cannot fake value.** (Fernando) He says Mr. Pullman responded to his authenticity just as viewers respond to the hosts, but the 'it is valuable to me' part must be there; the homes he built created home equity and net worth for families. _[Harold Pullman]_ — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **Founders forget to ask whether they are building something valuable to people, chasing valuations or 'something in AI' instead.** Cherie notes many startup founders want to build in AI without asking if it is valuable; Fernando adds that valuation-driven groupthink bubbles burst when real consumers do not value the product. — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **Mark Cuban is somewhat overrated as a business genius.** (Fernando) Yahoo wrote Broadcast.com down to zero within about a year of buying it, and Shark Tank investments seem to be mostly consumer products that are not very scalable, so he takes the under. _[Mark Cuban, Broadcast.com, Yahoo, Shark Tank]_ — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **Most problems that sink a company later are people problems, so poor team makeup or conflict resolution is a red flag; couples founding together are seen as a risk despite counterexamples like Canva.** Investors weigh whether a personal breakup could spill into the business, but Canva, founded by a couple, was a massive success. _[Canva]_ — [2025-07-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bu50AgK6UIw)
- **VCs need a multi-billion-dollar outcome, so they demand a large TAM, but an indefensible 'ten trillion dollar' TAM works against a founder.** What matters is not just total addressable market but how many of those people will actually convert; the hosts note Tiger Sisters has a narrower TAM than comedians but hits its audience more deeply. _[Tiger Sisters]_ — [2025-07-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bu50AgK6UIw)
- **Traction means the product is working and has product-market fit, measured by things like app downloads, month-over-month revenue and paying customers.** Mini exercise: pick a problem, estimate whether millions or billions of people have it, and articulate why you are the right team to solve it. — [2025-07-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bu50AgK6UIw)
- **Choose investors for the specific expertise they bring, not just the check, the way Shark Tank founders target a particular shark.** Robert Herjavec or Mark Cuban for software, Daymond John for apparel, Barbara Corcoran for real estate, Lori Greiner for CPG; the cap table (capitalization table) lists every investor in your company. _[Shark Tank, Mark Cuban, Barbara Corcoran, Lori Greiner, Daymond John, Robert Herjavec]_ — [2025-07-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bu50AgK6UIw)
- **VC money is not free: it comes with the expectation of extreme growth, which pushes startups into spend-now, figure-it-out-later behavior that can flame out.** WeWork and Quibi started strong with heavy funding and then collapsed; some investors demand biweekly or monthly updates, adding constant pressure. _[WeWork, Quibi]_ — [2025-07-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bu50AgK6UIw)
- **Do not take on investment unless you really have to; taking money too early or at all is one of the most common regrets founders share.** In Stanford GSB's 'Managing Growing Enterprises' class, founders' retrospectives repeatedly cite raising too early as a major mistake; investor money complicates hiring and operations and gives you an overlord. _[Managing Growing Enterprises (Stanford GSB)]_ — [2025-07-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bu50AgK6UIw)
- **Who you take on as an investor matters enormously because VC is a people business; founders vet investors through back channels and reputation.** 'We're hands-off, smart capital' is often just a sales pitch; the YC intranet has Yelp-style investor reviews where founders warn each other about investors who ghost after a term sheet. _[Y Combinator]_ — [2025-07-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bu50AgK6UIw)
- **Admitting 'I don't know what I don't know' when entering a new field is an invitation for help and has been a strength in building Tiger Sisters.** Neither sister had worked in media before launching a content-to-commerce venture; going into conversations openly as non-experts made people willing to teach them. _[Tiger Sisters]_ — [2025-07-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=McWujpZMzLw)
- **Design thinking means designing around what the user actually needs, not what you think the problem is; most products fail because they solve the wrong problem.** Invented at Stanford's d.school and popularized by IDEO (designers of the original Apple mouse), it works for any problem, from Google to the Gates Foundation, and is about empathy rather than being a designer. _[Stanford d.school, IDEO, Apple, Gates Foundation]_ — [2025-08-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NLxFGD_WaBw)
- **Design thinking has five steps: empathize, define the root problem, ideate without censoring, prototype something scrappy, and test by watching what users do, not just what they say.** User research is now an entire function at tech companies; observing behavior in testing is more reliable than self-report. _[design thinking]_ — [2025-08-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NLxFGD_WaBw)
- **Before building, spend real time observing customers in their natural setting.** Building AR beauty shopping at Snapchat, Jean's team spent an afternoon in a beauty store on Abbot Kinney in LA watching and interviewing shoppers to learn whether they needed to see color on their skin or needed to be in person. She says user research is one of the most enlightening and fun parts of building. _[Snapchat]_ — [2025-08-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NLxFGD_WaBw)
- **Test and learn: stop waiting to be perfect, ship a scrappy MVP, because a well-thought-out business plan is often nothing more than a fantasy.** Taught in Stanford's Startup Garage and in the book 'The Lean Startup'; the MVP teaches you what you need to know and your plan will almost certainly pivot once customers respond. It's a wake-up call for perfectionist students. _[Stanford Startup Garage, The Lean Startup]_ — [2025-08-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NLxFGD_WaBw)
- **Tiger Sisters itself is a pivot: the original pitch deck was for a reality TV show, and the MVP podcast launched with one camera at a kitchen table and bad mics.** Testing the TV idea with many conversations showed the macro environment wasn't right. Launching the podcast at low quality got audience feedback faster than perfecting the set first; they have been test-and-learning for nine months. _[Tiger Sisters]_ — [2025-08-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NLxFGD_WaBw)
- **Famous MVPs had almost no product: Dropbox's 3-minute video got 75,000 signups in a day, Zappos bought shoes at local stores and shipped them by hand, ClassPass ran on a spreadsheet, and DoorDash's founders delivered Palo Alto orders themselves.** Each proved demand manually before investing in technology or fulfillment; the DoorDash lore with Tony Xu began as a 'Palo Alto Delivery' website run by Stanford students. _[Dropbox, Zappos, ClassPass, DoorDash, Tony Xu]_ — [2025-08-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NLxFGD_WaBw)
- **Taylor Swift is the Let Them plus Let Me case study: she let Scooter Braun and the PE buyers keep her masters, then re-recorded them, timed it with the Eras Tour and came out more powerful and wealthier.** The re-recordings caused fans to rediscover and compare her music, reignited the fanbase, and she later earned enough from the Eras Tour to buy back her masters for roughly $300-350 million. The hosts note that without the saga she wouldn't be where she is now. _[Taylor Swift, Scooter Braun, Eras Tour]_ — [2025-08-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H-xnPI_rUqk)
- **If the environment you want doesn't exist, create it, as Reese Witherspoon did by founding Hello Sunshine.** A commercially successful actor, she was heartbroken by the lack of roles she wanted, so she built a studio telling women's stories; the hosts see Hello Sunshine as a model for where Tiger Sisters could go. _[Reese Witherspoon, Hello Sunshine]_ — [2025-08-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QUN5LWuRwu0)
- **People do not buy products, they hire them to do a job, and that job is usually emotional rather than purely functional.** The Jobs to be Done framework, created by Clay Christensen at HBS, is one of the most misunderstood ideas in product and marketing; at LinkedIn, Cherie's team asked whether people update their profile for visibility, validation or to get hired, because the answer determines what to build. _[Jobs to be Done, Clay Christensen, LinkedIn]_ — [2025-08-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IyFtCATnRpA)
- **Customers often hire a product for the narrative and community around it, not just its quality.** Jean says buyers of Sisters Matcha are partly purchasing the story of the small family farm in Wazuka, Japan that both sisters worked on and the documented journey of how it is made. _[Sisters Matcha, Wazuka, Japan]_ — [2025-08-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IyFtCATnRpA)
- **If everything disappeared, perseverance and sharing the story would remain; there is no embarrassment in failing, only in not learning from it.** (Maggie Sellers) Compounding money, experience, time and relationships puts you light years ahead of people who repeat mistakes; she cites a founder she backed who repeated the same fire-sale pattern from a previous business with the same outcome. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **Building a business around helping people is addictive because the daily messages saying 'it changed my life' put you in a flow state.** (Suzy Welch) Since the Values Bridge launched on May 6 about 65,000-66,000 people have taken it and more products are coming; when her kids tell her to take a day off she says 'I'm building something.' _[Values Bridge, Becoming You]_ — [2025-11-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eRJyWu1CgI0)
- **A rebrand made the hosts see themselves reflected in the world and gave them new respect for creative professionals who close the gap between vision and reality.** Tiger Sisters launched season six, was named a Spotify RADAR 2025 podcast, and shot new cover art at the Albright Fashion Library in LA after a month-plus process with designers. _[Spotify RADAR, Albright Fashion Library, Irene Albright]_ — [2025-11-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e2AV_kUtiVs)
- **The PLL didn't invent a sport; it rebranded and reintroduced professional lacrosse because the existing league wasn't serving players and fans in a modern tech and media environment.** (Paul Rabil) Lacrosse has been played in North America for thousands of years, in college since the 1800s, and was in the early-1900s Olympics; it returns in the 2028 Olympics. The PLL just finished its seventh season and launches the women's league (WLL) with four inaugural teams next year. _[Premier Lacrosse League, WLL, 2028 Olympics]_ — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **A sports league can't push its launch date like a tech startup because 200 players, ticket buyers, venues and a network are all counting on a fixed date.** (Paul Rabil) Paul contrasts event businesses with technology startups; the PLL launch was a dramatic series of moments to execute from 'good idea' to the start line. — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **A wholly-owned, tour-based league model gives economies of scale on costs and full IP ownership, letting you package sponsor and media-rights deals quickly and scale revenue nationally.** (Mike Rabil) The next evolution is bringing in local team owners who invest 365 days a year and build real estate around teams, because sports are communal and hyperlocal and that's what the national model was missing. _[Premier Lacrosse League]_ — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **Sports leagues need duration, not tech-style escape velocity; you pound the rock with consistent growth until a superstar or moment arrives.** (Mike Rabil) Venture LPs ask 'seven seasons, what's up?' but the NBA was tape-delayed and losing money in the early 80s until Jordan, Bird and Magic; the WNBA got Caitlin Clark. The PLL has long-term investors and early superstars, and is growing every year. _[NBA, WNBA, Caitlin Clark, Michael Jordan, Larry Bird, Magic Johnson]_ — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **Sports is the last great unifier and arguably America's last great export and lever for global influence.** Cherie attributes the 'last great export' framing to PLL investor Joe Tsai; sports is one of the last things that brings people together in person. _[Joe Tsai]_ — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **Sports is a great advertising business because it reliably elicits an uncertain feeling (anticipation then payoff or letdown) that every brand wants to attach itself to.** (Mike Rabil) Human beings are driven by feelings, and sports is one of the few products that delivers that state with surety, so brands buy placement next to it. — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **When competing for attention, don't service what you think fans want; build content, production and rules that entertain you, and bet that obsessed authenticity attracts others.** (Paul Rabil) Paul references Rick Rubin's 'The Creative Act' on the hosts' shelf and applies it to their podcast: have a unique point of view, be consistent, and enjoy the show you make. The PLL wants to be a top-five team sports league but may not get there in their lifetime for reasons outside their control. _[Rick Rubin, The Creative Act]_ — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **You can't force people to watch, but you can condition fans the way the NBA and NFL did by teaching them how to show up, attend and build rituals.** (Paul Rabil) Many lacrosse fans grew up watching the college game but never paid for a pro ticket; David Stern and Adam Silver conditioned NBA fans in the 80s and 90s, and NFL tailgating four hours before kickoff was created. The PLL does this while simultaneously building infrastructure. _[David Stern, Adam Silver, NFL]_ — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **Strength and speed get you out of the gate, but endurance over time, rooted in authentic passion, is what wins in sports, podcasts and the creator economy.** (Paul Rabil) 'Strength, speed and endurance' becomes the episode's motto; the hosts plan to make a carousel of it. — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **Women athletes have a faster and better path to becoming superstars, so building a platform around today's women's lacrosse stars makes business sense.** (Paul Rabil) Credit goes to 30 years of the WNBA and three iterations of pro women's soccer; women have always been featured more in advertising, are more empathetic and have more diverse interests reaching broader audiences. _[WNBA, NWSL]_ — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **Co-founders avoid blow-ups by respecting each other's lanes of ownership and horsepower rather than overlapping on the same work.** (Mike Rabil) Mike sees tech co-founder blow-ups when both are engineers or both are business people arguing over the same thing while another area of the room goes uncleaned. Big decisions are made together, but each owns their area. — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **The Tiger Sisters think they should disagree more, because their similar training makes them too aligned and more conflict would produce 10x crazier ideas.** Cherie copied Jean's career (both elite business school and tech) and pushed Jean into a CS degree so no engineer could question her as a PM; Jean went straight into an APM role from undergrad as one of four LinkedIn interns. Now they're adjusting from second-parent/younger-child to peers and co-founders. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **7-Eleven wins globally by localizing products through local licensees rather than a single menu, and the US opportunity is to Americanize onigiri the way pizza and tacos were.** (Jim Keyes) Japan has onigiri, Taiwan tea eggs, Korea kimbap, Sweden in-store breads. His Japanese board members balked at an American bento of BBQ and coleslaw. US food-safety rules (refrigeration at 35-40 degrees vs warmer in Japan, butter limits) dry out bread, but electronic cold-chain monitoring could justify petitioning regulators to relax them. _[7-Eleven, Starbucks, KFC, Taco Bell]_ — [2025-12-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wondntTp_cQ)
- **The hosts run Sisters Matcha, sourced from a small family farm in Wazuka near Uji, Kyoto, where they lived and hand-harvested leaves.** Cherie lived on the farm for over a month and both worked there; the farm is run by the farmer, his wife and four full-time employees. They gift it to Jim, an honorary 'Tiger brother'. _[Sisters Matcha]_ — [2025-12-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wondntTp_cQ)
- **Tiger Sisters grew fast in one year because the sisters run the podcast like product managers: relentless experimentation, reading every comment, keeping what works and shedding what does not.** Seasons 3 through 6 each looked different: season 3 was the first all-interview season (filmed standing up, which viewers found awkward), they moved from the office to a living-room set in season 5, ran an AMA from the Spotify studio in New York, and bought Aputure 300D II lights for season 6. Jean has 12-13 years in product, Cherie over 5. _[Spotify, YouTube]_ — [2025-12-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=M4HYavwtWzY)
- **Platform partners advise 'don't change too much', but take that with a grain of salt when experimentation is your DNA.** Spotify and YouTube partners cautioned against changing the format, yet the hosts credit adaptability and the same experimentation mindset they use in their careers and personal lives for the show's growth. _[Spotify, YouTube]_ — [2025-12-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=M4HYavwtWzY)
- **Launch with an MVP, but know when to level up and hire a talented specialist; the sisters wish they had invested in a professional rebrand sooner.** The original blue podcast cover was a photo-booth picture with no photographer. For season 6 they did a styled shoot at the Albright Fashion Library with designer Emily Sims, calling it 'the best money we've ever spent'; the new art stands out visually on the business and tech charts. _[Emily Sims, Albright Fashion Library]_ — [2025-12-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=M4HYavwtWzY)
- **Stay in every detail of your brand rather than handing it to an agency, so the result feels true to who you are.** The hosts were involved in colors, outfits, and how the older-sister/younger-sister duality came through in the rebrand; it cost a lot of time and energy but 'you can feel Jean and me in the rebrand'. — [2025-12-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=M4HYavwtWzY)
- **Hold off on sponsors until the product is right, then start selling to people you already know and whose companies your audience has met.** Jean's first-ever sponsor outreach was a June 12 text to four former guests; David Shim, co-founder and CEO of Read AI, replied immediately because they were about to begin marketing for the first time, becoming the show's first brand partner. _[David Shim, Read AI]_ — [2025-12-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=M4HYavwtWzY)
- **Audience trust is a creator's most important asset, so vet every sponsor for fit with your mission and only read ads you can stand behind.** The hosts decline many inbound sponsor requests for fit, timing or diligence reasons. Cherie, with over five years in content creation, says many creators just 'need to get a check' but partner selection requires conscience, ethics and discernment. — [2025-12-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=M4HYavwtWzY)
- **Apple's top 2025 episodes show huge demand for women's topics like perimenopause and female identity, but mostly from male podcasters, proving an underserved market for women speaking to women.** Cherie's takeaway: 'we don't need another bro podcaster asking about women's health, we need more women talking about women's issues,' and the goal is an ecosystem where female business voices reach parity rather than one or two in the top ten. _[Apple Podcasts]_ — [2025-12-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=M4HYavwtWzY)
- **Working for yourself lets you give 120% with no regrets in a way working 'for the man' never does.** Cherie says building Sisters Matcha with no industry contacts is like building the airplane while flying it, but because she loves it she can try her hardest. _[Sisters Matcha]_ — [2025-12-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NmdHxviASBg)
- **The best ideas arrive in the shower or in the ten minutes before sleep and after waking, and once you ship one thing you have a playbook you can reuse.** (Leah Wyar) Leah wonders how many people have genius ideas in those input-free moments and never act. Going through the process start to finish once gives you the confidence and template for the next thing. — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)

### Finance industry: VC, PE, banking, hedge funds (29)

- **VCs get pitched so often they will forget a bad first pitch, so let rejection wash off and come back with a second, better pitch.** (David Shim) Cherie relates that Goldman Sachs rejected her twice for internships in 2008 and 2009 before hiring her on the third try with a completely new set of interviewers; being forgotten worked in her favor. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **The skills that make a bad junior analyst can be irrelevant at the senior level, where banking becomes a relationship business.** (Imran Khan) Imran later returned to banking as a managing director and head of group and did very well because, by MD level, the job depends on people liking you rather than on PowerPoint and attention to detail. — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **Strategic planning at IAC meant deciding which company to buy next, a transformative set of deal experiences.** (Jeremy Liew) During his time they bought Expedia, Hotels.com, Match, Citysearch and Ticketmaster and sold USA Networks, Bravo, Sci-Fi channel and the TV/movie studio. _[Expedia, Hotels.com, Match, Citysearch, Ticketmaster, USA Networks, Bravo]_ — [2025-03-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=109Xypx-hM4)
- **Tiger Management is finance's 'PayPal mafia': its alumni funds (Tiger Cubs and even Grand Cubs) are among the biggest hedge funds in the world.** Wu was an analyst under Julian Robertson in the 90s before becoming a PM at Kingdon Capital and launching his own tech fund. Jean, who invested in hedge funds on Wall Street, names Tiger Global, Maverick, Lone Pine and Coatue as Tiger Cubs. _[Tiger Management, Julian Robertson, Kingdon Capital, Tiger Global, Maverick Capital, Lone Pine, Coatue, PayPal mafia]_ — [2025-03-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VuBM_XODr7A)
- **Valuation metrics like P/E and EV/EBITDA are just how investors express a company's worth, and the number means nothing outside the context of its category.** A price-to-earnings ratio of 20 can mean a company is doing amazingly or is below the bar depending on whether it is tech or industrials; do not be afraid of the acronyms. — [2025-04-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JGitajVC4IM)
- **Being a VC is a learning cheat code because every founder meeting, even one you pass on, teaches you a new domain from someone living it daily.** (Kofi Ampadu) No two days are the same, you roll up your sleeves on portfolio problems, and TxO (started 2020, Kofi joined 2021) operates as a startup within the firm, so he still flexes entrepreneurial muscles without founder-level stress. _[a16z TxO, Andreessen Horowitz]_ — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **Dating or married co-founders are a flag but not a huge one; investors invest in risk and just need to ask the awkward questions to de-risk it.** (Kofi Ampadu) The risk is that a personal breakup takes the company down, so it has to be drilled into during diligence rather than dismissed. — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **It is true, per the data, that raising money is easier for a white guy in a hoodie, but raising is hard for everyone because asking people for money is hard.** (Kofi Ampadu) Kofi frames it with radical empathy: even those with structural advantages experience fundraising as difficult. — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **A pitch deck is the first move in an investor-founder relationship, so lying in it is a bad start and diligence will expose fake customer logos anyway.** (Kofi Ampadu) Kofi rejects the idea that most decks are well-designed lies; founders should put real care into them because the deck opens a long relationship. — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **Attention to detail is the analyst's entire job because senior people zoom out on strategy and depend on juniors for accuracy on decisions worth hundreds of millions.** Goldman rule 8, 'someone will notice'; Jean's group managed hundreds of billions and now about a trillion dollars, and a missed comma could cost a client. Juniors who focus on details get rewarded. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-06-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eFpgcrlf2gk)
- **Goldman's perfection culture was somewhat self-important: nobody dies if an internal memo has a typo, yet everyone was stressed to the point of exhaustion.** At 21 Jean believed CEO Lloyd Blankfein's line that they were doing 'God's work'; even documents for the same 30 colleagues had to be perfect, which she says explains why it still takes her so long to write an email. _[Lloyd Blankfein, David Solomon, Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-06-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eFpgcrlf2gk)
- **Venture capital is high-risk, high-reward investing where VCs buy equity in early-stage startups rather than lending money, winning big if the startup takes off and losing everything if it fails.** Airbnb raised a roughly $600,000 seed round in 2009 and IPO'd 11 years later in 2020 at around a $100 billion valuation. _[Airbnb]_ — [2025-07-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bu50AgK6UIw)
- **Funding stages (pre-seed, seed, Series A, B, C, D, then growth equity) track the startup's size and growth stage, with Series A typically $3 million to $10-15 million.** Growth equity is the most mature bucket, usually after Series E, where revenue and numbers make success more predictable; the earliest startups may be just an idea with no traction or product-market fit. — [2025-07-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bu50AgK6UIw)
- **VCs specialize by industry vertical or by stage, and the two are linked: later-stage investing requires deeper industry expertise to parse the information.** AI used to be a vertical five to ten years ago but is now a horizontal, since every startup Cherie advises is being asked by investors how it uses AI. — [2025-07-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bu50AgK6UIw)
- **B2B SaaS (business-to-business software as a service) is a category VCs love because it is super scalable: build software once and sell it to more and more companies.** Salesforce, a CRM sold to other businesses, is the canonical example; use Crunchbase or PitchBook to look up a startup's valuation, last raise and investors. _[Salesforce, Crunchbase, PitchBook]_ — [2025-07-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bu50AgK6UIw)
- **VCs evaluate startups on three metrics, team, market and traction, and team is the most important, especially at pre-seed and seed when there are no numbers to look at.** Early pitch decks lead with team credentials; ex-Meta or ex-Google engineers signal training and a high hiring bar, while lacking a technical co-founder can count against you. _[Meta, Google]_ — [2025-07-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bu50AgK6UIw)
- **VC funds invest in portfolios of 20 to hundreds of startups expecting most to go to zero and a few to return 100x to 1000x, so a 3x or 5x outcome that is a win for you is an 'egg', a loser, to them.** Because of this, VC advice pushes founders toward small-probability moonshots rather than solid 3x-5x growth, and may not be the advice they would give if they were in your shoes. — [2025-07-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bu50AgK6UIw)
- **Private equity is defined by three things: private ownership, control (buying a majority stake), and a turnaround mindset of buying, improving and selling for profit.** Unlike buying a few shares on Robinhood, PE firms buy the whole company or a majority, increase efficiencies, and flip it. — [2025-07-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y95go30wv18)
- **PE differs from VC in that VC makes risky early-stage bets while PE targets larger, more developed 'fixer-upper' companies with inefficiencies, like flipping a house.** Brands that have been through PE include Burger King, Neiman Marcus, Dollar General, Dunkin' Donuts and Dell. _[Burger King, Neiman Marcus, Dollar General, Dunkin' Donuts, Dell]_ — [2025-07-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y95go30wv18)
- **PE firms make money via '2 and 20': roughly 2% annual management fees on the fund plus 20% carried interest on successful deals, where the real money is.** A $1B fund earns $20M a year just in management fees before any carry. _[2 and 20]_ — [2025-07-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y95go30wv18)
- **A PE fund's capital comes from three sources: limited partners (endowments, pensions, high-net-worth individuals, other funds), the firm's own money for skin in the game, and debt.** LPs like to see the management team invest its own money alongside the fees it charges. The debt portion was the most surprising to Cherie when she first learned about PE. — [2025-07-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y95go30wv18)
- **A leveraged buyout uses borrowed money to buy a company, then uses the company's profits to pay down the acquisition debt, letting the firm do a lot more with less of its own money.** Like buying a $1M house with $100K of your own money and $900K of debt, renting it out to cover the mortgage, and earning $100K on improvements for a 2x return on equity. _[LBO]_ — [2025-07-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y95go30wv18)
- **Leverage cuts both ways: Toys R Us's $6.6B LBO in 2005 carried $5.2B of debt, and interest payments starved the company of money to compete with Amazon, ending in 2017 bankruptcy and 30,000 lost jobs.** Debt can make millions or 'burn down the house'; the sisters remember Toys R Us as a childhood mecca. _[Toys R Us, Amazon]_ — [2025-07-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y95go30wv18)
- **PE gets its villain reputation from four levers: mass layoffs, prioritizing short-term profit over long-term health, asset stripping, and operating in sensitive industries like hospitals and education.** Selling a manufacturer's expensive machines raises profit but hurts long-term health; laying off 30% of hospital workers creates headline risk and the PE firm gets blamed for downstream effects. Media portrayals amplify the slimy parts. — [2025-07-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y95go30wv18)
- **A 'dirty secret' of PE is that most exits are sales to another PE firm, so funds aim to hit a profit level and move on rather than steward the company long term.** Jean learned in a Stanford private equity class that most PE exits are to other PE firms that take the company at its next size stage. _[Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-07-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y95go30wv18)
- **PE failures span industries, from Vice Media ($1.6B raised including from TPG, bankrupt 2023) to retail like J.Crew and Payless, but Blackstone's Hilton deal shows PE done well.** Blackstone bought Hilton, improved and expanded it globally, re-IPO'd it and made about $14B without mass layoffs. Outcomes depend on the firm's incentives, size, industry and turnaround style. _[Vice Media, TPG, J.Crew, Payless, Blackstone, Hilton]_ — [2025-07-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Y95go30wv18)
- **No true early-stage investor has never lost money; you must be okay with failure to succeed.** (Maggie Sellers) One company in 10 (or 30) returns the fund and the rest are losers; the same logic applies to betting on yourself and to talking openly about past failures. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **'I'm lucky to be here' thinking made Jean accept being the only analyst asked to photocopy and assemble deal memos as the only woman on her Goldman team.** She was one of five analysts in her group worldwide and felt she should do whatever it took; she says she lacked the sleep and brain space to confront the dynamic at the time, and that this mindset is typical of investment banking analysts. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **His biggest regret is letting Carl Icahn talk him into joining Blockbuster as public-company CEO instead of taking it private with PE, which would have pushed the debt maturity to 2012 and sailed through the crisis.** (Jim Keyes) After 20 years turning around 7-Eleven with technology, his plan was to partner with private equity, go private, transform to digital, and re-emerge public five years later; Icahn argued 'I'm the financial guy, you're the operator'. _[Carl Icahn, 7-Eleven, Blockbuster]_ — [2025-12-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wondntTp_cQ)

### MBA & elite education (19)

- **Stanford GSB's Touchy Feely class and its life-planning courses (including family trusts and finances) shape how graduates approach relationships and long-term planning.** An ex-boyfriend told Jean in 2012-13 that the class changed his life, which is why she applied to GSB (she was rejected once, and rejected by Goldman twice, but 'look at her now'); HBS instead had a ten-year letter exercise, and a GSB lecturer friend notes students are starry-eyed because life comes at you fast. _[Stanford GSB, Harvard Business School, Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **Business school and top employers are pre-screened dating pools because admission and success there already verify ambition, accomplishment and how someone behaves with others.** Cherie calls not making something work at Stanford GSB one of her biggest regrets; the sisters suggest looking one class year above or below your own, and Jean notes friends who married business school classmates 5-7 years after graduating. _[Stanford GSB, Harvard Business School, LinkedIn]_ — [2025-02-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaMVodL23LE)
- **A strong school culture like Stanford GSB or Dartmouth creates an almost compulsive familial bond between alumni that opens conversations anywhere.** At the conference dinner Cherie found the GSB alumni network far larger than she expected, with shared classes and inside jokes providing instant common ground; Jean says Dartmouth alumni have the same compulsion to talk about the school together. _[Stanford GSB, Dartmouth, John Redgrave (upcoming guest)]_ — [2025-03-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fb8gYzg6ikg)
- **Strategy is a roadmap for where to compete and how to win; without a clear strategy even the best product or company will not succeed.** Strategy is where HBS strives to differentiate itself; the hosts frame the episode as a mini Harvard MBA that saves you the $250,000 tuition (they spent $500,000 collectively on their two MBAs). _[Harvard Business School]_ — [2025-04-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JGitajVC4IM)
- **HBS's goal for finance is not to make every graduate a modeling genius but to make CEOs who can understand the CFO and the board and make decisions with confidence.** The CEO's job is not to be the expert; the CFO is the right-hand expert who helps make decisions with the information. Cherie prefers adjusting projections with finance counterparts over building models herself. _[Harvard Business School]_ — [2025-04-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JGitajVC4IM)
- **Networking is the biggest intangible benefit of an MBA, and it comes in three networks: classmates, faculty and professors, and alums and investors.** Cherie used classmates as beta guests for her first podcast, Cherie's Corner, to work out the kinks; Stanford lecturers Joel Peterson and Glenn Kramon appeared on the podcast and Allison Kluger, a media and entertainment lecturer, advised on the sisters' post-graduation plan. Many alums are the investors. _[Joel Peterson, Glenn Kramon, Allison Kluger, Stanford GSB, Cherie's Corner]_ — [2025-04-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dIFWvJ_i7HY)
- **An Ivy League degree is not a prerequisite for founding a company; it signals intelligence but not everyone smart or talented went to one.** (Kofi Ampadu) This is the reason TxO exists: nobody has a right to build a company or raise money because of a college name, and talent is distributed beyond elite schools. _[a16z TxO]_ — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **A brand like Harvard is an information shortcut, and leaning on it too much can blind you and dull your scrappiness.** (Fernando) He told his daughter a good school matters as proof of discipline, but Mr. Pullman did not care about his Harvard degree, only whether he could execute; the hosts admit they use their Harvard/Stanford names as a shortcut for credibility. _[Harvard, Apple]_ — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **The most valuable things from a $250,000 Stanford MBA were not strategy or finance content but mental frameworks for making decisions faster and living better.** Cherie frames the episode around three of the six most important GSB tools, each taught case-study style with a real company example and a practice exercise. _[Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-08-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NLxFGD_WaBw)
- **Speak before your thought is perfect: HBS makes class participation 50% of the grade and rewards saying anything at all, a muscle women especially need.** Cherie describes crafting the perfect smart-sounding comment in her head while the meeting moves on and the moment passes; Jean held herself to a groundbreaking-comment bar that men don't hold themselves to at all. The lesson: believe your contribution is worthwhile. _[Harvard Business School]_ — [2025-09-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HjebjpDU8-Q)
- **The most valuable things from a $250,000 Stanford MBA were the invisible soft skills, the mental operating systems for building trust and influence, not the hard frameworks and case studies.** Cherie frames the episode around three such frameworks she applies daily in building Tiger Sisters and in personal relationships. _[Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **Stanford's 'Touchy Feely' (Interpersonal Dynamics) course teaches feedback through a 12-person T-group that sits for three hours with no curriculum until conflict naturally arises and is worked through.** The class is highly competitive to get into, crying is a rite of passage, it is also embedded in Netflix's culture deck, and Jean's ex broke no-contact to say it changed his life. _[Stanford GSB, Interpersonal Dynamics, Netflix]_ — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **Education and experiences are investments that can never be taken away from you, even if you lose your job.** The hosts point to their MBAs as a fallback that is earned, reusable and permanent. — [2025-10-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q49Hzzez6Fo)
- **Exclusionary cliques in business school are bad business practice because the people you exclude are future co-investors, bosses and references.** Jean was surprised business school felt like high school with cliques; Maggie adds a CTO of a public company recently called her for a reference check on behalf of a board member's company, showing you never know who will be checking on you. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **Elitism does not end at the Ivy League admissions office; wealth saturates the campus and follows you into the classroom, dorm and dining hall.** Wealthy students move through the world assuming everything will work out for them and casually reference spring break in Europe or a parent who is a public-company CEO or board member; the assumption 'of course I'll get a Google internship because my uncle is the SVP' is the norm for them and alienating for outsiders. _[Google, Goldman Sachs, Morgan Stanley]_ — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **Outsiders at the Ivy League run on two learning tracks at once, the coursework and the unwritten campus culture, which makes them feel perpetually behind in the first two years.** While you study and hunt for internships, classmates already have roles lined up through family connections at Goldman Sachs, Morgan Stanley or Google, so their worries are not your worries. _[Goldman Sachs, Morgan Stanley, Google]_ — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **You cannot depend on the advising system to work for you; find upperclassmen or a peer mentor for a second opinion before making course choices.** Jean's assigned freshman advisor was a first-time ceramics professor who approved her taking Econ 26, the hardest class in Dartmouth's econ department with cold calls via a digital 'evil wheel of fortune'; she was the only freshman, 17 years old, and a single upperclassman would have told her to take Econ 003 instead. _[Dartmouth Econ 26]_ — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **Ivy League professors are rewarded for research and tenure, not teaching, so you do not actually get the cutting-edge minds the brochures advertise and brilliant researchers are often poor educators.** Cherie got a C in a CS class taught by a research-focused assistant professor who was unkind in office hours and left her feeling dumb, versus a kind tenured 65-year-old theory professor junior year who made her never feel stupid. Being a brilliant researcher and an excellent educator are starkly different skills. — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **Achievement (wanting visible success) is a value that business schools select for, even though Gen Z ranks it around number 11 of 16.** (Suzy Welch) Both sisters rank achievement in their top four, hence the name Tiger Sisters; Welch has it at number two herself and notes her Gen Z students ask 'who needs that?' — [2025-11-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eRJyWu1CgI0)

### Leadership & management (54)

- **It takes confidence to go max dumb, and a leader who models it makes the whole team unafraid to ask questions.** Jean told her Snapchat teams that an L6 can learn from an L2 and vice versa; Cherie says she now openly admits she knows nothing about CPG for Sisters Matcha because she is confident in her expertise elsewhere. Faulty assumptions come from being too scared to ask. _[Snapchat, Sisters Matcha, Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-02-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0OopMnFAeoE)
- **Hire people smarter than you rather than people you feel comfortable with; if you are the smartest person in the room you are in the wrong room.** The max-dumb mindset lets a founder pair with someone like Sheryl Sandberg who lays out the laws, rules and best practices so the founder can then decide which constraints to push back on. _[Sheryl Sandberg, Mark Zuckerberg]_ — [2025-02-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0OopMnFAeoE)
- **Every job is hard, and leaders should respect every job rather than assuming their own is the hardest.** (Imran Khan) Imran worked in a school library and a grocery store in college and says being a waiter is probably as tough as being a Wall Street analyst; the hosts add that this respect cascades down through culture when a leader believes it, and hypothesize it comes from leaders who have experienced hardship themselves. — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **Write notes to your future self so that when you become a manager you remember to listen to the people below you.** Jean received a 2013 email from herself via futureme.org reminding her that a manager had dismissed a feature idea of hers that turned out great, and to not do the same when she leads teams. _[futureme.org]_ — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **As you move into senior management, the number-one skill is increasing your leverage so one action kicks off ten workstreams that reach thousands of people.** Jean says this was the top feedback she received as a middle manager moving up at a social media app used by hundreds of millions; it is the corporate version of Pham's 'win every day through other people'. — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **Leaders who own a failure of character rather than spinning it earn more respect; Jeremy's honest 'I was disrespectful and got fired' is rare and made him more credible.** Most people, even when prompted, blame the business or don't frame failures as personal; being fired was also part of his origin story that led to the AOL role. — [2025-03-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=109Xypx-hM4)
- **'Strong opinions, loosely held' is the hallmark of the best leaders: real conviction combined with willingness to change your mind when new inputs arrive.** Jeremy said he holds the nationalization view 'strongly but weakly' because he's still learning the AI ecosystem's fundamental structures. Cherie saw this on her LinkedIn Learning team where passionate debates ended with people changing their minds. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2025-03-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=109Xypx-hM4)
- **Disagree and commit: you can hold an opposing view but must genuinely commit to the chosen path, and the worst colleagues are those who disagree and then commit begrudgingly with no plan forward.** A big cultural tenet at LinkedIn. Related: taking an action and being wrong beats taking no action, and not making a choice is itself a choice. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2025-03-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=109Xypx-hM4)
- **Every job is hard and every task deserves recognition, including the unglamorous internal fixes.** Jean ties Wu's lifeguard lesson to Imran Khan's 'every job is hard'. At LinkedIn, Friday engineering show-and-tells made pride visible; fixing an internal tool that saved PMs hundreds of hours got the same stage as headline features, which built an ownership culture. _[Imran Khan, LinkedIn]_ — [2025-03-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VuBM_XODr7A)
- **People who see everything as black and white leave no room for conversation; nuanced leaders weigh multiple viewpoints before forming one.** The hosts say talking to black-and-white thinkers yields talking points and lectures instead of interaction; Cherie attributes her situational view partly to being an NFJ on Myers-Briggs and says nuance matters more in an age of AI and politics. _[Myers-Briggs]_ — [2025-03-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VuBM_XODr7A)
- **Getting ahead of bad press with a real apology and a customer-service focus is part of competing on experience.** After an employee called the police on two Black men sitting in a Starbucks who had done nothing wrong, the company issued a forward apology and asked how to make it right; an HBR piece recently highlighted Starbucks going above and beyond on customer service. _[Starbucks, Harvard Business Review]_ — [2025-04-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JGitajVC4IM)
- **Leadership style directly shapes company culture, how performance is judged and how people grow, and performance translates to stock price.** HBS treats leadership as its biggest pillar, teaching everything from command-and-control to servant leadership; the hosts outline four common styles. _[Harvard Business School]_ — [2025-04-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dIFWvJ_i7HY)
- **Servant leadership flips the hierarchy so the leader serves the people who work for them, and it pays off in the bottom line through more invested employees and better customer experience.** Howard Schultz at Starbucks was among the first to give benefits to part-time baristas because employees cannot create a 'third space' for customers unless they feel it themselves, driving brand loyalty and revenue growth. The hosts note 'servant leadership' is a buzzy phrase worth dropping in conversation. _[Howard Schultz, Starbucks, Leaders Eat Last]_ — [2025-04-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dIFWvJ_i7HY)
- **Leading from the back and doing tasks alongside your team ('changing the printer cartridge') is more empowering than dictating from the front.** Cherie co-created with her engineering and design teams at LinkedIn rather than telling them what to do; her boss Albert gave her two miniature terracotta soldiers on her promotion as a reminder that leadership is how we serve. Military-background classmates share the 'leaders eat last' mindset. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2025-04-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dIFWvJ_i7HY)
- **Transformational leaders energize employees and create cult followings with a compelling vision, but the company needs a strong management team to survive their departure.** Steve Jobs could look you in the eye and make impossible deadlines happen, and customers lined up overnight for iPhones to get whatever he dreamed up; Elon Musk is cited as a modern example. After Jobs, Tim Cook 'doesn't hit the same' even though Apple remains wildly successful. _[Steve Jobs, Apple, Elon Musk, Tim Cook]_ — [2025-04-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dIFWvJ_i7HY)
- **Transactional (carrot-and-stick) leadership uses clear rewards and penalties, epitomized by Jack Welch's 'rank and yank' at GE, where the bottom 10% were fired every year and the top 10% got outsized bonuses.** GE massively outperformed in the 80s and 90s so this became the standard; Goldman let go the bottom 10% every year and Jean thought it was normal as a first job. Finance and consulting now call it 'up or out'. _[Jack Welch, GE, Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-04-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dIFWvJ_i7HY)
- **Up-or-out systems make people work harder and perform better, but at the expense of mental health and collegiality, and they fit sales orgs because results are judged within a quarter.** Sales teams live by 'eat what you kill' with no allowance for extenuating circumstances, whereas product and engineering projects take multiple quarters or years, so discrete metrics are harder to apply. LinkedIn's top sales performers went to 'Club' trips, Zynga's top studio each quarter (60-120 people) went to Hawaii or Vegas, and early Snap salespeople kept 1% of the ad revenue they sold. _[LinkedIn, Zynga, Snap]_ — [2025-04-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dIFWvJ_i7HY)
- **Laissez-faire leadership has no official hierarchy and lets teams self-organize around self-identified goals, unleashing creativity that top-down strategy would never produce.** Valve, the developer behind Steam, is the classic HBS case (also taught at Stanford). Jean notes Zynga's 'be your own CEO' motto and independent studios each with their own GM, CTO and head of product was laissez-faire applied at a company level. _[Valve, Steam, Zynga]_ — [2025-04-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dIFWvJ_i7HY)
- **Laissez-faire only works with hungry, extreme-ownership personalities and small, high-trust teams; in big tech it breeds complacency and 'rest and vest'.** Cherie's first reaction was 'nothing would get done' based on complacent big-tech teams where everyone looks around waiting; Jean counters that a two-person company like theirs is laissez-faire and could scale to six equally trusted people, though it is extremely fragile. — [2025-04-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dIFWvJ_i7HY)
- **Leadership styles are not one-size-fits-all; the best leaders weave elements of several styles into their own culture and approach.** The value of learning the framework is being able to pull the elements that make sense for your personal style, industry and the culture you want to create. — [2025-04-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dIFWvJ_i7HY)
- **Match leadership style to company stage: laissez-faire for a two-person startup, transactional once you add people and need a rubric, then transformational once roles are clear and you aim for the stars.** A transactional rubric lets people understand how they are measured; that base makes it possible to then ask people to go above and beyond their stated roles for something never done before. — [2025-04-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dIFWvJ_i7HY)
- **Being openly grateful and affectionate with female colleagues is a distinct leadership style: disagreement framed as 'I love you, but let's sit on this one thing' gets people to a middle ground and trickles down into a more collaborative team.** (Leah Wyar) Leah contrasts this with the era 10 years ago when women felt they had to be tough like men with an 'alpha' undertone; you can be direct with feedback without that energy, and teams now expect and respect it. — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **Being highly sensitive to a room's energy is a blessing and a curse: it lets you read and respond in real time without a script, but makes it hard not to take things personally.** (Leah Wyar) Leah says she is not a stoic leader; she goes in with a loose outline and reads what's going on. Sporadic difficult people can be steeled against, but being in that energy constantly is not OK. — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **Show up unemotional in the room and get it done, because you have an 'underbelly' of work wives, husband and therapist where you can rage and be raw afterward.** (Leah Wyar) During a crunch app launch (T-minus 2 weeks) she absorbs hard feedback stone-faced, locks in, walks out, takes a rage walk, and relies on muscle memory that she has seen this pattern through a million times. — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **As a manager, retain intellectually driven people by designing a hard, novel problem that also serves the business.** Jean gave a bored data scientist a complex hypothesis to chase; the analysis ended up producing a patent, and the real motive was retention. — [2025-05-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qZvfC4TOAD0)
- **Hold yourself to the highest standard the firm does: act as an emissary of the firm and apply the 'headline risk' test to everything you do, at work or outside.** Goldman drilled analysts to ask whether they would be comfortable seeing their action on the front page of the New York Times or Wall Street Journal; Jean says it made analysts paranoid but kept them conscious of doing right by the company. _[Goldman Sachs, Headline risk]_ — [2025-05-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qZvfC4TOAD0)
- **Goldman's perfectionism, where slides were corrected to the tenth of a millimeter, instilled a lasting pride in work and operational excellence that many younger workers lack.** Jean still proofreads emails far longer than Cherie; she argues everything you put out should clear a bar of greatness even if you move fast. — [2025-05-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qZvfC4TOAD0)
- **Speed of iteration matters too: a startup-bred VP sent typo-filled emails from his iPhone and got faster responses, so balance excellence against speed.** Cherie's LinkedIn VP would screenshot, circle and send feedback instantly; the hosts agree it is a balance and that different people hold different bars. — [2025-05-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qZvfC4TOAD0)
- **A trusting relationship with your boss or VP earns you mutual benefit of the doubt, which cuts your workload roughly in half compared with the mental gymnastics of a low-trust relationship.** Cherie's most fruitful relationship was with a VP at LinkedIn she worked with for years and was on texting terms with; if he snapped on a bad day she would not fall apart, and if she was off one day he trusted her. Jean has been on the other side and says the extra 'how will they take this' overthinking is ten times the work. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2025-05-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=p4gBwESgzPI)
- **The best way to get people on board is to make them part of the idea, ideally so they feel it was partly their idea that you are executing on their behalf.** Brainstorm sessions often serve this purpose: leaders enter with an agenda but the meeting lets participants feel their ideas matter, which is a balance of both. — [2025-06-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eFpgcrlf2gk)
- **As you get more senior you move away from being measured on individual output toward being measured on influence, which is why HR refuses to promote the 'brilliant jerk' 10x engineer.** The hosts say even the best engineer in the world should not keep being promoted if they cannot work well with others; relationships matter more and more with seniority. — [2025-06-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DubkuAz2meM)
- **Doing it for others, family, employees, a cause, is the softer side of motivation and is what gives a leader operating leverage.** (Fernando) His motivation was always protecting his family (mother and five siblings, now four children); he says a purely self-centered person lacks the charisma to get others to follow, and that a company becomes a broader family whose people you owe reciprocity for their sacrifice. — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **Leaders who practice vulnerability, such as admitting mistakes or asking for feedback, build higher-performing teams, so vulnerability is a career strategy too.** The hosts cite a Harvard Business Review finding that such leaders are 20% more likely to foster high-performing teams, and a study that 90% of people who open up feel less lonely and more motivated. _[Harvard Business Review]_ — [2025-07-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=McWujpZMzLw)
- **Sort decisions into Type 1 (irreversible, go slow and strategic) and Type 2 (reversible, experiment fast); treating every decision as irreversible slows you down and kills innovation.** From Jeff Bezos's Amazon memo. Hiring your first engineer is Type 1; changing a button color is Type 2 and deserves little brainpower. _[Jeff Bezos, Amazon, Type 1 vs Type 2 decisions]_ — [2025-08-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NLxFGD_WaBw)
- **Speed is a skill, but knowing which decisions deserve speed versus deep thought is strategy; startups die from slow decisions more than bad ones.** Mini exercise: list all pending decisions, sort them into Type 1 and Type 2, and run a small test on one reversible decision today. — [2025-08-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NLxFGD_WaBw)
- **Satya Nadella is the business case for Let Them: he let analysts and employees mock his empathy-first style as 'soft' compared to Steve Ballmer, stuck to mobile-first cloud-first, and took Microsoft to a $3 trillion market cap.** The hosts say he grew the company from roughly $300 million (as stated) in 2014 to over $3 trillion and is now cited in business school cases for quiet confidence and culture turnaround. Cherie, who spent almost five years at Microsoft-owned LinkedIn, says the kind, compassionate culture was tangible. _[Satya Nadella, Steve Ballmer, Microsoft, LinkedIn]_ — [2025-08-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H-xnPI_rUqk)
- **Sharing personal OKRs with your work team creates social accountability and lets colleagues support your goals.** Both sisters' teams at LinkedIn and elsewhere shared personal OKRs like learning piano or dancing; teammates would tell someone to leave for their gym class because they knew it was that person's OKR, and everyone rushed to hit goals before the quarterly review. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2025-08-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IyFtCATnRpA)
- **In tense meetings, write down inflammatory remarks verbatim to reflect on later rather than reacting in the moment, so emotion doesn't distort the data.** Case study: former PepsiCo CEO Indra Nooyi. Exercise: tonight, jot a triggering moment in two columns, exactly what was said/done versus how you felt, and ask whether the event or your interpretation caused the emotion. _[Indra Nooyi, PepsiCo]_ — [2025-09-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HjebjpDU8-Q)
- **Blocking a non-negotiable on your calendar is a power move and a way to define culture as a leader.** HBS case 'Sheryl Sandberg: Defining Leadership': she blocked family dinner daily at Facebook, showing priorities by what she did rather than what she said. _[Sheryl Sandberg, Facebook, Harvard Business School]_ — [2025-09-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HjebjpDU8-Q)
- **People grow from insights, not from praise or critique, and feedback only needs to be directional and timely, not perfect, to be useful.** The HBR 'feedback fallacy' concept; as a leader, normalize feedback as a habit rather than a performance critique. _[Harvard Business Review, The Feedback Fallacy]_ — [2025-09-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HjebjpDU8-Q)
- **Give feedback with SBI: name the situation (when and where), the observable behavior with no judgment, and the impact on you.** Feedback must be grounded in a specific observation no one can contest; example: 'in yesterday's client pitch, you interrupted me while I was presenting our strategy, and I felt undermined in front of the client.' _[SBI framework]_ — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **SBI is widely used in corporate performance systems: Microsoft trains all new managers in it and Adobe used it to move away from annual reviews, raising employee satisfaction.** The hosts cite these as evidence the framework extends beyond Stanford into real management practice. _[Microsoft, Adobe]_ — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **Amazon uses SCQA in the mock press release inside its six-page product launch memos to state the user pain point and what the product solves.** The hosts cite this as the corporate case study for the framework. _[Amazon]_ — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **When promoted over a work friend who turns resistant, address it head-on as a leader by refocusing on the shared project goal and acknowledging their contributions.** Don't make it about the promotion; come in with compassion and collaboration, which catches the person off guard in a good way and thaws the awkwardness of friendship meeting hierarchy. — [2025-11-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e2AV_kUtiVs)
- **Publicly credit a struggling teammate's contributions to the shared boss so they feel recognized and remember it's a team effort.** Frame it team-wide at milestones: thank person Y for driving X, person Z for Y, and the work bestie for their piece, so affirmation lands without singling anyone out. — [2025-11-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e2AV_kUtiVs)
- **If affirmation doesn't fix a non-contributing teammate, escalate to your manager with facts only, framed as project blockers, and never silently absorb their work.** State the project, due date and specifically what isn't being delivered, with emotion removed. Redoing their work overnight is unsustainable and a formula for disaster; this is your first lesson in scaling yourself as a manager. — [2025-11-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e2AV_kUtiVs)
- **Disagreements at an impasse can't always be solved logically, so revert to empathy and trade concessions ('you take this one, I'll take the next').** (Paul Rabil) Both brothers arrive with strong, well-researched positions; Paul is 'relentless' and more likely to bend rules to win. Conflict shouldn't be constant but you need friction to run fast, like a foot hitting the ground. — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **Wanting to be liked more than respected makes you fail at your actual job; a product manager who won't push back on engineering timelines isn't doing the job.** At 22 as a new PM on a male-dominated engineering team, Cherie would accept a five-week estimate rather than ask 'why not two,' even though her CS degree let her judge the estimate; now she sees negotiating timelines as squarely within her role. — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **No one follows a maybe: leaders deliver decisions rather than float ideas, so speak with conviction instead of asking for permission.** A manager once told Jean 'you are one of the most qualified people in this room, so why are you still asking for permission'; permission-seeking shows up as over-explaining, over-contextualizing and statements phrased as questions, though the fix is conviction, not bulldozing. — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **Change equals opportunity (C-E-O): the job of a CEO, and of anyone, is to embrace change because all commerce begins when something changes.** (Jim Keyes) The first three words he wrote in his book. He joined Blockbuster to take it to digital streaming, then had to pivot from transforming to saving the company when Lehman collapsed, restructuring and selling it to Dish Network. _[Blockbuster, Lehman Brothers, Dish Network]_ — [2025-12-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wondntTp_cQ)
- **Blockbuster's real failure was cash and balance-sheet management, not failing to keep up with technology, and the Harvard case study gets it wrong.** (Jim Keyes) Blockbuster had $1B of debt due in 2009 that couldn't be refinanced when banks shut in the 2008 crisis. In 2000 it partnered with Enron on bandwidth for streaming, and in 2007 Jim bought Movielink, a streaming venture of five of six major studios. The hosts add that cash flow is an entrepreneur's oxygen and propose rewriting the case study. _[Blockbuster, Enron, Movielink, Harvard Business School case study]_ — [2025-12-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wondntTp_cQ)
- **Vulnerability works strategically at work too: leaders who admit mistakes or ask for feedback are 20% more likely to foster high-performing teams.** Cited from Harvard Business Review; 90% of people who open up to others feel less lonely and more motivated. Exercise: share one vulnerable moment with a trusted friend and observe the effect. _[Harvard Business Review]_ — [2025-12-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NmdHxviASBg)
- **Women can lead with open gratitude and love, which makes direct feedback land better and creates a more collaborative team than the 'alpha' posture women once felt they had to adopt.** (Leah Wyar) Saying 'I love you so much, but there's one thing we need to sit on' gets a colleague to a middle ground far better than 'that's terrible, go change it.' Others on the team see and respect that leadership style, and it trickles down. — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)
- **There is beauty in being naive: you can learn a whole new business on the job by trusting smart people, giving up control and researching what you do not understand.** (Leah Wyar) Moving from print to digital media felt like post-grad work; Leah emailed herself terms to Google later. Now she is launching an app knowing nothing about apps, relying on a room of smart people each doing what they do best. — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)

### Power, status & office politics (46)

- **Disaster recovery exposes a widening class divide: who gets help depends on the reach of your network, not just the size of your loss.** West-side Palisades friends raised $40-50K on GoFundMe in three days, while many Altadena families on the east side could not reach $1,000 of a $5,000 goal; Altadena's resources were concentrated on the same block, so the whole community's support system burned at once. _[GoFundMe, Pacific Palisades, Altadena, Eaton Fire, 60 Minutes]_ — [2025-01-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_8AlNMC2l4)
- **Life-saving information in a disaster travels through elite whisper networks, and healthy air should not be a hoarded commodity.** Jean learned via a screenshot of an Instagram story that Air Doctor was selling $800 hospital-grade purifiers at cost for $150 through a phone line, called six times to get through, and then tried to share it widely because the 99% who need help most are the last to hear. _[Air Doctor]_ — [2025-01-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_8AlNMC2l4)
- **The Luigi Mangione discourse marks a tipping point: anti-capitalist, anti-government sentiment now cuts across all socioeconomic classes, unlike Occupy Wall Street.** Jean was working on Wall Street during Occupy, which felt blue-collar versus white-collar; today's revolt feels like a shared state of mind across classes, echoing 1960s anti-war energy, campus protests and youth protests in South Korea. Five to ten years ago the public would have unanimously called the shooter a monster. _[Luigi Mangione, Brian Thompson, Occupy Wall Street, Archduke Franz Ferdinand]_ — [2025-01-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_8AlNMC2l4)
- **A symbol becomes powerful when the messenger comes from inside the elite yet still suffered under the system.** The sisters argue Mangione's wealthy background and UPenn ties made him an 'acceptable' figure for the public to rally behind, while his own back injuries and medical bills showed even the moderately wealthy cannot escape the healthcare system; they were still disturbed by the lionizing rhetoric. _[Luigi Mangione, UPenn]_ — [2025-01-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_8AlNMC2l4)
- **Before taking on a project, ask your manager directly: 'Do you honestly believe this is set up for success?'** Jean asked this at LinkedIn when handed projects lacking VP backing or headcount, e.g. a Women in Product initiative, so she could know the real definition of success. Cherie, as a manager, told reports candidly when exec mandates were moonshots they wouldn't fully hit; asking requires a safe relationship with your manager. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2025-03-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VuBM_XODr7A)
- **Workplace politics starts in middle management, where you are the jelly in the sandwich policing what comes down from your boss and up from your team, and must learn to lead with influence rather than force.** (Leah Wyar) Early years are about developing thick skin in a cutthroat publishing industry; later the hard question becomes when to side with your team versus your boss. — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **Do not assume the boss you do dirty work for will protect you forever; one day they are gone and you lose your direct line overnight.** (Leah Wyar) At Cosmo, Leah had a direct line to the chief content officer and 24 hours later was under new leadership she had no relationship with; she concluded the company's direction was no longer for her and left. _[Cosmopolitan]_ — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **Men with finance backgrounds give direct rather than flowery feedback, and learning not to take that personally has helped her thrive under male bosses for the first time.** (Leah Wyar) The last 5 years are the first time she has had male bosses (three of them); some past female bosses had even harder delivery, but the style difference forced her to separate feedback from self-worth. — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **In traditional finance the decision-makers are much older, making senior relationships more intimidating than in tech where seniors are close to your age.** Jean made fewer close senior connections at Goldman than Cherie did at LinkedIn for this reason. — [2025-05-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qZvfC4TOAD0)
- **Never talk business in the elevator, sauna or any small shared space, because you never know who is listening.** At Goldman a leaked secret could blow up a deal worth hundreds of millions, and the elevator might hold reporters, the other side of a deal, or someone who could trade on the information; Cherie was approached after naming someone in an LA sauna. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-05-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qZvfC4TOAD0)
- **Information is power, and you can't gather information if you're not listening.** Cherie says she only fully understood this later in her career and returns to it throughout the episode, including in negotiation, where information gathering reveals the other side's real wants. — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **Where you sit in a meeting tells the room how you perceive your own role; people choose seats strategically, so choose yours deliberately.** Sit at the table (or next to the top person) to be perceived; sit on the side to stay out of it. Studies show women gravitate to the back; Sheryl Sandberg's 'take a seat at the table'. Jean as a LinkedIn intern note-taker unknowingly took the seat next to CEO Jeff Weiner. _[Sheryl Sandberg, Jeff Weiner, LinkedIn]_ — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **Meetings are 'kabuki theater': smart people have already coordinated and rehearsed what they will say before the meeting happens.** Cherie says almost nothing is ad hoc; behind-the-scenes alignment on seating and talking points is normal and not necessarily malicious. — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **These tactics are the unsaid rules of the modern corporate workplace; if you didn't learn them from family, you must learn them yourself and at least recognize when they're used on you.** The sisters had no parent or family insider to teach them the professional world's inner workings and sought mentors instead. Start with one skill rather than all four at once; the tactics apply to any sector, not just finance and tech. — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **Office politics feels 'icky', but you can only change a system once you hold power in it, which requires understanding the landscape first.** Cherie cites Stanford GSB professor Jeffrey Pfeffer's book on power: play within the rules of the existing framework to reach a position from which you can change it. _[Jeffrey Pfeffer, Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **The ick disappears when you truly believe in your mission, and you should stay within your own values rather than going Machiavellian.** Jean uses tactics only in service of what she believes is best for the business or team; Cherie has seen leaders take politics too far and lost respect for them, while others do it authentically and earn more respect. — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **Know the rules of the game are rigged against you, decide to win despite it, build allies, and only try to change the rules once you have earned a bully pulpit.** (Kofi Ampadu) Founders of color are underfunded and everyone knows it, so complaining about the rules mid-game does no good; a rookie cannot rewrite league rules, but as you make progress you use your platform to change them. Kofi uses his hyphenated identity as an advantage and a chance to debunk stereotypes. — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **Decisions are not made in the decision-making meeting; walk in already knowing with 85%+ confidence how every person in the room will vote and why.** Goldman rule 6, 'seek consensus and input from colleagues', in practice means back-channeling: one-on-one conversations to vet your idea in a low-stakes setting and address objections before the high-stakes meeting. Cherie calls it one of the dirty secrets of office politics that the top 1% of performers use, and says public corporate interactions are largely orchestrated. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-06-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eFpgcrlf2gk)
- **Find where the decision makers are already showing up, like the company intranet or a Slack channel with the CEO, and post your wins there rather than only in your small team channel.** (Lorraine K. Lee) When Prezi lacked LinkedIn's team win-announcement emails, Lee asked her boss how to reach everyone and used the company intranet instead.
