Target the right people at the right time rather than always going wide. _[LinkedIn, Prezi, Slack]_ — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **Loud people get promoted because they are visible, so the answer is not to be loud but to be strategic and make your own voice heard with substance.** (Lorraine K. Lee) Someone promoted only for being loud eventually stalls because they cannot back it up; do not let the loud annoying guy be a cloud over you, become the CEO of your own career. — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **Your boss does not have to like your personality, but they must like what you bring and you must make their life ten times easier.** Whether through a sunny disposition or being a 10x performer, your manager has to respect your work enough to do the behind-the-scenes labor a promotion requires. — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **VC is not just about money; it is about signaling, social proof, access and warm intros, which is why it is seen as clubby and who-you-know.** Landing Sequoia, a16z or Lightspeed on the cap table signals other funds to pile in; getting into Y Combinator is itself a signal and Demo Day gives exposure to every major VC. _[Sequoia, Andreessen Horowitz (a16z), Lightspeed, Y Combinator]_ — [2025-07-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bu50AgK6UIw)
- **Let Them is a privileged move: it only works when you hold the power, money or decision-making resources, not when you're the intern chasing a return offer or the only woman in the boardroom.** If your circumstances are fragile, saying 'let them' can carry real consequences, including for other women who may be judged by your behavior. — [2025-08-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H-xnPI_rUqk)
- **Success isn't only about merit; it's about influence, visibility and alliances, and if you don't understand power you lose to people who do.** From Jeffrey Pfeffer's Paths to Power class: power is not title, it is who controls resources, influences decisions and pulls strings behind the scenes; Pfeffer's disclaimer is that this is one way to operate, but you should know these moves exist. _[Jeffrey Pfeffer, Paths to Power, Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **Early in your career you benefit from power dynamics without seeing them; as you get senior, influencing decisions behind the scenes becomes part of your job.** Jean says she only recognized the power game in the last six years of her career and only started playing it in the last three; Cherie notes juniors aren't privy to how the sausage is made between execs. — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **Power isn't about being right; it's about having enough support and alliances to survive being right.** Carol Bartz, a highly capable CEO brought in to turn around Yahoo, focused on tactical execution and never built internal allies, so her objectively correct radical moves lacked support and she was fired by phone. _[Carol Bartz, Yahoo, Jeffrey Pfeffer]_ — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **Map your own power web: pick the five people who will influence your next raise, role or deal, classify each as advocate, neutral or unaware, and connect with one this week.** Being excellent without visibility is a silent career killer, especially if you're brilliant; those who understand the game won't tell you because it would give away their edge. — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **Reading the room, especially nonverbal cues and body language, is a source of power because it gives you information others do not have.** In meetings, even on Zoom, Cherie watches for widened eyes or distracted typing; if 80 percent of the room is unsupportive she repositions the pitch to address concerns, calls on the skeptic live ('Andy, do you have any questions?'), or handles them one-on-one afterward. She traces the skill to childhood hypervigilance. — [2025-09-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TkXvlgPXitc)
- **Women are conditioned from childhood to be likeable and make others comfortable, and carry that invisible labor into both work and relationships.** A 2021 McKinsey study shows women take on invisible labor like smoothing conflicts; the lone woman on a team gets 'voluntold' to plan happy hours, and as a Goldman analyst Jean was the only one of several analysts asked to compile the hundreds-page investment committee book every week, work you can only do right or screw up and that never helps promotion. _[McKinsey, Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **The biggest power move you can start today is making people feel important: remember their names, follow up, and send thank-you notes.** (Maggie Sellers) She hand-wrote a thank-you card to every one of the 16 wedding guests and every vendor, and admits she judges people who do not send thank-yous. When networking she does not talk about herself; if you posture to feed your ego you have already lost. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **The real benefit of the Ivy League is being exposed to how the elite corporate world works, as long as you do not let the gap debilitate you with resentment or embarrassment about where you come from.** Once you understand how the system works you can be part of it to better your life; much of the corporate world runs on the same unspoken rules you first encounter at these institutions. — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **Getting into the Ivy League is only level zero; inside there is a game within a game of Greek life, final clubs and secret societies with their own legacy admissions.** At Dartmouth secret societies tapped presidents of other campus clubs, while at Columbia you could not get tapped unless your parents were someone like a UN delegate; it is easier to get into a fraternity or sorority if an older sibling or parent was in it. Jean says Dartmouth was somewhat more meritocratic. _[Dartmouth, Columbia, Greek life, secret societies, final clubs]_ — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **Fraternities historically kept filing cabinets of every past exam in econ, bio and pre-med, giving brothers illicit access to answers plus the social cachet of being able to 'help' others study.** Jean says this happened 15-20 years ago, was widely known and never cracked down on; the hosts call it cheating but note it is a small example of passed-down 'fraternity knowledge' that lets some students ride a funicular up the mountain while others climb with a heavy backpack. Jean worked eight jobs at Dartmouth to earn money. _[Dartmouth]_ — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **Do not assume the adults at an institution are looking out for your best interest; they have their own incentives.** Cherie's high-school boyfriend, recruited to Harvard for football, was misled by a coach into believing quitting the team after an injury meant leaving Harvard, and nearly transferred to a community college; Jean's Dartmouth soccer-playing boyfriend, who had quit his team, told him that was false and he stayed. The coach wanted to maintain his roster at the cost of the student's education. _[Harvard football]_ — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **Privilege also buys protection from consequences: a classmate accused of academic dishonesty had her corporate-lawyer mother fly in to represent her at the school hearing and faced no real penalty.** Jean contrasts this with her sister's boyfriend, who had no one to advocate for him; access to that kind of representation is not something outsiders have. — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **Managers should let direct reports set the level of social media engagement and never be the one commenting on their posts first.** Accepting a follow request is a big step given the power dynamic; take cues from the employee and the corporate culture. Cherie was Facebook friends with her CTO and CFO at Zynga (FarmVille) because that was the culture, but there is little upside for a manager to over-engage. _[Zynga, FarmVille, Instagram]_ — [2025-11-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e2AV_kUtiVs)
- **If your boss over-engages on your Instagram, soft-block them from stories rather than removing them, which would force a confrontation.** 'Access revoked' is a perfectly fine move; you are allowed a life outside work and can control what a manager sees. — [2025-11-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e2AV_kUtiVs)
- **Apologetic workplace behavior is passed down from the women leaders you first model yourself on, creating an intergenerational chain that has to be consciously broken.** Jean's first boss, the director of an ocean preservation nonprofit, said sorry and thank you fifty times when assigning tasks to an intern; Jean subconsciously modeled that as how women behave at work, and notes an older generation's few-women-in-the-room context that no longer applies. — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **'Shrinking to fit' means softening yourself, qualifying thoughts, staying silent in meetings and downplaying achievements so people find you more palatable, and it costs promotions, pay and respect.** They cite Yale research that women who self-promote are penalized socially even when they outperform, which explains why self-aware women are conditioned to downplay, and quote Michelle Obama: 'I have been at every powerful table you can think of, and I'm still the most qualified person there.' _[Yale, Michelle Obama]_ — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **It is more important to be respected than liked; being liked is fleeting while respect, including self-respect, lasts.** Jean frames this as the key lesson of her 15-plus-year career, and says the biggest show of self-respect is being authentically yourself. — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **Setting an impossibly high bar for what you say in meetings keeps you silent while others contribute obvious points, and by the time you've perfected your thought the moment has passed.** Jean required her contributions to be novel, mind-blowing and perfectly phrased, while 90% of what others said was obvious; sometimes her point was ignored and then a VP of engineering said the same thing five minutes later to acclaim. — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **Cultivate male allies who will re-attribute your idea when someone else gets credit for it.** Cherie had a supportive male VP who would say 'I think Cherie said that before' when a point she made was later repeated and celebrated, so the original thought stayed attributed to her. — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **Two Asian American women hosting a top business and tech podcast is an extreme statistical white space, roughly 0.0225 percent, which makes continuing the show feel imperative.** A USC Annenberg study found 8 percent of business and tech podcasts are hosted by women and 7 percent of the top 100 podcasts are hosted by women of color; male hosts also book more male guests, so fewer women's voices get platformed overall. _[USC Annenberg School for Communication and Journalism]_ — [2025-12-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=M4HYavwtWzY)
- **Doing the hard work does not get you promoted; being seen does, because talent only pays off when it converts into credibility, influence and revenue.** The hosts frame the 'invisible game' as competence becoming credibility, credibility becoming influence, and influence becoming success and compensation. January is when scope gets assigned and promotions are decided earlier than most people think. — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)
- **Your boss does not have to like your personality to promote you, but they must like what you bring and you must make their life ten times easier.** Because a manager does so much behind-the-scenes advocacy for a promotion, they need to be invested; that comes from respect for your results or from your presence, ideally both. — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)
- **Workplace politics begins in middle management, where you are the jelly in the sandwich pleasing both your team and your boss, and must learn to lead with influence rather than force.** (Leah Wyar) Leah warns the boss you do dirty work for may disappear overnight; at Cosmo she lost her direct line to the chief content officer in 24 hours when leadership changed, and eventually left because she no longer believed in the direction. _[Cosmopolitan]_ — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)

### Relationships, dating & marriage (115)

- **Girlfriends carry you through life, so invest in close female friendships that let you share yourself fully and honestly.** Jean's Sonoma weekend with four HBS girlfriends revealed that in eight months every one of them had faced a major life event (near-death health scares, job changes, relationship changes) invisible from the outside; the group also reflects each member's best qualities back to her. _[Harvard Business School, Sex and the City]_ — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **Who you marry is the most important decision you will make, so it deserves the same rigor as career decisions.** The choice affects finances, children, family, daily health and habits (who you eat the most meals with, whether you go to the gym); the hosts argue the topic only gets trivialized because it is coded as a women's topic. — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **Conversations about relationships are just as important as conversations about career growth because life is holistic.** Jean was moved by Rachel Kirkconnell telling Alex Cooper she grieved imagined future children after her breakup; after her own engagement ended, the whole imagined future life, including imaginary children she had pictured for almost a decade, disappeared at once. _[Call Her Daddy, Rachel Kirkconnell, Alex Cooper]_ — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **Cherie's formula: romantic happiness = stability + juice + X factor.** Stability is feeling prioritized and safe (being picked up, dates planned, being thought of when apart); juice is chemistry, humor and intellectual connection plus physical attraction; the X factor is the person being genuinely interesting and passionate about something outside work. She treats it as guardrails, not a law of love. — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **Sharing what you need early in dating works like a career ladder: it gives the other person a clear rubric and reveals how they respond to feedback.** On a fourth date Cherie explained that a prior action had left the stability element missing; the man's response was 'I like you, let's make it happen,' which she read as a strong positive signal, analogous to promotion requirements in tech. — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **You cannot force chemistry: a partner who is perfect on stability but lacks juice is still incompatible long term.** Cherie dated a man for four months who brought chocolates, flowers and homemade chicken soup when she was sick, yet felt something was missing; the formula came out of a root-cause analysis (and a return to therapy) of why she did not like him more. — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **After a relationship, people over-index on whatever was missing last time, so check your criteria against all your past relationships rather than the most recent one.** Cherie realized every prior relationship was high-chemistry and 'fulfilling in the mind but not the heart', which made her overweight stability in the next one. — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **Love and best-friendship are not enough; interrogate your values, your partner's values, and how the combination plays out across life milestones as early as possible.** Jean's main lesson from an eight-year relationship and broken engagement is that she thought deep love plus building a life together was the whole formula; stress-testing values early through conversation and observed action would have served her better. — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **Charm and banter must be backed by action: after someone says a value matters to them, watch for them to show it.** Jean calls herself gullible for the intoxicating 'high school' feeling of wit and chemistry, and now wants a stability check that requires demonstrated behavior, not just words. — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **The book 'Eight Dates' gives a structured set of conversations couples should have at any stage: trust and commitment, conflict, sex and intimacy, work and money, family, fun and adventure, growth and spirituality, and shared dreams.** A man Cherie is dating gave her the book after only a few dates; the sisters read it as premarital counseling in disguise and as a signal that he is intentional, effectively initiating mutual interrogation from the start. _[Eight Dates (book), Stanford GSB Interpersonal Dynamics ('Touchy Feely')]_ — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **Dating outcomes depend on city: San Francisco is professional and its gender ratio favors women, while LA skews toward entertainment.** When Jean set her Hinge location to Pacific Heights for a weekend she got hundreds of likes she could not get through, raising a 'product market fit' question about where she dates; SF and New York are described as more professional cities. _[Hinge]_ — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **Stack-rank the five or six most important elements of life (career, health, family of origin, partner, future children, God) and compare lists with your partner to test value alignment.** Jean learned this exercise in couples therapy with her ex-fiance and was shocked at the big differences that surfaced even after eight years together; the earlier you do it, the more clarity you have. — [2025-02-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaMVodL23LE)
- **Partners don't need identical rankings, but each must be comfortable and happy with the other's stack ranking.** One person can rank children first and the other career first and it can work if they agree on the roles that implies, such as primary caretaker and breadwinner; at minimum the exercise forces the conversation. — [2025-02-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaMVodL23LE)
- **Being best friends who feel similar makes it easy to project alignment onto a partner and assume shared priorities that were never verified.** Jean says two otherwise well-adapted, successful people never got to the bottom of their priorities in eight years because those topics don't come up naturally in conversation. — [2025-02-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaMVodL23LE)
- **Couples counseling, including premarital counseling before engagement, is healthy and should be done earlier and more consistently rather than as a last resort.** Jean did double one-hour sessions every Sunday and was emotionally drained afterward; she wishes they had done it earlier, and notes a friend doing premarital counseling before even getting engaged, which is quietly becoming more common though still stigmatized. — [2025-02-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaMVodL23LE)
- **If you're not ready to do the values exercise with a partner, do it alone first: rank your own values, imagine theirs realistically, then work up to doing it together.** The conversation can happen in five-minute chunks with breaks to think, rather than one big sitting; Jean cautions to keep the imagined version rooted in reality, 'no limerence.' — [2025-02-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaMVodL23LE)
- **Do not use LinkedIn to hit on people; unsolicited romantic InMails are inappropriate, harassing to women, and can get your profile reported and removed.** Cherie, a former LinkedIn employee, says trust and safety teams actively fight this because LinkedIn profiles are public professional identities that cannot be made private. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2025-02-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaMVodL23LE)
- **LinkedIn is useful for dating only as a background-check tool to verify someone is who they say they are and to see first-, second- and third-degree mutual connections.** Cherie recommends running background checks for safety but says to take any outreach off-platform, e.g. via email. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2025-02-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaMVodL23LE)
- **Treating dating like a full-time work stream with a funnel and quota is extreme but reflects the intentionality some ambitious women adopt after prioritizing career.** Jean's friend paid a matchmaker $6,000 for three dates, joined clubs and all the apps, and teamed with a friend to cold-email LinkedIn prospects as each other's matchmakers; one man replied cc'ing his wife, and she ultimately met her fiance on Hinge and got engaged within seven months. _[Hinge, LinkedIn]_ — [2025-02-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaMVodL23LE)
- **As an entrepreneur without a school or office dating pool, treat everyone as a potential coworker and meet people through legitimate business introductions.** Jean's serious relationships all started as friendships from school or work; a friend reframed her situation as 'now everyone is your coworker,' and Jean accidentally got a date from a business coffee introduced by a friend. — [2025-02-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaMVodL23LE)
- **Freeze eggs, not embryos, to protect yourself, since an ex-partner can block use of shared embryos.** A friend of a friend froze embryos with her fiance after cancer, later divorced, and he refused to let her use them; Jean's doctor said freezing technology has improved so the success gap between frozen eggs and embryos is now only about 4%, so she chose eggs despite being about to get engaged. — [2025-02-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaMVodL23LE)
- **Going through egg freezing together is a gauntlet that reveals what roles each partner naturally takes; a true 50/50 partner should handle all the appointments, prescriptions and paperwork while the woman bears the physical burden.** Jean says the arrangement can differ as long as both agree, but if a man markets himself as a progressive 50/50 partner he must show it; Cherie calls it a better test than getting a dog together and part of dating 'information collecting.' — [2025-02-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaMVodL23LE)
- **Information from girlfriends is power; sharing personal experiences on fertility and dating empowers women to make life decisions with confidence, not just career ones.** Jean explains she shares her egg-freezing and counseling story because she gained so much wisdom from friends and wants to pass it on. — [2025-02-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaMVodL23LE)
- **For travel, who you're with matters more than the destination; a beautiful place is just a bonus.** (John Wu) Wu, an East Coaster, prefers warm Caribbean blue-water beaches with his family and thinks West Coasters under-appreciate them versus Hawaii. — [2025-03-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VuBM_XODr7A)
- **Dating in your late 20s approaching 30 is a life transition: conversations move from fun to compatibility, engagement and who wakes up with the baby, requiring more vulnerability and carrying higher stakes.** Cherie says she hasn't had these conversations before and finds them harder and messier, while Jean, newly single after a long engagement, is enjoying the lighter 'ha ha hee hee' phase. — [2025-03-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=I04_Nc8odHw)
- **Returning to dating after 8+ years can be as fun as before; a positive first experience matters because a string of bad dates delivers little cuts that crush the spirit.** Jean worried it would be a totally new paradigm being almost 10 years older, and the unknown felt more vast than ever, but it has been encouraging so far. — [2025-03-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=I04_Nc8odHw)
- **Choose a partner who wants you to be as or more successful than they are; the crux of a partnership is allowing each person to thrive, leapfrogging each other without competition.** (Leah Wyar) Leah dated people who didn't want an equal or more successful partner, and those relationships ended. Her husband Nick, 5 years younger and raised by a single mom to respect women, loved that she was many levels ahead in her career; together 21 years since 2004, now both running businesses and trading childcare when the other travels. _[Nick (husband)]_ — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **She knew her husband was 'the one' the day she met him and called her aunt the next day to say so, despite him being 22 and her 27.** (Leah Wyar) Over time you come to understand the depths of why that cosmic first impression was right, in her case his deep support for her ambitions. — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **Only partner with someone who has a growth mindset, because a couple must be able to grow and move in the same direction rather than 'this is who I am, don't try to change me'.** Jean frames it as dating a 'default yes' or 'default maybe' person over a 'default no'; a date who happily came to a Broadway rave and had the time of his life was her example. — [2025-05-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xPAIT2MpsuY)
- **Ask your partner 'How can I love you better?' as a daily mantra to root a relationship in growth.** Cherie learned it from two recently married friends who both work and ask each other this after long days; she aspires to that intentionality. — [2025-05-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xPAIT2MpsuY)
- **A supportive partner who does not second-guess your decisions is a cheat code, and a partner from an unrelated field gives you the 'regular person' perspective that breaks the tech echo chamber.** (Kofi Ampadu) Kofi's wife, a journalist, documentarian and photographer, shot product photos and helped with social posts for his startup; she is not a techno-optimist, so they debate rather than argue and she asks 'why do you need AI for that?' — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **Hold 'brown bag lunches' with your partner where each researches a topic and pitches it to the other, who asks questions.** (Kofi Ampadu) Topics range from the societal benefits of AI to why francophone African countries peg their currency to the French system; the shared skill is that both journalists and investors must ask questions before committing resources. Cherie frames it as wanting a partner who is a lifelong learning partner (the 'zombie apocalypse standard': don't be bored with that person). — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **Couples do best with a shared account for bills, investing and family expenses plus individual accounts for personal spending, regardless of life stage.** (Ryan Edlifson) Transparency yields better long-term outcomes than fully separate finances; partners typically know the joint balance and a ballpark of each other's personal accounts. Contributions need not be 50/50, but saver and spender partners must talk openly, as Ryan (saver) and his wife (spender) do. — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **Talk about money with a partner once you know you like them and it could be serious, and if you are uncomfortable, blame your financial advisor to open the conversation.** (Ryan Edlifson) Ryan googled the top 100 questions to ask before marriage with his wife; he suggests openers like 'my finance guy recommends six months of expenses saved, have you heard that?'. Cherie notes the person you marry is your most important financial decision because habits blend. — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **Increasingly, women are the higher earners; 7 of Ryan's top 10 clients are female and more clients are stay-at-home dads.** (Ryan Edlifson) Asked if they would be comfortable out-earning a partner, the sisters say yes if the partner is excellent in some other currency, like a NASA scientist; Ryan challenges whether being a great stay-at-home dad counts, and says people should do things for their own reasons as long as they know why. — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **A prenup should never be a relationship killer, and no honest, authentic money question should be, but standard prenups at $100K net worth are delusional.** (Ryan Edlifson) Prenups rarely come up with his 30s-40s clients; they can cover future earnings as well as existing assets, and are reasonable when someone explains why it matters to them. — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **An advisor learns more about you in an hour than your closest friends, so hidden behaviors like a secret engagement can surface as relationship red flags.** (Ryan Edlifson) Ryan saw no financial red flags in Jean's 8-year relationship with her ex-fiance, but in hindsight flagged that she kept the engagement secret and essentially planned her own proposal. — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **Stop making dating about the other person; center your own needs, because people-pleasing sacrifices your identity and builds relationships on a weak foundation.** Cherie says in her 20s she bit her tongue and let go of parts of herself out of fear of how the other person would feel, which she connects to a scarcity mindset; going into her 30s she wants to double down on who she has become. — [2025-06-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DubkuAz2meM)
- **Treat dating like a job interview: as much as 'I hope they like me', ask 'do I even like them?'.** Cherie notes people naturally bring the two-way evaluation mindset to job interviews but not to relationships; Jean says the difference is that when you are younger you are less secure in yourself. — [2025-06-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DubkuAz2meM)
- **Dating in your 20s is for fun, self-discovery and 'the lore'; heartbreaks feel devastating but they all pass.** Jean says she had no formula in her 20s and was open to all kinds of people, and told her younger sister not to take breakups so seriously, advice Cherie could not accept at 22-24. — [2025-06-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DubkuAz2meM)
- **In your 30s, don't date for potential or be 'Bob the Builder'; take someone at face value, where who they are is what they do, not what they say.** Jean says women are socialized to nurture and help partners grow, and she has watched exes become very successful. Her test: if someone claims to love marathons but is physically obese, trust what you see; an ex who 'loved road trips' never planned one and had no US license. — [2025-06-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DubkuAz2meM)
- **Love alone is not enough; the oxytocin of a relationship makes you believe you can overcome any obstacle, but practical support for a future family matters.** Jean says if love alone were enough she would still be engaged or married by now; if she has three children they need to be supported. — [2025-06-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DubkuAz2meM)
- **When you commit long-term you are marrying a lifestyle, not just a person: their family, holidays, life-planning horizon, politics, religion, health habits and socioeconomic class.** Jean admits this is unromantic but says as an inherently romantic person she has to teach herself these cold hard truths; whether someone plans five or ten years out versus rolling day to day is part of the lifestyle you opt into. — [2025-06-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DubkuAz2meM)
- **By the time you first think 'I don't think this is going to work', you have already cleared so many mental hurdles that the relationship is probably too far gone; end it then.** Looking back at every relationship she ended in her 20s and 30s, Jean wishes she had ended each the first time she had that thought, instead of trudging through and refusing to admit defeat (loss aversion). Cherie's corporate translation: hire slowly, fire fast. — [2025-06-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DubkuAz2meM)
- **Ambitious women should look for a partner who supports their dreams without ego, rather than matching credentials.** Jean says credentials can blind you in dating; at a bachelorette with 10 HBS women, many were the breadwinners and one friend realized an Ivy League and MBA match mattered less than someone who loves and understands her deeply. _[Harvard Business School]_ — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **You need a partner who sees you in your most vulnerable state and compels you to keep going.** (Fernando) After selling assets in 2007 and struggling in 2008, he nearly quit entrepreneurship for a job; his newly married wife told him she would not last with him as an employee and pushed him back into the ring harder than he pushed himself. — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **After a breakup, adopt the identity of 'someone who says yes to invitations' to break old routines and create serendipity for meeting people.** Jean set a target of going to about three events a month she'd normally skip; attending a friend's Super Bowl party she would have declined led to dating the host's childhood friend for a while. — [2025-07-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=McWujpZMzLw)
- **If you're not the same person you were in your 20s, you don't have to be the same person romantically; outgrowing relationships and friendships is part of growth.** From Esther Perel's Mating in Captivity: reframe your identity from 'someone who fails in relationships' to 'someone who learns from past mistakes and builds healthier dynamics'. _[Esther Perel, Mating in Captivity]_ — [2025-07-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=McWujpZMzLw)
- **Relationships are supposed to fail until you find the one, so dating is 'failing upward' rather than a record of failure.** Cherie reframes serial breakups as the design of dating; you can only keep one relationship at the end, and this links back to Clear's identity framework of internalizing 'I learn from relationships'. — [2025-07-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=McWujpZMzLw)
- **The reversible/irreversible lens applies to life too: dating is reversible, marriage is much harder to reverse because of the legal ties.** The sisters joke about which bucket divorce belongs in, noting it is sometimes reversible and sometimes not, and that marriage adds legal loopholes to any breakup. — [2025-08-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NLxFGD_WaBw)
- **In a lopsided friendship, name your need once, hand the other person one easy action to reciprocate, and then let them; if they wanted to, they would.** After a decade of always initiating with her friend 'Sam', Cherie asked her to schedule the next call; Sam texted a month later, then never replied to 'what time are you free?' and a year passed. Cherie hasn't thought about her since, which is the freedom the theory delivers. — [2025-08-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H-xnPI_rUqk)
- **Friendship breakups can be worse than romantic breakups, and ghosting is an avoidant way to end one that still tells you what you need to know.** Jean suspects Sam said exactly what Cherie wanted to hear on the call to avoid conflict, reading it from her own admitted conflict-avoidant tendencies. — [2025-08-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H-xnPI_rUqk)
- **Let Them works for exes too: when an ex wants to get back together, lay out the concrete things that need to happen and let their willingness to act (or brainstorm) show you who they are.** Cherie gave her ex a 'performance improvement plan' of pillars and even suggested using ChatGPT to brainstorm; he wasn't willing to do or brainstorm anything, which answered the question. _[ChatGPT]_ — [2025-08-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H-xnPI_rUqk)
- **Choose friends and partners who naturally do the things you do for them; what feels easy to you may be genuinely hard for them, and effort that isn't sustainable is not a match.** Jean's recent boyfriend was perceptive and tried very hard to meet her needs but was 'at his limit' and couldn't sustain it for life. — [2025-08-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H-xnPI_rUqk)
- **The language around you shapes your identity, so protect it: a partner who calls you a dummy daily chips away at you even if you know you're smart.** Cherie argues perception is largely what you say and hear; pairing a clear identity with matching language builds the best habits. — [2025-09-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HjebjpDU8-Q)
- **SBI works in personal relationships too, including with someone you're dating, though results may vary with immigrant parents.** Cherie used the 'I felt X when Y' framing with a man she was dating and he thanked her for raising it; she describes herself as being in a 'confrontational phase' where she no longer bottles things up. — [2025-09-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=5syGABv5hbw)
- **An ick is a mindset: when you genuinely like someone, nothing they do is an ick, and when someone is already on their way out, any small thing becomes one.** Case study: Cherie's foam Yeezy-style slides ick was 'the slide that broke the camel's back' for a guy already on the way out, while Jean found an ex who slept in mid-calf socks cute because she liked him, and Cherie tolerates tiny no-show socks on the man she is currently dating. — [2025-09-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Mi599tx1y80)
- **Cherie's ick is a handsome man who knows he is handsome; she prefers humble men with a 'superhero origin story' who were a bit nerdy or bullied as kids and don't realize they're hot.** She has dated self-absorbed men who love their own abs: 'we can't both love your abs'; women can think they're hot shit but she doesn't like it when men do. — [2025-09-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Mi599tx1y80)
- **Small behaviors on a first date are indicators of larger character traits, so it is fair to refuse a second date over them.** Cherie declined a second date with a man who said he'd ditch the group on a good powder day, reasoning that a 'selfish skier' who leaves you on the mountain will also leave you at home changing diapers while he golfs; he got defensive when she raised it. — [2025-09-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Mi599tx1y80)
- **A man who asks you out should solve basic problems like food on the date; having to pull out Google Maps yourself is an ick.** Jean had to buy her own chips at a drinks-only venue after the kitchen closed; the sisters say they problem-solve all day for work, their mom and themselves, and in a romantic relationship they want the man to take over and propose three restaurant options. — [2025-09-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Mi599tx1y80)
- **The most romantic gestures are acts of service that cost time and thoughtfulness rather than money.** A finance guy with a demanding schedule bought a Costco cookware set, simmered chicken soup from scratch for four hours while working, and delivered broth, veggies, chicken and pasta in separate Mason jars so Cherie could mix and match while sick; he could have ordered soup but time was the premium. _[Costco]_ — [2025-09-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Mi599tx1y80)
- **Romance is proving you listened and then acting on it to surprise and delight, repeatedly, not just once.** A man Jean dated showed up with a wrapped early edition of her favorite play 'Our Town' after she mentioned it casually, and DoorDashed a bouquet the morning after she joked 'where are my flowers?'; people want to feel seen, understood and appreciated, which are precursors to romantic love. _[Our Town]_ — [2025-09-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Mi599tx1y80)
- **Dimming your light to be a 65 percent version of yourself for a partner is exhausting and creates a negative flywheel where they fall in love with a version of you that isn't you.** Two of Cherie's exes laughed derisively at her ambitions and jokes, so she hid parts of herself to keep emotionally satisfying relationships going; the sisters say this also dupes the partner unfairly, and Jean links repressing yourself to inflammation and physical symptoms via the mind-body axis. — [2025-09-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Mi599tx1y80)
- **Ghosting means purposefully ignoring someone you have actually gone on dates with; stopping a dating-app chat before meeting carries no responsibility, like passing someone on the street.** Cherie regrets 'shirking' an older guy in college instead of saying she wasn't interested, which would have been the bigger-person move; Jean was told years later by a friend's new boyfriend that she had stopped answering him on an app and had no memory of it. — [2025-09-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Mi599tx1y80)
- **Being turned off by a partner's success or profession is a useful filter: such a person is narrow-minded and not the right one for you.** Cherie admits she downplays being a content creator on Hinge first dates because of misconceptions, especially among SF tech bros versus LA where it is a normal profession; Jean doesn't date strangers, is proud of her career and could not imagine being with someone turned off by it. _[Hinge]_ — [2025-09-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Mi599tx1y80)
- **Dating a coworker should be approached with caution, clear boundaries and even a contingency plan for the breakup.** Both sisters have done it (Jean at Goldman outside her direct group and after Zynga; Cherie at LinkedIn) and it ended in disaster or awkwardness; the anxiety of possibly bumping into an ex in the lobby occupies brain space, and the outcome depends on your personality, coworkers gossiping and company culture. _[Goldman Sachs, Zynga]_ — [2025-09-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Mi599tx1y80)
- **Exes who treated you poorly do not get to say they are 'proud' of your later success; you got there despite them, not because of them.** Cherie compares congratulatory texts from exes who now see her on their For You page to an absent father showing up at graduation; she would name their group chat 'Fans'. She also doesn't stay friends with exes, finding it odd once in a new relationship. — [2025-09-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Mi599tx1y80)
- **How a man cares for his feet reflects how he carries himself.** Jean says feet don't need to be manicured but can't be dirty; Cherie hates tiny no-show socks on men because they grip the foot too tightly and show 'toe cleavage'. — [2025-09-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Mi599tx1y80)
- **You are the product of the five people you spend the most time with, so take stock of them and write one thing you admire about each.** If you cannot think of something you admire, they may not deserve to be in your inner circle; your energy and time are your most precious resources and it is fine to recalibrate. — [2025-09-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TkXvlgPXitc)
- **Treat your energy as a luxury good that not everyone can afford, and drop relationships where generosity is not matched.** Paraphrasing Taylor Swift; the hosts add that if your natural giving level is an 8 and theirs is a 3 you will always be disappointed, which is especially draining for givers like Jean. _[Taylor Swift]_ — [2025-09-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TkXvlgPXitc)
- **You do not need 100 friends; 5 to 10 ride-or-dies who see into your soul is all you can maintain, so use the word 'friend' selectively.** Cherie wishes she had known this in her early 20s when feeling well-liked mattered to her ego; people met on a night out are acquaintances or peers, not friends, and lowering the bar for new acquaintances removes pressure. — [2025-09-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TkXvlgPXitc)
- **People in your life fall into three categories: true friends, fans, and those there to teach you a lesson as a case study.** From a TikTok Jean saw; she notes women especially default to bucketing everyone as a friend, and some lessons take a long time to learn. — [2025-09-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TkXvlgPXitc)
- **Different friends for different seasons: a 'not now' is about their capacity, not about you.** People may be dealing with medical, family or relationship matters you will never know about, so do not assume rejection is personal; plenty of like-minded people are out there. — [2025-09-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TkXvlgPXitc)
- **Do not marry for money, but treat who you marry as the most important financial decision of your life and read a partner's money as an indicator of drive, ambition and lifestyle.** You share meals, buy a house and raise kids together; what matters is less the savings balance than what it shows about the person, and whether two lifestyles that get less flexible with age can merge. — [2025-10-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q49Hzzez6Fo)
- **Money attached to someone else only counts if it comes with generosity and trust; otherwise the strings make you a prisoner in the lake house.** A friend's ultra-wealthy boyfriend's trust fund (lake house included) required him to move back to his hometown, meaning she would have had to uproot her life to live his parents' plan, which ended the relationship. — [2025-10-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q49Hzzez6Fo)
- **If a business partner missed deadlines and went dark for days you would end the deal, but high-performing women let it slide in dating and over-give to people who are not showing up.** Framing for the episode: stop over-functioning and act like the CEO of your own life. — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **Showing up fully for someone who has not chosen you is like waiting for a deal that is never going to close; a CEO makes the hire and the decisions rather than sitting on the sidelines.** Examples: pouring in effort early on chemistry alone, or being the partner who is blamed if the wedding gift for his college friend does not show up. Mini exercise: name one way you shrink yourself in dating that you would never tolerate in business, then ask what your CEO self would do. — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **Do emotional due diligence before getting serious, examining communication style, dating history and how they handle commitment, the way an investor checks financials and leadership before buying a company.** Skipping the unsexy values conversations leaves you in the deep end wishing you had discussed the important topics before feeling so deeply. — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **Both partners being committed to repair after conflict is one of the strongest predictors of a successful relationship; it is about whether you fight well and make up, not being perfect.** Cited from the Gottman Institute; you can only learn this by seeing how someone communicates during a disagreement and how you move past it. _[Gottman Institute]_ — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **Ask better questions on dates, like what happened in your last breakup and how you handle conflict, and notice how they talk about exes, friends and family.** Cherie jokes 'I'm nosy, I want to know' to make the timing feel natural, whether on date one or date five. — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **Words mean nothing without consistent action; in your 30s it is 'you say this, now show me'.** Cherie says in her early 20s she was charmed by words of affirmation and promises that never had action behind them. — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **Watch how someone handles discomfort, mistakes and things going wrong, and track whether you feel calm or on edge around them.** Jean's tennis date: he proposed it, failed to book a court, got an attitude when someone claimed it, and she ended up doing unpaid emotional labor soothing him for his own mistake. — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **High-achieving women drawn to projects fall into dating someone's potential instead of their patterns, and can wait months or years for the imagined version to appear.** Cherie spent years on and off with someone she was more excited about as a future version; Jean says you can lose yourself putting your goals on the back burner for a partner who does not reciprocate, even without malice. — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **Bring investor-style clarity and KPIs to relationships, expecting certain progress by certain milestones, because without intentionality romance slips into complacency.** A strong connection without consistent follow-through is just a marketing campaign. — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **Defining your deal terms (non-negotiables) is not high maintenance; it is setting filters that save your time and theirs, and you cannot expect someone to meet terms you have never articulated.** Examples: only dating someone who takes accountability, or who plans a certain number of dates; write down two or three non-negotiables and recommit to one boundary you broke in the past. — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **Being picked up from the airport is a legitimate non-negotiable if that is how you feel loved, and if someone cannot meet your terms that is not a failure but your filter working.** Cherie was previously afraid to ask for this act of service though it meant a lot to her; she allows reasonable exceptions like work meetings, and notes LAX at 10 p.m. Sunday makes the effort mean even more. — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **Emotional equity means both people put in effort to plan, solve problems and make things work, rather than one doing the heavy lifting while the other coasts.** A 2023 Pew study found women in heterosexual relationships report carrying 2.5 times more emotional labor than men, and 65% feel overwhelmed. _[Pew Research]_ — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **Three-question litmus test: after a date do you feel more or less like yourself; are you chasing their attention or asking whether they deserve yours; and if you stopped initiating would the connection die or deepen.** You must pass all three, since fun dates that leave you feeling yourself can still come with no communication in between; the test applies to friendships too. — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **Who you marry is the most important decision of your life: it affects finances, family, children and is the person you will share the most meals with, so run the litmus tests early.** Marrying someone who causes more emotional strife than connection is a burden you can overcome but that creates a much bigger headache down the road. — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **Noticing an imbalance does not mean ending the relationship; repair it in three steps: track the emotional labor for a week, talk about it with I-statements, and make a request rather than a demand.** Example: 'I've noticed I've been buying all the wedding gifts for your friends, could we share that responsibility?' A real partner will co-create; a mismatch will resist or deflect, so either way you get your answer. — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **The five frameworks are shift from proving to choosing, practice emotional due diligence, trust consistent behavior over potential, define your deal terms, and build emotional equity through shared effort.** The episode is really about self-worth, power and confidence, entering relationships from clarity not scarcity; the power move is to make one decision using the frameworks this week. — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **Marriage is a business relationship and who you marry is the most important financial decision you will make.** (Maggie Sellers) Maggie calls her husband her 'life co-founder' and argues that treating the marriage as a relationship with contracts, responsibilities and negotiations, as unsexy as it is, sets you up to enjoy it instead of putting your head in the sand. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **If you spend more time planning your wedding than your prenup, you are planning a wedding day, not a marriage.** (Maggie Sellers) Maggie and her husband spent more time planning their prenup than their wedding; a prenup lays out roles, responsibilities, who handles child care and what happens when a woman takes time off work. Her number one tip: it takes far longer than you think. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **The right person will make you feel more secure in the relationship through a prenup, not less.** (Maggie Sellers) The process was hard and at moments she wondered if it would work out, since for the first time the couple was not on the same team, but working back to the middle before assets were further entangled made her more sure of her life partner than anything else. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **Start couples therapy before you have problems and bring an agenda to each session.** (Maggie Sellers) Maggie and her husband began seeing a couples therapist one month into dating, doing a two-hour block monthly with a pre-agreed agenda, so they did not need separate premarital counseling during the prenup. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **Your full attention is a gift; do not undervalue giving it, and small moments of presence have huge ripple effects.** Her therapist's example: a parent taking two seconds to look at what a 7-year-old wants to show them versus saying 'not now' shapes the child's sense of worth; her busy husband gives her that presence daily, which her ex-fiance could not. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **To people who say a woman dates for money, the answer is 'know your worth and date higher, stop dating losers'.** (Maggie Sellers) Maggie bought her own house at 26, took her first startup public, and for six years put money into female-founded businesses instead of fashion (her first Hermes bag came only this year); she wants a partner with equal ambition, not a bankroll. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **Dating for money is insecure by design because there is always someone richer, better looking or more famous; a purpose-driven partnership is what feels safe.** (Maggie Sellers) She and her husband aim to be a 'purpose couple' rather than a 'power couple', combining his serial entrepreneurship and philanthropy with her mission of helping women know their worth. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **Partners should have matching levels of strongly presenting values like radius, beholderism and belovedness, or the relationship will be a constant fight over what matters.** (Suzy Welch) The Values Bridge gives couples a conflict or harmony score; her daughter and fiance share their top six values, while Cherie's early-20s boyfriend mocked her big dreams and would have scored near 100% conflict. Welch's advice: give a serious partner the test. _[Values Bridge]_ — [2025-11-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eRJyWu1CgI0)
- **'No last best deal' applies to love: believing this partner is the last one available makes you settle, and settling never leads anywhere good.** (Suzy Welch) A 55-year-old friend stayed with a man who treated her terribly because 'who else is there going to be'; he left her for a younger woman and she later happily found someone else. — [2025-11-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eRJyWu1CgI0)
- **A dual-career couple works by sharing values, talking constantly, knowing their lanes and deeply respecting each other's expertise.** (Suzy Welch) She was never CEO of GE managing 400,000 people and Jack had never written or edited books; they let each other be good at what they were good at. It also helped that Jack had high agency while she is low-agency and consensus-driven. _[Jack Welch, GE]_ — [2025-11-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eRJyWu1CgI0)
- **In disagreements, start from the inside mantra 'maybe he's right' and hear the other person out without defensiveness; it often makes them more open to your counterpoints.** (Suzy Welch) When Jack announced a changed vacation plan her gut said 'worst idea ever' but she asked why, listened, then laid out where they differed, and he often said 'I never thought of that.' It rests on trusting that he loved her and would never hurt her. _[Jack Welch]_ — [2025-11-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eRJyWu1CgI0)
- **Money is the number one reason couples fight, so initiate the finance conversation directly and without awkwardness before moving in together.** Say 'I'd love to talk about our finances, let's find a time,' and do it in a cozy private setting like a night in with wine, pajamas and your banking apps open, never at a restaurant or in public. — [2025-11-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e2AV_kUtiVs)
- **Cover three questions in the first money talk: your approach to saving and spending, whether and how you budget, and your long-term goals.** Long-term goals include retirement, buying a house or a big travel fund; don't just ask how much your partner makes. — [2025-11-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e2AV_kUtiVs)
- **Use exploratory bridge questions, such as how their family talked about money growing up and what they would do with $10,000 tomorrow, to ease into tactical finance topics.** The $10,000 question comes from friend Ramit Sethi; it reveals whether a partner leans toward travel, investing or retirement and feeds naturally into the long-term goals discussion. _[Ramit Sethi]_ — [2025-11-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e2AV_kUtiVs)
- **Treat the first one or two money conversations as data collection: watch whether your partner engages at least 50/50 or is defensive, secretive or avoidant.** Don't run a four-hour marathon or demand bank statements; open with one question, lower expectations, and invite them to co-drive and revisit the topic over multiple sessions to build shared operating principles. — [2025-11-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e2AV_kUtiVs)
- **A couple merging finances needs an operating system of aligned principles, or differing assumptions will make cohabitation crumble quickly.** Jean concedes 'operating agreement' sounds unsexy but says this is your most important relationship and the beginning of merging money. — [2025-11-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e2AV_kUtiVs)
- **A well-paid boyfriend who Venmo-requests half of every little thing is a red flag unless you operate the same way.** Cherie says penny-counting isn't how she wants a relationship; Jean adds it can work if both partners speak the same system, and the friction arises only when one is transactional and the other isn't. _[Venmo]_ — [2025-11-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e2AV_kUtiVs)
- **Money behavior is never just about money; it reflects where a person sits on the transactional-to-generous spectrum, and a life partner should be within one standard deviation of you.** Venmo habits extrapolate to chores, responsibilities and childcare later. Acquaintances can be Venmo kings and queens, but inner-circle people like a boyfriend or bestie must share your values. — [2025-11-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e2AV_kUtiVs)
- **In close friendships, alternate treating each other rather than splitting every bill, and match a transactional friend's system only if that's clearly how they operate.** Cherie was hurt when a bestie Venmo-requested $2.80 for a water bottle after she had treated them repeatedly; you cannot change how people were raised to think about money, so she treated it as a mini red flag. _[Venmo]_ — [2025-11-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e2AV_kUtiVs)
- **Over-explaining why you can't do something, even to friends, is a form of apologizing for taking up space.** Cherie used to give three explanations for missing a barbecue when a single reason would do, because she felt extra obliged. — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **Only be with a partner who has a growth mindset, because a couple has to be able to grow in the same direction.** Cherie rejects the 'this is who I am, don't try to change me' stance; Jean adds it's about default-yes versus default-no people, citing a date who happily joined a Broadway rave. A married couple they know asks each other nightly 'how can I love you better?' — [2025-12-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NmdHxviASBg)
- **Adopt the identity of 'someone who says yes to invitations' to create serendipity when starting to date again.** Newly single, Jean aimed to attend three things a month she'd normally skip; going to a Super Bowl party despite not liking football led to dating the host's childhood friend. — [2025-12-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NmdHxviASBg)
- **Rewrite your narrative from 'someone who fails in relationships' to 'someone who learns and builds healthier dynamics'; relationships are supposed to fail until you find the one.** From Esther Perel's Mating in Captivity: if you're not the person you were in your 20s, you needn't be the same romantically. The hosts call it romantically failing upward and link it to Atomic Habits' identity concept. _[Esther Perel, Mating in Captivity, James Clear]_ — [2025-12-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NmdHxviASBg)
- **Choose a partner who wants you to be as successful or more successful than they are; supportive partners leapfrog each other rather than compete.** (Leah Wyar) Leah dated people who could not handle her success before meeting Nick, five years younger and raised by a single mom to respect women; she called her aunt the day after meeting him to say she had found her husband. After 21 years they trade off travel and childcare as two executives. — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)

### Family, childhood & siblings (15)

- **If you can, house evacuated friends; in a fast-moving fire, situations change within minutes.** Cherie's gym closed mid-yoga because the Sunset Fire had started seven minutes north, with flames visible behind a Target and CVS; meanwhile the sisters hosted friends evacuated from the Eaton fire who had headaches from the smoke. _[Sunset Fire, Eaton Fire]_ — [2025-01-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_8AlNMC2l4)
- **First-generation Americans can lack the shared cultural references that multigenerational families absorb at home.** Cherie recognized zero Bob Dylan songs in the film because her immigrant parents never played that music at home; she grew up on Britney Spears and NSYNC, which shaped why the biopic did not land for her. _[Bob Dylan]_ — [2025-01-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_8AlNMC2l4)
- **An entrepreneurial mindset is built by early childhood work and immigrant-family hustle, where more hours reliably means more money.** (David Shim) From age six David shopped at Costco and stocked his mom's deli (learning to turn Coke cans label-forward); his father sold cars with limited English; at 11-12 he delivered newspapers with his mom driving because he was too scrawny to bike. _[Costco]_ — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **There is no strict pattern that childhood rule-breakers become the successful adults; Imran was a strict rule follower and still built an unconventional career.** Imran followed rules growing up in Bangladesh to avoid corporal punishment at school and from a tough mother, and has never had a speeding ticket; the hosts note most guests claim to have been rule breakers. — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **If your family did not teach you presentation skills like hair and makeup, learn them intentionally on your own, the way the Kardashians' father paid for professional makeup lessons when the girls hit their teens.** The hosts' mom invested heavily in skincare, a generational benefit, but wore little makeup, so Cherie learned the smokey eye at 13 from Michelle Phan YouTube tutorials. _[Kardashians, Michelle Phan, YouTube]_ — [2025-06-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eFpgcrlf2gk)
- **Calling out a loved one for settling is worth doing but must be done rarely, from concern, and framed as a possible blind spot rather than distrust.** Cherie raised concerns about Jean's last relationship only once or twice, saying 'I trust you to make the decision, but this is something you might not see'; it's a type-one irreversible decision, and if you're wrong you still have to attend the wedding. Jean's near-call-out was urging Cherie to take a Bain Capital Ventures internship instead of only making content before GSB. _[Bain Capital Ventures, Stanford GSB, LinkedIn]_ — [2025-08-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QUN5LWuRwu0)
- **Financial dependence on parents can trap adults in their late 30s into living their parents' values instead of their own.** Jean has seen grown adults unable to make their own life decisions because the power over their lifestyle sits with parents who fund it. — [2025-10-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=q49Hzzez6Fo)
- **A co-founder with a different brain is essential, and being brothers is what kept the company alive through clashes because they refuse to give up on each other.** (Mike Rabil) Mike says if Paul weren't his brother he probably would have quit after too many co-founder clashes. Growing up, 22 months apart, they had a rule to always play on the same team in pickup sports because opposite teams ended in fist fights. — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **Working with a sibling shortcuts professionalism and triggers parent-child and birth-order dynamics, so feedback lands as an attack even when it comes from care.** (Mike Rabil) After Jean missed a red-lines email on the biggest brand deal ever (10x any prior deal), Cherie's 'put me on the email' implied she wouldn't have missed it and felt like parenting from the younger sister. Paul notes 40 years of sibling history is 'psychological warfare' baked in; Mike says a non-sibling partner would have led with empathy first. — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **Family businesses that make it get undying loyalty, commitment and respect that you'll never find in a non-sibling partner.** (Paul Rabil) The ingrained rivalry is why many families don't go into business together, but for those who do, no one is as committed as a sibling. — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **Children of immigrants achieve to prove the family's sacrifice was worthwhile and to buy belonging and safety.** (Jane Marie Chen) Chen arrived in the US at four speaking no English, one of the only Asian families in her neighborhood; the hosts add that you feel you owe it to your ancestors to make the most of your life. — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)
- **Cycles of abuse get normalized in Asian culture as cultural norms, and silence about them is complicity.** (Jane Marie Chen) She did not recognize being hit with a belt or slapped as abuse until her late 30s; a Taiwanese American friend from a violent home took his own life, which woke her up to speak out with her parents and community. — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)
- **Peace with parents who hurt you comes from compassion for their whole history without condoning the behavior, holding boundaries and an open heart at the same time.** (Jane Marie Chen) Writing about how her parents grew up let her see how trauma seeps through lineages; she now tunes into bodily contraction around toxic behavior and chooses not to be around it, while carrying resentment would only hurt herself. _[Like a Wave We Break]_ — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)
- **Confronting your parents is not about changing them; it is about taking the hand of your inner child and standing up for her.** (Jane Marie Chen) Leadership coach Diana Chapman of the Conscious Leadership Group pushed her to have the conversation; she went in with no expectation her parents would change, and the outcome was learning to trust herself. The hosts extend this to partners and friends: speak your truth, and whether they absorb it is on them. _[Diana Chapman, Conscious Leadership Group]_ — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)
- **Healthy boundaries with family make your yeses stronger and let you engage more wholeheartedly without underlying resentment.** (Jane Marie Chen) Raised as the holidays approach, since people who never learned boundaries growing up find them hardest to set with family. — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)

### Mindset, confidence & psychology (133)

- **Distinguish health risks you can act on from those you must simply acknowledge and live with, then make your choices.** Cherie's thorn is doom-scrolling about microplastics in tea bags, red dye, salmonella egg recalls and endocrine disruptors in perfume and dry shampoo; the sisters conclude you act where you can and accept the rest rather than freak out internally. — [2025-01-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_8AlNMC2l4)
- **Excessive media exposure of an actor's real personality undermines their ability to disappear into a role.** Reviewing A Complete Unknown (they rate it about 6.5 versus 7.8 on IMDb), Jean felt Timothee Chalamet's giggles broke character, comparing him to Brad Pitt and George Clooney, whereas Cillian Murphy's avoidance of press preserves mystery that helps audiences suspend disbelief. _[A Complete Unknown, Timothee Chalamet, Bob Dylan, Cillian Murphy, Brad Pitt, George Clooney]_ — [2025-01-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_8AlNMC2l4)
- **Seeing people respond to your vision feels better than almost anything else in the world.** (David Shim) David and Jean describe being in a flow state working every day and weekend when passionate about what they're building. — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **Doubt and hard questions from others are more useful than silence because they tell you exactly what to address next time.** The sisters note that pushback without crashing out is part of entrepreneurial resilience; getting nothing back means you were 'just practicing talking'. — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **Water off a duck's back: positive and negative feedback should affect you the same way; don't react too high to highs or too low to lows.** Cherie's former boss Eric Wollenbacher taught this equilibrium mindset, which mirrors David's 'let it wash off you'. _[Eric Wollenbacher]_ — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **DILMO: Do It, Learn, Move On, an acronym Cherie coined in 2021 for letting go after action.** Cherie says moving on is the hardest part and she has been trying to make DILMO happen for years, including in a TikTok. _[DILMO]_ — [2025-01-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tWxcgyd051c)
- **Frame past mistakes as 'if I had a time machine I would do X differently' rather than as regrets, because it forces a root-cause retrospective.** Saying 'I regret it' is closed-ended and fatalist; the time-machine phrasing extracts the lesson. Vu was admitted to YC twice (admission rate roughly 2-5%) partly because he could iterate on what he got wrong at 18-19. _[Y Combinator]_ — [2025-02-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0OopMnFAeoE)
- **Never get over-excited or over-depressed: when everyone is uber-hyped be nervous, and when everyone is depressed expect the day after tomorrow to be beautiful.** (Imran Khan) Imran says this is his natural personality (his dad was hurt when a gift got only an 'OK' reaction); the hosts note that internally he was even-keeled but externally cheerful at Snapchat, and contrast it with lore about Jeff Weiner's early hot-headed reputation at LinkedIn. _[Jeff Weiner, LinkedIn, Snapchat]_ — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **Being dismissive of young people is a career killer; as you age, who you learn from should flip from older people to people in their 20s and 30s.** (Imran Khan) Older people's learning curves plateau while younger people take more risks and bring new experiences; Imran credits working with 20-somethings at Snapchat as the best thing about joining, and the hosts say this belief is what let him build his relationship with Evan Spiegel. _[Snapchat, Evan Spiegel]_ — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **It is OK to be 90% perfect when speaking; audiences are human and are rooting for you, not waiting for you to fail.** (Imran Khan) Imran says who cares if a speech flops, that is what makes you human; Cherie adds a Stanford public speaking course's takeaway that listeners want you to succeed and recover when you stumble. _[Stanford]_ — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **Small daily actions compound into big results, per Atomic Habits: habit-stack and never skip more than two or three days.** The hosts extend compounding from finance to habits, citing the gym and Cherie's 2025 goal of eating 40 different fruits and vegetables a week, tracked on paper, and recommend James Clear's Atomic Habits. _[Atomic Habits, James Clear]_ — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **Studying Gen Z and Gen Alpha now helps you anticipate how society will act over the next 10 to 30 years.** Jean says she is obsessed with forming her own hypotheses about younger generations' driving needs and motivations because they will shape the future world. — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **It will rain in everyone's life, so build support systems before the storm hits.** Jean cites Stanford professor Joel Peterson: the only guarantee in life is that rain will fall (a death, a health crisis), and the point is to have the people who get you through it. _[Joel Peterson, Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **Being newly single means getting to fall in love again, which is the juice of life; hold that hope alongside the fear of being alone.** Jean admits existential dread about being single forever but says she has done the work to enjoy her own company, loves her activities and alone time, and concludes that life is about holding both hope and hopelessness at once. — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **Life is a series of micro failures; hearing 'no' more often is a signal to change how you approach problems and people, and a no today may be a gift that opens a better yes later.** (John Redgrave) Applies beyond career to raising kids, school and love life. Successful people 'incept' failures in their mind as things they turned around and focus on the learning rather than the mistake, running through brick walls by going through, around or over obstacles. — [2025-02-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pAWpvZ2vn_Q)
- **Humans learn more from failure than success, so if you never put yourself in a position where you might fail you are fundamentally capping your potential.** (John Redgrave) Both Redgrave and Cherie took classes from Stanford GSB professor Joel Peterson, who told students their biggest problem was that they had not failed enough; Redgrave only truly experienced failure once he started building companies. _[Joel Peterson, Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-02-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pAWpvZ2vn_Q)
- **High achievers who have never failed face a harder fall: the higher you climb before your first failure, the more devastating and harder to recover from it is, so seek micro failures early to build 'bouncy bones'.** Jean's analogy of little kids bouncing when they fall learning to ski versus an older person with brittle bones; the hosts link it to 'failure training' from David Shim's episode. Actionable takeaway: ask what small thing outside your comfort zone you can try and possibly fail at this week. _[David Shim]_ — [2025-02-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=pAWpvZ2vn_Q)
- **Rules are a construct; reason from first principles and challenge any rule that does not make sense.** (Peter Pham) Pham says media and pop culture define what things are 'supposed' to be, so he always asks why; if a rule makes sense he follows it, if not he does not mind breaking it. He was a literal rule breaker as a kid, committing crimes at 10-11 while growing up seven to a one-bedroom apartment in Santa Fe Springs and Norwalk. — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **Remove wanting from your life: wanting means you are not happy until you get the thing, so be present and grateful instead.** (Peter Pham) His advice to his 35-year-old self, framed as a Buddhist principle after a retreat with Mongolian monks; he says he already has everything he needs. The hosts note this matches the 'I have everything I need' mantra printed on their Sisters Matcha can. _[Sisters Matcha]_ — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **A terrible memory is a superpower: your future self will forget the bad thing anyway, so do not waste energy wallowing on it.** (Peter Pham) Pham says nobody wants to be around you when you give off sad energy. The hosts observe a Season 3 trend of guests with deliberately short memories who move on and keep iterating. — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **Supporting other people's wins lets you feel like you are winning every day, the same psychology that keeps sports fans loyal.** (Peter Pham) Pham says his happiness is driven by other people's success, which makes venture incubation the perfect job for him; COVID was terrible because he could not be around people. The hosts connect this to why customers want to feel part of a founder's story. — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **Depression is a luxury of time that Americans have; most of the world is too busy with war, famine and where to eat.** (Peter Pham) Pham relays a Buddhist monk's observation and says having grown from nothing and travelling to see poverty gives him gratitude, e.g. noticing they are sitting in a five-star hotel. — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **Your greatest weakness is often your greatest strength in a different context: the self-confidence that got Jeremy fired is likely what let him seize the big next opportunities.** Cherie's example: she reads rooms extremely well (even on Zoom at LinkedIn) but historically over-analyzed people's feelings and softened her own expression to avoid hurting anyone, which she is now unlearning post-Stanford 'Touchy Feely'. _[LinkedIn, Stanford Touchy Feely (Interpersonal Dynamics)]_ — [2025-03-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=109Xypx-hM4)
- **Being a childhood 'rule exceeder' out of fear of authority doesn't preclude eventually making your own rules; seeing someone a few steps ahead who started the same way shows what could be for you.** Jean strongly identified with Jeremy's 'rule exceeder' phrase and his statement that at some point you get to make your own rules and follow your interests. Cherie was 'shaken to her core' by a friend casually turning left on a red light with no cars around. — [2025-03-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=109Xypx-hM4)
