- **You can only connect the dots looking backwards, so don't sweat apparent mistakes; believe things happen for a reason and keep a positive attitude.** (Jonah Goodhart) Jonah cites Steve Jobs's commencement speech; the hosts debate whether this is backward-looking attribution bias but conclude that regret energy is better spent forward-looking, and note humans are naturally loss-minimizing rather than gain-maximizing. _[Steve Jobs]_ — [2025-03-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UYtMgkz8brA)
- **If you are in a grueling season where you truly could not do better, do not be hard on yourself: 'I'm doing the best I can every day' is a sufficient mantra.** Looking back at two years at Goldman, Jean concludes she was just trying to survive and could not have done better; Cherie uses a Spotify meditation several times a week that opens with that mantra. — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **Metrics-driven fitness goals can become unhealthy: Cherie's pandemic rule of beating a Peloton personal record every ride made her skip workouts on days she did not feel 100%.** She now recognizes she can be extreme and unforgiving with herself, so the 30-day challenge is explicitly framed as not a punishment. _[Peloton]_ — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **Changes never happen overnight; you are the captain of your own ship, and results come from consistency over weeks and months.** Cherie says she has never had an extreme relationship with food because she always understood progress is on a delayed timeline; if she feels bad about her body, the answer is to go to the gym more consistently, not to purge or starve. — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **Replace 'where there's a will there's a way' with 'embrace what's in front of you and keep going on the path you're on' as the way to change the course of your life.** (Michelle Huang) Michelle moved from forcing outcomes to believing in what is meant to be; the hosts close the episode on her line 'How do you change the course of your life? Embrace what's in front of you.' — [2025-03-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fb8gYzg6ikg)
- **Good things happen to good people, but only over the long run; cutting corners can pay off short term but loses over a long time horizon.** (Michelle Huang) Michelle's stated hot take, tied to keeping a long-term mentality about how you treat people and do things. — [2025-03-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fb8gYzg6ikg)
- **Let go of dreams that were not meant for you so you can receive future opportunities instead of staring at the closed door.** Cherie paraphrases a Helen Keller quote that when one door closes another opens, but you must look for the open window rather than the closed door; she and Michelle both subscribe to the idea that what will happen will happen, a belief leaders hold but rarely discuss publicly. _[Helen Keller]_ — [2025-03-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fb8gYzg6ikg)
- **The advice to give your 35-year-old self is 'just relax, it's all going to work out', even though you would never have accepted it at the time.** (John Wu) Wu said this with a business school classmate and they laughed that neither would have listened back then. Jean describes the low-grade anxiety of feeling you should have 'made something of yourself' seven years out of school; Wu says that ambition speaks well of your character. — [2025-03-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VuBM_XODr7A)
- **Success is as much about how resilient you are as how successful you are; resilience is an acquired trait most successful people share.** (John Wu) Wu's first reflected-on failure: as a high-school achiever who always made the team, he walked on to college baseball, didn't bother checking the list, then found he was cut. Each level of the pyramid gets more competitive. — [2025-03-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VuBM_XODr7A)
- **You can't be grateful for a failure until you've reflected on it, and failure training early builds resilience for later.** The hosts note Wu reworded 'failure you're most grateful for' to 'failure I was able to reflect on'. Cherie's Stanford professors told students 'you haven't failed enough, life has been too easy for you'; David Shim earlier called this failure training. _[Stanford GSB, David Shim]_ — [2025-03-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VuBM_XODr7A)
- **As a kid, test boundaries when no one is watching; as an adult, use the belief system you built from that testing and go back to first principles.** (John Wu) Wu was a rule follower around his parents and a rule breaker otherwise. The hosts pattern-match this with Vu Tran's 'always question your constraints' and argue a belief system should stay dynamic, which takes open-mindedness and neuroplasticity most people find scary. _[Vu Tran]_ — [2025-03-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VuBM_XODr7A)
- **Both people in an argument can be right; holding two opposing thoughts without reconciling them is a sign of cognitive maturity.** Cherie's ex told her in 2020 'we can both be right', which undid the immigrant-family instinct that debate must produce one winner. She gives the example of believing in God and in evolution simultaneously. — [2025-03-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VuBM_XODr7A)
- **Cognitive behavioral therapy beats plain talk therapy because it gives you evergreen tools and frameworks you can use in real time for the rest of your life.** Jean prefers directive therapists who assign homework and cite studies over the movie-style 'how did that make you feel'; she wants the PhD to share the knowledge rather than ask what she thinks. Cherie, by contrast, has only done talk therapy and developed her own bespoke frameworks and pattern recognition. _[Cognitive behavioral therapy]_ — [2025-03-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=temJGDdDgwM)
- **To stop catastrophizing, project the scenario forward and name the best case, the worst case and the most likely case, which drains the power of 'what if'.** A CBT technique Jean learned over ten years ago and still applies; you realize the outcome will probably be middle of the road rather than either extreme. _[Cognitive behavioral therapy]_ — [2025-03-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=temJGDdDgwM)
- **Spiritual health means feeling connected to something larger than yourself, and it is a spectrum from faith to mantras and manifesting that need not involve religion.** Jean finds faith most beautiful when it is a one-on-one relationship with God divorced from an institution, and dislikes religion used as an excuse for bad behavior; Cherie prays daily for wisdom and patience and jokes that God then sends tests of both. — [2025-03-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=temJGDdDgwM)
- **Build mantras by taking your biggest insecurity and flipping it into its positive opposite, then repeat it to rewire your brain's operating system.** Stanford GSB professor Joel Peterson, now 77, derived his mantras from where he struggled most: 'It's not about me' to counter self-centeredness at the height of his career, 'I am not my emotions' because he ran hot, plus 'I have everything I need' and 'I am grateful'. Cherie copied all four and writes them in her journal repeatedly whenever she feels overwhelmed until she feels centered. _[Joel Peterson, Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-03-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=temJGDdDgwM)
- **The smartest people are the least afraid to say they don't know, and they acknowledge there's always more to learn even when they're billionaire investors.** Jeremy Liew prefaced his hot take on nationalizing AI investment with 'I have a lot more research to do' and called it a strong opinion weakly held, which the hosts tie to the 'max dumb' concept. _[Jeremy Liew]_ — [2025-03-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=I04_Nc8odHw)
- **Fear of looking dumb in front of peers holds you back in class, business school and the workplace; if you don't speak up you only hurt yourself, and others usually share your question.** Studying computer science at Columbia in male-dominated, cutthroat classes, Cherie wouldn't raise her hand despite professors being kind one-on-one; this became her 'villain origin story' as president of Columbia Women in Computer Science, building a psychologically safe community with conferences and networking events. _[Columbia Women in Computer Science, Columbia University]_ — [2025-03-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=I04_Nc8odHw)
- **Emotional self-regulation, never getting too excited or too depressed, is a source of power for long-term success because failures don't drag you down.** Imran Khan attributes this trait to his success riding roller coasters as an investor on massive deals and as Snapchat's chief strategy officer through the IPO. _[Imran Khan, Snapchat]_ — [2025-03-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=I04_Nc8odHw)
- **A college scarcity mindset carried into a high-paying first job creates fear-driven paralysis around money, and the cure is running the numbers once.** Cherie was paralyzed for about five months after graduating, agonizing over a $30 Uber despite a big salary, and discussed it in therapy; the fix was calculating how much she could spend, save and put into her 401(k). Jean was money-avoidant too but was saved by a lucrative job and 90-hour weeks that left no time to spend. — [2025-04-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e9C6UDbAIdw)
- **Reframe risk as 'what's the best that can happen': if the new role fails you can go back to what you did before, but if it succeeds you learn an entire new business.** (Leah Wyar) Leah told herself that if she didn't succeed in entertainment she could return to beauty; the hosts relate this to their own leap from tech into podcasting and a physical product (Sisters Matcha). _[Sisters Matcha]_ — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **Don't let anyone weaponize you and take the emotion out: when your CEO dislikes something, he dislikes what's on paper, not you; feedback is meant to make you better even when delivered badly.** (Leah Wyar) After 25 years working she still practices this daily. Compartmentalize the energy, box it up on a shelf, find the fixable core of the feedback, and don't treat it as a crystal ball saying you don't belong. — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **Growth mindset is not a personality type; saying 'I'm not a growth mindset person' contradicts the concept, because anyone with a fixed mindset can choose to adopt a growth mindset.** Carol Dweck (Stanford) contrasts the fixed mindset ('I'm either good at it or not') with the growth mindset ('with effort and perseverance I can figure it out'); growth-minded people route around obstacles while fixed-minded people see each obstacle as proof something can't happen. _[Carol Dweck, Stanford]_ — [2025-05-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xPAIT2MpsuY)
- **Doing one very hard thing roots a growth mindset in your body so that every later challenge feels solvable.** Cherie's grueling computer science major, with all-nighters and seeking every resource, was the hardest thing she has done and left her feeling she can do anything. — [2025-05-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xPAIT2MpsuY)
- **Growth mindset exercise: identify a limiting belief and rewrite it as a mantra, 'manifestation but better'.** Stanford professor Joel Peterson believed he let his emotions control him and turned it into the mantra 'I am not my emotions'. _[Joel Peterson, Stanford]_ — [2025-05-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xPAIT2MpsuY)
- **Reframe nervousness as excitement before a high-stakes presentation; the physical sensations are the same and the label changes the experience.** Evan Spiegel gave Cherie this tip; Jean learned the same nervous-to-excited reframe in a business school executive presence class and notes studies support it. _[Evan Spiegel]_ — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **Do a quick burst of cardio (jumping jacks, push-ups, jumping in place) right before presenting to align your racing heart with your body.** Credited to Jean's former boss Eric Wellenbacher; she no longer power-poses. Public speaking is feared more than death, and matching physical motion to the elevated heart rate makes her feel focused. _[Eric Wellenbacher]_ — [2025-05-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=geh8gKByS08)
- **Whenever you feel stressed, ask one foundational question: what can I do to change it? If nothing, stop stressing; if something, start small.** (Kofi Ampadu) Kofi says this protects his mental health, since stressing about something you cannot change only damages you; most actions that matter start small and minute. — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **Anything worth having is hard now, easy later, so optimize for the long term.** (Kofi Ampadu) Going to the gym and waking early suck at first with no visible results; once results show, the habit becomes easy. Lasting change means accepting that things get harder in the short term. — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **Make space for failure by time-blocking your wallowing: one day on the couch, then move forward.** (Kofi Ampadu) Expecting 100 percent happy days is a delusion; excuses are the cheapest thing to buy and spiraling is easy, but unless it is an extreme case like a job loss, a miss usually leaves you in the same spot you were in, so just reconfigure and continue. — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **Failure is healthy: if you are not failing you are not trying hard enough, and aiming for perfection is why large companies never do anything worthwhile.** (Kofi Ampadu) Kids learning basketball are told to go faster; adults wait for the perfect moment. Kofi cites Adam Grant's finding that the best language learners start speaking on day one and embarrass themselves, which he is applying to learning French. Cherie admits she must be honest when scoring her days rather than stretching the metric. _[Adam Grant]_ — [2025-06-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=SYjFmv6vZtE)
- **Ask the dumb questions; assuming is dangerous and pretending to know makes you a 'midwit' who makes wrong decisions.** Goldman rule 9. Cherie grew up scared to question authority and held her tongue in high school and college classrooms, but over the last decade adopted 'go max dumb', a phrase from Season 3 guest Vu Tran; the right people respect a smart person asking basic questions. _[Vu Tran, Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-06-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eFpgcrlf2gk)
- **Earning a million before 30 is delusional as a benchmark; beyond a point, extra income adds negligible happiness and everyone always wants more.** (Ryan Edlifson) Ryan received a rent eviction notice at 28 and lived on $2,000 a month, and says he was as happy then as now earning far more; contentment with current income matters more than chasing the next dollar. — [2025-06-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YikViCdKtDQ)
- **Reframe every breakup, rejection or lost opportunity as 'what did I learn about myself?' to make it less devastating and stay in an abundance mindset.** Cherie's tactical practice going into a date or relationship; Jean generalizes it to all of life since you always gain something regardless of outcome. — [2025-06-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DubkuAz2meM)
- **It's actually not about you: not taking rejection personally in dating, friendships and careers is incredibly freeing.** Jean says as an eldest daughter, type A, parentified people-pleaser she took everything personally, including a promotion she was told she deserved and did not get. Her exercise: when feeling bad, put yourself in the other person's shoes and ask what reason they had that has nothing to do with you, like a driver cutting you off to reach their kids. — [2025-06-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DubkuAz2meM)
- **Anything hard, whether a breakup, leaving a job or a marathon, sucks first and then life gets better; the near-term suck is why people avoid challenging things.** Cherie cites a chart from her Stanford GSB professor Graham Weaver showing life as a series of dips that trend up and to the right when you do hard things. _[Graham Weaver, Stanford Graduate School of Business]_ — [2025-06-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DubkuAz2meM)
- **Trust your intuition: a niggling discomfort or stomach problem is your body telling you something your brain refuses to recognize.** Jean says she squashed that feeling many times in her 20s and 30s; her suggestion is to notice when and around whom you feel off. She predicts science on the gut-brain axis and microbiome will validate intuition within 20-30 years. — [2025-06-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=DubkuAz2meM)
- **Sharing an individual accomplishment stops feeling cringe when you frame it as what was hard and what you learned so it is useful to others.** Jean says group wins were easy to promote because she wanted her team credited, but solo wins felt unnatural until she reframed them as context that helps people; everyone likes a helpful teammate. — [2025-06-30](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8E164swLCxo)
- **Early adversity is a huge advantage, not something to begrudge.** (Fernando) Fernando grew up in complex circumstances on the South Texas/northern Mexico border, lost his father at 12, and says those conditions were a real advantage that shaped his drive. _[Forbes Billionaire list]_ — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **Most constraints on what you can achieve are self-imposed; in a $30 trillion economy there are almost infinite pockets where wealth can be created.** (Fernando) He says you need the stomach for it, belief in your own ability, and the ability to provide value to others; if what you do provides value, you get rewarded. — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **Everybody has fear; understand it, compartmentalize it, then leverage it and lean into it with an aggressive, almost resentful energy.** (Fernando) Real estate was comfortable but software and engineering scared him when starting Crexi; he says people who succeed go right at their weakness head-on, and that it is not a soft feeling but an aggressive one. — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **The mind must be trained like a body; if you are comfortable, you are not doing it right.** (Fernando) The hosts compare it to Batman training in The Dark Knight; Fernando says self-awareness of your soft spots does not come naturally and requires suffering through deliberate training. _[The Dark Knight, Batman Begins]_ — [2025-07-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0KKNxVYHcy8)
- **Practice conviction over consensus: identify what you deeply believe that others don't, and live a contrarian life on it.** Both hosts left highly successful corporate tracks to be creators, and Cherie counts being unmarried and child-free in her 30s as contrarian choices. — [2025-07-14](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bu50AgK6UIw)
- **Make the habit part of your identity: ask 'what would a healthy person do?' because an embraced identity is harder to break than a goal.** Saying 'I am an active person, that's why I go to the gym three times a week' is presented as the core premise of Atomic Habits. _[James Clear, Atomic Habits]_ — [2025-07-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=McWujpZMzLw)
- **Nearly half of daily actions are habitual, so building the right habits is high-leverage.** The hosts cite Duke University research finding 45% of daily actions are habitual, and assign a mini exercise: pick one goal, break it into tiny habits and write it down. _[Duke University]_ — [2025-07-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=McWujpZMzLw)
- **You can be vulnerable about inexperience without losing confidence by anchoring on self-belief: 'I'm not an expert here, but I'm an expert elsewhere and I can learn anything.'** Jean describes this as inherent to her approach; Cherie jokes it sounds like an interview answer, and both warn never to tell a recruiter your weakness is perfectionism because they see through it. — [2025-07-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=McWujpZMzLw)
- **Other people can see you more clearly than you see yourself, so say out loud about yourself what respected friends say about your change.** Jean's bonus exercise: when processing a reinvention, borrow the words others use ('you're so courageous') and claim that identity for yourself. — [2025-07-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=McWujpZMzLw)
- **The clarity you're seeking is probably hiding behind one very simple test, and if you wait for the perfect moment someone scrappier will beat you.** The mini exercise: pick an untackled problem and test it today with a Google Form, an Instagram post or a text message. — [2025-08-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NLxFGD_WaBw)
- **The Let Them theory: let people do, believe and behave however they want instead of spending your energy managing their decisions, interrupting the rumination loop and freeing time to control your own life.** The hosts cite studies that monitoring other people's feelings raises your stress and cortisol by almost 30% (the 'emotional labor tax'). It is powerful because it's radical: you put the thing you've obsessed over in a box and throw it away. _[Mel Robbins, The Let Them Theory]_ — [2025-08-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H-xnPI_rUqk)
- **Don't take 'Let Me' too literally as a lone-wolf approach: the best things are built with others, so invite collaboration, help and resources.** The hosts reframe it as 'let me and all the people who want to help me.' — [2025-08-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H-xnPI_rUqk)
- **Icebox exercise: keep a note (icebox, parking lot or backlog) and write down every urge to jump into drama instead of responding immediately, to build the Let Them muscle.** It buys back bandwidth, cools emotions, and becomes a 'graveyard' of things that felt urgent but didn't matter when you review it weeks later, training you to be less reactive. In heated text exchanges, waiting is better 9 times out of 10, and getting on the phone beats texting. — [2025-08-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=H-xnPI_rUqk)
- **Value drift is the slow disconnection from what matters to you, showing up as burnout, resentment and loss of identity before you notice the cause.** Settling is a steady drift rather than a dramatic event, so people don't realize they're there until they are. _[value drift]_ — [2025-08-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QUN5LWuRwu0)
- **High achievers rationalize settling with lines like 'I'm lucky to have this', 'it's not that bad' and 'everyone would kill for this job or relationship'.** Hearing yourself say these is a sign you are settling and not admitting it; shiny, Instagrammable exteriors make it harder to let go because of the dissonance between looking good and feeling good. — [2025-08-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QUN5LWuRwu0)
- **Complete the sentence 'I'm scared to walk away because...' to reveal where you've tied your worth.** Naming and labeling the fear makes it easier to see whether you are actually settling. — [2025-08-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QUN5LWuRwu0)
- **Cognitive dissonance, the distress your body signals when actions and values misalign, explains the irritability and anxiety of staying in the wrong job or relationship.** The concept came from a 1957 Stanford study; people stay too long because of fear of regret, sunk cost fallacy and external validation. Flip the question: if you're afraid of leaving, what if you were afraid of staying, which may be more dangerous long term? _[cognitive dissonance, sunk cost fallacy, Stanford]_ — [2025-08-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QUN5LWuRwu0)
- **Admitting to yourself that you are settling is often the hardest step and can take years; do that first, then come back to the exercises.** Cherie plays 'good cop', suggesting listeners bookmark the episode and return once the realization has settled in their body. — [2025-08-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=QUN5LWuRwu0)
- **Billion-dollar companies use expected value rather than gut instinct, and you can borrow the same math for personal decisions.** Netflix uses it to greenlight shows, VC firms to pick investments, and Amazon for pricing models; 'gut instinct is cute, but billion-dollar companies use math'. _[Netflix, Amazon]_ — [2025-08-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IyFtCATnRpA)
- **Staying stuck is worse than being wrong; a decision framework lets you be wrong safely with the most informed choice available.** People delay decisions from fear of looking wrong. Cherie cites LinkedIn head of product Tomer Cohen's saying 'I may be wrong, but I'm not confused', which Jean adapts to 'I may be wrong, but I have clarity'. _[Tomer Cohen, LinkedIn]_ — [2025-08-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IyFtCATnRpA)
- **Separate fact from feeling: resilience comes from creating space between stimulus and response, not from having no emotions.** Drawn from cognitive behavioral therapy and Susan David's HBR article 'Emotional Agility'; HBS leadership classes teach emotional regulation alongside leadership and professors say 'separate signal from noise'. _[Susan David, Emotional Agility, Harvard Business Review, Harvard Business School]_ — [2025-09-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HjebjpDU8-Q)
- **Use the prompt 'The story I'm telling myself is...' to separate perception from reality and impact from intent.** A Brené Brown tool the sisters also learned in Stanford's Interpersonal Dynamics (Touchy Feely) class, where the core lesson is separating intent from impact; writing the story down keeps you talking only about impact. _[Brené Brown, Stanford Interpersonal Dynamics]_ — [2025-09-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HjebjpDU8-Q)
- **Don't set goals, set your identity: say 'I am a writer' rather than 'I want to write', because identity drives action.** From James Clear's 'Atomic Habits'. Cases: Beyoncé's Sasha Fierce persona as mental priming for performances and interviews; Simone Biles' affirmations ('I am powerful, I am unshakable, I am the best gymnast in the world') as identity locking via neuro-linguistic programming. _[James Clear, Atomic Habits, Beyoncé, Simone Biles]_ — [2025-09-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HjebjpDU8-Q)
- **Every morning say your 'I am' statement out loud (I am a CEO, I am calm, I am unshakable) and take one action that reflects it.** Cherie plays Davina Ho's morning mantra tracks on Spotify and repeats after them for 15-20 minutes, sometimes on the drive to the gym; the sisters ask listeners to share their identities in the comments. _[Davina Ho]_ — [2025-09-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=HjebjpDU8-Q)
- **Pre-reward yourself before annoying but necessary money admin to lock in.** Cherie gets a Philz mint mojito iced coffee before tackling time-consuming financial tasks so the caffeine helps her focus. _[Philz Coffee]_ — [2025-09-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_L_wX5RMxh4)
- **Doubt is the most nefarious D: women are 29% more likely than men to say they don't feel investment-ready, and self-limiting beliefs cap what you can achieve.** Vanguard data; a Stanford GSB study shows women consistently under-apply for stretch roles, so they miss promotions and raises and forfeit over half a million dollars in lifetime earnings. _[Vanguard, Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-09-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_L_wX5RMxh4)
- **Everyone toggles between three money stories, survival, victim and creator, and victim is the sneakiest because you have the resources but hold yourself back.** Survival is 'I just need to make rent'; victim is 'money controls me, the system is rigged' (e.g., avoiding downloading a banking app or making a budget); creator is 'money is a tool I deploy.' You can be in different stories for rent, food and travel at once. _[money story framework]_ — [2025-09-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_L_wX5RMxh4)
- **Adopt the creator mantra 'money is a tool I deploy' and celebrate progress rather than demanding perfection.** Repeating it makes money feel like a tool at your disposal rather than a boogeyman; the mini exercise is to comment which story you are in now, by category, to feel less alone. — [2025-09-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_L_wX5RMxh4)
- **Acknowledging and confronting where you are is the first step to changing your money behavior.** The hosts say they have both been in survival and victim stories and drift between them across areas of life, and invite listeners to 'confront yourself with us.' — [2025-09-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_L_wX5RMxh4)
- **Being good at something is dangerous because satisfaction masks stagnation; like a knife that only chops the same vegetables, you go dull without new challenges.** Reaching the zone of excellence is a worthy goal that takes years of honing, but once you have been there a long time it is time to break out and evolve. — [2025-09-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TkXvlgPXitc)
- **Audit your own attraction and dynamic patterns, and break the ones that have not led to fulfillment.** Cherie has two types, the stoic hot guy and the charismatic center-of-attention guy, and the second has not worked out; mini exercise: write down your last two or three relationship patterns and the early signs you ignored. _[Dating 101 episode / The Bachelor]_ — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **Your attention, time, energy and love are your most valuable resources, so the opportunity cost of giving them to the wrong person, including rumination, is high.** Time not spent on that person would go into growing yourself; someone living in your head rent-free is still consuming attention. — [2025-10-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jxzfgQG1BP4)
- **You cannot shame people for trying; if you do, you are the loser.** (Maggie Sellers) Her husband went out to play professional football on a national stage last year, risking public ridicule; even the most successful people keep moving the goalposts and reinvesting in themselves. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **Daily journaling and 'love notes' to yourself are how you rewire a toxic brain that assumes people are mad at you.** (Maggie Sellers) Before every Instagram post she thinks 'people don't like me, why would they want to see this'; therapy once a week is like a trainer without daily exercise, so the daily practice is what changes the pattern. She now posts the love notes publicly to take back the narrative. _[HSR love notes]_ — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **Keep old journals so you can see how far you have come, and reframe stress as 'it is a privilege to be overwhelmed'.** (Maggie Sellers) Rereading her journal from when she called off her engagement made her appreciate that she now lives what she once dreamed of, even though the goalpost keeps moving. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **The next phase of growth is doing things for internal gratification rather than others' affirmation, though almost everyone successful is still motivated by something.** (Maggie Sellers) Maggie admits she is motivated by proving people wrong and by others' approval (citing Scott Galloway being open about money and affirmation as motivators); Lucy Guo's motivation evolved from proving her tiger parents wrong, to money and status, to solving hard problems. _[Scott Galloway, Lucy Guo]_ — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **Sabotage like tearing pages out of shared textbooks shows how desperate students become when they feel they must withhold resources from peers just to survive.** The hosts call it demonic behavior but note it means the person was pushed to the limit; it is dark for that person and cruel to everyone else, especially those who then cannot pass the weeder classes. _[Wharton]_ — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **When you feel like you are drowning, float first and swim later: stop thrashing, breathe, grab onto someone, and zoom out.** Pick one thing you can control today as your anchor; take a 20-minute nap or walk to reset your nervous system because rest is essential, not wasted; community is the best flotation device; and remember you are building an entire life, not passing a single test. The framework applies to a first job as much as school. — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **Not getting the prestige job can feel like the end of the world because young people define themselves by school and catastrophize without a five- or ten-year perspective.** Jean recalls feeling like the biggest failure for not landing a Goldman internship after years of hard classes and jobs; 'my life is over' is a dangerous mindset that the Ivy pressure can produce. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **Beholderism, caring deeply about how things look including yourself, is often a proxy for wanting harmony and control in a chaotic world, and it is the top value of almost every influencer.** (Suzy Welch) Jean's top value is beholderism; she read interior design books at 12 and moves flowers that clash with a room. Welch says no value is good or bad, and that naming it lets you explain yourself to others. _[Architectural Digest]_ — [2025-11-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eRJyWu1CgI0)
- **Values are mostly stable from about age 25, but a seismic life event such as a death, divorce, child or broken engagement can reshuffle them temporarily.** (Suzy Welch) Welch's belovedness fell to 16 after her husband died; Jean's is at 11 a year after ending an eight-year engagement, which Welch expects to move back up within six to twelve months. — [2025-11-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eRJyWu1CgI0)
- **When you are living your own life you feel 'exquisitely alive', and that internal signal, not friends and parents checking boxes, is the real test.** (Suzy Welch) The outside world confirms your life is perfect while you know inside; if you have never felt that aliveness you do not even know what you are missing. — [2025-11-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eRJyWu1CgI0)
- **Social media makes you compare yourself against lives built on values you do not actually hold; check the envy against your own rank order.** Cherie feels a pang seeing 20-person bachelorette parties but belonging is her lowest value; Welch's high-radius daughter is having a wedding with no bridesmaids and no parties because that is not who they are. — [2025-11-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=eRJyWu1CgI0)
- **Hard conversations get easier with trial and error, and repeating them builds self-confidence.** Money, power and love problems can be broken into strategies and frameworks; it's not about being perfect the first time. — [2025-11-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e2AV_kUtiVs)
- **Imposter syndrome is healthy and is what drives most top athletes and entertainers, but you should check whether it comes from 'not enough' or insatiable desire, both of which are vices.** (Paul Rabil) Coaches wove it into sports culture ('they're better than us, this is a trap game'); the imaginary rival in West Texas working harder gives you the last rep. Paul carried the judgment of being paid $15,000 at the top of pro lacrosse while NBA/NFL/MLB peers got rewarded, and that followed him into business rooms. — [2025-11-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=CU74FCSbrRE)
- **Women apologize more not because they are more sorry but because they are conditioned into constant self-monitoring, starting with being rewarded for being 'conscientious' as girls.** Being praised for conscientiousness on report cards turns into lifelong self-vigilance; men apologize less simply because they don't consider themselves to have done anything wrong. — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **Stop couching your ambitions with 'this might sound crazy' or 'I know this sounds cheesy'; say the big goal plainly and own wins out loud.** Both sisters catch themselves prefacing Tiger Sisters' mission this way, partly out of fear it won't work; Jean points out they hit number one business podcast in the US within a year from nothing, which is proof the ambition isn't crazy. Exercise: ask where you minimize ambition to make others comfortable and name one thing you're proud of that you haven't owned out loud. — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **Toxic gratitude is when 'I should just be grateful to have this job' becomes a shield for fear or unworthiness, disguised as humility.** It sounds like 'they didn't have to pick me, I'll take whatever I can get'; Jean stayed in a dream job far too long, using gratitude to paper over things that were deeply wrong, and Cherie compares it to sunk cost reasoning. — [2025-11-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FqVBuNxYDRI)
- **Achievement is often a trauma response: people with perfect resumes frequently come from histories of trauma and achieve because they feel they are not enough.** (Jane Marie Chen) Chen sees this especially among entrepreneurs and social entrepreneurs; no number of degrees, accolades or babies saved could fill a void she could not articulate, which led her to burn out. _[Embrace]_ — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)
- **When you rely on external validation the goalposts keep moving; everything you seek externally you have to find internally.** (Jane Marie Chen) Her overachiever part was protecting a very young part that never felt worthy; she spent most of her life wanting the world to prove her worth through achievements. — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)
- **Pressure from family sacrifice creates healthy drive until it tips into needing to prove, and you must become aware of which motivation is operating.** (Jane Marie Chen) Her mission with Embrace was healthy purpose and simultaneously a shadow side of proving she was OK; 'my pain became my purpose' had both sides at once. _[Embrace]_ — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)
- **Parts work (Internal Family Systems) means recognizing your overachiever, perfectionist, inner-critic parts as protectors and treating each with compassion rather than banishing them.** (Jane Marie Chen) Protector parts guard the parts carrying shame, rejection and abandonment; an ex nicknamed her warrior part Genghis Khan. She recommends the book No Bad Parts and a free parts-work exercise on her website. _[Internal Family Systems, No Bad Parts]_ — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)
- **Say the words you wished a parent had said directly to your inner child; it only lands when it comes from yourself.** (Jane Marie Chen) At a van der Kolk psychodrama retreat she recruited a Santa-looking man and then a friend to play her ideal father reading scripted lines and felt nothing; reading the same lines to a photo of herself at five on her desk finally moved her. _[Bessel van der Kolk, psychodrama]_ — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)
- **Control is an illusion; give something your all and then surrender the outcome, and define success as living your values rather than external outcomes.** (Jane Marie Chen) The minute she stopped trying to control Embrace, Tony Robbins appeared out of nowhere and saved the company; she threw her single advance copy of her book into the ocean in Honolulu as a ceremony of releasing it to the universe. _[Tony Robbins, Embrace, Like a Wave We Break]_ — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)
- **Over-optimizing every situation leaves no room for serendipity that may be beyond your imagination.** Jean notes high achievers try to make everything as good as possible, but the best outcome may be something you could not have pictured, so some things must be out of your control. — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)
- **Self-compassion is a rubber coating around the glass ball of your identity so failures bounce rather than shatter you; you are worth more than the sum of your achievements.** Cherie's image of untangling achievement from identity; Chen worries younger people let a post's like count destroy their sense of self. — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)
- **You can't control what happens around you, but you always have agency over how you respond, so refuse to be a victim of your circumstances.** (Jim Keyes) He couldn't change being born into a 'screwed up situation' but chose not to let others determine who he would be. The hosts frame it as being evolved enough to accept the world while exercising self-determination. — [2025-12-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wondntTp_cQ)
- **Confidence is a prerequisite for changing yourself, and it isn't innate: anyone can build it by doing the work.** (Jim Keyes) Most people fall down by believing they aren't smart or good enough, often because they listen to others; learning is the path to accomplishing virtually anything. — [2025-12-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wondntTp_cQ)
- **The three-word recipe for confidence is 'prepare to win': the more prepared you are, the more confident you are and the better your odds.** (Jim Keyes) Given to him by Harvey Mackay, author of 'Swim with the Sharks Without Being Eaten Alive'. Tom Brady wasn't born confident; he studied game tape until he could walk on the field with absolute confidence. Pilots aren't nervous in turbulence because of simulator hours. Being more prepared than the other person is a way to take agency. _[Harvey Mackay, Swim with the Sharks Without Being Eaten Alive, Tom Brady]_ — [2025-12-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wondntTp_cQ)
- **Building confidence isn't tricking your brain, it's using neuroplasticity to reprogram preset patterns, including fear, with new ones.** (Jim Keyes) Applied to love: people revert to the same bad relationship patterns; learning to respond with agency instead of reacting to a partner may be the answer to a happy life. Cherie adds that imagining yourself winning in your mind's eye is powerful. — [2025-12-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wondntTp_cQ)
- **Relentless positivity, as opposed to toxic or faked positivity, keeps you focused on what you want in an already negative world.** Cherie says she naturally skews positive; Jim agrees that genuine positivity comes from blocking out noise, while faked positivity is toxic because it isn't from within. — [2025-12-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wondntTp_cQ)
- **Americans aren't getting dumber but lazier: easy access to information lets perception (taking things at face value) replace perspective (critical thinking).** (Jim Keyes) Critical thinking is what separates humans from other species and should be used to determine truth rather than accepting what you read or see on the news. — [2025-12-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wondntTp_cQ)
- **High achievers who try to control every outcome leave no room for serendipity; keep imagining things bigger than you can plan a ladder to.** Jean kept joking 'wait until they see me on a billboard' and in 2025 Spotify put Tiger Sisters on a digital billboard at Penn Station as one of about five US RADAR podcast creators. Cherie says it was not even on her vision board and the lesson is she needs to dream bigger. _[Spotify RADAR, Penn Station billboard]_ — [2025-12-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=M4HYavwtWzY)
- **The neutral zone feels slow day to day but is where things actually get built.** Jean, an impatient go-go-go person, says the reflection period felt long but small daily reflection compounded; exercise: name one ending, how you feel, and one possibility that could bloom if you embrace the neutral zone. _[William Bridges]_ — [2025-12-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NmdHxviASBg)
- **Growth mindset is a mindset, not a personality type; saying 'I'm not a growth-mindset person' contradicts the concept.** Anyone with a long-held fixed mindset can adopt a growth mindset; fixed-mindset people see obstacles as proof something can't happen, growth-mindset people see something to go around. _[Carol Dweck, Stanford]_ — [2025-12-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NmdHxviASBg)
- **Doing one very hard thing gives you a body-deep belief that you can figure out anything.** Cherie says surviving a grueling computer science major with all-nighters rooted her growth mindset so deeply that any later challenge feels figure-out-able. — [2025-12-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NmdHxviASBg)
- **Rewrite a limiting belief as a mantra, which is 'manifestation but better'.** Stanford professor Joel Peterson turned his belief that he was overly emotional into the mantra 'I am not my emotions'; the exercise is to identify one limiting belief and rewrite it with a growth-mindset approach. _[Joel Peterson, Stanford]_ — [2025-12-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NmdHxviASBg)
- **Reframe 'I'm scared' as 'I'm excited' since humans are change-averse but forced change can lead to beautiful things.** The hosts say change forced on you is uncomfortable but with frameworks in place it can be taken in stride. — [2025-12-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NmdHxviASBg)
- **Say out loud about yourself what respected people say about you, because others can see you more clearly than you see yourself.** Jean reframes 'I'm someone who breaks engagements' into 'I'm brave enough to leave a perfect-on-paper 8-year relationship for something better,' saying it for the first time on air after hearing it from others. Official exercise: write the old story, then rewrite it to reflect growth and aspirations. _[Esther Perel]_ — [2025-12-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NmdHxviASBg)
- **When someone sees something in you that you do not see in yourself, trust it and at least try, asking 'what's the best that can happen?' instead of the worst.** (Leah Wyar) Leah says she would never have raised her hand for the entertainment role; her boss saw it for her. The hosts connect this to their own leap from tech into a podcast and physical product with Sisters Matcha. _[Sisters Matcha]_ — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)
- **Take the emotion out of feedback in the room: the CEO not liking your work is not him not liking you, so find the fixable core and fix it, then process the feelings outside with work wives, husband and therapist.** (Leah Wyar) After 25 years Leah still has to practice this. Her tactic is to box the energy and put it on a shelf, walk in stone-faced, take it in, walk out and go on a 'rage walk.' Having an outlet where all feelings are allowed is what makes it possible to show up unemotionally and execute. — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)

### Health, wellness & burnout (50)

- **Set a concrete, trackable health challenge such as eating 40 different fruits and vegetables a week to make eating intentional.** Cherie learned the 40-plants-a-week gut-health target from a Delta in-flight masterclass and writes down each qualifying whole food on paper weekly; even without hitting 40, the tracking makes her more mindful and creative in cooking. Herbs, garlic and ginger count. — [2025-01-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_8AlNMC2l4)
- **Get annual bloodwork done and act on the results with targeted supplements.** Both sisters got 2025 blood tests showing genetically high cholesterol and borderline vitamin D deficiency; Cherie now takes vitamin D and C, which she says matters even more during wildfire smoke exposure. — [2025-01-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_8AlNMC2l4)
- **Alarming US health news is usually not doomsday but a delayed catch-up to regulations other countries adopted years earlier.** Jean argues the FDA red-dye ban and similar stories reflect ingredients already banned in Europe roughly a decade ago; she deliberately buys drugstore skincare and consumables from European, Japanese and Korean brands because those markets have stricter regulation. _[FDA]_ — [2025-01-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_8AlNMC2l4)
- **Structural or urban burn is not a normal wildfire, and the AQI you see on your phone does not capture its hazards.** Many burned houses predate the 1984 asbestos ban, and burned EVs, TVs and laptops release chemicals not measured by AQI; PM2.5 particles were spread by Santa Ana winds of 80-100 mph. An N95 handles brush smoke but nobody knows what is in urban burn. _[AQI, Santa Ana winds]_ — [2025-01-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_8AlNMC2l4)
- **Escapist, well-crafted TV is a legitimate tool to turn your brain off during emotionally heavy news cycles.** They recommend Yellowstone ('cowboy Succession' with nuanced Native American storylines and beautiful cinematography), Love Island All Stars and The Traitors as low-effort relief, and Severance and Black Mirror (episodes Hang the DJ, USS Callister, White Christmas) for thought-provoking viewing. _[Yellowstone, Kevin Costner, Taylor Sheridan, Succession, Love Island, The Traitors, Severance, Black Mirror]_ — [2025-01-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=b_8AlNMC2l4)
- **When you are in a winter funk, push yourself gently to do activities while not being too hard on yourself.** Jean describes a bout of seasonal affective disorder (worsened by the LA fires and pneumonia), the temptation to nest and do nothing, and almost skipping the trip that ended up being the most rejuvenating thing she did; a bright 'happy lamp' also helps. _[seasonal affective disorder]_ — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **Egg freezing deserves its own serious conversation and is one of Cherie's biggest regrets not having done earlier.** Cherie mentions lacking corporate funding for it; Jean says she has heard different advice and will explain next episode why Cherie need not be regretful. — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **Not freezing eggs while at a corporate job with coverage is one of Cherie's biggest regrets, though the medical guidance five years ago discouraged it for women in their 20s and 30s expecting kids by their mid-30s.** A 24-year-old Microsoft PM friend froze hers before Wharton, which impressed Cherie; Jean says OBGYNs and fertility doctors once discouraged it, but the sentiment has shifted toward preventive freezing, partly driven by fertility startups' marketing. _[Microsoft, Wharton]_ — [2025-02-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaMVodL23LE)
- **Egg freezing is grueling and like a mini pregnancy: self-injected hormones, patches, daily blood draws, mood and weight changes, and the burden falls entirely on the woman.** Jean and her partner botched the first expensive dose while mixing it at home; she references Ronny Chieng's Netflix special about helping his wife inject hormones. _[Ronny Chieng, Netflix]_ — [2025-02-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RaMVodL23LE)
- **Find your pause: build a daily, weekly or longer break from email, text and social media, because pausing is the only way out of the cycles society pulls you into.** (Jonah Goodhart) Jonah's advice to his 35-year-old self; he cites the religious Sabbath as the original model of stepping out of participation for a period, and Jean notes millennials are now baking sabbaticals into career plans. — [2025-03-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UYtMgkz8brA)
- **The 'happiness quotient' may matter more for longevity than diet or exercise, so figure out what makes you happy rather than aiming to retire.** (Jonah Goodhart) A concept from Jonah's oldest brother: Charlie Munger lived to 99 and Warren Buffett is still working on fast food and soda, while some perfectly fit people die early or are unhappy; the difference is they figured out what drove them. _[Warren Buffett, Charlie Munger]_ — [2025-03-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UYtMgkz8brA)
- **Distracted rest (TV while multitasking) is not pausing; true rest is disconnected, like walks, watercoloring or jewelry making.** Jean admits her wind-down is Love Island while still working; Cherie suggests trading connected rest for off-Wi-Fi rest, and the sisters consider instituting a weekly day of pause since they currently work every day. _[Love Island]_ — [2025-03-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UYtMgkz8brA)
- **Your relationship with your body is made of three elements: genetics (uncontrollable), upbringing (mostly uncontrollable but can be mitigated), and your everyday routine (controllable).** The sisters grew up with a single mom, lots of microwave dinners and Hot Pockets, no desserts except orange juice and fruit, rice rather than bread, and no pressure about weight, which shaped what they crave today. — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **Weight gain usually comes from unnoticed shifts in eating patterns when your environment changes, such as starting college or a new job.** Jean gained weight in college eating a full panini for lunch, a big sushi roll for dinner, Egg McMuffins and unlimited desserts; Cherie gained 15-20 pounds in junior year of high school from daily salami sandwiches during the inactive winter months and lost it by switching to filling salads with tuna or vegetables. — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **A lot of women have disordered eating that passes as normal because diet culture is so popularized; counting every calorie and weighing yourself daily is not actually normal.** Jean's first restrictive phase, calorie counting and daily weigh-ins, started after her college weight gain; she later did three-day juice cleanses with coworkers every few months and once packed six bottles of juice into her ski bag for a Beaver Creek trip, which she now calls dumb. — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **Diet alone will not make you healthy: two years of eating only Just Salad at Goldman while working 90-100 hour weeks and sleeping four to five hours still led to weight gain.** Goldman's 200 West building was designed so you never leave (cafeteria, doctor's office, gym with laundered gray t-shirts and shorts, competitive heart-rate spin classes), but a 45-minute workout does not compensate for a desk-bound body, terrible sleep and weekend binge drinking. _[Goldman Sachs, Just Salad, Invesco]_ — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **Health rests on four pillars, diet, exercise, sleep and stress/cortisol, and if all are bad at once you should pick one to work on rather than tackling everything.** There is no silver bullet; at different points in life all, none or one of the pillars are working. Set an intention on one pillar at a time and accept that the routine for each area does not have to stay the same for your whole life. — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **Crazed fitness kicks are not sustainable and are how burnout happens; the goal is a routine that fits your life today and is repeatable.** Jean's Goldman associate Alexander Cochran tried SoulCycle two-a-days across the street from the office in 2010 and lasted a week. Jean's sustainable version came at Zynga, where she ran a few miles every morning before showering and eating breakfast at her desk. _[SoulCycle, Zynga]_ — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **Gamify consistency with a stretch challenge you can feel good about even if you miss it, like trying to go to the gym 30 days in a row and celebrating a 10-day personal record.** Cherie has never hit 30 but is the most consistent she has ever been; Jean argues for baking in flexibility (e.g. five rest days within 30) so the goal is actually reachable. The test is whether the goal still lets you feel successful when you fall short. — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **Stop outsourcing how you feel to the scale; judge your body qualitatively and intuitively, and accept a winter weight and a summer weight.** Jean stopped weighing herself after her scale's batteries died a year ago and never replaced them; her daily mood used to depend on the number. Activity and daylight differ by season, so a single year-round goal weight does not make sense. — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **Buy clothes you love in two sizes, and stop fixating on being a size 26 when you look and feel better in a 27 or 28 at different times of the year or your cycle.** One of Jean's best friends buys Reformation dresses in two sizes; the hosts say if you have the expendable income you should do this for everything you love. _[Reformation]_ — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **Pick workouts suited to your body and your current phase of life rather than what is trendy; HIIT and Barry's are not for everyone.** Jean felt nauseous and lightheaded in HIIT classes and quit Barry's (loud music, 25-30 people, no individual attention, injury risk); both used to run 3-6 miles a day and half marathons but no longer run. Finding what works takes experimentation and can change over time. _[Barry's]_ — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **Do the hardest thing first: if getting to the gym is the hardest part of your day, go first thing in the morning, because a 5 PM plan will be put off.** Cherie admits she loves how she feels after working out but not the activity itself, and is drawn to lying in bed scrolling. Jean instead relies on an 'appointment mechanic', booking a class with a no-show strike so she cannot skip. — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **Sign up for a race (5K, 12K or half marathon) as a forcing function to train consistently, ideally with friends who hold you accountable.** Jean used half marathons to scare herself into working out until it stopped working and she winged one race day without training, which she does not recommend without a plan. — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **Feed your gut microbiome by targeting 40 different fruits, vegetables, legumes and nuts a week, as a stretch goal you do not punish yourself for missing.** Cherie picked this up from a gut-health documentary on a Delta flight; the thesis is that the gut controls sleep and attention. With Cherie cooking, the sisters hit 40 for a couple of weeks and dropped to about 30 in weeks without grocery shopping. — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **Systems, guardrails and frameworks (like the 40-plant target or morning gym time) matter because the sisters know they will default to processed food and inactivity without them.** They are candid that part of the motivation is vanity and looking good at a certain weight, but the mantra has shifted to 'strong, not skinny', which Cherie has written on her wall with 'skinny' crossed out. — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **Scandinavians are the happiest people in the world because they have low expectations, so a rare sunny day after a long winter brings real joy.** (Michelle Huang) Michelle spent two weeks across Stockholm and Copenhagen in summer and asked locals the secret; the hosts turn it into the formula happiness equals reality minus expectations, so lower expectations plus decent reality yields happiness. _[Stockholm, Copenhagen]_ — [2025-03-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fb8gYzg6ikg)
- **What got you here will not get you to the next level; ambitious type-A people must invest in mental health as much as physical health.** The hosts frame therapy, spiritual health and mantras as the mental counterpart to the diet and exercise discussed in their previous episode, arguably just as important for a healthy, wealthy, joyful life. — [2025-03-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=temJGDdDgwM)
- **Therapy is transformative because you go in with one presenting problem and end up unpacking upbringing, family and career.** Cherie first used free campus counseling in college during a hard sophomore year and a senior-year breakup, then returned in 2019 as a new grad over a romantic issue and went consistently for a year; she says she is who she is today because of that work. — [2025-03-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=temJGDdDgwM)
- **'Therapist dating' is real: you will not click with every therapist, so try several until you find one whose demeanor, background and method fit you.** Jean switched away from a college therapist because she felt unable to discuss body-image distress with someone who was obese, and a male friend avoided an Asian woman therapist because it felt too much like talking to his mom. Jean found her archetype, an older gay man using CBT in NYC around 2011, and has sought that type in every city since. — [2025-03-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=temJGDdDgwM)
- **Therapy is not a walk in the park; it is the mental equivalent of a gym workout that builds emotional muscle and breaks old patterns of thinking.** Cherie says it is the hardest and best thing she has ever done because confronting your own bad and good parts is terrifying; Jean compares it to changing how you have moved your body every day of your life. You can set expectations to ease in and build rapport before tackling the deepest issues. — [2025-03-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=temJGDdDgwM)
- **Once experienced with therapy, walk in with your own agenda for the session so you know what you want to get out of it.** Cherie tells her therapist which topics she wants to cover and treats leaving in tears as a sign she is working through something; a college therapist who was a Black woman also taught her the concept of intersectionality, showing therapy can be academic and growth-oriented. — [2025-03-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=temJGDdDgwM)
- **EMDR (eye movement desensitization and reprocessing) is another option that combines talk therapy with eye movements to process trauma.** Jean initially thought it sounded like a bit much, but a girlfriend who loves it and found it hugely helpful opened her mind to trying different modalities. _[EMDR]_ — [2025-03-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=temJGDdDgwM)
- **There should be zero stigma around antidepressants and SSRIs; they are a viable path to getting better.** Multiple girlfriends recently told Jean they are on SSRIs with no fanfare, and the conversations carried no shame; you need not announce it, but know it is available and talk to a medical professional. — [2025-03-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=temJGDdDgwM)
- **A streak-based goal like being active 30 days in a row works even when you keep resetting the clock, because the goal itself keeps you showing up.** Cherie has made it 10 days then 14 days before a skipped day reset her count, yet she is in her best physical shape in years and sees visible abs for the first time, which she calls a lagging indicator of consistency since January. — [2025-03-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=I04_Nc8odHw)
- **It is OK to pause: slowing down to care for your mental and physical health helps you get further, or 'slow down to speed up'.** Advice from Jonah Goodheart, which felt like permission from a very successful person against the media image of founders always grinding. David Shim applies the same idea to public speaking by stepping away from a speech so it processes in the background. _[Jonah Goodheart, David Shim]_ — [2025-03-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=I04_Nc8odHw)
- **Therapy gives you a toolkit: workplace reactions to tone and energy trace back to childhood experiences, and naming their source lets you control the cortisol rush instead of panicking.** (Leah Wyar) Leah didn't think she needed a therapist before 2001 and now says it changed her life; she traced a reaction back to 8th-grade friends dropping her. The first 18 years are so impressionable that these moments erupt in the workplace in weird ways. — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **As women, we deal with biology that competes with career growth, and there is no universal formula; it's a bespoke problem each woman solves with her friends, work wives, therapy and resilience toolkit.** (Leah Wyar) Even with a partner who cries every ounce of it with you, women take fertility struggles as their own fault; Leah calls it 'our blessing and our burden at the same time.' — [2025-04-28](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yObnjfFejUI)
- **Don't break the chain: mark an X on a visible calendar for every day you do the habit so the streak itself becomes the satisfying reward.** Cherie aims for 30 consecutive gym days and tracks them with Xs; Jerry Seinfeld reportedly used the same method to write a new joke every day. The sisters offer a free downloadable habit tracker. _[Jerry Seinfeld, Don't Break the Chain]_ — [2025-07-21](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=McWujpZMzLw)
- **A personal OKR for mental health could be three therapy sessions, journaling twice a week and one full rest day per quarter.** Jean's example for the mini exercise, which Cherie says she will borrow. _[OKRs]_ — [2025-08-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=IyFtCATnRpA)
- **Productivity is the output of every pillar of your life, so sleep, food, relationships, therapy and cutting alcohol are productivity tools.** Cherie stopped drinking because it lowers her productivity and works on each pillar a little at a time; in her 30s she feels in control of her habits and therefore her outcomes. — [2025-09-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TkXvlgPXitc)
- **Therapy and intense self-work have seasons; like a publicist, you use them when there is something to work on, then enjoy the fruits.** (Maggie Sellers) After therapy every other week from calling off her 2023 engagement until about six months ago, she is now in 'enjoyment mode'; she compares it to celebrities alternating creation time and promotion time, a lesson from working with big celebrities. — [2025-10-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=u6hxzkt6MOE)
- **Ivy League pressure is not just the workload but constant competition with tough curves, fights for internships, and professors too busy for students.** Cherie found sharp elbows studying computer science at Columbia, where freshman classmates who had already studied CS refused to help on group projects; Jean heard that Wharton students refused to share notes and even ripped the key pages out of library textbooks so others could not use them. _[Columbia, UPenn Wharton]_ — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **Studying until 5 AM is a status flex and rite of passage in the first two years, but if you are in the library at 5 AM you are not managing your time well.** At Columbia's 24-hour Butler Library people chugged Red Bull and stayed until 4-5 AM in a 'Nerd Olympics' where smart people were the cool kids; Cherie got caught up in unhealthy all-nighters until senior year, when she gained balance and cared less about what others were doing. _[Butler Library, Columbia]_ — [2025-10-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qkNnsxjdnlA)
- **Trauma is not left in the past; it rewires the brain and nervous system and is stored in the body and limbic system, so you cannot talk your way out of it, you have to feel your way through.** (Jane Marie Chen) Reading The Body Keeps the Score was a turning point; talk therapy never worked for her, and in business school she refused to discuss her childhood with a therapist. _[Bessel van der Kolk, The Body Keeps the Score]_ — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)
- **You cannot CEO your way through healing; instead of doing more, slow down and feel the heartbreak, fear and anger.** (Jane Marie Chen) After Embrace shut down she bought a one-way ticket to Indonesia with a surfboard and kept a spreadsheet of healing modalities, pivoting when one failed, before realizing the answer was stillness with the help of teachers, healers and friends. Jean says the fixer, A/B-testing product-manager mentality is exactly what she has to set down. _[Embrace]_ — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)
- **Driving unhealthily hard lets you burn bright for a while and then burn out completely; sustainable impact requires daily self-care.** (Jane Marie Chen) When Embrace shut down after 10 years she had a full mental and physical breakdown that took a year and a half to recover from, and learned her mind was not invincible. _[Embrace]_ — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)
- **To feel again after years of numbness, sit in stillness and notice small physical sensations, since every emotion has a bodily correlate, and heal in community rather than isolation.** (Jane Marie Chen) 'We cannot heal what we cannot feel'; safe friends, therapists and teachers relax the nervous system enough to access emotions. — [2025-12-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wL4BAe4lBAM)
- **Use 'don't break the chain' with a visible calendar of X's to sustain a streak.** Cherie aims for 30 consecutive gym days and marks each on a calendar; Jerry Seinfeld used the same method to write a new joke daily. The hosts offer a free downloadable habit tracker. _[Jerry Seinfeld]_ — [2025-12-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=NmdHxviASBg)
- **Therapy matters because workplace reactions to tone and energy trace back to your first 18 years, and naming the root gives you a toolkit and control.** (Leah Wyar) Leah traced a cortisol rush at work back to eighth-grade friends who dropped her. Knowing where the anxiety comes from means you know it will not last and what to do, instead of panicking about why you feel that way. — [2025-12-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KTlJZKNQRDc)

### Life design, goals & reinvention (90)

- **There are two types of people: those who stay on 'the path' and those who realize there is not only one path.** (Vu Tran) The path is school, good college, master's, junior to senior in an industry; some people wake up at 29 asking what they have done with their life. Vu's first job at 16 was flipping repaired instruments bought on eBay and Craigslist because he did not want to mow lawns or bus tables, which taught him he could create his own path. — [2025-02-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0OopMnFAeoE)
- **Travel to Japan because seeing a society not built on Western ideology makes you question your own constraints.** (Vu Tran) Clean streets, on-time subways and electronic toilets make you ask why things cannot be different at home, e.g. why the New York subway is not like Japan's. Vu spent four months near Nara doing brain research while living surrounded by rice paddies. _[Japan]_ — [2025-02-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0OopMnFAeoE)
- **'Waking up' from the prescribed path is a choice that requires time and energy to consider, often only possible after stepping back.** Jean took a sabbatical after nearly seven years at Snapchat, severely burned out, and used it for unpaid internships, watercolor and jewelry-making before deciding whether to leave. Cherie recalls Stanford professors' final lectures urging MBAs with 'the biggest safety net in the world' not to default to middle management at a consulting firm. _[Snapchat, Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-02-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0OopMnFAeoE)
- **Failing at the prescribed path can be a 'wake up' moment that leads to a better-fitting career.** Jean only looked at product management after failing to land PE or hedge fund roles out of Goldman Sachs fund-of-funds investing; she references Vu Tran's 'the path and waking up' idea and Graham Weaver's realization at a PE shop that a colleague loved the work in a way he never would. _[Goldman Sachs, Vu Tran, Graham Weaver, Stanford Graduate School of Business]_ — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **Life can have many acts; reinvention is possible at almost any stage even if it gets harder with kids and mortgages.** Jean says she is on her third act in her 30s and anticipates more, and the hosts speculate that midlife crises are wake-up moments where people feel it is too late to change. — [2025-02-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6zHp5ywD7og)
- **Write an over-long annual goals list so hitting half of it still feels like 100 percent.** Jean's yearly '35 things to do at 35' list actually contains about 70 items mixing small goals and stretch goals; this year one item was simply to be on a dating app to get back into the groove of dating. — [2025-02-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Klo3QMyc9Fo)
- **Define a North Star you genuinely love thinking about and every decision compounds toward it, as Kim Kardashian did with fame.** Jean recalls home videos of Kardashian at 16-17 saying she would be famous; her politics involvement and PE/investment fund all expand the total addressable market for her name. Loving money can be a legitimate motivator in the same way. _[Kim Kardashian]_ — [2025-02-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rfDi_KmXq6Q)
- **A family gap year and safari in Tanzania and Kenya is a strong recommendation: it reminds you humans are 'just bags of meat' who got to the top only through our brains.** (Jeremy Liew) Jeremy stepped back from new investments at Lightspeed at the end of 2021 and traveled internationally with his family from summer 2022 to summer 2023, spending a month each in Tanzania and Kenya. He skipped Kilimanjaro because 'that sounds like a lot of work.' _[Tanzania, Kenya]_ — [2025-03-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=109Xypx-hM4)
- **'The two most dangerous words anyone can say are not now': people delay their real goal with excuses that aren't real blockers, so first clock how often you say it.** Cherie cites Stanford GSB lecturer Graham Weaver ('not now, I'll wait for the promotion / until the kids leave'). Jean adds the PM maxim that you can't improve what you don't measure. _[Graham Weaver, Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-03-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=109Xypx-hM4)
- **Pick habits that are personal to you to design a life of n-of-one; the more your habits come from someone else, the less happy you are.** (Jonah Goodhart) Jonah is a big believer in daily habits and says choosing ones that matter to you over time yields more success in life. — [2025-03-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UYtMgkz8brA)
- **If you love your work you never want to retire; the goal is to keep building and teaching into your 80s.** Cherie points to Stanford GSB professors Glenn Kramon, Joel Peterson and Irv Grousbeck (89 and still teaching, who told her over coffee he will teach 'until I start drooling'); Jean's long-held dream is to retire into a professorship at Dartmouth with a house on Occom Pond. _[Irv Grousbeck, Joel Peterson, Glenn Kramon, Stanford GSB, Dartmouth]_ — [2025-03-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UYtMgkz8brA)
- **Aim to be the coolest 89-year-old with friends of every generation, because staying connected to young people keeps you on the pulse.** Cherie sets this as a North Star; Jean thinks it is hard without grandchildren or a teaching role since generations rarely mix socially, referencing Imran's point about having a pulse on young people. — [2025-03-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UYtMgkz8brA)
- **Travel somewhere completely different from your world, like Tokyo, as a way to pause and think differently.** (Jonah Goodhart) Jonah prefers Tokyo over Aspen or the Hamptons because Japanese society takes honor in daily work, has no trash on the streets, and is successful yet totally different from America. _[Tokyo, Surf Lodge, Snow Lodge Aspen]_ — [2025-03-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=UYtMgkz8brA)
- **Where you live and who your friends are largely determine your health: New York social life is brunch and drinks, while San Francisco and LA are daytime cities where hiking or biking with friends is normal.** Wherever you live, deliberately find people who want activity as the form of hanging out, e.g. pickleball or new-grad sports leagues; Cherie joined a dodgeball league for the social element and is going on a pickleball date, and both are drinking much less. — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **Set goals so you can reach 100%: Jean's annual '36 things to do at 36' list has 70 items so hitting 36 counts as full success.** This contrasts with Cherie's stretch-goal mindset; both agree the point is being intentional rather than punishing yourself. — [2025-03-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FLkeTDLuo9U)
- **The advice for a 29-year-old is to be more patient: life is nonlinear, good things happen to good people at different stages, and you will have your own moment even if you do not know when.** (Michelle Huang) Right after Stanford GSB Michelle was anxious to join the next big thing and be part of everything her peers were doing; that drive was good for ambition but bad for mental health. The years around 29 are the most painful because of anticipation, and once you cross 30 life goes on and is great. _[Stanford GSB]_ — [2025-03-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fb8gYzg6ikg)
- **Life is so long that people reinvent themselves all the time and hit their stride at different times; a friend's timeline is not the best timeline for you.** (Michelle Huang) Jean quotes Michelle directly and adds that just because something did not happen for you at the same time as your friends does not mean it will not happen. — [2025-03-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fb8gYzg6ikg)
- **For ambitious people work life and personal life are intertwined, so a personal rupture reshapes a career and vice versa.** The hosts note Michelle was one of the few guests to bring her personal life into a career conversation; Jean relates it to 2024, when she left her seven-year corporate job as head of product at Snapchat and ended an eight-year relationship and engagement, leaving her asking 'who am I?' when identity was tied to both title and partner. _[Snapchat]_ — [2025-03-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fb8gYzg6ikg)
- **Twenty-plus years after business school, you still won't feel you have arrived, because the bar keeps moving forward.** (John Wu) Wu retired after a hedge fund career and came back as an operator; the hosts read this as a growth mindset rather than being hard on himself, and note his North Star seems to be contribution rather than achievement. _[Harvard Business School]_ — [2025-03-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VuBM_XODr7A)
- **Chase atelic goals (contribution, learning, impact) that have no finish line, rather than telic checkbox goals that leave you empty once achieved.** From the Greek 'telos' (end). Cherie's early telic goal was making partner at Goldman Sachs; her goal now is ongoing impact on people worldwide. Wu's continual reinvention is framed as atelic. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2025-03-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VuBM_XODr7A)
- **Multiple careers are possible: a storied investor with no operating experience can retire and then become president of a venture-backed tech company.** Wu started investing in crypto in 2014, the investments drew him in, and he came out of retirement to run Ava Labs, an a16z-backed blockchain platform; the hosts cite this as evidence you can do many different things. _[Ava Labs, Andreessen Horowitz]_ — [2025-03-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VuBM_XODr7A)
- **Maximizing earnings early buys freedom later: decisions Cherie made at 19 let her run a podcast and a bootstrapped startup full-time at 29 without venture or angel money.** The hosts stress this is only one option; if you have no loans and want to explore, that is fine, but be conscious about whether earning potential is what you want to optimize for.
