- **People are in desperate search for community and authentic connection, which explains behaviors like LinkedIn humblebrags.** (Rahm Emanuel) He reads LinkedIn posts as attempts, if self-centered, to be part of something bigger; Jean, who worked at LinkedIn five years, says it is where people build community and personal brand. _[LinkedIn]_ — [2026-04-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ptYgxhdshaA)
- **Writing publicly about people you admire and then emailing them can start a mentor relationship that leads to your first check.** (Jason Wilk) In 2008 Wilk blogged about Mark Cuban's TechCrunch 40 keynote on ten ways to get him to invest, emailed the post to the address Cuban gave the crowd, kept pitching ideas, and Cuban became Dave's first check; Cuban later called him a great investment on stage in front of 150 people. _[Mark Cuban, TechCrunch 40]_ — [2026-05-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hxtgSnFdQ3E)
- **EQ matters more than IQ or 'aura' because AI is democratizing intelligence, so the ability to build relationships, collaborate and self-reflect is the biggest differentiator in the modern workplace.** (Evan Spiegel) Everyone will soon have access to far-above-average intelligence; the technical problems worth solving are too complex to do alone, so doing anything interesting requires working with other people. — [2026-05-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0IYBJXS-1t8)
- **No one will tell your story or care about your career more than you, so marketing yourself is a job: decide the values and features you want to be known for when you enter a room.** Cherie posts daily on Instagram and LinkedIn; Jean adds two mindsets learned from her: treat personal branding as a fun, lighthearted project, and remember times someone else's content genuinely helped you, so sharing is helping people. _[LinkedIn, Instagram]_ — [2026-06-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iAt9bx4ENpA)
- **Putting yourself out there, whether content or events, opens room for collisions and opportunities you cannot predict.** Hosts' takeaway from Ally's story: be brave. — [2026-06-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iAt9bx4ENpA)
- **Community is real life: look at the person next to you, put your ask on the board, and both offer and receive help.** The live show included a reciprocity ring and whiteboard for asks, from recommendations to babysitters to travel tips. _[reciprocity ring]_ — [2026-06-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iAt9bx4ENpA)
- **Borrowed credibility, like 'Jean suggested I reach out,' or a top VC on your cap table, works as an information shortcut in cold outreach.** Jean learned this from Stanford writing professor Glenn Kramon: it only works if the referrer is connected to and respected by the recipient. 'Fellow Dartmouth grad' emails and founders chasing Andreessen Horowitz, Sequoia or Lightspeed on the cap table are the same mechanism. _[Glenn Kramon, Andreessen Horowitz, Sequoia, Lightspeed]_ — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **The real power of an elite network is casual, reciprocal access: you are not really friends unless you can text someone and get a reply right away.** When companies pitched the sisters CPG deals in an industry they knew nothing about, Cherie texted a friend who is a top expert; she reviewed the contract and sent back a quick spreadsheet on the math within minutes. This echoes Imran Khan's line from an earlier episode that you're not his friend unless he can just text you. _[Imran Khan]_ — [2026-06-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=x9IXVlikKpk)
- **Networking is all about intentionality: whenever you think of someone, act on it immediately with a short, casual note rather than agonizing over the email.** Cherie sends articles to professors like Glenn Kramon (former New York Times editor of 36-37 years) the moment she thinks of them, adding a quick update on what she is working on. Jean admits she over-edits emails and creates a barrier; the fix is to write as if texting a friend. It is a muscle that gets less scary with repetition. _[Glenn Kramon, New York Times]_ — [2026-06-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=x9IXVlikKpk)
- **Treat people like they are already your friends, because people generally want to be friends with you and will reciprocate.** Jean says adopting a casual, friend-like mindset toward intentional networking outreach is what she is trying to do more of; unless you're emailing the prime minister of Norway, a 'hey' is fine. — [2026-06-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=x9IXVlikKpk)
- **To replicate an Ivy League network without the degree, offer help before you ever ask for it; networking and mentorship are giving-first, two-way streets.** Networking gets a bad reputation because most people 'take take take.' Even a junior person can help someone five steps ahead: Cherie sent Glenn Kramon an article about LinkedIn profiles and he replied within 15 minutes that he would include it in his next lecture, a tangible contribution that built goodwill without trying. _[Glenn Kramon, LinkedIn]_ — [2026-06-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=x9IXVlikKpk)
- **Assuming good intent can change the trajectory of your life and career; you occasionally get burned, but far more often you gain relationships you'd never have had.** (Dr. Suzy Welch) Jack Welch went to parties of strangers saying 'could anything be more exciting', treating everyone as a friend he hadn't met yet. _[Jack Welch]_ — [2026-06-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Asm0bCXqd1s)
- **Give feedback with the SBI model (situation, behavior, impact), grounded in specific observation no one can contest, and say 'I felt' rather than 'you made me feel'.** Taught in Stanford's Touchy Feely (Interpersonal Dynamics) course, where 12 classmates sit in a circle for three hours with no curriculum and practice on the conflicts that arise; it is also part of Netflix's culture deck, Microsoft trains new managers on it, and Adobe used it to move away from annual reviews. Feedback fails when people jump straight to impact and skip situation and behavior. _[Touchy Feely, Interpersonal Dynamics, Netflix, Microsoft, Adobe, SBI framework]_ — [2026-07-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gxzHnCraPqI)
- **Never assume another person's intent ('crossing the net'); speak only to observed behavior and its impact on you, which defuses defensiveness.** Cherie used this with a guy she was dating and he thanked her for telling him. Workplace example: 'In yesterday's client pitch you interrupted me while I was presenting, and I felt undermined and less confident in front of the client.' She warns results may vary with immigrant parents. — [2026-07-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gxzHnCraPqI)
- **Make power mapping less transactional by figuring out how you can help the people on your map; the first step of networking is having something to offer.** In one morning Cherie connected a friend leaving big tech to her classmate Alex for solopreneur advice, and in turn introduced Alex, who was fundraising, to a partner at the VC firm she used to work at. People who hoard connections play it close to the chest; putting pieces together with an abundance mindset makes more things blossom, and you want to be thought of as 'an Alex' who is willing to help. — [2026-07-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gxzHnCraPqI)
- **Your ideas are only as powerful as your ability to communicate them, so use SCQA (situation, complication, question, answer) to get to the point in 30 seconds because being clear is more powerful than being smart.** From Barbara Minto, taught by Matt Abrahams, who coached Cherie for her TED talk. Example pitch: 'CAC is rising faster than LTV; if we don't adjust we'll overspend; we shift 40% of spend to UGC creators.' Amazon uses SCQA in the six-page memo and mock press release written before every product launch. Test: if a friend can repeat your idea back in one sentence, you did it right. _[Barbara Minto, Matt Abrahams, SCQA, Amazon, Think Fast Talk Smart]_ — [2026-07-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gxzHnCraPqI)
- **Being too smart can hold you back if you cannot convey ideas simply; practice simplifying your language.** The hosts point to Donald Trump's communication ('this bad, very bad', 'big beautiful bill') as an example of why dumbed-down words land. In Glenn Kramon's Winning Writing class at Stanford, each session opened with ten terms to simplify. _[Donald Trump, Glenn Kramon, Winning Writing]_ — [2026-07-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gxzHnCraPqI)
- **Clarity means simplicity: break things down into simple terms through inbound clarity (listening effectively) and outbound clarity (communicating simply).** (Jim Keyes) Keyes cites Da Vinci and Coco Chanel on elegance in simplicity and says we over-complicate everything in a world of information. Cherie reframes clarity as a force field protecting your goals from negative inputs. _[Leonardo da Vinci, Coco Chanel]_ — [2026-07-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bq53ee_o4AM)
- **Talking with great leaders teaches communication by osmosis: Keyes makes complex concepts feel simple and conversational without lecturing.** Rewatching, the hosts realized the conversation was casually a business masterclass covering supply chain, cold-chain management, cash management and cross-cultural business, delivered with clarity and catchphrases like 'prepare to win' and 'change equals opportunity'. _[Jim Keyes]_ — [2026-07-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bq53ee_o4AM)
- **Always take the recruiter's call, and if the role isn't right for you, nominate three great people you know for it.** (Jen Wong) Wong was not on Reddit's original COO call list because the search targeted San Francisco and she is a diehard New Yorker; a friend who was called said it wasn't for him but recommended her, which led to a meeting with Steve Huffman at an SFO airport lounge. _[Reddit, Steve Huffman, Time Inc.]_ — [2026-07-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-dANhTbV-JM)
- **Use the metaprompt 'this is an important X; read it, assess its goal, analyze it for shortcomings against that goal, then rewrite it to address them' to get structural rather than sentence-level edits.** (Kat Getzy) Kat uses it for sensitive documents like negotiation emails and job descriptions; the hosts compare it to the SAT writing section. — [2026-08-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BS2FoFnt-E0)
- **At a big event, find allies you are comfortable debriefing with at the end of the night in your pajamas, because that is where the real nuggets of wisdom surface.** From a week as roommates in Cannes: the 3 a.m. fast-food kitchen-table conversations were the ones Kat wrote down and kept thinking about, and they plan to film that debrief next year. _[Cannes]_ — [2026-08-17](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BS2FoFnt-E0)
- **LinkedIn is no longer just for active job seekers; it is your online front page and personal brand, and not having an optimized profile is a detriment.** The hosts cite a New York Times piece asking whether LinkedIn is entering its 'post-cringe era'; keeping people updated on what you are doing lets organic opportunities arrive outside your direct job. _[New York Times 'post-cringe era' article]_ — [2026-08-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qCAHgny6a4Q)
- **Create the narrative you want about yourself before one is created for you; the point of LinkedIn is presenting yourself so more opportunities become available.** Cherie calls the profile sections underused 'real estate' where you can talk about yourself in your own voice. — [2026-08-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qCAHgny6a4Q)
- **Write your About section with the 'three S's': share who you are, sell what you do and offer, and become search optimized in your audience's language.** This is Cherie's own framework from her Stanford GSB lecture; search optimization means adding metadata via the About text so people actually find you. _[Three S's framework, Stanford Graduate School of Business]_ — [2026-08-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qCAHgny6a4Q)
- **Aim to be 'one in a million, not one of a million': a good About could only describe you.** Borrowed from Cherie's writing professor Glenn Kramer. Example: a VP of product whose one sentence spans the first US hybrid SUV, a number one app, a children's book and 'some parts of this page' (he led the LinkedIn profile team). _[Glenn Kramer]_ — [2026-08-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qCAHgny6a4Q)
- **Senior people can afford smiley faces, emojis and heavy personal content; if you are junior and want to be taken seriously, lead with professional substance.** A COO's About lists five things of which three are personal (BJJ brown belt, dancer, powerlifter), but his Harvard, Google and LinkedIn history supports it; the hosts say such people have 'earned the right' to be playful. — [2026-08-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qCAHgny6a4Q)
- **If you are building an audience or a business, make your About heavy on the 'sell' with a clear call to action.** A founder's About reads 'My goal is to teach you how to start your own business one day. Follow to get my lessons learned building a billion dollar company'; a slight stretch is fine as long as the rest of the profile supports it. — [2026-08-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qCAHgny6a4Q)
- **An emoji-bulleted list of memorable, numeric, tongue-in-cheek facts is a strong intermediate format because it is easy to parse and impossible to confuse with anyone else.** The example profile mixes 20+ years of exec roles and $10M raised with semi-pro basketball in Europe, top-three archery in Germany, travelling with his own tea and meditating with monks; Cherie's Stanford class splits 50/50 between 'cringe' and 'hilarious', but everyone remembers him. — [2026-08-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qCAHgny6a4Q)
- **Short, constrained writing is harder and more valuable than long paragraphs, especially now that AI can generate paragraphs in a second.** Many About sections are unreadable paragraphs; the hosts prefer a few specific sentences boiled down. — [2026-08-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qCAHgny6a4Q)
- **Structure your About as mission (share), call to action with contact (sell), then fun facts (one-in-a-million talking points).** Cherie lists 'looking to collaborate: partnerships@tigersisters.co', then facts like living on a Japanese tea farm, co-founding Sisters Matcha, loving Hamilton, and being a LinkedIn Learning instructor; personal connection points beat purely professional ones. _[Sisters Matcha, Hamilton, LinkedIn Learning]_ — [2026-08-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qCAHgny6a4Q)
- **Generic openers like 'passionate about solving complex product challenges, collaborating and driving results' are bad because they could be anyone and sound AI-written.** Cherie flags a friend's profile opener and 'I welcome conversations of any kind' as one-of-a-million filler to avoid. — [2026-08-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qCAHgny6a4Q)
- **Write the About in first person so your own voice shines through; third person feels removed, like a biography rather than an autobiography.** Jean notes some people find first person boastful, but readers know it is about you and first person reads stronger. — [2026-08-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qCAHgny6a4Q)
- **Test your About with one question: what is the one sentence you want people to remember after reading it? If you cannot answer easily, revise.** The closing exercise; part two of the masterclass will review listener-submitted profiles. — [2026-08-24](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=qCAHgny6a4Q)
- **Your headline is prime real estate and should carry two or three ideas at most, not a six-item sentence, so people can grok what you are about instantly.** A submitted profile strung together scholar, marketing, community builder, scholarship help and 'engaging and connecting people wherever I land'; the hosts advise cutting generic lines that could describe a million people. — [2026-08-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nu45PVvbkxA)
- **Every empty section of a LinkedIn profile is unused real estate; the banner in particular is underutilized and can show you as a leader.** One submitter filled her banner with photos of herself speaking at a podium and receiving awards plus keywords 'scholar, connector, activator', which the hosts said made her look like a leader at first glance. — [2026-08-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nu45PVvbkxA)
- **Use the name-pronunciation recording feature, and if you are 'extra', pack a greeting and call to action into those ten seconds.** Cherie's recording says 'Hello, welcome to my profile... I'm a podcast host and startup co-founder. Follow me to learn more about venture capital, media, startups and technology', which she calls a black-diamond tactic that takes five seconds to record. — [2026-08-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nu45PVvbkxA)
- **Listing your current top university and a co-founder title in the headline is an effective information shortcut, but drop awards nobody outside your niche recognizes.** For a Stanford student's headline 'Stanford University, co-founder at X, Diana Award 2024', the hosts liked the first two and questioned the award since readers have no context. _[Diana Award]_ — [2026-08-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nu45PVvbkxA)
- **An About section containing only an email address is better than nothing because it is a call to action, but one dialed first-person sentence about your mission would 10x the profile.** Advice to the student founder: say what your company and award are about and what you care about, following the beginner formula from part one. — [2026-08-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nu45PVvbkxA)
- **Have a public profile photo where you are smiling, looking directly at the camera, and with your body facing toward the profile text, because LinkedIn's research found those get the most clicks.** Insider knowledge from the profile team; some people have no photo or restrict who can see it, which hurts discoverability. Cherie admits she has ignored the advice for an artistic looking-away photo. _[LinkedIn profile team]_ — [2026-08-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nu45PVvbkxA)
- **A headline that pairs a credential with a mission, like 'CFP financial planner helping first-generation wealth builders', gives credibility and purpose in one line.** The hosts hold up this submitted headline as concise and clear even without knowing what CFP stands for. _[CFP]_ — [2026-08-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nu45PVvbkxA)
- **If your About is long, break it into sections with subtitles, shorter paragraphs, bullets or strategic emojis so readers are pulled from one part to the next.** One submission ran eight paragraphs; Cherie's own profile uses a 'fun facts' subtitle to guide the eye. — [2026-08-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nu45PVvbkxA)
- **Numbers pop out of dense text and pull the reader's eye through, so include quantitative figures when writing a lot.** Jean noticed her eyes went straight to '80+ high net worth households' and '124 trillion' in a text-heavy About even before understanding them. — [2026-08-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nu45PVvbkxA)
- **Avoid 'broetry', the one-line-per-sentence bragging style that LinkedIn staff actually named, because it reads as AI-written and insufferable.** Example from the LinkedIn Lunatics subreddit: 'I stayed up for 72 hours straight... I hit a new gear I never thought possible'; commenters noted 72 hours awake is when you go to the ER. _[LinkedIn Lunatics subreddit, Broetry]_ — [2026-08-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nu45PVvbkxA)
- **Relating a personal-life lesson to work can be a great window into who you are, but only if done tactfully and not as a bald lead-in to an empty lesson.** The 'I had a baby, here's what it taught me about B2B' post is the failure mode; done well, the same move shows the personal side alongside the professional. — [2026-08-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nu45PVvbkxA)
- **The leading principle for any LinkedIn post is to deliver value: ask what you can teach and what insight about your industry or career others would benefit from.** If your posts are not engaging, or you are scared to start, reframe around teaching rather than bragging or one-upping; the hosts say that content belongs on X/Twitter and want LinkedIn to be a place people learn from each other. — [2026-08-31](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nu45PVvbkxA)
- **Never open an email with 'hope you are well'; the first five words must make the reader smile or get to the point, and pleasantries belong at the end.** (Glenn Kramon) At least half of his Stanford class admits to the habit, and AI does it too, so it makes you 'one of a million'. People read on phones and may only see five words; 'cheers' or 'hope you're well' at the sign-off means the same thing without wasting the opening. The colder the email, the more important it is to get straight into it. — [2026-09-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6SIi_BQ0p2g)
- **Open cold emails with a researched, non-offensive personal connection that makes the recipient ask 'how did she know that?'** (Glenn Kramon) He opened with Cherie by asking if she worked with Evan Spiegel. Use LinkedIn, Instagram and, best of all, people who know the target; a student had been babysat by the current Green Bay Packers coach. If the ask is too important to open with a hobby, put it in a PS so they leave feeling good, per Maya Angelou's line that people never forget how you made them feel. _[Evan Spiegel, Green Bay Packers, Maya Angelou, LinkedIn]_ — [2026-09-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6SIi_BQ0p2g)
- **Do not let AI scare you off correct writing like the em dash or words like 'delve'; what your writing needs is your own voice.** (Glenn Kramon) Students switched to hyphens to avoid looking AI-generated, but a hyphen is wrong and an em dash is correct. Cherie, who ran Dartmouth's peer writing program, says she edits out AI tics like 'so here's the ask:' but keeps the em dash. _[Dartmouth College]_ — [2026-09-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6SIi_BQ0p2g)
- **A one-in-a-million email combines warmth and competence, and competence is shown with specific cinematic examples, not vague labels.** (Glenn Kramon) Instead of 'I'm resilient': 'my first startup went bankrupt and as a result I conserved cash and managed the most efficient early-stage sales team in biotech.' Instead of 'Swiss Army knife': 'at 17 I managed social media, customer retention, finance and HR for my family's diner struggling through COVID.' Examples via colleague Rachel Konrad. _[Rachel Konrad]_ — [2026-09-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6SIi_BQ0p2g)
- **Instead of spraying 50 emails, pick the three to five people you most want a reply from and craft an undeniable email for each.** (Glenn Kramon) A Slack salesperson ignored three times found the target loved telenovelas and paid the star Rogelio on Cameo to send a video message, and got her answer. Comedian Nardwuar gets rappers to open up by handing Snoop Dogg a VHS of his first movie and Pharrell his first album, 'Rump Shaker'. _[Slack, Cameo, Nardwuar, Snoop Dogg, Pharrell Williams]_ — [2026-09-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6SIi_BQ0p2g)
- **Kramon's 12 rules for email: know your audience; say what you like and would like; know your main point; clear personal urgent subject line; start with what they don't know; be more formal if unsure; start fast; be shorter; vary sentence length; write as you would speak to friends; add warmth or humor; assume it lands on page one of the New York Times.** (Glenn Kramon) Jean read the full list from his Winning Writing lecture; rule 12 means write nothing you would not want distributed to everyone you know. _[Winning Writing, New York Times]_ — [2026-09-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6SIi_BQ0p2g)
- **Subject lines must be personal and signal what matters to the recipient, because some people get 1,000 emails a day.** (Glenn Kramon) Mention his favorite player Bryce Love and he will open it; 'my insights from our latest meeting' beats 'insights from latest meeting'. Fake-urgency subject lines like 'your account has been hacked' used to sell something are morally wrong and a bad practice. _[Bryce Love]_ — [2026-09-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6SIi_BQ0p2g)
- **Start with what your audience doesn't know, not what it does, and get the ask into the first sentence.** (Glenn Kramon) A pitch to Jeff Bezos opened with two paragraphs telling him he had transformed industries; the rewrite was 'Dear Mr. Bezos, I can help Alexa help patients needing personalized follow-up care. A Stanford MD/MBA, I'd appreciate a few minutes...'. Kramon met Bezos 25 years ago and notes Amazon's writing culture rejects time-wasting memos. _[Jeff Bezos, Amazon, Alexa]_ — [2026-09-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6SIi_BQ0p2g)
- **The most common cold-email mistake is asking for something; instead show how you can help them, because it's about them, not you.** (Glenn Kramon) Openers like 'I can expand your presence in Southeast Asia' or 'I've crunched the numbers and found a big business opportunity for you' keep people reading. Tim O'Brien, his first NYT hire (now running Bloomberg Opinion), walked into his interview with the first 10 stories he would write. Substitute 'company' for 'country' in JFK's 'ask not' line. _[Tim O'Brien, Bloomberg Opinion, John F. Kennedy]_ — [2026-09-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6SIi_BQ0p2g)
- **Warm up a cold email by dropping names of mutual contacts, or better, getting that person to forward your email with an endorsement.** (Glenn Kramon) People respond because they don't want to let down or anger their friend; the hosts cite 'our mutual friend Matt Abrahams suggested I speak with you' as their favorite tactic. _[Matt Abrahams]_ — [2026-09-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6SIi_BQ0p2g)
- **The two most important words in a cold letter are 'like you', but the comparison must be specific, not generic.** (Glenn Kramon) 'Like you, I started my entrepreneurial journey young: I bought candy wholesale and retailed it at school' or 'like you, I'm building a business in an industry where people like us are underrepresented' creates a visceral bond; 'like you, I believe AI will change our futures' is one of a million. — [2026-09-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6SIi_BQ0p2g)
- **A properly crafted email is handmade: it could only be received by that one person, even if the core body is reused across 20 recipients.** (Glenn Kramon) Kramon called Jean's summary the most important point of the episode. Student Khalil Fuller's Gift Card Bank got supermarket CEOs to respond by noting one had started at the very bottom and likely knew Depression survivors; the nonprofit has since raised $80-100 million, including after the LA wildfires. _[Khalil Fuller, Gift Card Bank]_ — [2026-09-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6SIi_BQ0p2g)
- **When a cold email gets no reply, don't give up: follow up gently, add something fresh of value, and escalate subject lines to 'third try' or, for people you know, 'do I offend?'** (Glenn Kramon) If no reply in the first few days, wait and send 'just ensuring you saw this' or a relevant new article. The few who never respond are people you probably don't want to work with anyway; Kramon himself has sent ten unanswered emails to Google co-founder Sergey Brin and now uses it as a class story. _[Sergey Brin, Google]_ — [2026-09-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6SIi_BQ0p2g)
- **Always respond to email, even with 'thanks but no thanks', because non-responders are assumed to be arrogant, aloof or overwhelmed.** (Glenn Kramon) Responders are assumed warm and personal and people want to work with them; anything beats ghosting. When he polls his class, more Stanford MBAs report being ghosted than ghosting. — [2026-09-07](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6SIi_BQ0p2g)

### Money, investing & personal finance (45)

- **A family office is a dedicated team handling all aspects of a family's financial life, and its real product is peace of mind and family well-being, not just growing wealth.** (Holly MacDonald) Services span investment management, tax planning, estate planning and philanthropy. Bessemer's average client has around $100 million under management; the firm handles about $200B AUM. _[Bessemer Trust]_ — [2026-01-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=N3dGhIFWMNY)
- **The most important starting question for any wealth is its purpose: preserve, grow, pass on, or give away, because that dictates structure, tax planning and asset allocation.** (Holly MacDonald) Passing wealth to the next generation implies certain structuring; growing it for philanthropy implies a different allocation. If the purpose is the next generation, ask what values you want passed along too. — [2026-01-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=N3dGhIFWMNY)
- **About two-thirds of Bessemer's clients are first-generation wealth creators, and they want wealth to expand their children's choices, not give them an easy life.** (Holly MacDonald) Inheritance can let a child be an entrepreneur or a social worker while keeping their desired lifestyle; the last thing a gritty founder wants is kids with no view on how the money was made. _[Bessemer Trust]_ — [2026-01-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=N3dGhIFWMNY)
- **The most common goal is preservation, but pure preservation fails because inflation erodes wealth, so every family needs at least a strategy to maintain purchasing power.** (Holly MacDonald) Whether the aim is big growth or just keeping up with inflation, some growth strategy is required. — [2026-01-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=N3dGhIFWMNY)
- **Family offices differ mainly in their long time horizon, which lets them hold illiquid assets like private equity and venture capital that early-career people can't afford to lock up.** (Holly MacDonald) PE and VC return capital in 5-10+ years; someone unsure whether they'll need a house down payment in five years shouldn't over-allocate. Family offices can also chase bespoke one-off deals because they have a full team analyzing them. Bessemer has built ways for small pools (even a grandchild's account) to access top private-market managers. — [2026-01-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=N3dGhIFWMNY)
- **Anyone can run a 'mini family office' for themselves or with a partner: agree on the why, automate money movement toward savings, spending and giving, and review with regularity.** (Holly MacDonald) Set automatic transfers into separate accounts or ledgers so you don't have to remember each paycheck. The hosts call it a 'family office of one'. — [2026-01-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=N3dGhIFWMNY)
- **Talking more openly about money, especially with a partner, raises the odds of meeting financial goals; money is becoming less taboo.** (Holly MacDonald) Industry research and Bessemer's client experience show greater communication correlates with goal achievement and a positive experience. Start walking before running: open the conversation with parents gently rather than cornering them about inheritance. — [2026-01-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=N3dGhIFWMNY)
- **The next wave of feminism is the ability to choose, girl boss or trad wife, and that choice is only real when you have your own financial freedom.** 'Get your own bag and make your own decisions' so every choice is made with a clear head and no external force pushing you into one archetype. The hosts note choices are now also projected publicly on social media and shaped by whether your friend group shares your archetype. — [2026-02-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=d4dHxBl2A2E)
- **Never fall in love with something that cannot love you back: being willing to walk away from a deal makes you more likely to win it, and there is no 'last best deal'.** (Codie Sanchez) Suzy Welch says there is no last best deal except the one you did and wish you had not; Warren Buffett says he never thinks about deals he missed, only the ones he did but should not have. _[Suzy Welch, Warren Buffett]_ — [2026-02-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=s3fe7uZlKMI)
- **Invest somebody else's first $500,000 rather than your own, and start with partial acquisitions instead of blowing up your whole life.** (Codie Sanchez) A Goldman mentor told her the first $500K in deals is almost sure to be lost, so learn on someone else's dime; men have long done 'club deals' on the 19th hole and own pieces of friends' businesses. Learning how to do a deal is like the Matrix: even if you never buy, you will negotiate and evaluate roles and companies differently. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2026-02-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=s3fe7uZlKMI)
- **Give employees a choice between stock options and RSUs so they can manage their own risk tolerance.** (Mandy Ginsburg) Options are higher risk and higher potential reward; RSUs feel more cash-like. Match offered the choice and Mandy has introduced it at other companies; most humans, given the choice, pick the less risky outcome. _[Match Group]_ — [2026-03-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ifV2k4vcvrE)
- **The biggest change in investing is the democratization of knowledge: Reg FD, the internet, social media and now AI give retail investors the same information professionals once got from private management meetings.** (Imran Khan) Thirty years ago Wall Street got one-on-one meetings and exclusive conferences; now CEOs debate on X and Reddit and you can hear from customers who hate a product as well as those who love it, giving a more holistic view than the five customers a pro might meet at a user conference. _[Reg FD, X, Reddit]_ — [2026-04-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AHOKAM47sS0)
- **The first rule of investing is to understand what you are investing in, because if you don't understand it you don't understand the risk, and investing is ultimately pricing risk.** (Imran Khan) Khan says even professionals make this mistake, which is why there are many sectors he simply doesn't touch; don't listen to somebody else, be educated about it yourself. — [2026-04-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AHOKAM47sS0)
- **Read the boring legal documents (S-1, 10-K, 10-Q) on a company's investor relations site, because companies spend hundreds of hours writing them and they legally must be accurate.** (Imran Khan) Khan worked on the Alibaba IPO and took Snap public; he also listens to what management says, and when he sees something questionable on social media he reads the comments and verifies against other sources. _[Alibaba, Snap]_ — [2026-04-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AHOKAM47sS0)
- **Curiosity is the essential trait of a good investor: don't ask one question, ask ten whys, and don't believe anything you read on social media until you've dug through it.** (Imran Khan) The hosts summarize his method as read the source material, ask why five times, and apply critical thinking to every source. — [2026-04-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AHOKAM47sS0)
- **The market likes to make a fool of the greatest number of people, so whenever something is consensus you should be most fearful because the risk is probably not priced in.** (Imran Khan) The market by default prices consensus, so the sharpest moves in any asset (stocks, bonds, art, real estate) happen when something non-consensus occurs; this does not mean you must take a contrarian bet, only that you must be careful. — [2026-04-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AHOKAM47sS0)
- **Being contrarian for its own sake is wrong because consensus can be right for years and a contrarian can be wrong for a very long time; timing matters.** (Imran Khan) He cites the investor in 'The Big Short' who was wrong for a long period before the housing crisis proved him right, and the hosts add shorting Japanese bonds as another example. _[The Big Short]_ — [2026-04-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AHOKAM47sS0)
- **The market has always been speculative, because buying anything is predicting the future; retail YOLO traders are no more speculative than the 'sophisticated' professionals who took down the economy trading CDS in 2008.** (Imran Khan) Khan says demeaning retail investors is unfair: many are smart, tech-savvy people who work at tech companies and understand the businesses, they simply lacked the East Coast Wall Street background he also lacked until junior year of college; being right 51 percent of the time makes you a genius in the market. _[Robinhood]_ — [2026-04-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AHOKAM47sS0)
- **Never buy a stock because someone told you to, because they may have a different pain threshold and time horizon; do your own work and make your own decision.** (Imran Khan) Banks call his fund constantly pitching stocks and convincing pitches sometimes trigger trades too early without full understanding, which never work out; if you don't enjoy investing, go to a professional instead. — [2026-04-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AHOKAM47sS0)
- **Never buy a stock just because it is cheap or short one just because it is expensive, since anyone can compute a P/E; ask why the market is pricing it that way and whether that fear is true.** (Imran Khan) A stock at 10x earnings is cheap because the market fears something; if the consensus fear is wrong you can earn incredible returns, but sometimes the consensus is right for a long time. Valuation should be the last stop, telling you how much risk (or good news) is already priced in. — [2026-04-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AHOKAM47sS0)
- **The entire US capital market runs on trust, and when a stock is very cheap you must ask whether management or the company broke that trust.** (Imran Khan) Nobody verifies every line of a filing; investors trust disclosures, auditors and non-omission of material facts. Trusted, predictable businesses earn higher multiples. America's trust-based markets and tolerance for honest failure let it fund Chinese tech like Alibaba and Tencent in the 1990s-2010s when their home markets could not. _[Alibaba, Tencent]_ — [2026-04-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AHOKAM47sS0)
- **If investing feels intimidating, start small, even $5 via fractional shares, but only if you are genuinely curious; never give up something because of fear, and never do something that doesn't excite you.** (Imran Khan) Barriers to investing have fallen dramatically over his 30-year career; like marathoners, take baby steps and don't put all your money in on day one. _[Robinhood]_ — [2026-04-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AHOKAM47sS0)
- **Investing is something you get better at the longer you do it because pattern recognition compounds; start small so you don't put all your chips in before you have built it.** (Imran Khan) A peer who joined a hedge fund straight out of college from an East Coast investing family had 15-20 years of cycle experience while Khan was still figuring out banking; the hosts add that pattern recognition transfers across verticals and is why they built Tiger Sisters so quickly using lessons from prior roles. _[Tiger Sisters]_ — [2026-04-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AHOKAM47sS0)
- **Get children interested in investing early, even with fake money, because financial sophistication and discipline matter and it is much easier to ruin assets than to earn them.** (Imran Khan) Khan admits he failed to get his own kids interested so far; the hosts mention wanting to write a children's personal finance book. — [2026-04-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AHOKAM47sS0)
- **No money is ever free: inherited or family wealth always comes with conditions that invisibly shape a person's life decisions, so dig into it if you date someone expecting an inheritance.** Cherie has seen multimillionaire heirs build their lives around money they know is coming, and only in the relationship does it become clear that decisions are driven by the money's stipulations. Wealth not earned through hardship may not have shaped character. — [2026-04-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PsDiVdzKIBo)
- **You will see five to eight major economic downturns in your adult life, and being ready with capital to deploy in each one can be the difference between being OK and being very comfortable.** (Vivian Tu) In March 2020 everyone was selling while rich people bought on the way down and kept buying; the market rebounded by August and the S&P 500 has since returned roughly 25% a year, rivaling private investments. The precondition is that the rest of your finances are safe first, and downturn bargains like secondhand designer goods or travel deals may not rebound like the index. _[S&P 500]_ — [2026-05-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ULlhVsNmU9M)
- **It is not technically a recession (two quarters of negative GDP) but it is a 'vibecession' driven by a K-shaped economy where the rich get richer and everyone else feels squeezed.** (Vivian Tu) Regular people feel it at the grocery store, gas pump and in rent even without a technical downturn. — [2026-05-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ULlhVsNmU9M)
- **Never try to call the bottom; invest a fixed dollar amount every month so you automatically buy fewer shares when prices are high and more when they are low.** (Vivian Tu) Even the smartest guys on Wall Street do not know where the bottom is; if she knew she would be retired. On a clearly major drop or rise you can decide to buy more or pause, but the default is to invest all the time. — [2026-05-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ULlhVsNmU9M)
- **Before agreeing to be a bridesmaid, ask whether you would be comfortable Venmoing this person $5,000; if not, attend as a guest instead.** (Vivian Tu) Dress, shoes, flights, hotel plus engagement party, bachelorette and shower add up to thousands. Her script: congratulate, state your budget and constraints candidly, say you do not want to limit their moments, and ask what to prioritize or offer to support in other ways; the bride can then say skip the engagement party, offer to cover costs, or invite you as a guest. — [2026-05-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ULlhVsNmU9M)
- **Calculate your FU number by pricing your perfect year and dividing by 0.04; hers is $25 million invested, which throws off $1 million a year at a conservative 4% return.** (Vivian Tu) The $1 million covers a mortgage in a high-cost metro, private school for two kids, a full-time NYC nanny at roughly $135,000 with benefits, vacations, supporting her parents and taxes. Count only assets that actively throw off gains, not a house you would have to borrow against. — [2026-05-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ULlhVsNmU9M)
- **Your car is not an investment: it loses 10% the moment you drive off the lot and can be worth half its price by year five.** (Vivian Tu) Only rare supercars like the six McLarens made this year appreciate; nobody is dying for your base-model Lexus. She has no car in Miami, running a cost-benefit on lease, insurance, gas, maintenance versus Ubering, and notes rain is a free car wash. _[McLaren, Lexus]_ — [2026-05-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ULlhVsNmU9M)
- **Lease, buy or rideshare depends on your use case: lease for job-transfer flexibility, buy the minivan you will drive into the ground, Uber if you are a hermit who goes out to drink.** (Vivian Tu) A friend in Texas leased because he might be transferred to LA, New York or London; Jean returned a three-year lease with under 5,000 miles. There is no perfect answer for everybody. _[Toyota Sienna]_ — [2026-05-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ULlhVsNmU9M)
- **You do not have to own a home to be doing well financially; a house only makes sense if you will stay at least five to seven years to amortize closing costs.** (Vivian Tu) People buy too small and then have three kids, or get a job elsewhere; property taxes, HOA or common charges and maintenance add up, like her $10,000 HVAC repair after a Japan trip. Homeownership can be part of your American dream but does not have to be. — [2026-05-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ULlhVsNmU9M)
- **For anyone living paycheck to paycheck, the best return on investment is cutting expenses by 10-20%, not a magic investment or crypto token.** (Jason Wilk) Quoting Mark Cuban; Dave's data shows customers spend about $400 a year on bank fees, which is a good place to start. The hosts note most advice jumps to investing when expense management comes first. _[Mark Cuban]_ — [2026-05-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hxtgSnFdQ3E)
- **You do not need a night nurse; buy a second-hand Snoo instead and resell it when done.** (Natasha Leggero) Everyone in Hollywood told Natasha she needed a night nurse; she paid one about $2,000 for a month, felt it was pointless since breastfeeding meant she woke anyway. The Snoo simulates womb sounds and motion and trains the baby to self-soothe; buying and reselling used (avoiding rented or fifth-hand units) can net out to near zero. _[Snoo]_ — [2026-05-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OzsWwmX-D50)
- **Most baby gear cost is hype: buy second-hand, use Buy Nothing groups, and pass your stuff on when you outgrow it.** (Natasha Leggero) Natasha bought a Doona stroller second-hand for $400 versus thousands new, comparing it to picking the $1 wedding chairs over $4 ones; Sabrina advocates an informal network where middle- and upper-class parents give gear away. _[Doona, Buy Nothing groups]_ — [2026-05-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OzsWwmX-D50)
- **Capitalism is fundamentally fair because you only get rewarded when you provide value to others.** (Fernando De Leon) If what you do does not provide value to society you are not rewarded; valuation bubbles burst when the only people who value a product are the insiders assigning the valuation rather than actual consumers. The hosts partly push back: opportunity is not equal, but the value-exchange principle is fair. — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **The pursuit of wealth was never about material things but about safety and protecting the family.** (Fernando De Leon) As the only American citizen among six siblings after his father died, Fernando used his English and citizenship as a 'golden ticket' to create income; supporting his mother and family was 100% of his goal and remains the seminal motivation. — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **Asset and company ownership is the most hidden secret in American capitalism, and you are never too young to own an asset.** (Fernando De Leon) He told his 21-year-old nephew to stop renting, buy a townhome or duplex, lease the second half to pay the mortgage, and own it outright after a 15-year amortization at age 36, even if it means living within his means and skipping restaurants. — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **If you cannot invest in a great company directly, own the real estate it leases so its rent becomes your royalty on its revenue.** (Fernando De Leon) Chick-fil-A is owned by a family trust, so he built the building on land he owned in San Angelo, Texas and leased it: $200,000 rent on $2 million of sales meant one in every ten pieces of chicken was effectively sold on his behalf. He repeated this with Starbucks, 7-Eleven, Raising Cane's, Chase Bank and CVS on small deals big real estate firms thought were beneath them. _[Chick-fil-A, Starbucks, 7-Eleven, Raising Cane's, Chase Bank, CVS]_ — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **Crypto is a legitimate store of wealth in countries with hyperinflation and dysfunctional governments, but has few fundamentals and should be approached lightly.** (Fernando De Leon) Growing up he saw Mexican currency devaluations every time a president left; people in Nigeria, Latin America and Asia derive real value from crypto and remittances. He invested, did well, took his wins and ran, and would not go as far as Charlie Munger's 'dog shit' verdict. _[Charlie Munger]_ — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **Give yourself permission to say out loud that you want to build wealth; being explicit about the goal makes everything align to it.** The hosts note women in particular are shy about it, that 'financial independence' implies a ceiling, and that money is a protective measure giving freedom and autonomy; they cite Kim Kardashian being unabashed about wanting fame, and that affluence is among their own top five values from the Suzy Welch exercise. _[Kim Kardashian, Suzy Welch, Forbes 250 Greatest Self-Made Americans]_ — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **Five finance terms every leader must know are revenue vs profit, cash flow, unit economics, forecast and runway, and valuation metrics.** Revenue is money in the door, profit is what remains after bills; Netflix's billions in content spend show up as assets, so cash flow can differ sharply from profit. Unit economics is margin per subscriber (a $10 subscription that costs $12 loses money) or per can for Sisters Matcha; runway is cash divided by burn ($200k at $100k/year burn equals two years); P/E and EV/EBITDA only mean something relative to the company's category. _[Netflix, Sisters Matcha, price-to-earnings ratio, EV/EBITDA]_ — [2026-06-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=x9IXVlikKpk)
- **'There is no last best deal': sellers, realtors and stock promoters manufacture urgency, and remembering another deal always comes prevents stupid purchases.** (Dr. Suzy Welch) Welch says this saved her many wrong turns, especially in real estate. The hosts adopted it as a mantra and extend it to relationships, since believing someone is the last best deal is how you settle; Welch's 55-year-old friend stayed with a terrible partner because 'who else is there'. — [2026-06-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Asm0bCXqd1s)
- **Fight the sunk cost fallacy: when you have made a financial, job or relationship mistake, own it publicly, take the hit and get out now.** (Dr. Suzy Welch) Welch sold a house she had fixed up after two years because it was the wrong place, despite friends' objections. Decision science shows we are wired to pour more money on problems to save face; people respect you more for cutting losses, not less. _[Sunk cost fallacy]_ — [2026-06-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Asm0bCXqd1s)

### Entrepreneurship & startups (71)

- **If the environment you want doesn't exist, create it: Reese Witherspoon left commercially successful acting to found Hello Sunshine so she could tell women's stories.** Hello Sunshine was born from a problem that broke her heart (no roles she wanted) and became a massively successful studio; the sisters cite it as the model they aspire to for Tiger Sisters. _[Reese Witherspoon, Hello Sunshine]_ — [2026-01-05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xjw7AkB4dn4)
- **To break out you have to stop being self-reliant and bring in help, both in business and in life.** Jean says in 2025 they did not bring in enough outside resources; a stylist, Emily Tyra, is one recent addition and 2026 should include a long list of people supporting them. _[Emily Tyra]_ — [2026-01-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4lZ8EsTExrQ)
- **Whitney Wolfe Herd is the original girl boss, but the media celebrated her partly because she still fulfilled the archetype of beautiful, brilliant mother, setting a possibly unattainable bar.** She rang the NASDAQ bell with her 18-month-old son on her hip as the world's youngest female billionaire and youngest woman to take a company public. Cherie saw her speak twice at Stanford GSB and, as a new grad at LinkedIn, recalls women thinking 'this is what it could be' while also wondering 'you good?' and whether she 'girl bossed too close to the sun'. _[Whitney Wolfe Herd, Bumble, NASDAQ, Stanford GSB, LinkedIn]_ — [2026-02-09](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=d4dHxBl2A2E)
- **The fastest way to become a CEO is not starting a company but buying one: the third path of 'buy then build' takes an existing profitable business from one to two instead of zero to one.** Most people think the only routes are grinding 15+ years up the corporate ladder or starting a startup and 'emotionally collapsing in a WeWork'. Acquiring a mature business means you inherit a very different set of problems than a founder creates, but you are an entrepreneur and CEO immediately. — [2026-02-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=s3fe7uZlKMI)
- **Searching is unglamorous, multi-year sales work where most owners say no, so searchers need stamina, endurance and emotional management.** Jean interned in 2009 for two Stanford GSB grads running a search fund and saw that it is day-in, day-out research and cold outreach, like knocking on a house with no for-sale sign. Owners are emotionally attached after 20-40 years and can back out right before signing, diligence can reveal skeletons, the economic environment can shift, and you must also manage investors and a co-searcher relationship. _[Stanford GSB, Dartmouth]_ — [2026-02-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=s3fe7uZlKMI)
- **Buying a business is for people who like operating, leading teams and handling messy real-life situations; it is not for anyone who wants passive income, hates managing humans, or wants to work from Bali.** You will be changing the printer cartridge and making decisions without a 47-page strategy deck; there is nothing passive about it. — [2026-02-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=s3fe7uZlKMI)
- **The belief that you need your own capital to buy a business is a lie that serves private equity and large corporations; the real gap is knowledge, not money.** (Codie Sanchez) 60% of businesses sold involve some seller financing, and PE firms themselves use leveraged buyouts, i.e. other people's money, just as homebuyers use mortgages. Private equity is the most profitable asset class over any 10-year rolling period, and Sanchez has seen first-time buyers do deals for $0 and for $50 million. — [2026-02-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=s3fe7uZlKMI)
- **There are three legs of the stool for buying a business without cash: seller financing, sweat equity, and expertise-to-equity.** (Codie Sanchez) Most small business owners are 65+, and more than 60% have no transition plan, so they will accept a share of future profits. Sweat equity means earning part or all of the business through work, like startup equity; expertise-to-equity means trading a specific skill such as marketing for a percentage of ownership. — [2026-02-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=s3fe7uZlKMI)
- **Startups are a bad deal for founders: most businesses never make money, the ones that do lose money for three to four years, and the average entrepreneur earns $46,000-$67,000 a year.** (Codie Sanchez) VCs know only one in ten startups will make it, which is fine for the VC but not for the founder; you can often make more as an employee. Both Sanchez and Jean had a 'look down the hall' moment in finance (Jean at Goldman) realizing they did not want the corner-office life, and the best part of entrepreneurship is becoming unemployable. _[Goldman Sachs]_ — [2026-02-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=s3fe7uZlKMI)
- **For a first acquisition, buy a boring, bond-like business so simple grandma understands it, with checks coming in for at least five years; never buy someone else's pain or a turnaround.** (Codie Sanchez) Unprofitable businesses that have not figured it out are the 'ugly house on the nice block' and the surest way to lose money. Boring businesses do not grow like a hockey stick but keep paying if you take the right actions. — [2026-02-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=s3fe7uZlKMI)
- **Three diligence rules: make sure the seller is not lying too badly, make sure the P&L matches the tax return (and buy on the cheaper number if it does not), and make sure you are buying a business, not a job.** (Codie Sanchez) Every owner lies a little, via inflated add-backs, understated hours, or asking you to pay for 'future potential'. Diligence is a lot of work but formulaic, and a business needs enough systems, processes and employees that you do not end up doing everything. — [2026-02-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=s3fe7uZlKMI)
- **Virality starts with authentic, surprising content; platform tactics like titles, thumbnails, length and 30-second retention are layered on afterward and kept out of the creative process.** (Andrew Yaffe) Dude Perfect has analysts testing multiple thumbnails and titles across languages, but Yaffe deliberately keeps that in the background so it does not interfere with the creative team's authenticity. _[Dude Perfect, YouTube]_ — [2026-03-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jy1d4_UQA4o)
- **Half a digital brand's audience may already be international, so translate everything and program for global sports.** (Andrew Yaffe) Dude Perfect had done nothing explicitly international in 14-15 years despite half its YouTube audience being global; now every long- and short-form piece is translated into 15 languages, a cricket short drew 60 million views, and the guys presented the Champions League final man-of-the-match trophy. _[Dude Perfect, Champions League]_ — [2026-03-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jy1d4_UQA4o)
- **In a world drowning in content, authenticity, consistency and persistence pay the highest returns.** (Andrew Yaffe) Asked how Tiger Sisters could grow like Dude Perfect, Yaffe notes the guys have been at it over 15 years; the hosts add that their most outsized audience response comes from vulnerable stories about mistakes and breakups. _[Tiger Sisters]_ — [2026-03-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jy1d4_UQA4o)
- **There are millions of niches on the internet big enough to build a business; make content for people like you and let the algorithms find them.** (Andrew Yaffe) Dude Perfect's founders say 'we make content for people like us,' now sports-loving dads; women in business going through similar things is a large enough starting audience for Tiger Sisters before branching out. — [2026-03-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jy1d4_UQA4o)
- **Apply the Disney flywheel: ask what other products and experiences your community wants, because a great in-person experience makes fans more likely to buy merch and watch videos.** (Andrew Yaffe) Five live tours in six years, the latest hitting 20+ cities in NBA-sized arenas at 95% sold out; a free fan fest recreating video stunts had hundreds lined up and had to close an hour before each show, proving fans want to do things with the brand, and leading toward Dude Perfect World. _[Walt Disney, Dude Perfect World]_ — [2026-03-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jy1d4_UQA4o)
- **The experiential economy for families has boomed post-pandemic, and few brands are better positioned to get kids and families to go out and do things.** (Andrew Yaffe) Parents constantly report kids spending three hours in the driveway recreating shots; Yaffe uses his six- and four-year-olds as a home focus group. — [2026-03-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jy1d4_UQA4o)
- **Run the live tour to maximize fan connection, not profit; the affinity pays dividends for years.** (Andrew Yaffe) After ten years of interacting only through comments and views, the founders now high-five hundreds of kids in arenas of 12,000, creating connection a video cannot. — [2026-03-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jy1d4_UQA4o)
- **Measure brand affinity systematically with focus groups, surveys and data-sharing partners rather than just feeling it.** (Andrew Yaffe) Since he joined, Dude Perfect runs weekly parent focus groups and regular pulse surveys; hydration partner BodyArmor shared purchase data on a co-branded product sold at Walmart and Kroger, revealing how each brand affects the other. _[BodyArmor, Walmart, Kroger]_ — [2026-03-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jy1d4_UQA4o)
- **Focus on a defined audience (families) instead of mass appeal, because deeper relationships unlock more business than breadth.** (Andrew Yaffe) Coming from the mass-market NBA he initially chased 18-34 general sports fans, then shifted in his first year to families with 6-16 year olds; the hosts note this also solves the aging-audience problem since kids are always being born. _[NBA]_ — [2026-03-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jy1d4_UQA4o)
- **VCs evaluate startups on three things, team, market and traction, and team matters most at the earliest stages.** Pre-seed and seed companies often have no revenue, so investors bet on founders' credentials (ex-Meta or Google engineers signal training). Lacking a technical co-founder, or founding with a romantic partner, are seen as risks, though Canva was founded by a couple. _[Canva, Meta, Google]_ — [2026-04-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9cdiQonikNU)
- **TAM must be defensible; claiming a $10 trillion market for 'all men and women' works against a founder.** Investors want multi-billion-dollar outcomes, but what matters is how much of the total addressable market will actually convert. Cherie uses Tiger Sisters as an example of a narrower but deeper TAM versus comedians' broad but shallow one. _[Tiger Sisters]_ — [2026-04-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9cdiQonikNU)
- **Do not take VC money unless you really have to; taking investment too early is one of the most common founder regrets.** Founders visiting Stanford's Managing Growing Enterprises class repeatedly cite taking funding too early or at all as a top mistake because it adds pressure and complicates hiring and operations. 'You have an overlord at that point.' _[Managing Growing Enterprises (Stanford GSB), WeWork, Quibi]_ — [2026-04-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9cdiQonikNU)
- **Understand VC incentives: funds need 100x-1000x outliers from a portfolio of dozens or hundreds, so a 3x or 5x outcome that is great for you is a zero for them.** This is why investors push grow-at-all-costs strategies and their advice may not match what they'd do in your exact shoes; the fund is optimizing its own bottom line. Before any opportunity, ask who is involved and what they are optimizing for. — [2026-04-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9cdiQonikNU)
- **Who you take on as an investor matters as much as the money, and founders vet investors' reputations behind the scenes.** Some VCs pitch themselves as hands-off 'smart capital'; YC has an internal Yelp-style review system where founders warn each other about investors who, for example, ghosted after issuing a term sheet. _[Y Combinator]_ — [2026-04-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9cdiQonikNU)
- **Don't cofound or hire your best friend unless you're prepared for the consequences: the shorthand and inherent trust that make the friendship strong lead to looser contracts and undefined expectations.** Friends assume they're on the same page and skip defining things they'd define with a stranger. 'It's not personal, it's business' can never be true with a friend. One of Cherie's best friends refuses to hire friends because she values the friendship too much. — [2026-04-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PsDiVdzKIBo)
- **The best companies start when the founder is solving a real personal pain point.** (Jason Wilk) Wilk was constantly overdrafting his checking account while starting his first business, paying $34 and up to $100 a day in fees; after selling that company he used his cushion to attack the problem. _[Dave]_ — [2026-05-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hxtgSnFdQ3E)
- **You do not need domain experience to disrupt an industry; a dedicated entrepreneur working seven days a week learns faster than an MBA's couple of hours of class a day.** (Jason Wilk) Wilk had no banking or fintech background and had to convince banks and processors to work with Dave when bank partnerships, debit processing and ACH for small-dollar lending barely existed; 'anyone can do it.' — [2026-05-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hxtgSnFdQ3E)
- **Second-time founders are much more successful because hiring, firing, fundraising and growth planning become muscle memory.** (Jason Wilk) His Y Combinator company taught him to raise capital and meet investors like Mark Cuban, so Dave got 'off to the races' much faster; the hosts call this chutzpah built from prior exits. _[Y Combinator, Mark Cuban]_ — [2026-05-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hxtgSnFdQ3E)
- **A downturn can build a moat: when VC funding dries up, smaller competitors die and the leaders take share, and high rates push credit-denied customers to alternative products.** (Jason Wilk) Seeing a fintech with 12 million consumers trade at a $50 million market cap shut off venture markets for Dave's competitors; big banks cutting credit cards and personal loans sent new customers to Dave. Patience was well worth it. _[Dave]_ — [2026-05-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hxtgSnFdQ3E)
- **A direct-to-consumer public company should resist becoming B2B while in recovery mode because investor clarity about who you serve drives valuation.** (Jason Wilk) Wilk is asked constantly about licensing the underwriting model but says mixing D2C and B2B muddies the story; instead Dave plans longer-duration loans for things like plane tickets and school books. _[Dave]_ — [2026-05-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hxtgSnFdQ3E)
- **In a regulated industry you must genuinely believe in the products you ship, and expect to have to educate regulators used to legacy models.** (Jason Wilk) Dave's original free-to-borrow, optional-tip model drew regulatory scrutiny ('who would tip a financial institution?'), but it makes sense for customers paying $400 a year in overdraft fees; tips averaged a few dollars and Dave has donated over $20 million to Feeding America. _[Dave, Feeding America, FTC, DOJ]_ — [2026-05-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hxtgSnFdQ3E)
- **Lenses succeeded because they were an invitation to create that lowered the barrier to self-expression and gave people a reason to share.** (Evan Spiegel) Early on people asked why they would ever take a selfie, but once they could vomit a rainbow or wear dog ears they wanted to share those moments with friends; AR lenses are now used more than 9 billion times a day. _[Snapchat lenses]_ — [2026-05-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0IYBJXS-1t8)
- **Design differs from art because design has an objective; once you understand exactly what a product is trying to achieve, the design reveals itself.** (Evan Spiegel) Snapchat's core purpose was sending an image very fast, so it opens to the camera, made picking friends on the send-to page easy, and co-founder Bobby Murphy invented a faster image-sending method that cut a minute-long upload to seconds. _[Snapchat, Bobby Murphy]_ — [2026-05-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0IYBJXS-1t8)
- **Snapchat is deliberately less polished and more playful so people feel comfortable expressing themselves, because an app that takes itself too seriously kills in-the-moment sharing.** (Evan Spiegel) Before Snapchat every photo was meant to be saved forever so people only shot special events; in 15 years the majority use case of the camera shifted to communication. Casual colors and design create a place to talk with friends rather than a portrait studio. Cherie contrasts it with LinkedIn, which feels professional but not alive. _[Snapchat, LinkedIn]_ — [2026-05-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0IYBJXS-1t8)
- **In the AI era any software feature can be copied in minutes, so a durable moat must be an ecosystem or network effect, and owning the full hardware and software stack is how Specs stays hard to replicate.** (Evan Spiegel) Snap has been relentlessly copied, so it built defensible assets: an AR developer ecosystem, a content ecosystem and a messaging business with network effects. For Specs it owns the developer tools, rendering engine, operating system and a self-designed optical engine, compounding technical advantages over 12 years. _[Specs, Snap]_ — [2026-05-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0IYBJXS-1t8)
- **The defining characteristic of great entrepreneurs is an incredibly high pain tolerance, because innovation is by definition misunderstood and constantly criticized.** (Evan Spiegel) Jensen Huang, Jeff Bezos and Elon Musk were ridiculed for their visions, and Musk saved Tesla from bankruptcy; early Snapchat investors said good luck competing with Facebook. Acknowledging upfront that people will call your idea stupid builds the resilience to execute through public-market short-termism. _[Jensen Huang, Jeff Bezos, Elon Musk, Tesla, Facebook]_ — [2026-05-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0IYBJXS-1t8)
- **Having a co-founder or co-pilot makes putting yourself out there and building anything easier because you can bounce ideas off each other.** Jean, a very private person, made a complete career pivot into media and credits having her sister as a partner; Cherie notes Jean's transformation over two years shows anyone can do it. — [2026-06-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iAt9bx4ENpA)
- **Founder imposter syndrome persists even after success when you started something by accident out of a need.** (Ally Maki) Ally started AAGC by asking 'does anyone else feel this way' and people came from everywhere; she still thinks of herself as 'just an actor' and is learning to step into 'I am a founder'. _[Asian American Girl Club]_ — [2026-06-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iAt9bx4ENpA)
- **A founder's only responsibility is to do everything they said they would do; carrying the weight of the next Black or female founder is not their job.** (Kofi Ampadu) Underrepresented founders often refuse to quit because they fear closing the door for those after them. Kofi says investors know it is a high-failure environment; trust is lost when founders promise XYZ and do ABC, not when an honest effort fails. — [2026-06-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6aXU9HjWnK8)
- **Resilience is a double-edged sword: being too resilient compounds your opportunity cost.** (Kofi Ampadu) After several pivots that are not working, refusing to give up can cost a founder more than walking away would. Kofi says he constantly has this conversation with founders. — [2026-06-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6aXU9HjWnK8)
- **Being 'all in' as a founder means cutting your burn, including your pride: Kofi gave up his Manhattan apartment to live in his sister's basement.** (Kofi Ampadu) He could not hold two truths, a nice apartment where friends pre-gamed and a claim that the company needed money. He contributed groceries and childcare for his niece instead of rent, and it became a good story for VC pitches about how committed he was. — [2026-06-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6aXU9HjWnK8)
- **Founders who raise money should pay themselves the least on the team, enough to live but not enough to save for vacations, because the payoff comes at the end.** (Kofi Ampadu) Kofi frames founder salary as part of reducing burn and signaling commitment. — [2026-06-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6aXU9HjWnK8)
- **Being a founder was the lowest-earning but happiest period of Kofi's life because of complete agency over solving problems.** (Kofi Ampadu) As an employee you often cannot solve the issue in front of you; as a founder you can hire, learn or build your way out. His first company, H2Melon, was a health beverage brand when $10 12-ounce juices were normal; his entrepreneurial streak started with burning mixtape CDs and charging underclassmen lunch-delivery fees in high school. _[H2Melon]_ — [2026-06-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6aXU9HjWnK8)
- **Building a company is a marathon run at a fast pace, not a permanent 90-hour week; some miles are five-minute pace and some seven.** (Kofi Ampadu) In the true-or-false segment Kofi rejects the idea that founders must work 90 hours a week; there will be such weeks, but breaks matter, which he tells founders at TxO retreats. — [2026-06-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6aXU9HjWnK8)
- **Y Combinator is not a scam because it has product-market fit: even taking 5% for no money, people would still apply for the network of potential customers.** (Kofi Ampadu) Kofi says for a SaaS founder, other successful YC companies become customers and feedback sources, which alone justifies the value. _[Y Combinator]_ — [2026-06-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6aXU9HjWnK8)
- **You do not need an Ivy League degree or coding skills to start a company, but you must bring something valuable such as selling, marketing or hiring the best talent.** (Kofi Ampadu) A degree signals intelligence but is not a prerequisite, which is the premise of TxO. With AI tools, non-coders can build, but do not be the 'idea person' whose only valuable asset is their engineer. Kofi also says raising is statistically easier for a 'white guy in a hoodie' but hard for everyone, building in public gives real customer discovery and signals speed to investors, and a pitch deck that lies is a bad start to the investor-founder relationship. _[TxO]_ — [2026-06-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6aXU9HjWnK8)
- **When you realize you are delivering more value than your hourly fee reflects, renegotiate to capture equity instead.** (Fernando De Leon) At 14 he was paid a few hundred dollars for translation, realized he was actually enabling developers to execute their business plan, then used his grandmother's relationship with a union leader to secure job permits and traded them for 5% equity in the building, repeating the deal four or five more times by age 15. — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **Find new opportunities by asking what the opposite of a bad business is and entering adjacent fields from your area of edge.** (Fernando De Leon) Offered an office building he disliked (high capex, tenant rollover, unpredictable cash flows), he asked what its opposite was and decided data was the best commodity to own in commercial real estate, co-founding Crexi, now the second-largest purveyor of commercial real estate data with ~5 million users, hundreds of thousands of subscribers and ~600 employees. _[Crexi]_ — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **Starting from zero, replace capital with creativity, persuasion, salesmanship and sweat equity to create value you can later extract.** (Fernando De Leon) In Dallas at 24 he optioned land with a piece of paper, persuaded the city to rezone from agricultural to residential to raise its value, convinced engineers to work before being paid, and got 80-something landowner Harold Pullman to contribute land he had thought unbuildable for 40 years; it took a year to get Army Corps of Engineers and city approvals. _[Harold Pullman, Army Corps of Engineers]_ — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **When the traditional path requires capital and gatekeepers, pivot to a version you can build on your own terms.** The hosts originally pitched Tiger Sisters as a reality TV show, hit Hollywood strikes and the capital and relationship requirements of streaming, mourned it for three days, then launched a podcast from their dining table with a $400 camera because the barriers to entry were nearly zero. _[Tiger Sisters]_ — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **Founders chasing AI hype or a valuation number forget the only thing that matters: whether consumers actually derive value from the product.** (Fernando De Leon) Valuation groupthink inside a bubble bursts when the goods reach real consumers who find they get nothing from them; authenticity and value to the end user must permeate the work and cannot be faked. — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **Mark Cuban is overrated as a business genius.** (Fernando De Leon) Broadcast.com was sold to Yahoo, which wrote its value down to zero within about a year and a half, and Shark Tank investments appear to be mostly non-scalable consumer products. _[Mark Cuban, Broadcast.com, Yahoo, Shark Tank]_ — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **Companies must choose between cost leadership and differentiation; you cannot be the cheapest, the best and the highest quality all at the same time.** Walmart is the cost leader ('everyday savings'); Starbucks chose differentiation via premium pricing, the third space, customer service and small touches like writing names on cups. Sisters Matcha went the differentiation route because its Okumidori cultivar makes up only 2% of Japanese matcha and supplier costs make a cost-leadership brand impossible and off-brand for them. _[Porter's generic strategies, Walmart, Starbucks, Sisters Matcha]_ — [2026-06-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=x9IXVlikKpk)
- **STP (segmenting, targeting, positioning) is the backbone of any marketing campaign: pick one segment and build the whole brand around its hopes, frustrations and values.** Warby Parker broke Luxottica's near-90% grip on eyewear by segmenting buyers into luxury, cost-conscious and millennial groups, targeting cost-conscious millennials at a $95-$100 price point who are online-first and care about social impact (buy a pair, give a pair), and positioning as modern, chic, accessible and digital-first. If you try to serve everyone you serve no one; Warby Parker is now valued at over a billion dollars. _[Warby Parker, Luxottica, Ray-Ban, Toms, Rothy's, Blueland]_ — [2026-06-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=x9IXVlikKpk)
- **The number one product-development lesson from HBS is that you cannot guess at a customer's problem; you have to identify it precisely, test relentlessly and pivot when new data arrives.** Netflix is the case: it A/B tests everything down to the thumbnail shown per user (Jean notes she always gets Henry Golding thumbnails). Cherie met Reed Hastings at Stanford GSB and did a book report on his book. _[Netflix, Reed Hastings, A/B testing]_ — [2026-06-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=x9IXVlikKpk)
- **Starbucks' competitive moat looks weaker a year later because Luckin Coffee and Blank Street are taking share with novel drinks and younger cachet.** Luckin has a $10B market cap vs Starbucks' $110B but already 31,000 stores (including its first ten in the US) vs Starbucks' roughly 30,000, and won China with products like a coconut velvet latte and a Moutai baijiu coffee. Blank Street, valued at $500M and reportedly raising $100M at about a $1B valuation, appeals to Gen Z in a way Starbucks struggles to. _[Luckin Coffee, Blank Street Coffee, Starbucks, Blue Bottle, Moutai]_ — [2026-06-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=x9IXVlikKpk)
- **A new coffee entrant can beat Starbucks on how fast it iterates its menu and how good the drinks are, because many customers are tired of Starbucks.** Cherie says Starbucks 'is not really for me' anymore and that special items like coconut foam or sea salt maple lattes live in smaller cafes; if Luckin iterates like that it puts Starbucks in a pinch. Jean counters that Starbucks keeps innovating (e.g. jumping on the matcha craze) to hold share, even if its matcha is bad. _[Starbucks, Luckin Coffee]_ — [2026-06-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=x9IXVlikKpk)
- **Conscious consumerism and give-back programs, once a novel differentiator for millennial brands, are now taken for granted as part of many companies' DNA.** Toms' buy-a-pair-give-a-pair shoes 10-15 years ago and Warby Parker's glasses program created brand loyalists who felt the brand represented their morals; later brands like Rothy's and Blueland carried the sustainability bend forward. _[Toms, Warby Parker, Rothy's, Blueland]_ — [2026-06-22](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=x9IXVlikKpk)
