- **Co-regulate with a partner by first regulating yourself, then asking consent to share, and using I-statements to explain the trigger rather than projecting onto them.** (Sabrina Zohar) When her partner Ryan said 'no' to an outing, she spiraled, realized she felt seven and it was her dismissive father, then said 'when you said no I shut down; it reminded me of my dad; could you share your why so I don't feel alone on an island.' They have never had that issue since. — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **Boundaries don't keep people out, they protect what's in; they are for you, and no one can cross your boundary, only reveal whether they respect it.** (Sabrina Zohar) A match who mocked her 'let's meet in person, no endless app chat' preference as 'rushing to the altar' didn't cross her boundary, he showed he didn't respect it. If you don't take calls after 9, someone calling doesn't cross the boundary; you cross your own by answering. A DBT practice: think of 'future you' when setting the boundary. _[DBT]_ — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **Boundaries should be flexible rather than rigid, and setting them means being OK with disappointing people, judged by whether you are being hurtful or harmful.** (Sabrina Zohar) Declining lunch because of a big meeting may hurt feelings; refusing to drive a friend to surgery for a self-care day is harmful. You can afford to hurt feelings, not to harm. — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **How people respond when you set a boundary is deeply revealing; if they are offended rather than just disappointed, maybe they aren't meant to be in your life.** (Sabrina Zohar) Jean, at 30 and in her 'Saturn return', has been setting boundaries in friendships over the past year and a half. Sabrina ended a ten-year codependent friendship after a 14-hour shoot day when the friend replied 'you wasted my time' to her saying she needed to go home early, and says she has never been better. — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **Enter new relationships with boundaries already set and make people earn their place rather than handing over 'the keys to the castle' because they say it's a pretty house.** (Sabrina Zohar) Frame past patterns as 'what did I used to accept that I'm no longer willing to' and take accountability without shame: 'I allowed it, maybe out of trauma or attachment, and now I choose differently.' — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **If you feel 'too much' in dating, you are usually with people who lack bandwidth or are emotionally unavailable; if I'm too much, go find less.** (Sabrina Zohar) Using IFS 'fusion' language: you have anxious attachment, you are not it; ask who taught you that you're too much. Her father had no patience for her emotions, but her partner thinks she is 'just enough' because she stopped shrinking. Cherie relates via feeling like a burden, which traces to a 6-7 year old raised by a single mother without bandwidth. _[IFS (Internal Family Systems)]_ — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **Internet dating gurus like 'if he wanted to, he would', 'sprinkle sprinkle' and 'daddy academies' are outcome-driven formulas that teach self-abandonment and keep people single longer.** (Sabrina Zohar) Business works as 'do X get Y' but relationships don't; want and do are different parts of the brain. She blocked a creator who called her advice 'low value' for endorsing coffee dates; she argues a 30-minute coffee respects her time more than a 2-3 hour dinner with a stranger, and worth comes from morals and ethics, not whether it's dinner or drinks. Gurus give formulaic behaviors without teaching the underlying value system. _[Shera Seven, if he wanted to, he would]_ — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **Trying to control another person's actions or the outcome never works; a strong relationship is two self-healed pillars communicating effectively.** Sabrina broke every 'rule' with her partner (slept together the first night) but told him plainly 'I don't do casual; if you call it's because you want to build something, don't waste my time.' He later said he loved that she wasn't afraid to lose him, only afraid of losing herself. They make a conscious choice to choose each other every day. — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **An even-keeled partner who grounds you is the first call in a crisis.** (Andrew Yaffe) Married nearly 20 years since college, he says he swings high and low while his wife brings him back to reality, and he does not thank her enough. — [2026-03-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jy1d4_UQA4o)
- **Use AI as a reframing device for relationship problems, even through a playful lens like astrology, because having something to react to helps you see what feels right.** A friend uploads dating conflicts and asks for an interpretation based on the partner's sign; the value is a third POV that pushes you to think differently, not advice to follow. — [2026-03-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4J3C-Rrzpo4)
- **Do not put anyone on a pedestal: once you decide someone is extraordinary, you edit out every reason they are not.** Jean dated a cancer survivor whose story made her assume maturity and morality; she ignored him telling her she'd be 'perfect at 112 lbs', pressuring her to quit her job, and cheating with an ex-girlfriend Jean had even lent a designer dress to. Friends said she was 'so out of his league', which people only say when they mean it. — [2026-04-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PsDiVdzKIBo)
- **Pedestal check exercise: ask whether you like the person or the idea of them, name the prevailing reason for the pedestal, then tally their actions for and against that attribute.** Ask what you like beyond resume values like job, school or accolades, and whether they've exhibited traits that make you independently respect them. Jean says she wishes she had done this tally. — [2026-04-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PsDiVdzKIBo)
- **Mind the age gap: what matters is not the number of years but the life stage, and when you are the younger woman a power imbalance is baked in whether or not the other person exerts it.** Cherie at 19-20 dated a Harvard grad 4 years older working at McKinsey who became half boyfriend, half mentor; she second- and triple-guessed everything she said and it failed because she was so in her head, even though he was kind. The older person inherently has more financial power, life experience and emotional leverage. _[McKinsey, Harvard]_ — [2026-04-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PsDiVdzKIBo)
- **Listen to your gut when something feels off in a power-imbalanced situation, even if you can't name it yet.** At 20, Jean went on a ski date with a 33-year-old ex-Goldman hedge fund founder she met at a Tuck private equity conference at Dartmouth; at his house (with taxidermy) she felt 'I'm not in control' and left. At 37 she says dating a 13-year gap would be fine because both are formed adults. _[Goldman Sachs, Tuck School of Business, Dartmouth]_ — [2026-04-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PsDiVdzKIBo)
- **Age gap relationships work best when both people are fully formed adults with financial independence and strong boundaries; timing matters more than the number.** Financial independence matters so you never feel you owe the other person for what they provide, especially when you are in a vulnerable state. — [2026-04-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PsDiVdzKIBo)
- **Don't date for money, date for track record: ask whether you'd still date them if they lost it all, because resilience and ambition let them rebuild.** Look for trajectory, not current status; you want someone who can build wealth with you, not just someone who has it. A June 2025 National Library of Medicine study of 3,700 households found financial resilience buffers the negative effects of economic setbacks on happiness and mental health, and 'every 10 years something happens that has never happened before.' _[National Library of Medicine study (June 2025, 3,700 households)]_ — [2026-04-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PsDiVdzKIBo)
- **Partner attributes to look for over time: resilience, discipline and work ethic, long-term thinking, ability to rebuild after setbacks, plus loyalty, integrity and character.** These can only be observed after being with someone through uncomfortable or challenging situations. Loyalty applies if you yourself are a loyal person. — [2026-04-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PsDiVdzKIBo)
- **Treat dating like a scientist collecting data points: zoom out of your own head and observe why the person acts as they do and how you react, because decisions should be about what makes you happy.** Character reveals itself when you bring something up or ask follow-up questions in a challenging situation. Every relationship is data on where your boundaries are and when you speak up. — [2026-04-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PsDiVdzKIBo)
- **Don't shit where you eat: workplace relationships bring power imbalance, reputation consequences and emotional fallout, and breakups don't stay inside the bubble.** One third of US workers have dated a coworker (SHRM); a 2024 SHRM survey found 68% of HR professionals cite favoritism perceptions and 61% cite conflicts of interest as top concerns. Cherie dated someone at her first job on a different team and was distracted worrying about running into him in the cafe when she wanted to focus on her career. _[Society for Human Resource Management (SHRM)]_ — [2026-04-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PsDiVdzKIBo)
- **If you do date a coworker later in your career, discuss what happens if you break up up front, like a prenup for the workplace relationship.** Timing matters: more mature people at the same company can make it work, but young people have no space for the mess and drama at work. — [2026-04-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PsDiVdzKIBo)
- **Beware the love bomber: an 8-hour first date followed by a drop-in visit days later is a red flag, even when it's the person's natural salesperson personality rather than deliberate manipulation.** Jean, fresh out of an 8-year engagement, didn't clock the intensity because she was used to daily contact with a live-in fiance. Only when she saw the guy charm her friends did she realize being loved by everyone was his default mode, and that he was a self-described people pleaser. — [2026-04-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PsDiVdzKIBo)
- **Moments of apartness early in dating are when you can actually evaluate how you feel; love bombing removes them and feels amazing, which is exactly why you need a pause.** Being love bombed makes you feel like the best person in the world and critical thinking and the prefrontal cortex go out the window. — [2026-04-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PsDiVdzKIBo)
- **Don't carry boundaries calibrated for a long-term relationship into a new one; new people must unlock levels of access by earning trust over time.** Jean gave a brand-new partner the lax boundaries that took 8 years of trust to build with her ex. - **In business and love, charisma is not credibility: apply the same skepticism to an overselling suitor that you'd apply to an overhyped product ('prove it, where's the free trial').** Trust the people who build slowly and consistently over time; be as discerning with your time, emotions and body as you are with your money. — [2026-04-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PsDiVdzKIBo)
- **To find a partner, be who you are, get out of the house and meet people separately from your sibling, use friends and blind dates, but don't rush it.** (Rahm Emanuel) He met his wife Amy on a blind date in their 30s (she thought he was arrogant, he thought she was cold, 'we were both right'), 31 years ago. - **Friends who commiserate when you are down but go silent when you win are seeing a mirror of the work they did not do; cut frenemies and judge your success by whether you cheer for yourself.** (Vivian Tu) Responding to the hosts' loss of peer support after leaving corporate jobs, she asks herself daily whether she is proud of her work. - **The red thread in money conversations with friends is not money but whether they love you; a bride who goes bridezilla over a budget never loved the friend, only being a show pony.** (Vivian Tu) These mature conversations require prep: the bridesmaid must actually know her budget rather than pulling a number from thin air. — [2026-05-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ULlhVsNmU9M)
- **Run your bachelorette like user research: send an anonymous survey on dates, budget, vibe and location so no friend feels constrained by money.** (Vivian Tu) She sent a SurveyMonkey seen only by her because her friends range from pro bono attorneys to big-three consultants; every invitee later copied the survey for their own bachelorettes. _[SurveyMonkey]_ — [2026-05-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ULlhVsNmU9M)

Cherie did two rounds. — [2026-05-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OzsWwmX-D50)
- **Freezing eggs rather than embryos is now nearly as successful and preserves optionality when you are not sure about your partner.** Jean froze eggs while on the verge of an engagement that later ended; her doctor said improved freezing technology has narrowed the success gap between eggs and embryos, which she says 'saved her life' by not tying her to an ex. — [2026-05-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OzsWwmX-D50)
- **Fertility preservation relaxes the 'musical chairs' pressure on women in their late 30s and 40s so they do not settle into a bad relationship just to get the kid.** (Sabrina Jalees) Sabrina notes men do not face this predicament; Cherie says having her eggs frozen means she no longer feels pressure to find the right person right now. Egg freezing should be seen as a new timeline, not a concession. — [2026-05-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OzsWwmX-D50)
- **US fertility is at a historic low and the question has flipped from 'why wouldn't you have kids' to 'why would you'.** The hosts cite the Congressional Budget Office projecting 1.58-1.63 births per woman as of 2026, after an all-time low of about 1.6 in 2024, and a stat that for the first time more US births are to women in their 40s than to teens. _[Congressional Budget Office]_ — [2026-05-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OzsWwmX-D50)
- **Keep a list of negotiables and non-negotiables after each breakup; the non-negotiables grow and reveal why past relationships failed.** (Natasha Leggero) Natasha's superficial wants (British, plays guitar) gave way to essentials: a partner who lets her shine, understands her drive, won't cheat (a roving eye 'eats you alive'), and genuinely wants kids, which is how she knew he'd be a good parent. — [2026-05-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OzsWwmX-D50)
- **The traits that make a good life partner, kindness and empathy that solve problems as a couple rather than fighting for one's own way, are the same traits that make a good co-parent.** (Sabrina Jalees) Sabrina says beauty is the first draft but kindness makes it sustainable; she knew her wife was 'the mother of my children' when she pulled out a giant box of art supplies on an early trip. People who think about others' experiences and adventures will do the same for a kid. — [2026-05-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OzsWwmX-D50)
- **Red flags do not change; if there are red flags, run.** (Sabrina Jalees) Sabrina initially said couples can 'get there together' over a long arc before kids, but reverses after noting Tiger Woods' drunk-driving pattern was a Chelsea Lately topic 15 years ago: 'patterns be patterns'. _[Tiger Woods, Chelsea Lately]_ — [2026-05-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OzsWwmX-D50)
- **Meeting a partner after both of you already have companies forces you to build a strong family structure from the start despite the chaos.** (Evan Spiegel) Spiegel met Miranda Kerr at 24 at a friend's dinner in New York where Harper's Bazaar editor Glenda Bailey predicted they would marry; she messaged him a month later and their first date was his first-ever Kundalini yoga class. He knew young that he wanted a big family and to marry young because his own parents had him late; they now have four boys. _[Miranda Kerr, Glenda Bailey, Harper's Bazaar]_ — [2026-05-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0IYBJXS-1t8)
- **A partner who has already handled public scrutiny can teach a private person grace and poise under attention, and both partners should act as coaches unafraid to say the tough thing.** (Evan Spiegel) Miranda grew up in the public eye and helped Spiegel, who disliked attention and feared public speaking, handle sudden fame; she is brutally honest with him (including fixing his dry skin) and he tries to be a gentle but real coach for her business and leadership. Friends and family who keep it real are essential. _[Miranda Kerr]_ — [2026-05-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0IYBJXS-1t8)
- **The most practical dating advice is to be clear upfront about what you are looking for and skip the games.** (Evan Spiegel) Early on Miranda said she was just figuring things out after a marriage; 24-year-old Spiegel replied that he wanted something serious and it would not be the right fit otherwise. Communicating what matters in a loving, positive way forms the foundation of a strong relationship and avoids wasting time. _[Miranda Kerr]_ — [2026-05-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0IYBJXS-1t8)
- **Adult friendships need intentional, recurring structure, like an annual retreat with a fixed group.** Jean and about 20 women from her HBS class meet for one weekend every year, sometimes with programming like a fireside chat with a classmate who is CFO of a public company. _[Harvard Business School]_ — [2026-06-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iAt9bx4ENpA)
- **Friendship is all about follow-up, and it takes two; it is fine for friendships and your social life to fade in and out across seasons.** Cherie says a friend may be in a busy season with work or a new partner, and it is okay to be very social for a quarter or a year and then less so. — [2026-06-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iAt9bx4ENpA)
- **Audience survey results: the top dating red flag is having no friends, people overspend most on eating out and DoorDash, and AI is the number one thing people pretend to understand.** From the Tiger Family Feud segment polling about 150 attendees; 'we're like a family' was voted the worst corporate phrase and girls trips tied with new hobbies as the best heartbreak cure. — [2026-06-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iAt9bx4ENpA)
- **A partner in a totally different profession is an asset because they puncture the tech echo chamber and give a 'regular person' perspective.** (Kofi Ampadu) Kofi's wife is a journalist, documentarian and photographer who took product photos for his startup, is skeptical of AI hype, and asks 'why do you need AI for that?' They hold 'brown bag lunch' nerd dates where each researches a topic (e.g. why Francophone African currencies are pegged to the French system) and pitches it to the other. The hosts say a partner should be a lifelong learning partner: the 'zombie apocalypse standard' is not being bored with them. — [2026-06-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6aXU9HjWnK8)
- **Surround yourself with people who do not second-guess your decisions but will pick you up if you fall.** (Kofi Ampadu) Kofi credits his wife as his 'eternal co-founder' through the peaks and valleys of entrepreneurship, and notes it helps to have an outlet outside your actual co-founder. — [2026-06-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6aXU9HjWnK8)
- **In a relationship you need a partner who sees you at your most vulnerable and compels you to keep going.** (Fernando De Leon) After selling assets in 2007 and struggling on his first deals back in 2008, he nearly quit entrepreneurship for a job; his newly married wife told him they would not last if he did, because he had to be an entrepreneur, and pushed him harder than he pushed himself. — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **For ambitious women, matching credentials matters less than a partner who loves you, understands you deeply and supports your goals without ego.** At a bachelorette with 10 HBS women, the hosts noted many friends turned out to be the breadwinners and one said the same-credentials requirement was not what mattered; Fernando adds that his wife embracing his family was foundational. _[Harvard Business School]_ — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **Partners need to share strongly presenting values like radius (desire for systemic change) and belovedness, or the relationship will be a constant fight about priorities.** (Dr. Suzy Welch) The test gives couples a conflict/harmony score; Welch's daughter and fiance had identical top six values. Cherie recalls an ex in her 20s who wanted a plain house in the suburbs while she dreamed of impact, which Welch calls a near-100% conflict. _[Values Bridge conflict/harmony score]_ — [2026-06-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Asm0bCXqd1s)
- **Belovedness, the desire for an intimate partner relationship, often shows up as a top value with a huge gap for women who have been told to 'girlboss' their careers.** (Dr. Suzy Welch) Welch sees women with belovedness at number one and a 98% gap who never felt able to say they also want marriage. She recommends both partners have belovedness in the same tier (both top five or both bottom five). — [2026-06-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Asm0bCXqd1s)
- **A dual-career marriage worked because the partners shared values, talked constantly, and respected each other's lanes of expertise.** (Dr. Suzy Welch) Welch was never CEO of GE managing 400,000 people and Jack never wrote or edited books; he had very high agency and she very low, and she thinks two high-agency partners would have been a different story. _[Jack Welch, GE]_ — [2026-06-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Asm0bCXqd1s)
- **When your partner says something you disagree with, silently assume 'maybe he's right' and hear them out non-defensively before stating where you differ.** (Dr. Suzy Welch) Welch started from the supposition that Jack loved her, trusted him and wanted the best for the marriage; sometimes he was right, and other times her openness let her explain her view so he'd say 'I never thought of that'. The hosts note this takes immense self-regulation. _[Jack Welch]_ — [2026-06-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Asm0bCXqd1s)
- **The 'swag gap' is real: a couple with very different levels of beholderism struggles to coexist even if they look perfect on paper.** Jean ended things with a man who later sent her a Wall Street Journal article on the swag gap; she wishes credentials on paper could capture someone's love of art and aesthetics. The hosts say everyone judges a book by its cover in how you show up. _[Wall Street Journal 'swag gap' article, Codie Sanchez (women who wear makeup make 30% more)]_ — [2026-06-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Asm0bCXqd1s)
- **The more you say no to certain things, the stronger your yeses become, and healthy family boundaries let you engage more wholeheartedly without underlying resentment.** (Jane Marie Chen) Jane says boundaries with family are hard because we want to love them, especially around holidays, but boundaries remove the angst that poisons the relationship. — [2026-07-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jGj7bm3v0kY)
- **Writing mantras at the top of your journal every morning can carry you through a breakup by making you less attached to the outcome and more grounded in yourself.** During a breakup in business school Cherie wrote Peterson's four mantras daily for about a month; she says the journal looks like a crazy person wrote it, but it genuinely made her feel better and more centered. _[Joel Peterson]_ — [2026-08-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3125mje95W0)
- **The most consistent finding of Harvard's 85-year happiness study is that positive relationships, not career, money, exercise or diet, keep us happier and healthier and help us live longer.** Peterson's slides cite the study begun in 1938 with 724 participants questioned at two-year intervals. The hosts say this is why the podcast covers money, power and love, including platonic, family and self love. _[Harvard Study of Adult Development, Joel Peterson]_ — [2026-08-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PHdNriPxQgk)
- **The person you choose as your partner is the most important decision you will make, because it shapes how you feel every single day.** Whether you come home stressed or relaxed depends on your person. Peterson brought his wife Diana into class so students could hear how the life was engineered from her side; being 'self-partnered' as Emma Watson put it is equally legitimate if nobody meets your criteria. _[Joel Peterson, Diana Peterson, Emma Watson]_ — [2026-08-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PHdNriPxQgk)
- **Look for a partner who is complementary rather than identical, like a striver paired with roots.** Peterson describes himself as the striver climbing Kilimanjaro and planning trips while Diana is the tree keeping the family rooted in home and traditions; with their seven kids he came from above with discipline while she got down on the floor at their level. Cherie likens it to a founder hiring for the skills they lack. _[Joel Peterson, Diana Peterson]_ — [2026-08-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PHdNriPxQgk)
- **Operationalize love: list five to seven key people, set an objective for each relationship and define how you will measure it, because flowy love without objectives falls by the wayside.** Cherie's example for best friend Esha: objective 'reignite our friendship,' measures monthly calls, a yearly ski trip, small touchpoints like sending memes, and monthly handwritten cards. Other objectives include 'maintain homeostasis,' 'deepen relationship,' 'grow deeper in love'; measures include weekly FaceTimes, weekly dates and planned trips. _[Joel Peterson]_ — [2026-08-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PHdNriPxQgk)
- **Listing your five to seven people is also an accounting exercise that reveals whether your energy matches your intentions.** Jean points out you may discover you spend lots of energy on someone you don't intend to be close with, or only talk monthly to someone you want to build with; Cherie crossed an ex-best-friend off her list, freeing time for the others. — [2026-08-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PHdNriPxQgk)
- **Write two lists for a life partner, must-haves and nice-to-haves, and be honest that some attributes like physical attractiveness fade over 20-30 years.** Peterson's attribute list includes shared values, strong work ethic, parallel politics, family compatibility, common roots, sense of humor, a great gene pool ('hybrid vigor'), devotion and fidelity, similar tastes, smart and shared vocations. Jean says parallel politics never came up for her until the last couple of years but now clearly belongs on the list. _[Joel Peterson]_ — [2026-08-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PHdNriPxQgk)

### Family, childhood & siblings (24)

- **Incel vocabulary like Chad, giga-Chad, mewing, mogging and looksmaxxing has become ambient culture that even 10-year-olds absorb.** (Ron Busby) Ron's 10-year-old cousin understands these terms without buying in, which primes him to navigate what other men are saying; a 'Chad' is an effortlessly formidable alpha archetype that incels compete against and that is itself denied the ability to be insecure or broken. _[Chad, 4B movement]_ — [2026-02-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F783rcVaDqw)
- **Difficulty with boundaries usually traces back to childhood, including 'little t' traumas, not only big traumas.** (Sabrina Zohar) Growing up in a narcissistic household where the narcissist is the sun and everyone orbits them, saying no meant being hit or alienated, so she learned 'play quiet and they'll love you', which is maladaptive as an adult. A mother working full time and being absent is a little-t trauma that still leaves a mark without the parents being bad. — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **The most powerful role is parent, because parents shape the next generation and family is the longest-serving institution in human history.** (Rahm Emanuel) Asked to name the most powerful role besides president, he answered 'parent' before discussing titles; family is the least discussed topic because people fear sounding judgmental, yet nurturing it is the most important thing society can do. — [2026-04-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ptYgxhdshaA)
- **Intense sibling competition and fierce loyalty can coexist: the brothers tell each other they're stupid but will 'kill' anyone who criticizes one of them.** (Rahm Emanuel) He, Ari and Zeke grew up 16 months apart sharing a room, with grandparents, a foster brother and patients' children living with them. Today all three families, their kids and spouses are on one group chat where the cousins critique the brothers. _[Ari Emanuel, Zeke Emanuel]_ — [2026-04-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ptYgxhdshaA)
- **Protect family rituals like regular dinners: no child was ever spoiled by being told too often they were loved.** (Rahm Emanuel) Despite not controlling his schedule, he kept four family dinners a week including a Friday Sabbath dinner so the kids knew who they were and their moral teachings. He quotes his pediatrician father on love, and says three doors determine a child's trajectory: home, school and place of worship. — [2026-04-27](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ptYgxhdshaA)
- **Immigrant parents often confuse looking rich (multiple cars and houses) with being rich; the billionaires she has met follow no such rules about what wealth means.** (Vivian Tu) Responding to the hosts' mom doubting they are doing well without multiple houses, she says truly wealthy people define wealth however they want, but also challenges the hosts to ask why they feel comfortable loosening the purse strings given the opportunity cost early in a lucrative career. — [2026-05-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ULlhVsNmU9M)
- **Anyone you respect who has kids will say it is the best part of their life; parenting is the next 'level' of the game where you shift from playing to shepherding.** (Sabrina Jalees) Sabrina admits the daunting cost of shelving your own needs, money and travel, but argues the rewards (a child teaching you 'God is hope plus love', people not addicted to phones) cannot be imagined by the childless. — [2026-05-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OzsWwmX-D50)
- **You don't get a 'bad kid'; whatever issues arise become the ride you are on, and how you handle them is your unique path through parenting.** (Sabrina Jalees) Sabrina says even behavioural issues are part of the journey, while acknowledging rare extreme cases; the core lesson is getting up after you fall down, the same trait that separates the graduates who 'make it'. — [2026-05-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OzsWwmX-D50)
- **If you struggle to like an aspect of your kid, it is really something you feel about yourself; your patience for them reflects your patience with yourself.** (Sabrina Jalees) Answering Jean's question about parents who secretly don't like their kids, Sabrina calls it inner-child work: kids are an extension of you, everyone should be in some therapy, and parents who outsource all parenting are the ones who end up disconnected. — [2026-05-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OzsWwmX-D50)
- **Social algorithms scare each demographic into thinking they are doing parenting wrong; the guests started their podcast to discuss the imperfections.** (Sabrina Jalees) Just as the sisters' feeds show 'pregnancy nose' TikToks, parents' feeds say pack this lunch or never say that to your kid; Sabrina says treat any slip as a lesson rather than a catastrophe. _[Good Enough podcast]_ — [2026-05-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OzsWwmX-D50)
- **Two kids is the sweet spot in a coastal city; three is too many pregnancies and too much competition for attention, and 'one is an accessory, two is a lifestyle'.** (Natasha Leggero) Natasha (one child, born when she was 43) says a single child needs constant playdates, but three kids in LA is a step backwards for a career woman; if you live somewhere cheaper in the middle of the country, have five. Both sisters say they ideally want three. — [2026-05-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OzsWwmX-D50)
- **Gen Z is a generation of realists because they grew up inside the hard reality that millennials only hit as speed bumps later in life.** (Evan Spiegel) Millennials grew up in the optimistic 90s and then hit 9/11, the financial crisis and the pandemic, so they retain optimism shaped by reality; Gen Z grew up with unaffordable education and housing and AI-shaped job prospects, so they focus on doing the best they can in a complicated world. _[Flynn (Spiegel's 15-year-old son)]_ — [2026-05-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0IYBJXS-1t8)
- **Teen freedom should be traded for demonstrated responsibility in an ongoing conversation, and that applies to social media the same as staying out late.** (Evan Spiegel) When Flynn wants more freedom, he must stay in touch with his mom, share which friend's house he is at and the parent's number, and come home when he said; demonstrated responsibility builds trust that earns more freedom. Independence at 18 is a gradual process, not a switch. _[Flynn]_ — [2026-05-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0IYBJXS-1t8)
- **Parents need to have a 'tech talk' with their kids the way they have the sex talk, focused on the real online risks like sextortion and drugs.** Cherie realizes she never had a conversation with her parents about using technology safely. Spiegel says schools do not teach these risks, so Snap built The Keys, an online safety program with a pop quiz that parents and teens do together; the hosts say it belongs in health class. _[The Keys (Snap online safety program)]_ — [2026-05-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0IYBJXS-1t8)
- **Immigrant and first-generation pressure to lift the whole family can make the youngest child act like the eldest and become entrepreneurial.** Fernando, youngest by 10-16 years, was the only US citizen and put the responsibility of improving all six siblings' lives on himself; the first person he told about getting into Harvard was the border agent who had watched him cross daily for school. The hosts wonder what share of the Forbes 250 are immigrants or first-gen. _[Forbes 250 Greatest Self-Made Americans, Harvard]_ — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **Children of immigrants over-achieve because of cultural expectations, the pressure to justify their parents' sacrifice, and a sense that nothing is ever good enough.** (Jane Marie Chen) Jane says no number of degrees, accolades or babies saved could fill the void, which she traces to childhood abuse: a child wonders 'what's wrong with me' and the adult tries to prove they are worthy. The hosts add the feeling of carrying the weight of all your ancestors and 'living out their dreams'. — [2026-07-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jGj7bm3v0kY)
- **Achievement becomes safety and belonging for kids who grew up as outsiders.** (Jane Marie Chen) Jane moved to the US at four without a word of English, in one of the only Asian families in her neighborhood, with parents who did not speak English; achieving gave her a sense of belonging. — [2026-07-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jGj7bm3v0kY)
- **Cycles of abuse get normalized in Asian culture as a cultural norm, so calling them out publicly helps others feel seen and less alone.** (Jane Marie Chen) Jane did not recognize her childhood beatings as abuse until her late 30s; the suicide of a Taiwanese-American high school friend from a violent home made her realize her silence was complicity. The hosts note that 'saving face' in Asian culture makes her candor especially brave. — [2026-07-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jGj7bm3v0kY)
- **Have compassion for your parents' whole story and lineage while still holding boundaries; understanding does not condone abuse but it brings inner peace.** (Jane Marie Chen) Writing the book meant exploring how her parents grew up and how trauma seeps into family systems. Her practice is to be open-hearted and boundaried simultaneously, tuning into her body's contraction around toxic behaviors and then choosing not to be around them. Holding resentment hurts yourself; her greatest freedom was feeling it and letting it go. — [2026-07-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jGj7bm3v0kY)
- **Confronting your parents is not about changing them; it is about taking the hand of your inner child and standing up for her, so you can trust yourself.** (Jane Marie Chen) Leadership coach Diana Chapman of the Conscious Leadership Group pushed Jane to talk to her parents, which she called the scariest thing she has ever done. She went in without expecting change, since expecting others to behave a certain way creates suffering; the hosts note saying your piece is itself the therapy whether or not the other person absorbs it. _[Diana Chapman, Conscious Leadership Group]_ — [2026-07-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jGj7bm3v0kY)
- **Write a two-page family history by interviewing parents, aunts, uncles and siblings, because you are where you come from whether or not you like it, and it anchors you instead of floating through the world.** Peterson shared his own story of grandparents, a father raised on an Iowa corn farm, childhood jobs and relatives in the World Wars. Guiding questions: where did family members grow up, what hardships did they face, why did they make their choices, what values kept getting passed down, what patterns kept repeating. For the sisters, education and hard work were triple-underlined values passed down for socioeconomic mobility. _[Joel Peterson]_ — [2026-08-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3125mje95W0)
- **Your family history does not define you, but you should not close your eyes to it; tracing beliefs and traditions back to events like a displacement a hundred years ago reveals patterns.** The hosts acknowledge some people come from troubled histories and the exercise can be uncomfortable; do only the parts you can, since even starting puts you ahead of 99.9% of people who never reflect on it. — [2026-08-03](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=3125mje95W0)
- **Couples should take stock of both family histories and intentionally choose which customs to carry into the next generation and which generational traumas to break.** Building on the family-history exercise from part one, Peterson taught that it is up to both partners to pick the best of both backgrounds; Cherie had never thought about it that deliberately before. _[Joel Peterson]_ — [2026-08-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PHdNriPxQgk)
- **Co-founders who are also sisters, co-hosts and roommates need an explicit objective for their relationship: staying fulfilling, energy-giving and fun, not just mission-driven.** Doing the exercise live for each other, the sisters set the baseline as not pissing each other off and joke that the measure is co-founder therapy. — [2026-08-10](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PHdNriPxQgk)

### Mindset, confidence & psychology (104)

- **Settling rarely feels like misery; it usually feels 'fine,' which is why it is so insidious.** A job going fine, a partner perfect on paper, shrinking yourself to keep the peace. They cite a Harvard Business Review figure that 85% of people worldwide feel actively disengaged at work, which they call settling, not just burnout. Choosing comfort 'kills off your future self.' _[Harvard Business Review]_ — [2026-01-05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xjw7AkB4dn4)
- **High achievers rationalize settling with lines like 'I'm lucky to have this,' 'it's not that bad,' and 'everyone would kill for this job.'** Hearing yourself say these is a sign you're settling but not admitting it; it is hardest when life looks shiny and Instagrammable on the outside, creating cognitive dissonance between looking good and feeling good. — [2026-01-05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xjw7AkB4dn4)
- **Complete the sentence 'I'm scared to walk away because...' and your fear will reveal where you've tied your worth.** Naming and labeling the fear makes it easier to judge whether you are actually settling. — [2026-01-05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xjw7AkB4dn4)
- **Cognitive dissonance between your values and your actions shows up physically as irritability and anxiety; your body knows before your brain does.** They cite the term's origin in a 1957 Stanford study. In the post-episode reflection Cherie adds that tightness in your chest while listening is data you can act on. _[cognitive dissonance (1957 Stanford study)]_ — [2026-01-05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xjw7AkB4dn4)
- **People stay too long because of fear of regret, sunk cost fallacy and external validation; flip it by asking what if you were afraid of staying.** Staying may be more dangerous in the long term than leaving; the scariest thing is a future of regret for not having acted. _[sunk cost fallacy]_ — [2026-01-05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xjw7AkB4dn4)
- **Simply admitting to yourself that you're settling can take years and may be the most important first step.** Jean gives listeners grace: fully embody the realization, then come back to the exercises days or a week later. Cherie adds that big changes (breakups, job changes) make life harder before it gets easier, but you won't regret it in 1, 5 or 10 years. — [2026-01-05](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xjw7AkB4dn4)
- **Be comfortable with who you are and never try to be something you're not; the trappings of rank can wall you off from others.** (Admiral Mike Rogers) Rogers says he didn't much like the trappings of being a four-star admiral, and that his favorite part of a 37-year career was being a junior ensign on a destroyer learning something new every day. — [2026-01-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bk56RJgqcFc)
- **Be ready to argue the contrary of your own recommendation, because everything has trade-offs.** (Admiral Mike Rogers) In the White House Situation Room, Obama asked for Rogers's opinion and then told him to argue the opposite, using the Socratic method; it took a mental reset but he recognized it as part of a sound process. _[Barack Obama, Socratic method]_ — [2026-01-12](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bk56RJgqcFc)
- **Trust your first instinct because it reflects subconscious processing of information, but where you can afford to, give yourself time before acting on it.** (Holly MacDonald) Jean says she has moved from over-analyzing to trusting her first instinct after repeatedly finding it was right; Cherie always feels the gut signal but tends to rationalize it away. _[Fortune Questions]_ — [2026-01-19](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=N3dGhIFWMNY)
- **Fresh starts at temporal milestones like a new year or birthday genuinely work as reset points.** The hosts reference the Wharton 'Fresh Start Effect' study showing landmarks are good times to reset and start anew. _[Wharton, Fresh Start Effect]_ — [2026-01-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4lZ8EsTExrQ)
- **List far more goals than you need (69 to hit 37) so you can include trivial, guaranteed and wildly aspirational items and still finish the year feeling 100% successful.** Jean used to feel dissatisfied hitting 20 of 36, so she expanded the list; items ranged from 'file taxes on time' and 'donate clothes' to attending her Dartmouth 15-year reunion. _[Dartmouth]_ — [2026-01-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4lZ8EsTExrQ)
- **Vision boards work by keeping your vision in front of you daily so you subconsciously work toward it, and they only have to make sense to you.** The sisters have vision-boarded for ten years, made their biggest posters ever for 2026 from magazine clippings (jewelry, travel, fruit, horses for the Fire Horse year), and Cherie has hosted vision board parties for girlfriends at Stanford. _[Stanford]_ — [2026-01-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4lZ8EsTExrQ)
- **Naming your biggest failure matters because it is the thing you can change going forward rather than repeat.** Jean says she is proud of what they built and would not change how they did it, but she can change how she goes about 2026. — [2026-01-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4lZ8EsTExrQ)
- **Stop caring what acquaintances think of you; people who talk about people negatively are a different category from people who talk about ideas and growth.** Jean wants to leave behind worrying about whether old friends mock her evolution; she cites a podcast clip where a creator suspected friends were sharing clips of him and the response was that they are not his friends. — [2026-01-26](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4lZ8EsTExrQ)
- **You cannot make progress on a problem until you name it, discuss it, and put it out into the world.** The hosts frame the whole episode as the first step in solving male loneliness: naming and discussing it openly so it becomes understandable rather than something to fear. — [2026-02-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F783rcVaDqw)
- **Loneliness statistics are probably underreported because loneliness is self-reported and admitting it to yourself is painful.** Cherie notes it 'makes you even more lonely to admit that you're lonely', so the data relies on men recognizing and confessing a feeling they don't want to own. — [2026-02-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F783rcVaDqw)
- **There is a difference between feeling lonely and being alone; you can be alone in a healthy way and lonely while surrounded by people.** Jean felt lonely in late 2025 despite living with her sister and having a boyfriend because her life had narrowed to three relationships and work; Cherie praises solo activities like going to movies and dinners alone as refreshing 'you time'. — [2026-02-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F783rcVaDqw)
- **The more useful frame than 'male loneliness' is 'male alienation', which points to the society, economy and educational system creating the problem.** (Ron Busby) Ron argues everyone feels some loneliness and it shouldn't be pathologized as unique to men; alienation names who benefits, e.g. a social media infrastructure that profits from people being on phones rather than outside together. — [2026-02-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F783rcVaDqw)
- **Anger is a useful and necessary driving force for change; if we are not angry we won't be motivated to fix things.** (Ron Busby) Ron makes this point when Cherie gets upset about men only now confronting body standards women have faced for decades. — [2026-02-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F783rcVaDqw)
- **Men should read feminist literature to build a baseline understanding of a world where women are equals and partners in building community rather than sexual objects.** (Ron Busby) Ron cites Judith Butler and Nikki Giovanni as thinkers who have long written about how patriarchy harms men, and promises a starter reading kit in the show notes. _[Judith Butler, Nikki Giovanni]_ — [2026-02-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F783rcVaDqw)
- **Lonely men are ripe for exploitation, so interventions should be careful that the 'service' they are directed toward actually benefits the community.** The hosts caution that purpose-seeking men can be steered toward harmful causes; accepting that men can be victims lets us intervene in that victimization with more care. — [2026-02-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F783rcVaDqw)
- **Empathy is not a finite resource, and deep empathy for lonely men is required to make progress on the problem.** Cherie's takeaway is a new appreciation for men who are both opting into and victims of the situation; Jean resolves to ask the platonic men in her life directly whether they are lonely and how dating and therapy are going. — [2026-02-02](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=F783rcVaDqw)
- **The best ROI is spelled YOU: investing in your own skills has unlimited upside while every other asset class is capped.** (Codie Sanchez) For the next 90 days, learn as much as possible about money, ideally deal-making, via 'skill stacking' as Scott Adams describes; her $47 three-day virtual MSM Live event is her cheapest entry point. Information alone is not enough: 'if information was enough, librarians would be rich', so time-box learning, then go implement. _[Scott Adams, MSM Live]_ — [2026-02-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=s3fe7uZlKMI)
- **Prepare to win: a pound of pre-production is worth ten of post, and fear mostly bleeds away once you have prepared.** (Codie Sanchez) The hosts add that women tend to over-prepare, and building the muscle means learning you need less preparation each time; 'prepare to win' also means expecting to win. — [2026-02-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=s3fe7uZlKMI)
- **Love yourself more than you need to be loved by others, so that you are OK walking away, saying no, and choosing rather than needing a partner.** (Sabrina Zohar) Her mother's saying; most online dating advice is about self-abandonment (how do I get you to like/validate/choose me), and if you are doing that you have already lost yourself. — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **The number one tool for anxiety is the 'power of the pause': put one minute between stimulus and response so you respond instead of react.** (Sabrina Zohar) Knowing the theory is like knowing how to deadlift intellectually; you still need to practice the implementation. The pause is step one before any regulation technique. — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **When triggered, ask 'how old do I feel and where did I learn this?' because the reaction is usually a childhood core belief projected onto the other person, not about them.** (Sabrina Zohar) When someone doesn't text back and 'the pinch doesn't match the ouch', you rarely feel like a 35-year-old; you feel seven, and it's your dad. This gives you agency and reveals that you hyper-fixate on people who don't offer clarity because it gives you dopamine. Her book on patterns, 'Why Am I Like This?', comes out in October. _[Why Am I Like This? (book)]_ — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **Showing yourself self-compassion releases more dopamine than external validation, so validating your own feelings closes the loop in your brain instead of waiting for someone else.** (Sabrina Zohar) Saying 'given everything I've been through, no wonder this makes me feel this way' gives the brain finality, and the hosts note it breaks the cycle of needing the text back. — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **The difference between intuition and anxiety is urgency: intuition is calm and immediate, anxiety is frantic and urgent.** (Sabrina Zohar) Fear responses are fight, flight, freeze and fawn (and anxious people can be avoidant too). 'Why haven't they called me' is urgency; a secure response is 'they're at work, I'll hear later' without assuming ill intent. — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **Growth requires learning to grieve, because people who benefited from your lack of boundaries will be threatened when you start setting them.** (Sabrina Zohar) Expanding your 'window of tolerance' (how much you can handle before hyper-arousal or shutdown) means having uncomfortable conversations and accepting that some friendships have run their course. She notes she once 'married her father' in a toxic relationship and got out by learning to say no. _[window of tolerance]_ — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **You won't feel better until you feel; an emotion takes about 90 seconds to run its course if you sit with it instead of shaming and blaming yourself for it.** (Sabrina Zohar) Describe the feeling's color, shape and texture and it dissipates. The brain loves to finish things, so if you don't create your own closure it will hyper-fixate until it gets one. Feeling bad about feeling bad ('I should be over it') is shame learned from survival tactics. — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **Grieve by doing inner-child work: speak to your younger self with the compassion adults didn't give you, telling her it was never her job to make people happy.** (Sabrina Zohar) Sabrina walks Cherie through this live, and posts a photo of 'little me' on Instagram daily to reconnect. Attacking yourself with 'you should have known better' is talking to a six-year-old the way adults once did. _[IFS / parts work]_ — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **'I should have seen this coming' is the illusion of control, learned as childhood hypervigilance, and the social consequences you fear from walking away usually never materialize.** Cherie describes a traumatic friendship breakup a year ago with a friend she treated like a sister who couldn't accept her boundary; she stayed too long to avoid rocking friend-group boats, but after ending it none of the feared consequences happened and she feels better. 'If me saying something pushes you away, I never had you to begin with.' — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **Self-abandonment is saying yes when you want to say no, and society teaches women in particular to stay small; if you don't stand for something you fall for everything.** (Sabrina Zohar) Every woman she has met has said yes instead of no in some sexual, professional or personal context. Even 'go kiss your uncle' teaches kids they have no choice, but as an adult you do. Rewiring takes repetition and 'small micro yeses' (she has to do 3 breaths and say 'I'm safe' before every meal due to blood-sugar issues from dysregulation). — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **You are not a snowflake: everyone shares the same core fears ('is there something wrong with me, do I belong, am I too much'), and that makes you human, not broken.** (Sabrina Zohar) She prefers 'a longer table, not a higher fence'; being on an island is what makes you feel alone. — [2026-02-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=42BkJGOjQLo)
- **AI is a good starting place for people who have no one to talk to, using prompts like 'act as an expert life coach, ask me questions one at a time, no personally identifiable information, then give five actionable strategies.'** The prompt comes from a Forbes article and targets procrastination but can be adapted to anything, such as late-night sleep habits; it follows up their episode on male loneliness. _[Forbes]_ — [2026-03-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4J3C-Rrzpo4)
- **AI solves the cold-start problem of journaling by asking you a daily question that covers your day and your progress on self-improvement goals.** Both sisters have journaled on and off for years and say the awkward 'what do I write' moment stops many people; a friend also asks for tailored affirmations to step into her power as a startup founder. — [2026-03-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4J3C-Rrzpo4)
- **Upload photos of handwritten journal entries and ask what behaviors or actions to think about further, as a supplement to, not replacement for, therapy.** A friend who already sees a therapist uses this for thought starters; the hosts suggest using it as pre-work so you arrive at therapy sessions better prepared. — [2026-03-16](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4J3C-Rrzpo4)
- **Asking is a muscle to train in every domain, and girls must be taught to speak up at puberty, exactly when they start to pull back.** (Mandy Ginsburg) The hosts note it is especially hard if you did not grow up asking; it applies to promotions, relationships and family, not just pay. — [2026-03-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ifV2k4vcvrE)
- **Saying a goal out loud or writing it down makes it real and helps it happen.** Cherie has been doing The Artist's Way daily journaling and finds that written-down thoughts tend to happen; Mandy agrees that voicing an ask moves it from your brain into the physical world. _[The Artist's Way]_ — [2026-03-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ifV2k4vcvrE)
- **Compete with yourself more than with others.** (Mandy Ginsburg) Asked who she secretly competes with, Mandy answered herself, and says she knows it. — [2026-03-23](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ifV2k4vcvrE)
- **Choose conviction over consensus and live a contrarian life based on something you believe that others don't.** Both sisters left hyper-successful corporate tracks to be creators; Jean adds that being unmarried and childless by choice is her contrarian bet. — [2026-04-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=9cdiQonikNU)
- **Nobody stays very successful for long without strong pain tolerance; start small to learn your pain threshold, because when you can't take the pain you make mistakes.** (Imran Khan) He has seen hedge fund managers whose losses wrecked their lives and marriages; such people should never be in investing. The same applies to athletes and founders. — [2026-04-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=AHOKAM47sS0)
- **The pedestal in business is the halo effect: one impressive trait, like charisma or a Stanford-dropout pedigree, outshines all the red flags and people stop doing diligence.** Elizabeth Holmes was pattern-matched to Zuckerberg and Jobs as a dropout (the show about her is literally called The Dropout) and Adam Neumann was treated as god-like, while everyone ignored how they behaved inside their companies. When the story gets too good, people stop doing emotional or investor diligence. _[Elizabeth Holmes, Theranos, Adam Neumann, WeWork, Mark Zuckerberg, Steve Jobs, The Dropout]_ — [2026-04-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PsDiVdzKIBo)
- **Know your own boundaries (physical, time, emotional) before entering a relationship, especially if you know you seek external validation, or the other person will lead you wherever they want.** Cherie admits she is susceptible to 'you're obsessed with me, I love this' due to growing up with immigrant parents, so she needs to know herself and her boundaries first. — [2026-04-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=PsDiVdzKIBo)
- **There is a 'perception gap': people almost always appear more confident than they feel because internal states like a pounding heart do not come across.** (Matt Abrahams) Students who watch themselves back consistently report 'I appeared more confident than I felt', which becomes a major confidence boost; you know what you intended to say and what your body felt, the audience does not. — [2026-04-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aTPhjLku45Y)
- **Self-judgment with the volume 'turned up to 11' eats cognitive bandwidth and kills connection; aim for connection, not perfection.** (Matt Abrahams) Your brain is like a CPU with limited bandwidth; every window of evaluation taxes it so you cannot adapt and adjust. In the improv game 'Shout the Wrong Name', a student froze because calling a chair a cat was 'not wrong enough' since both have four legs. _[Shout the Wrong Name, Adam Tobin]_ — [2026-04-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aTPhjLku45Y)
- **Audiences are rooting for you, not grading you like American Idol judges, and there is no single right way to communicate.** (Matt Abrahams) Speakers imagine judges holding score cards while the audience is open-armed waiting for value; watching an awkward speaker is unpleasant, so people want you to succeed. The hosts add that listeners do not know what you were supposed to say. — [2026-04-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=aTPhjLku45Y)
- **Loss aversion makes a $100 loss hurt more than a $100 gain feels good, so avoid checking your portfolio when the market is down and stay the course.** (Vivian Tu) She admits checking her own portfolio on a down day and having to laugh and take her own advice. The hosts propose a women's financial group chat as emotional support to buy rather than panic, since men effectively have one on Reddit. _[Reddit]_ — [2026-05-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ULlhVsNmU9M)
- **Unbrainwash yourself with a daily mirror conversation: every dollar you made you earned, you are paid market rate, you are the only person who can do what you do, and you owe nobody anything.** (Vivian Tu) She reminds herself each morning that 'only good things happen to lucky girls like you' and that she now has what she dreamed of five years ago. — [2026-05-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ULlhVsNmU9M)
- **A visible target keeps you going: like never hiding the Peloton progress bar, knowing exactly how far you have left to run toward your number is motivating.** (Vivian Tu) She compares it to a trainer telling her whether the set is 12 or 15 reps and Barry's counting down 60 seconds; if you are someone who swipes away the tracker this benchmarking may not help you. _[Peloton, Barry's]_ — [2026-05-04](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ULlhVsNmU9M)
- **Twenty years of competitive golf, a self-sufficient individual sport, built the thick skin, self-belief and grit that transfer directly to entrepreneurship.** (Jason Wilk) His original goal was to go pro; when he was not good enough, redirecting the same determination into business was an easy transition and he says he has never questioned his self-belief. He also grew up middle class with no backstop, always needing a job after school and never living beyond his means. — [2026-05-11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=hxtgSnFdQ3E)
- **You decide how you feel about getting older (and about parenting): if you plan on having a hard time, you will, and if you reframe it as accumulating wisdom and power, you won't.** (Sabrina Jalees) Sabrina's mother told her this when she worried that early teenage comedy success would make aging hard; Sabrina says this choice-of-frame is at the core of her parenting philosophy. — [2026-05-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OzsWwmX-D50)
- **Use parenting as an opportunity to break perfectionist habits rather than fearing it will amplify them.** (Sabrina Jalees) Jean fears entering motherhood as an A+ student fixated on doing everything best. The guests cite Marie Kondo publicly abandoning her tidiness system after another child, and Sabrina's wife loosening her military-cleanliness upbringing because it is not sustainable with two kids. _[Marie Kondo]_ — [2026-05-18](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=OzsWwmX-D50)
- **Breaking your routine, through travel, museums or exercise, is the most reliable way to generate new ideas.** (Evan Spiegel) Spiegel is highly routine-oriented, so changing his frame of reference is what unlocks creativity; he came up with a new idea in the car that morning. He finds the most fun is building on a seed of an idea with a team. — [2026-05-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0IYBJXS-1t8)
- **Everyone is creative, but creativity gets suppressed by fear as we age, so the fix is a repeatable creative process rather than waiting for lightning to strike.** (Evan Spiegel) The Stanford product design program teaches need-finding by talking to people, iterating on many solutions, taking them back for feedback, prototyping and testing; making the process structured removes the scary, amorphous part and empowers many people to create. Cherie adds that she took an improv class taught by a professional clown at Stanford, and quotes friend Jim Keyes: creativity is intelligence having fun. _[Stanford product design program, Jim Keyes]_ — [2026-05-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0IYBJXS-1t8)
- **Being the first to a new content genre means enduring being called cringe until the world catches up and makes rules around you.** Cherie pioneered 'this is what it's like to be a product manager at a big tech company' short-form videos around 2020-2021; people initially called it cringe and companies later wrote social-media-at-work policies because of the genre, which Jean links to the same persistence entrepreneurs need. — [2026-05-25](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0IYBJXS-1t8)
- **What got you here will not get you to the next step, and the teeth-gritted grind mentality of your 20s is not sustainable into your 30s.** Cherie says grinding late nights got them their careers but burns you out; there is no honor in grinding just to grind, and she is shedding that belief for the new decade. — [2026-06-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iAt9bx4ENpA)
- **Investing in your whole self, including appearance, is healthier than believing your only value is your brain, a message Asian women rarely hear.** Cherie learned hair, took a makeup class with Kelly Zhang and got her colors done in the last year and wishes she had done it earlier instead of focusing only on being smart and hardworking. _[Kelly Zhang]_ — [2026-06-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iAt9bx4ENpA)
- **Success is not grinding alone; it comes from bouncing ideas off other people and from friendship.** Jean points out Cherie is working today with her best friend from college. — [2026-06-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iAt9bx4ENpA)
- **A 'failure' with 70 views is still 70 real people, and you never know who is in the room: Ally's self-made YouTube video with her dog got her cast in Toy Story without an audition.** (Ally Maki) Pixar's casting department found the video on YouTube and said 'that's the character'; the hosts tie it to the Lady Gaga line that in a room of 100 people you only need one to believe in you. _[Toy Story, Pixar, Lady Gaga]_ — [2026-06-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iAt9bx4ENpA)
- **Beautiful things come from moments of discomfort, and the second half of running toward the hard thing is knowing people will support you on the other side.** AAGC would not exist without people who wore the shirt or talked about it in rooms; teaming up is a morale booster because 'this is hard, but at least we're in this together'. _[Asian American Girl Club]_ — [2026-06-01](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=iAt9bx4ENpA)
- **Use your 'hyphens' (immigrant, person of color) as an advantage and treat every room as a chance to debunk a stereotype.** (Kofi Ampadu) As a Ghanaian-American immigrant, Kofi says people of color usually worked hardest to get into the room, and he asks himself which stereotype he can debunk while there. — [2026-06-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6aXU9HjWnK8)
- **Whenever you are stressed, ask the one foundational question: what can I do to change it? If nothing, stop stressing; if something, start with the small action.** (Kofi Ampadu) Kofi says this protects his mental health. During the 2020 BLM protests he channeled stress into a TechCrunch article, 'Black Minds Matter', analyzing how underrepresented Black guests were on prominent startup podcasts and urging hosts to book Black product, engineering and investing experts. The hosts call this pairing high agency with acceptance of what you cannot control. _[TechCrunch, Black Minds Matter, Black Lives Matter]_ — [2026-06-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6aXU9HjWnK8)
- **Self-awareness is the basis of self-growth, and you need a measurement system of your own to know your baseline.** The hosts say you cannot get where you want to go without knowing where you are; Kofi's daily method is time-intensive, but the point is to design your own daily, weekly, monthly or annual check-in, as the sisters did in their goal-setting episode. — [2026-06-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6aXU9HjWnK8)
- **Make space for failure by time-blocking your wallowing to about a day, then move forward, because excuses are the cheapest thing to buy.** (Kofi Ampadu) Kofi allows himself a day on the couch with Netflix and junk food when a goal is missed, then reconfigures. Unless it is something like a job loss, a setback usually leaves you in the same spot you were in before, so just pick up from there. — [2026-06-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6aXU9HjWnK8)
- **Failure is healthy; if you aim for perfection before acting you will move slowly and never try new things, which is why large incumbent companies do nothing worthwhile.** (Kofi Ampadu) Kofi cites coaches telling kids 'if you're not failing you're not trying hard enough' and Adam Grant's finding that the best language learners start speaking on day one and accept embarrassment; he is applying this while learning French. His version: move fast, just don't break things. _[Adam Grant]_ — [2026-06-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6aXU9HjWnK8)
- **Anything worth having is hard now and easy later, so optimize for the long term.** (Kofi Ampadu) Like going to the gym, the early phase sucks and shows no results, but once results arrive the habit becomes easy. Kofi likes being around 'captain of my ship' people who accept short-term pain for long-term change. — [2026-06-08](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6aXU9HjWnK8)
- **Early adversity is a huge advantage, not something to begrudge, and most constraints on what you can achieve are self-made.** (Fernando De Leon) Fernando grew up on the South Texas/Northern Mexico border, lost his father at 12 and was the youngest of six, yet says those circumstances were a real advantage. In a $30 trillion GDP economy there are near-infinite pockets where wealth can be created if you have the stomach and self-belief. _[Forbes self-made score]_ — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **Everybody has fear; the move is to understand it, compartmentalize it and leverage it with a certain aggressive resentment rather than let it stop you.** (Fernando De Leon) Real estate was comfortable but software and engineering scared him; he says the people who succeed go right at their weakness head-on with anger rather than a soft feeling. The brain must be trained like a weak spot in the body, and if you are comfortable you are not doing it right. _[Batman / The Dark Knight]_ — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **When a problem looks like a binary yes-or-no, find a creative way to get it off the binary.** The Chick-fil-A workaround inspired this rule; Cherie also applied it by combining her LinkedIn Learning ed-tech career with content creation to become one of the first education-based influencers rather than choosing one or the other. _[Chick-fil-A, LinkedIn Learning]_ — [2026-06-15](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sZFplGf0Y08)
- **The Values Bridge rank-orders your 16 values and measures the 'authenticity gap' between how much you hold each value and how much you are actually living it.** (Dr. Suzy Welch) Built with a team of engineers and psychometricians as the basis of Welch's PhD; about 65-66,000 people took it in the first weeks after its May 6 release. The hosts say it gives you a vocabulary to name things you already felt. _[Values Bridge, Wittgenstein: 'the limits of my language are the limits of my world']_ — [2026-06-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Asm0bCXqd1s)
- **No value is good or bad; values are choices with consequences and the tool lets people talk about them without judgment.** (Dr. Suzy Welch) Welch has eudaimonia at 15 of 16 and says her joy is in work and achievement; Jean was embarrassed that beholderism was her number one until Welch reframed it as a common value that is also one of her own top values. — [2026-06-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Asm0bCXqd1s)
- **Achievement (wanting success others can see) is only the number 11 value for Gen Z but ranks high for business school types, which is why both sisters have it in their top four.** (Dr. Suzy Welch) Cherie has achievement at number 2 and Jean at number 4; Welch says business schools select for it and Gen Z students ask 'who needs that'. — [2026-06-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Asm0bCXqd1s)
- **Beholderism, caring how things look including yourself, is often a proxy for wanting harmony and control over the one thing you can control: your environment.** (Dr. Suzy Welch) It is the top value for almost every influencer Welch has worked with; Jean read dozens of interior design books at age 12 and rearranges flowers that clash with a room. Welch advises Jean to check a future partner's beholderism because it bumps into restaurants, houses and vacations. _[Architectural Digest, Vogue runway shows]_ — [2026-06-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Asm0bCXqd1s)
- **Values are fairly stable from about age 25, but seismic life events like a death, divorce or having a child can reorder them, so results are a snapshot in time.** (Dr. Suzy Welch) Jean's belovedness dropped to 11 after breaking an eight-year engagement in which she thinks she over-expressed it; Welch's own belovedness went to 16 after her husband Jack died. The hosts plan to retake the test yearly to track the gap. _[Jack Welch]_ — [2026-06-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Asm0bCXqd1s)
- **Treat your mistakes as 'the wisdom project': wisdom comes from what you did wrong, not what you did right.** (Dr. Suzy Welch) Welch says she gathered her wisdom from a long list of mistakes; the hosts connect this to Cherie's mantra DILMO, 'do it, learn, move on', invented around age 22, where moving on is the hardest part. _[DILMO (do it, learn, move on)]_ — [2026-06-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Asm0bCXqd1s)
- **Confronting yourself with an objective tool is painful but the most revealing thing you can do, and accepting every part of yourself makes you more successful and happier.** The hosts value that the test is science-based, unlike Myers-Briggs or Enneagram, and that it asks 'whose life did I just live', a question that scares Cherie into looking at her authenticity score honestly. Cherie's gap on her top five values was low, which felt affirming. _[Myers-Briggs, Enneagram]_ — [2026-06-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Asm0bCXqd1s)
- **Abundance mindset comes from having abundance: earlier desperation (needing a job to pay student loans) was rational, and now being in a good place lets you choose partners and brands.** Cherie says she didn't have the privilege of an abundance mindset when she graduated with loans; Jean says holding too tightly to 'the only chance' is scarcity mindset she held onto too long. — [2026-06-29](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Asm0bCXqd1s)
- **For many high achievers with perfect-looking resumes, achievement is a coping mechanism and a trauma response to feeling 'not enough'.** (Jane Marie Chen) Jane says people with perfect outsides often come from histories of trauma; they feel they must do more, be more and prove more, and burn out in the process. She sees this especially in entrepreneurs and social entrepreneurs. _[Like a Wave We Break, Embrace]_ — [2026-07-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jGj7bm3v0kY)
- **Pressure to succeed creates healthy drive until it tips into 'needing to prove', so develop awareness of what your motivations really are.** (Jane Marie Chen) Jane says wanting to do good for healthy reasons and needing to prove yourself coexisted in her; the hosts extend this: if your drive comes from a place you are comfortable with, use it as courage, but if it is to prove something to the exterior world it is the darker side of the shadow. — [2026-07-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jGj7bm3v0kY)
- **Internal family systems 'parts work' treats your overachiever, perfectionist and inner-critic parts as protectors of younger parts carrying shame, and asks for compassion toward each rather than banishing them.** (Jane Marie Chen) Jane realized her save-the-world part was protecting a very young part that never felt enough; an ex nicknamed her warrior part 'Genghis Khan'. She recommends the book No Bad Parts and offers a free parts-work exercise on her website. _[Internal Family Systems, No Bad Parts]_ — [2026-07-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jGj7bm3v0kY)
- **Everything you seek externally you have to find internally, because when you rely on external validation the goalposts keep moving.** (Jane Marie Chen) At a van der Kolk psychodrama retreat Jane recruited a Santa-Claus-looking man and later a friend to play her 'ideal father' reading scripted lines, and felt nothing. She only felt something when she read the lines ('you're enough, you're worthy') to a photo of herself at age five that sits on her desk. _[Bessel van der Kolk]_ — [2026-07-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jGj7bm3v0kY)
- **Control is an illusion; give something your all and then surrender the outcome, because the magic happens when you let go.** (Jane Marie Chen) For ten years Jane tried to control everything at Embrace and met setback after setback; the minute she let go, Tony Robbins came in out of nowhere and saved the company. Before her book launch, overwhelmed by pressure to make it a bestseller, she held a ceremony in Honolulu throwing her only copy into the ocean (then burning and burying it under a tree). Jean adds that over-optimizing leaves no room for serendipity beyond your imagination. _[Tony Robbins, Embrace]_ — [2026-07-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jGj7bm3v0kY)
- **Self-compassion is the rubber coating on the glass ball of your identity: untangle achievement from who you are so one failed speech or unpopular post cannot shatter you.** Jean offers the glass-ball image; Jane endorses it, saying we are worth more than the sum of our achievements or our social media likes, and worries about a younger generation whose sense of self collapses when a post underperforms. — [2026-07-06](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=jGj7bm3v0kY)
- **The clarity you are looking for is probably hiding behind one very simple test, and if you wait for the perfect moment someone scrappier will beat you to it.** The mini exercise is to take a problem you have not tackled and test it today with the simplest possible tool: a Google Form, an Instagram post or a text message. — [2026-07-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gxzHnCraPqI)
- **Staying stuck is worse than being wrong; a decision framework lets you be wrong safely, so adopt 'I may be wrong, but I'm not confused.'** People delay decisions out of fear of looking wrong. Cherie quotes LinkedIn chief product officer Tomer Cohen's saying, which she would adapt to 'I may be wrong but I have clarity.' The exercise is to map two or three outcomes for a decision living rent-free in your brain; it does not have to be perfect, just on paper. _[Tomer Cohen, LinkedIn]_ — [2026-07-13](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gxzHnCraPqI)
- **Change equals opportunity: you cannot control what happens in the environment, but you have full agency over how you respond to it.** (Jim Keyes) Keyes notes the acronym CEO in 'change equals opportunity' and says all commerce begins and ends with something changing. He took the Blockbuster CEO job to move it to digital streaming, could not predict the Lehman Brothers collapse, and had to pivot from transforming the company to saving it through restructuring and a sale to Dish Network. He could not change being born into a screwed-up situation, but chose not to be its victim. _[Blockbuster, Lehman Brothers, Dish Network]_ — [2026-07-20](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bq53ee_o4AM)
- **Confidence is not something you are born with; it is learned, and the formula is three words: prepare to win.** (Jim Keyes) Keyes got the phrase from author Harvey Mackay (Swim with the Sharks Without Being Eaten Alive) while writing his confidence chapter.
